# Job 20-42

## CHAPTER 20

This chapter contains Zophar's second and last charge against Job. Eliphaz and Bildad give a third charge afterwards. But Zophar forbears, either because Job had satisfied him, Chap. 21, before his turn came to speak again, or else because he wanted matter, or else because he saw Job so resolute in his own defense, that he made account beforehand that he should lose his labor. Compare this chapter with the 11. and you shall find, that here is nothing new, except it be, that being with his fellows beaten from their first opinion, that wicked men could not thrive in the world, and that by the strength of Job's arguments, he does, as they had done before, confess they may thrive for a while, but come to a shameful ruin, and gives Job no hope of restoration, as he had done before, Chap. 11. He was nothing moved with Job's miseries, nor his entreaties, nor his solemn profession of his faith, but takes occasion from Job's last words, Chap. 19. 29. full of love to him and his fellows, to fall more foul upon him than before. In this chapter. 1. There is an insinuating preface, ver. 1, 2, 3. 2. A large narration from verse 4. to the last verse. 3. A brief conclusion in the last verse. In the preface see first the speaker described, verse 1. 2. His necessity of speaking, as he conceived, verse 2. 3. The cause of his speech, and manner, how he would speak, verse 3.

*{Then}* See on Chap. 4. 1. & 8. 1.

*{answered}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Zophar the Naamathite}* See on Chap. 2. 11.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Therefore do my thoughts cause me to answer, and for this I make hast.}* He could no longer hear Job with patience, but his thoughts burning within him long for a vent. I had thought to have let you go, and not have meddled any more in this matter, but that your contumelious words do compel me to take another course, and to give you this ready answer.

*{Therefore}* He intimates that this necessity of speaking arose from Job's last words, You bid us to be afraid of the sword, and of God's judgments, this speech of yours urges and hastens me to give an answer. I cannot with patience hear you any longer, but must interrupt you. Others refer it to the words following, Therefore my thoughts urge me to answer, because I have been reproached by you, vers. 3.

*{do my thoughts}* I do not speak rashly though I make haste to answer, I have thought seriously of the matter, before I speak. I have a multitude of arguments in my mind to oppress you withal, and I must utter them. See on Chap. 4. 13.

*{cause me to answer}* See on Chap. 15. 13. Or, *answer me*. That is, prompt me, what I should say to you by way of answer.

*{and for this}* The former cause makes me not only to answer, but to do it quickly. It is so great a reproach, that I cannot bear it any longer. Others read it, *and because*. That is, because I am of a quick apprehension, and my nature urges me to a speedy answer.

*{I make haste}* Heb. *my haste is in me*. I am afraid, I shall forget many things spoken by you, if I do not answer quickly.

**V. 3. ***{I have heard the check of my reproach, and the spirit of my understanding causeth me to answer.}* Two things moved Zophar to answer at this time. The one, because he conceived that Job had put a great disgrace upon him not to be borne. The other, because he thought himself wise enough to answer for himself, and to remove this aspersion from him. As I have heard your sharp rebukes, so my knowledge will afford me sufficient matter to make you an answer.

*{I have heard}* See on Chap. 13. 17.

*{the check of my reproach}* Such a reproof, as tends to my disgrace. He means in those words of Job, Chap. 19. 29. which were spoken to his fellows, as well as to him, but he applies them to himself particularly. See on Chap. 19. 5. 2 Sam. 13. 13.

*{and the spirit}* See on Chap. 15. 2. upon the word, *Wind*. He means his reasonable soul here, though some understand it, as if he spoke of the Spirit of God.

*{of my understanding}* Heb. *out of my understanding*. It makes me to speak out of that understanding God has given me, and not out of passion, as you deserve, I should speak to you.

*{causeth me to answer}* See on verse 2. For the word see on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 4. ***{Knowest thou not this of old, since man was placed upon the earth}* Now he comes to the narration of those judgments, which shall fall on wicked men, and destroy their prosperity. This he propounds in this verse and the next, amplifies from thence unto the last verse and concludes all in the last verse. In the proposition he appeals to Job's conscience by way of interrogation in this verse, and sets down the thing to be proved in particular in the next verse. He secretly taxes Job of gross ignorance, or willful opposition of the truth. Is it possible, O Job, that you should be ignorant of this, which almost every man knows, and which is proved by the examples and experience of all ages, since men began to inhabit the earth? I cannot think you so ignorant, but rather fear that you dispute against your conscience.

*{Knowest thou not}* Certainly you do or should know it. The word not is not in the original, but must be added to make up the sense. As it should be also, 1 Sam. 2. 27, 28. Did I not plainly appeare, and did I not chuse him, &c.?

*{this}* Which I shall propound (in the next verse) of the sudden destruction of wicked men.

*{of old}* That this was of old. It signifies long since, or a long time past, to wit, from the beginning of the world, as it is expounded in the words following. Sometimes it signifies long hence, or a long time hereafter, as Psal. 132. 14.

*{since man}* From the beginning of the world. Since it was first inhabited. This is no new thing, but has been God's constant proceeding from the beginning. And therefore it is a marvel, that so wise a man should be ignorant of it. Or, *since Adam*, the first man of all.

*{was placed}* Heb. *he did place man*. To wit God, who uses to place and seat men, where he pleases in the world. It is translated placed, Jer. 5. 22.

*{upon the earth}* See on Chap. 9. 24.

**V. 5. ***{That the triumphing of the wicked is short, and the joy of the hypocrite but for a moment}* In this verse is the hinge of the whole controversy. Job had by force of argument driven them from one fort already, that God did not use to give prosperity to wicked men, now they fly to a second, that if he do, yet it is but for a while, and he quickly ruins them.

*{That the triumphing}* That the pomp of the wicked cannot be fetched from the times of antiquity, and that the joys of hypocrites vanish away in a moment. The word signifies crying out for joy or singing, as men in great prosperity use to do.

*{of the wicked}* See on Chap. 3. 17.

*{is short}* See on Chap. 17. 12. It is not long since it began. It is of no antiquity. Heb. *from neer*. So this phrase is used for gods, that came newly up, Deut. 32. 17.

*{and the joy}* Their prosperity, which makes them rejoice and sing.

*{of the hypocrite}* See on Chap. 8. 13. This may be added, lest Job should say, I am no wicked man, I worship the true God, and then Bildad might reply, yet you may be a hypocrite, and so it seems you are, because your prosperity ends in misery, and God's hand lies heavy upon you.

*{but for a moment}* The beginning of the verse sets out the beginning of their prosperity: the end of it sets out the end thereof. It is but a while since it began, and it will soon come to an end.

**V. 6. ***{Though his excellency mount up to the heavens, and his head reach up to the clouds}* He now sets out the sudden ruin of ungodly men by divers elegant similitudes of things that make a great show, and suddenly decay. The first is taken from a tree exceeding high like that in Nebuchadnezzar's dream, Dan. 4. 11. yet suddenly cut down. The flourishing whereof is set down in this verse, and the perishing of it in the next. Their height is so great in their prosperity, that they may seem to be lifted up into heaven, and with the crown of their head to touch the clouds, but their fall shall be great and sudden, Psal. 37. 35, 36. And all this is spoken to prove Job a hypocrite, because he fell so low from so great a height.

*{Though}* See on Chap. 9. 15. & 16. 6.

*{his excellency}* Or, *height*. For look what men excel others in, therein have they outstripped others. Hence kings and princes are called נשיאים because they are higher, and more excellent than others.

*{mount up}* Heb. *ascend*. The word עלה 1. signifies to go up, as Gen. 35. 1. 2. To excel others, Prov. 31. 29. 3. To shoot forth, as boughs and blossoms do in a tree, Gen. 40. 10. 4. To set out the coming forth of the light in the morning, Gen. 19. 15. 5. To vanish away, and appear no longer, Gen. 17. 22. 6. To come into one's mind, Jer. 7. 31. 7. To set out the arising of anger to show itself in a man's countenance, 2 Sam. 11. 20. 8. For the male cattle's leaping on the female, Gen. 31. 10. 9. To increase, 1 King. 22. 35. Here it may be used in the first or third sense, for going up, or shooting forth.

*{To the heavens}* That grow to the greatest height. A parabolical speech to set out an extraordinary height of a thing, Gen. 11. 4. Isa. 14. 13, 14. Dan. 4. 11, 20, 22. Matth. 11. 23. For the word, see on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

*{and his head}* See on Chap. 19. 9.

*{reach}* Though he were as prosperous as a tree is high, whose top reaches up to the very clouds, beyond which no man can see.

*{to the clouds}* Heb. to the cloud. Eliphaz had used this similitude of a tree, Chap. 5. 3. to show the spreading of wicked men like the roots of a tree: here Zophar goes on with it to set forth their abundant prosperity by the large, and high branches of the tree, which are more visible than the roots.

**V. 7. ***{Yet he shall perish for ever, like his own dung: they which have seen him, shall say, Where is he?}* The miserable and detestable end of the hypocrite is set out here by the similitude of man's excrements, which are cast away, as things loathsome.

*{Yet he shall perish}* Notwithstanding his former height, he shall be brought very low.

*{for ever}* So as he shall never recover his prosperity more. It comes from a word, that signifies to overcome. Misery shall overcome him, he shall be undone.

*{like his own dung}* A fit similitude taken from that, which every man understands. Nothing is more contemptible, than our excrements. We cast them away with contempt and disgrace, nor ever look after them more, but let them perish. Some translate it, *as soone as turned about*. But the former reading is better, 1 King. 14. 10. Zeph. 1. 17. 2 King. 9. 37. Hence idols are in disgrace called in the original, gods of dung, or stinking gods.

*{they which have seen him}* They who admired at his prosperity before and took special notice of it. For the word see on Chap. 19. 27. It is very likely that Zophar strikes at Job with his own weapons, for he had foretold such an end to come to himself, Chap. 7. 8.

*{shall say}* See on Chap. 16. 1. They shall not only think so, but speak of this great change to others with admiration, Psal. 58. 11. Gen. 27. 33.

*{Where is he?}* We wonder, what is become of him, that was lately so high above others: now he lies low enough, Isa. 14. 16, 17.

**V. 8. ***{He shall fly away as a dream, and shall not be found: yea he shall be chased away, as a vision of the night}* Now he sets out the vanity, and sudden departure of wicked men's prosperity by the similitude of a dream; showing it to be imaginary and fleeting.

*{He shall fly away}* Like a bird. So the word signifies; and that is very swiftly.

*{as a dream}* Some understand it of the dream itself, which many times is as soon forgotten, as dreamed, and cannot be recalled by the dreamer, nor others. Such was Nebuchadnezzar's first dream, Dan. 2. 5, 8, 10. But it is better to be understood of the thing dreamed of, for dreams many times do remain, after men are awake, as Nebuchadnezzar's second dream did with him, Dan. 4. 10. The pleasure of a wicked man's prosperity shall pass away quickly, like the joy of a dream, when a man in his sleep imagines he has found a great treasure, and takes much content therein, but as soon as he awakes, he is very sad, because he sees no such thing, but finds himself very poor. Psal. 73. 19, 20. Isa. 29. 7, 8.

*{and shall not be found}* The wicked man's prosperity shall not be found. Or, the wicked man himself, that prospered so. He shall perish with his wealth, Act. 8. 20. Others read it, *which cannot be found*. Meaning the thing dreamed of; as before. Heb. *and they shall not finde it*. To wit, they that dreamed of wealth, shall not find it when they awake. For the word, see on Chap. 17. 10.

*{yea, he shall be chased away}* Driven away out of his stately habitation.

*{as a vision of the night}* That epithet is added, because some visions were seen by day, Chap. 4. 13. A vision is more than a dream. It is some visible shape appearing to a man in his sleep, which is not in every dream.

**V. 9. ***{The eye also which saw him, shall see him no more, neither shall his place any more behold him}* His destruction shall be not only sudden, but also so great, that they who saw him today, shall tomorrow see him nowhere, neither shall the men of the place, where he lived, ever more set eyes on him. For the exposition of this verse, see on Chap. 7. 8, 10. where Job speaks of as much himself, and Zophar here twits him with his own words, as if he would prove him a hypocrite out of his own confession.

*{The eye also}* He is so clean vanished out of sight, that the eyes of those men, who looked on him before, shall never have any further sight of him. The word is sometimes taken for the instrument of seeing, as here, and Chap. 7. 8. sometimes for the sight itself, as Chap. 15. 15. & 18. 2.

*{which saw him}* In his flourishing estate, and prosperity. For the word see on Chap. 19. 27.

*{shall see him no more}* Heb. *shall not adde*. To wit, to see him again.

*{neither shall his place any more behold him}* He shall no more be seen nor found in his place by any inhabitant there. By the place may be meant the men of his house, city, or country. See on Chap. 18. 21. Others read it thus, *Neither shall the eye of his place any more behold him*. That is, the eye of the men of his place. And they repeat the word Eye out of the former part of the verse, because the word place, agrees not with the verb behold, in gender. Others thus, *Neither shall any eye behold him any more in his place*. Either at all, or in his former condition. As place seems to be taken Chap. 18. 21. All is to this end to show the great alteration, that shall light on the hypocrite.

**V. 10. ***{His children shall seek to please the poor, and his hands shall restore their goods.}* Now he goes on to set out the miseries that fall upon the wicked man, when once his prosperity begins to decline; and first he mentions the unhappy condition of his children, which must needs be very low, when they shall be glad to humor the lowest of the people. Yet this falls on them very justly, for their parents oppressed and ruined many families to enrich their children, and therefore it is just with God, that their posterity should be brought so low, that they should for want be forced to fawn upon the poor. And they must be very poor, that are forced to flatter poor men.

*{His children}* See on Chap. 14. 21. upon the word *Sons*.

*{shall seek to please}* Either to keep them from molesting them, and revenging themselves on them for the cruelty of their fathers towards them, or else to supply their wants, who are now grown poorer than they whom their parents oppressed to make them rich. The latter is right, for if their parents restored all in their lifetime, what need further revenge on their posterity? See the notes following on this verse.

*{the poor}* See on Chap. 5. 16. Others read it thus, *The poor shall oppresse his children*. He by his riches and might did oppress others, and make them poor: but his children shall be brought so low that they shall be oppressed by the poor, who will take all away, and leave them nothing. How great a judgment it is for one poor man to oppress another, see Prov. 28. 3.

*{and}* Rather, *for*. For here is a reason given, why his children should be so poor, that they should be forced to please other poor men, namely because the father before his death should be forced to restore all his ill gotten goods, and so have nothing at all left to provide for them. And they being as most rich men's children, brought up idly in expectation of great means from their parents, know not how to live of themselves.

*{his hands}* Those hands that took it away, must restore it again. For the word see on Chap. 15. 23.

*{shall restore}* Heb. *make to return*. For the word see on Chap. 15. 13. He shall be compelled to make restitution by law, or force.

*{their goods}* Heb. *his strength*. That is, the goods, which he has by his power unjustly taken away from others. For the word see on Chap. 18. 7. Or, *his iniquity*, that is, riches gotten by iniquity, Luk. 16. 9.

**V. 11. ***{His bones are full of the sin of his youth, which shall lie down with him in the dust.}* Returning now to the wicked man himself he sets out his ruin from the cause of it, the sins of his youth. And illustrates it by a similitude taken from a riotous young man, who by his debauched courses gets some foul disease, which first consumes his flesh, and afterward the marrow of his bones, and growing incurable leaves him not, till it bring him to his grave. So God's judgments upon the wicked man for his former sins consume him, and bring him to dust.

*{His bones}* It has his name from strength in the original, for the bones are the strongest part of the body, yet sin can pierce through the flesh, and infect them also, and so the whole man consumes, when not the tender flesh only, but also the harder bones waste away. No light punishment shall fall on him, which may be felt outwardly only, but so heavy an one, that shall pierce through and vex his most inward parts. So great afflictions are set out in Scripture by wasting the bones, Psal. 32. 3. & 38. 3. & 102. 3.

*{are full}* As he has had abundance of sin, so shall he have abundance of pains.

*{of the sin of his youth}* Formerly and long since committed by him, God will call him to account for those sins he has forgotten. Here Zophar pays Job again with the words of his own mouth, Chap. 13. 26. It comes from a word that signifies to lie hid, because young men lie hid in the family, and put not themselves forth to public employments in Church or Commonwealth. Or, *of his secret sins*. So this word is translated, Psal. 90. 8. And it may well befit this place. He thought his lusts and oppressions so closely carried, that they would never come to light: but God brings them to light long after by his judgments upon him in his old age.

*{which shall lie down with him}* His sin shall as it were follow him to the grave, and there bring him to corruption, and cleave to his dust, till his body be raised again to judgment. For the word see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{in the dust}* Heb. *on the dust*. The dust in the grave shall be like a bed, on which he shall lie. See on Chap. 14. 8. upon the word *Ground*.

**V. 12. ***{Though wickedness be sweet in his mouth, though he hide it under his tongue.}* An elegant similitude taken from gluttons, who devour the meat they like, not considering whether it be wholesome or unwholesome, nor regarding any measure. And so sometimes devour poison instead of meat; and sometimes surfeit on wholesome meats, which although they seem pleasant to him at the first, yet afterwards prove his ruin. This similitude he follows to the end of verse 23. And interlines the allegory with the thing intended, speaking in the same sentence sometimes of the one, and sometimes of the other. Which consideration helps much to the understanding of the text. As in this verse it is the meat, that is sweet to the mouth, and hid under the tongue, but wickedness is sweet to the soul and affections, and hid there. It appears verse 15. that this allegory is here intended to set out the oppression of wicked men, whereby they impoverish others, and hurt themselves in the end. Wealth is compared to pleasing food desirable for sweetness. Evil acts in getting, to a surfeit, or poison. Getting of it to digesting of such food, which yields much pleasure. God's punishments seizing on the oppressor in the end to a surfeit causing vomiting, and leaving emptiness, or poison burning in the bowels, and bringing death, as if a man were bitten by a viper, or some other venomous beast. The first sin was by eating the forbidden fruit, which makes this similitude the more pertinent. The pleasure gotten by eating, and oppressing is set out ver. 12, 13. The hurt that follows is described to the end of ver. 23. The sum of this verse and the next is, that as a glutton likes the relish of his meat, and keeps it in his mouth as long as he can, that he may the more taste the sweetness of it, before he let it down into his belly to nourish him: so the oppressor pleases himself with his manifold inventions how to oppress others, and rolls them up and down in his mind before he execute them.

*{Though}* See on Chap. 9. 15. & 16. 6.

*{wickedness}* רעה Is taken sometimes for evil of sin, Psal. 51. 4. sometimes for evil of trouble either falling on themselves, Jer. 28. 8. or by them brought upon others, 1 Sam. 20. 13. Gen. 50. 17. Here it is taken for the mischief intended to others by the oppressor.

*{be sweet}* Pleasing like sweet meat to the palate. Prov. 5. 3, 4.

*{in his mouth}* There we taste our meat. When it is gone down into the belly, it is past the palate and yields no more relish. For the word see on Chap. 15. 5. So sin is sweet to the soul in thinking of it, as God's word is to good men, Psal. 119. 103.

*{though he hide it}* Though he keep his purpose of oppressing others, as close in his mind, as gluttons keep their meat under the tongue, that no man may perceive the pleasure he takes in it. For the word see on Chap. 15. 18, 28.

*{under his tongue}* See on Chap. 15. 5. Hereby may be meant pleasant words, under which the oppressor hides his cruel purposes.

**V. 13. ***{Though he spare it, and forsake it not, but keep it still within his mouth.}* Though it be so sweet to him, that he will by no means leave thinking of it, as a man keeps his meat from going down, while he tastes any sweetness in it. The same thing mentioned in the former verse, though set out in other words, to show more fully the great delight oppressors take in plotting mischief to others. His intent is to accuse Job for a hypocrite, who made a fair show of piety, and goodwill to others, but took pleasure in plotting their ruin to enrich himself.

*{Though he spare it}* Though he abstain from letting it down into his stomach, and keep it in his mouth, that he may the longer taste of the sweetness of it.

*{and forsake it not}* Let it not for a time go down into his belly.

*{but}* So this particle is translated, Chap. 21. 1.

*{keeps it still}* The Heb. word carries a prohibition with it. He forbids it to go down, and keeps it as it were by force in his mouth.

*{within his mouth}* Heb. *in the midst of his palate*.

**V. 14. ***{Yet his meat in his bowels is turned, it is the gall of asps within him}* He goes on in the similitude, and shows, that as the glutton satisfied with the taste of his sweet meat, lets it down at length and then finds the inconveniences of it, so the oppressor at length executes his delightful plots, and perishes by them. And first he sets down his inward discontent in this verse. 2. His loss of his riches so gotten in the 15. verse. 3. His following ruin in the 16. verse. As the glutton's food, when once it comes into his belly is as bitter, as it was pleasing in his mouth: so shall the oppressor's pleasing plots prove very bitter to him in the end.

*{yet his meat}* Heb. *his bread*. See on Chap. 15. 23.

*{in his bowels}* In his belly. מעים 1. All the inward parts in Scripture phrase come under the name of bowels, Jer. 4. 19. 2. It signifies the heart, the chiefest of the inward parts, Psal. 40. 8. 3. The gravel that lies in the Sea, as the bowels within the body, Isa. 48. 19. Here it is taken in the first sense for the inward parts, whereof the belly is a part.

*{is turned}* The nature of it is changed, and it is become bitter, and unwholesome like the gall of asps, as follows in this verse.

*{it is the gall}* It is as the gall; the note of likeness is often left out to make the sentence the more emphatical. See on Chap. 7. 7. He had sweet thoughts in plotting, he shall have bitter ones, when he has executed his cruel devices.

*{of asps}* Whose gall it seems is very bitter.

*{within him}* As the glutton's meat is very bitter in his belly, so the oppressor's thoughts in his heart. So this phrase is used, 1 King. 3. 28. Psal. 94. 19. Heb. *in the middle of him*. The word קרב 1. Signifies the midst of anything, Exod. 8. 22. 2. The inwards which are in the midst of living creatures, Lev. 1. 9. & 3. 3. 3. The thoughts which are within the heart, Psal. 5. 9. & 49. 11. Here it is taken in the second sense, if you understand it of the glutton, but in the third, if it be understood of the oppressor.

**V. 15. ***{He hath swallowed down riches, and he shall vomit them up again: God shall cast them out of his belly.}* As the glutton's meat either because of the unwholesomeness of it, or his taking too much of it, puts him to a great deal of pain, until such time, as he have violently cast it up again: so the goods ill gotten by the oppressor shall lie so sore upon his conscience, that he shall never be quiet in mind, till he have made restitution. He shall never digest his goods, which he has ill gotten and heaped up: but with extreme pain and torment he shall cast them up raw and indigested, God himself as it were pulling them violently out of his belly.

*{He hath swallowed down}* A metaphor from wild beasts, who swallow down their prey, and so destroy it. So rich men swallow down other men's riches greedily, easily, suddenly, secretly, irrecoverably, so that no man can tell what is become of them, no more than of the lesser fishes swallowed by the whale.

*{riches}* Other men's wealth and estates gotten from them by fraud and oppression, and converted wholly to his own use. For the word see on Chap. 15. 29. In all this he strikes at Job, as if he had gotten his great riches unjustly, and lost them again by the just judgment of God.

*{and he shall vomit them up again}* His wealth shall not long tarry with him, like meat which the stomach cannot endure, but soon casts it up again, Prov. 23. 8. He shall find more trouble to be rid of his ill gotten wealth, than he found pleasure in getting it. As a glutton vomiting up his morsels forgets the former sweetness of them.

*{God shall}* It may be he would keep them, but God shall force him to restitution. For the word God, see on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{cast them}* Take all away from him, and leave him as poor as a man cast out of his inheritance, that has nothing left, whereon to live. So much the word imports, Josh. 3. 10.

*{out of his belly}* Out of his house where he thought he had laid them up as safe as the glutton thought he had done his meat in his belly. For the word see on Chap. 15. 2.

**V. 16. ***{He shall suck the poison of asps: the viper's tongue shall slay him}* Here follows the lamentable end of the oppressor. He shall not only lose his wealth, but his life also by God's most just and severe judgment.

*{He shall suck}* He shall as surely be slain, as if he had sucked in poison. It is likely that he alludes to the great earnestness of oppressors to get away other men's goods, as children suck eagerly, little thinking those riches will poison him.

*{the poison}* The word ראש signifies the head, and because the poison of asps is in their head, it also signifies poison.

*{of asps}* Which is thought to be most mortal, and to bring a most quick and painful death to him that takes it.

*{the viper's tongue}* Another kind of poisonous beast, whose poison seems to be in his tongue. For the word Tongue, see on Chap. 15. 5.

*{shall slay him}* The word הרג signifies. 1. To take away the natural life of living creatures, Gen. 27. 41. 2. To destroy things that grow, Psal. 78. 47. 3. To threaten death by the prophets, which comes certainly to pass. As if the prophet's mouth were a sword to kill men, Hos. 6. 5. Here it is taken in first sense, to set out the fearful end of oppressors.

**V. 17. ***{He shall not see the rivers, the floods, the brooks of honey and butter.}* Though God give his blessing to others in abundance, yet the oppressor shall have no part thereof. If after his fall he labor to be rich again, yet God will cross him so as that he shall not obtain that plenty, which he may in his folly promise himself. The sum is, he shall never more enjoy his former plenty.

*{He shall not see}* For the signification of the word see on Chap. 19. 27. upon the word *Behold*. But when it has the preposition after it, it signifies beholding a thing with delight, or rather enjoying it, as Eccles. 2. 1. Psal. 106. 5.

*{the rivers}* Hereby is intended plenty of all good things flowing in like rivers and streams, Isa. 41. 18, 19. & 48. 18. Joh. 7. 38. It comes from a word that signifies to divide. For rivers are divided from the Sea, and divide themselves into streams, and divide shires, provinces, and kingdoms.

*{the floods}* The word carries with it plenty, and swiftness, as floods bring plenty of water and run apace.

*{the brooks}* The word signifies valleys also, because brooks are for the most part in valleys. These three words are used to set out a great increase of worldly comforts with variety. Some read it, *The floods of the brooks*. That is, those swelling waters that make the brooks arise and overflow.

*{of honey and butter}* These being two principal parts of nourishment in those parts, Isa. 7. 15. are used to set out plenty. And thereby is set out not only abundance of such things as are wrought without man's help, but also such as are produced by man's labor. They are called rivers, floods, and brooks, because the honey seemed to flow out of a fountain when the bees brought it forth in trees and holes of rocks without the help of man, as the rivers run. And the butter came from the milk, which flows from the cows, like streams.

**V. 18. ***{That which he laboured for, shall he restore, and shall not swallow it down: according to his substance shall the restitution be, and he shall not rejoice therein}* Here in plain words he speaks what before he spoke figuratively under the metaphor of vomiting, ver. 15.

*{That which he laboured for}* That is, his goods gotten with much pains. Or, That which the poor man labored for, and he by fraud and oppression got away from him, Deut. 28. 33. Job 39. 11. Psal. 78. 46. & 109. 11.

*{shall he restore}* To the poor, that labored for it, and from whom he took it. For the word see on verse 10. and on Chap. 15. 13.

*{and shall not swallow it down}* Here seems to be a contradiction to verse 15. where it is said he has swallowed down riches. But the answer is, either he speaks of his pains after his impoverishing, and before his violent death to get new riches, which he cannot swallow down as he did the former, but shall find God crossing him in all his labors, so that either he gets nothing, or has no time to eat of it, it is so soon taken from him again: or else he means by swallowing down not possession of his former riches, which he had already, but what he has gotten formerly, shall not any longer nourish him, or do him any good, though he have it: For we swallow meat, not only to make it sure, but also to nourish us.

*{according to his substance shall the restitution be}* Heb. *according to the substance of his exchange*. Some understand it thus. He shall fully restore all that he has gotten from the poor, either by the sentence of the judge, or to quiet his own conscience, or the poor shall take it by violence from him. Others take the word of exchange for recompense, as it is used, Chap. 15:31. and interpret it thus, He shall be as poor as ever he was rich before.

*{and he shall not rejoice therein}* He shall have little pleasure in his riches, when he sees them all taken away again.

**V. 19. ***{Because he hath oppressed, and hath forsaken the poor; because he hath violently taken away an house, which he builded not}* He sets down the causes of his ruin, to wit, his oppressing and forsaking the poor.

*{Because he hath oppressed}* What can be more just, than that he who has undone others, should be undone himself? The Heb. word signifies to smite, or shake, or crush.

*{and forsaken the poor}* Or, *and left men poor*. When he had undone them, he left them in a poor condition, and took no pity of them, but turned them out of doors as follows. Others read it, *Therefore he shall leave them poor*. To wit, his own children, Psal. 109:10.

*{because he hath violently taken away}* He was so covetous and cruel, that he not only took what he met withal, but invaded men's houses, and by force took away their goods from them.

*{an house}* See on Chap. 15:28.

*{which he builded not}* He has taken the houses of those men whom he has made poor, which he never paid for building of. Others take building for finishing or furnishing. He took away poor men's houses, but before he could make them fit for his own dwelling, God took him away. And then it would be read, *therefore he shall not build it*. The word בנה signifies. 1. To build a house, or a city, 1 King. 9:10. 2. To repair, or fortify a house or city, 2 Chron. 16:1. 3. To bring forth children, which do preserve the house or family, Ruth 4:11. 4. It is put for preserving men, exalting them, or making them to prosper, Mal. 3:15. They that work wickedness, are set up. Heb. *built up*.

**V. 20. ***{Surely he shall not feel quietness in his belly, he shall not save of that which he desired}* He proceeds to show how miserable this oppressor shall be in the end, when he shall have no quietness within him, nor content without him. And this he speaks to afflict Job, who as he had lost all, so Zophar conceives, he could have no quietness within himself.

*{Surely}* So this particle is translated, Job 8:6. yet here it may be, Therefore. See on Chap. 19:28. Because he has spoiled the poor, therefore he shall find no quietness in his own spirit.

*{he shall not feel}* Heb. *know*. To wit, by experience, or find it in himself. So the word is used, Eccl. 8:5.

*{quietness}* See on Chap. 16:12.

*{in his belly}* He continues still in the former metaphor of a glutton, who takes in so much to please his appetite, that his belly grumbles, and is unquiet, till he cast it up again. So a covetous man so overloads himself with other men's goods, that by God's just judgment it breeds in him much grief of mind, and many pangs of conscience. See on Chap. 15:2.

*{he shall not save}* Heb. *he shall not deliver*: Or, *snatch away*. The spoilers shall take away all from him, as he did from others, and he shall be able to save none of his goods.

*{of that which he desired}* Out of all his riches, and goods, which he so much delighted in, and desired to keep, he shall not keep any one thing to comfort him in his extremity. So ב is used, Exod. 14:28. Others read the whole verse thus, *Because he felt no quietnesse in his belly, he shall not save of that which he desired*. He would gladly have saved some of his goods, but because he was so covetous, that his heart was never satisfied with wealth, he shall lose all. So it sets out both his sin, and punishment. But the former reading is better, which sets down his sin, verse 19. and his punishment here.

**V. 21. ***{There shall none of his meat be left; therefore shall no man look for his goods}* As the glutton when he has cast up all again, has no food left in his belly, so he shall be brought so low, that he shall not have any of his goods left, or a bit of bread to eat. And whereas other rich men have very many friends and kindred gaping after their goods, he shall be so poor before his death, that no man shall regard him, that has nothing to leave them.

*{There shall none of his meat be left}* Heb. *There shall be no remainder of his meat*. He shall be deprived of all his riches, which he swallowed like meat, verse 15. others understand it of his children or kindred, and read it thus, *There shall be no remainder for his meat*, that is, he shall have neither child, nor kinsman, that shall inherit his riches, for he shall have none left for them to expect, as follows in the verse. And thus the word for the most part, if not altogether, in Scripture is used for the remainder of men, not of other things, as vers. 26. Chap. 18:19. & 27:15. For the remainder of other things they use not this word שריד, but נותר as Exod. 29:34. Lev. 6:16. & 7:16.

*{therefore}* Seeing no man is left, but his posterity cut off, therefore there shall be none to look after his goods.

*{shall no man}* None of his posterity: or, shall he not. Seeing he has nothing left, he shall have no hope at all of recovering his riches.

*{look for}* Wait for or expect his goods after his death.

*{his goods}* Heb. *his good, or goodness*. But it is frequently used for riches, or goods, as Gen. 24:10. Deut. 6:11.

**V. 22. ***{In the fulness of his sufficiency, he shall be in straits: every hand of the wicked shall come upon him}* The destruction of the oppressor is now further set out by his enemies. 1. Such as have been oppressed by him in this verse. 2. God the just judge, verse 23. 3. War, and weapons, verse 24,25. 4. Darkness and fire, ver. 26. 5. Heaven and earth, ver. 27. He amplifies the loss that shall come upon him in this verse by the circumstance of time. It shall come, when it is least expected, and feared, when his prosperity is at the highest. He sets it out by the former similitude of the glutton, when he has filled himself to his own content.

*{In the fulness of his sufficiency}* When he has fully enriched himself, so as might abundantly content him. Though a covetous man never think that he has enough. When he has scratched and scraped together, so much as might satisfy the most greedy, and covetous wretch, that lives upon the earth, Luk. 12:19. or when he thinks he has enough to live happily. Or, when he has glutted himself with all manner of dainties, Luk. 16:19,22,23.

*{he shall be in straits}* He shall be brought suddenly to extreme necessity, and great wants. A wonderful change.

*{every hand}* None shall keep away, but every one formerly oppressed by him shall lay hold on him to be revenged. For the word hand, see on Chap. 13:21. & 15:23. Or, *each hand*. They shall be so offended at him for impoverishing them, that they shall lay load upon him with both hands.

*{of the wicked}* One wicked man shall ruin another. Or, *of him that is in misery*. The poor shall set upon him for want of means, chap. 3:20. Or, *of the laborer*, whose wages he detained. Or, *of him that was troubled by him*. The fruit of whose labors he has gotten away. shall come upon him, see on Chap. 15:21.

**V. 23. ***{When he is about to fill his belly, God shall cast the fury of his wrath upon him, and shall rain it upon him, while he is eating}* Now he sets out God's severe proceeding against the wicked oppressor, amplified also by the time which God chooses to afflict him.

*{When he is about to fill his belly}* Or, *When he hath enough to fill his belly*. The meaning is, either that this judgment comes upon him, when he is pampering himself in his plenty, as verse 22. or else if he have somewhat left to fill his belly afterwards, God pours his wrath upon him, as he is feeding on it. For the word Fill, see on Chap. 15:2. For the word belly, see on Chap. 15:2. This may be understood also of getting abundance of riches, see on verse 15,20,21.

*{God}* Who is mentioned vers. 15. and who uses to rain down judgments in anger upon ungodly men, and who is often spoken of, and spoken to in this book.

*{shall cast}* Or, *send forth*, He will no longer keep in his judgments in the chains of his mercy, but let them forth to be revenged on this wicked oppressor. For the word, see on Chap. 14:20.

*{the fury of his wrath}* Not ordinary, but extraordinary judgments upon him, the sad effects of his furious wrath. Heb. *the burning of his nose or face*. As a man no longer hides his anger, when he shows it in his countenance. These two words are often used together in Scripture to set out God's severe wrath, Numb. 25:4. 1 Sam. 28:18. 2 Chron. 29:10. For the word, wrath, see on Chap. 14:13.

*{upon him}* Upon the oppressor spoken of before.

*{and shall rain it upon him}* Or, *make to rain upon him*. To wit, the heavens. For God's judgments are as it were showered down thence like rain, Psal. 11:6. Rom. 1:18. An allusion to the destruction of the old world, or of Sodom, Gen. 19:24. upon which place God's wrath came like a shower upon them that expected it not in a sunshining day, God's judgments can no more be resisted, than rain from heaven falling upon our heads. The rain also sets out plenty of judgments as there are of drops in the rain.

*{while he is eating}* God will suddenly destroy him, while he is feeding himself, Numb. 11:33. or getting abundance of riches, which is his food, verse 15. Or, *into his flesh*. That is, God will pour his wrath upon his body, or upon the flesh which he eats, or wealth that he gets, so that neither shall do him any good at all. All this reflects upon Job, who had showers of judgments like a violent storm of rain falling upon himself, and his children in the first chapter, and therefore is by Zophar condemned for an hypocrite.

**V. 24. ***{He shall flee from the iron weapon, and the bow of steele shall strike him through}* God is here compared to a great warrior, who comes armed with a sword and a bow to destroy the oppressor, who while he thinks to avoid the one, is slain by the other, as Ahab was, 1 King. 22:30,34. The meaning is, that while the wicked man seeks to avoid one danger, another unseen, farther off, and more grievous falls upon him, and destroys him. As it befalls a soldier in the war, who thinking himself safe, while he can keep off his enemies' sword, is unexpectedly slain with an arrow from afar.

*{He shall flee}* That is, if he flee from one peril, he shall certainly fall into another, Isa. 24:18. Jer. 48:44. Am. 5:19. And for the phrase, see Mal. 1:4. They shall build, but I will throw down. That is, If they do build, I will throw it down again. For the word, see on Chap. 14:2.

*{from the iron weapon}* The sword, spear, or battle-axe wherewith men fight nigh at hand. For the word, iron, see on Chap. 19:24.

*{and the bow}* That is, the arrow shot out of the bow. For that is it, which pierces, yet the bow is said to do it, because it sends out the arrow, as the carpenter is said to cut, though the axe do it immediately.

*{of steele}* Of such matter were their bows made then, as the heads of our crossbows are now.

*{shall strike him through}* From afar, and unexpected, whereas a sword wounds nigh at hand, and may more easily be prevented. Or, *shall change him*, that is, shall kill him. Heb. *shall pass through him*. So the word is used, Isa. 8:8.

**V. 25. ***{It is drawn, and cometh out of the body, yea the glistering sword cometh out of his gall; terrors are upon him}* God is here compared to a valiant warrior, who having spent his arrows upon his enemy comes nearer to him, and fights with his sword, till he have thrust him into the gall, and left him subject to the terrors of death. Zophar still pursues Job with his own words, see Chapter 16:13.

*{It is drawn}* To wit, the arrow, that struck him through, verse 24. and then God finding him not slain, uses his sword to destroy him. Or the sword spoken of afterwards in this verse. For the antecedent taken out of the words following, see on chap. 18:13. Heb. *He draws it*. And it is to be understood either impersonally as before. (See the like on chap. 3:3.) or, God draws it, verse 23. to wit, his arrow, or sword, Deut. 32:41,42. or lets the string go, that the arrow may fly out.

*{and cometh out}* The word יצא 1. signifies coming out of a place, as Gen. 4:16. 2. being born of one, or coming out of his bowels, Gen. 15:4. 3. coming out of the ground, as flowers and corn do, Job 14:2. 4. the coming forth, or arising of the sun, or stars, as Gen. 19:23. 5. the publishing of any thing by word of mouth, Esth. 1:17. 6. the failing of the heart when it is so fearful, that it is as it were gone out of the body, Gen. 42:28. 7. laying out of money, 2 King. 12:12. 8. going forth to war, Gen. 14:8. 9. going out of danger, or escaping by lot, 1 Sam. 14:41. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{of the body}* גוה comes from a word, which signifies eminency, and is used 1. for pride, Jer. 13:17. 2. for the back, which in beasts is higher than other parts of the body, Prov. 10:13. 3. by a figure of the part for the whole, for the whole body, Ezek. 1:11. 4. for a dead carcass, Judg. 14:8. 5. for the middle or inward part of a thing, Job 38:5. From among men. Heb. *out of the middle*, to wit, of the city or place of their habitation. Here it is taken in the third sense, as it is translated. The arrow or sword, that did pierce the oppressor's body is drawn out of it. Others take it for drawing the arrow out of the quiver, or sword out of the sheath, which is as it were the body of it, Numb. 22:23,31. Judg. 8:10.

*{yea the glistering sword}* The word properly signifies lightning, yet here it is put for the bright blade of a sword, which glisters like lightning. So it is used, Deut. 32:41. Nah. 3:3. Hab. 3:11. Ezek. 21:10. but with addition of the word, sword, or spear, which here is omitted.

*{cometh}* See on chap. 14:20. upon the word, *Passeth*.

*{out of his gall}* This intimates a deadly wound, for when the gall is let out, it kills men. See on chap. 16:13. others read it, *it cometh glistering out of his gall*. It being more terrible to the oppressor wounded to see the point of the arrow or blade of the sword, not only glistering with its own brightness, but also with the color of his gall, which it has pierced, and so prognosticates present death to him.

*{terrors are upon him}* The terrors of death, which now he sees and feels approaching affright his soul, see chap. 18:11. The Dutch translation reads it thus. Men shall draw out the sword, it shall come forth with the life, and glistering with his gall, it shall come forth: there shall be shrieks or lamentations for him. That is, he shall be suddenly taken away, and his shall be frighted, and weep for him.

**V. 26. ***{All darkness shall be hid in his secret places: a fire not blown shall consume him; it shall go ill with him, that is left in his tabernacle}* He still pays Job with those things, that befell him, for he had many calamities, which in Scripture phrase are called darkness, and some of his cattle were devoured by fire from heaven, and such as were left of his family doubtless were in a poor condition like himself, chap. 1. As if he had said, where the oppressor thinks he may most safely hide himself, there shall he find no assurance, but all full of obscurity and trouble. A flame of fire breaking out of its own accord without any man's blowing shall utterly destroy him, and they of his house shall not prosper after him.

*{All darkness}* All manner of trouble. The word כל as it signifies particulars, so sometimes it imports only some of all kinds, as Proverb. 1:13. For darkness, see on chap. 15:22,23.

*{shall be hid}* Troubles shall be like thieves that hide themselves in places of advantage to rob passengers. See on Chapter 18:10.

*{in his secret places}* Where he thinks to hide himself from the storm of God's anger. Others read it thus, *all darknesse is laid up for his hid treasures*. That is, God or men have taken order, that he shall lose his riches as well as his life, though he hide them never so secretly. So this word is taken, Psal. 17:14.

*{a fire}* Some heavy judgment, which like fire shall destroy him. So fire is used to set out consuming judgments. Amos 1:4,7,10,12,14. See on chap. 15:34.

*{Not blown}* A judgment not invented by those whom he has wronged, no nor by any man else, but sent immediately from God, like a fire arising of itself, no man knows how; and burning down an house. Calamities, the causes whereof are unknown, shall suddenly come upon him, and consume him. Others understand it for a secret fire not blown up to make any flame. And that agrees well with what went before. As fire not breaking out, burns all within, so a secret judgment shall consume him and his treasures in their secret places. Others understand it of a great fierce fire, that is broke out already, and needs no blowing. And then it sets out some great and eminent judgment lighting suddenly on the oppressor, like a fire that sets the house all in a flame before men perceive it. Others take it for unquenchable fire, that needs no blowing, nor cannot be blown out, like that Matth. 3:12. And then it sets out such a judgment, that cannot possibly be removed, but will certainly ruin him.

*{shall consume him}* Heb. *shall eat him up or devour him*. See on chap. 15:34.

*{it shall go ill}* Such as are not destroyed with him, but abide in his house after him of his posterity shall never thrive, but be always crossed by God for his sins.

*{with him that is left}* See on vers. 21.

*{in his tabernacle}* See on chap. 5:24.

**V. 27. ***{The heavens shall reveal his iniquity: and the earth shall arise up against him}* Because Job before chap. 16:17,18. had called heaven and earth to bear witness of his innocency, here Zophar puts him in mind, though in general terms, as speaking of another, that heaven and earth had borne witness against him already, and been God's instruments in taking away his children, and cattle by wind, fire, Sabeans, &c. Chap. 1. As if all creatures high and low did conspire to ruin him, and give men cause to believe, that God's curse was upon him and his for his sins.

*{The heaven}* See on chap. 9:8. & 11:8.

*{shall reveal}* Shall discover his secret sins by God's open judgments upon him for the same coming from heaven.

*{his iniquity}* See on chap. 7:21.

*{and the earth}* See on chap. 15:19.

*{shall rise up against him}* See on chap. 14:12.

**V. 28. ***{The increase of his house shall depart, and his goods shall flow away in the day of his wrath}* Some tie these words to the former thus: Heaven and earth shall bring judgments upon him, for the one shall not bring forth such plenty of provision for his house as formerly, and the other shall rain down floods of waters to carry away that little crop, which he has. Others understand it generally of the loss of his provisions by thieves or any other way.

*{The increase}* Such things as grow out of the earth, or on trees yearly. Some understand it of his children sent into captivity. But the word is never so used.

*{of his house}* Rather *for his house*, as Broughton reads it. For they were such as the earth brought forth for the use of his household. For the word, House, see on chap. 15:28.

*{shall depart}* Either the earth shall not bring them forth to him, or they shall be taken away by thieves, or burnt, or some way or other taken from him, see chap. 14:20.

*{and his goods shall flow away}* They shall be quickly scattered, as waters flow away. Or, *they shall be carried away wholly, and violently, as with a flood*. Others read it thus, *they shall flow away*. That is, the oppressors themselves; as Psal. 90:5. Or, *there shall be floods*. You need not enquire how they shall depart, God will send floods to sweep them clean away.

*{in the day of his wrath}* At that time, when God shall manifest his wrath (vers. 23.) against them by plaguing them, chap. 24:1. Prov. 6:34. & 11:4. Isa. 13:13. Lam. 2:21. Ezek. 22:24. Zech. 1:15. Rom. 2:5. For the word, Day, see on chap. 14:14. For Anger, see on chap. 14:13.

**V. 29. ***{This is the portion of a wicked man from God, and the heritage appointed to him by God}* Here is the conclusion of Zophar's speech, suitable to Bildad's, chap. 18:21. This and no other portion can a wicked man expect from God. Or, if he do expect any better inheritance, he will be deceived, for this is that which God has God has allotted to him. Every word has an emphasis. Men may count wicked men happy, but by God's decree they are miserable. They may divide the wealth and honors of the world between them for a time, but God has provided a far other portion for them, and that by way of inheritance never to be parted from them.

*{This}* All these miseries, and the great destruction, which I have spoken of.

*{is the portion}* A metaphor taken from banquets, wherein every man had his portion or meat, and drink assigned to him, Gen. 43:34. Or from dividing goods by lot, wherein every man had that part that befell him, Psal. 16:5. or by gift without lot. Gen. 25:6. The meaning is the punishment he may as justly and surely look for, as a child does for his portion. See chap. 27:13. & 31:2. Psal. 11:6. Isa. 17:14.

*{of a wicked}* See on chap. 3:17.

*{man}* See on chap. 14:1.

*{from God}* The just judge, who will give every man his right. It comes from a word that signifies power, for all power and strength is from God. אלהים is used 1. for the true God, Gen. 1:1. And it is the first name, that is given to him in the Scripture. 2. for idols and false gods. As the picture of a man is called a man; so are they called gods, because they seem so to men, though they be utterly unworthy of that name, as having no divine power at all. Psal. 96:5. with Psal. 115:4. &c. Isa. 37:19. 3. for images of the true God, whom Jeroboam worshipped in the calves, 1 King. 12:28. and the Israelites in the wilderness, Exod. 32:5,8. 4. for angels, which are nearest to God in glory and power, far exceeding all earthly and bodily creatures in both. Psal. 8:5. 5. for great men, that come nearest to God in state, and power, especially magistrates and judges. Psal. 82:1. 6. to set out the greatness or excellency of any thing, though unreasonable, wherein it exceeds other things of the same kind, and comes nearest to God, Jon. 3:3. Nineveh was an exceeding great city, Heb. *a great city of God*. Here it is taken in the first sense for the true God.

*{and the heritage}* That which shall as surely befall him, as the inheritance to the heir, chap. 27:13. & 31:2.

*{appointed unto him}* Heb. *of his decree*. Or, *sentence*. To wit, that God has passed upon him. Others take it for his own blasphemous speeches against God, which shall be punished with heavy judgments. 1 Sam. 2:3,10. Jude ver. 15.

*{by God}* God as a just judge has allotted to him, and as a powerful God will execute it upon him. For the word see on chap. 8:3,5.

## CHAPTER 21

**Verse 1. ***{But Job answered and said}* Now Job answers to Zophar's last speech, chap. 20. which was the last of his second conflict with his friends. Note 1. the preface from verse 1. to verse 7. 2. The body of the answer from thence to the last verse. 3. The conclusion in the last verse. In the preface 1. the speaker is described verse 1. 2. He uses a gentle preface to mollify his former roughness of speech, that they might the more willingly hearken to him, and the better understand him, and not interrupt him, as Zophar had lately done. He persuades them to hear him patiently. 1. From the end of their coming, which was to comfort him, and that they could not do, if they would not hear him lay open his griefs, verse 2. 2. From the duty of one man to another, to hear him, before they censure him, verse 3. 3. From God's example, who heard his complaint quietly, and did not interrupt him, verse 4. in the beginning of it. 4. From the trouble that would come to his spirit, if they should deny him a vent for his sorrows, in the end of verse 4. 5. From the abundance of misery, which they saw on him already, verse 5. 6. From his own sad apprehension of it, verse 6.

*{But}* So this particle is translated, chap. 6:1.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1:1.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16:1.

**V. 2. ***{Hear diligently my speech, and let this be your consolations}* You pretend that you come to comfort me. How will that appear, when you will not with patience hear me utter my mind? This is the least comfort I can expect from you.

*{Hear diligently}* Heb. *Hearing hear*. The doubling of the word increases the signification, as Gen. 2:17. *In dying, thou shalt die*. That is, *thou shalt surely dye*, as it is there translated. For the word see on chap. 13:17.

*{my speech}* Which now I am to speak; and do not interrupt me, before I have done.

*{and let this be}* Your patient and quiet hearing of me.

*{your consolations}* Instead of those comforts you should, and seem to be willing to give me in my miseries. If I can have no more comfort from you, let me have audience. Or, *and this shall be your consolations*. I shall take this as a great comfort from you, that you let me vent my sorrows quietly in your hearing, which hitherto you have exasperated by your speaking.

**V. 3. ***{Suffer me that I may speak, and after that I have spoken mock on}* If you will hear me quietly defend mine own cause, I shall endeavor to speak such things, as shall not be a fit object of mocking, but if you think otherwise when you have heard my arguments patiently, I will as patiently bear your scoffs.

*{Suffer me}* The Hebrew word נשא has many significations. 1. To bear or bear with one, or suffer, or forbear, Gen. 13:6. 2. to lift up or advance, Isa. 5:26. 3. to produce or bring forth by words, Exod. 20:7. Not taking God's name is not taking it into the mouth, or not speaking of it. 4. to reckon or number, Numb. 31:49. 5. to take or to receive, 2 Chron. 11:21. 6. to regard or honor, 2 King. 3:14. 7. to remit or pardon, Psal. 32:5. 8. to take away, consume, or burn, 2 Sam. 5:21. Here it is taken in the first sense, for bearing with him, and letting him speak quietly, though he should utter something, that might be burdensome to them.

*{that I may speak}* That I may defend my cause, and answer your objections: see on chap. 13:22.

*{and after that I have spoken}* After I have said all I can or purpose to speak in my justification.

*{mock on}* Heb. *Do thou mock on*. Turning his speech to Zophar (and his eye also, very likely) who had last spoken, and whose whole speech he takes to be a great and continued mocking of him. And therefore he speaks now to him in the singular number, whereas before he spake to them in the plural number for audience. Neither does he give him leave to mock at him, but rather intimates, that he had no color to mock at him before, and conceives that he should now so solidly defend his cause, that Zophar should be ashamed to mock him any more. As if he had said, Then mock on, if you can pick any hole in my speech. When I have cleared my innocency, I shall not be troubled at your taunts.

**V. 4. ***{As for me, is my complaint to man? and if it were so, why should not my spirit be troubled?}* It is in vain for me to complain to you, I being conscious of mine own innocency will make complaint to God; for I see already, if I should be tried by my friends, it would go very hard with me, and my spirit would be sore troubled, as well it might.

*{as for me}* An emphatical expression, you may think I look no farther than men; but you are deceived, I look up to God.

*{is my complaint}* The declaration of my miseries.

*{to man}* See on chap. 14:1. Or, *of man*. If I had no greater things to complain of than man could inflict, I had less reason to complain so much, and yet I might have just cause of sorrow then, but I have greater cause to complain, for God's hand is heavy upon me. See the like chap. 6:2,3,4. Psal. 38:1,2.

*{and if it were so}* If I had to do with man only, I might justly be troubled, I see so much in your unkind dealing with me.

*{why should not}* He speaks very earnestly, and makes them judges. Can you that condemn me of impatience, show me any reasons, why I should not be troubled at such great calamities, as are upon me? Yea though they were such only as men could inflict upon me. Much more when much heavier things are inflicted upon me by God. Having such extraordinary pressures upon me, I may justly complain, and it is a very hard thing to keep a measure in complaining.

*{my spirit}* See on chap. 15:2.

*{be troubled}* Heb. *be shortened*. For the spirit of man is contracted with griefs, and enlarged with joy, Exod. 6:9. Judg. 10:16. & 16:16.

**V. 5. ***{Mark me, and be astonished, and lay your hand upon your mouth}* He accuses them of inhumanity, that passed by his sore afflictions, as light things, and persuades them to look better upon him; and to show that they have not put off all sense of humanity, by pitying him, and speaking no more against him. And that seeing him more miserable than other men, they should with patience and silence hearken to what he had to say for himself.

*{Mark me}* Heb. *Look upon me*. Consider my former upright life, and present miserable state. You will find that never any godly man was so sorely afflicted, and therefore pity me. Or, mark my words, which I shall now speak for myself, and they will convince you fully. For the word see chap. 5:1. Turn you toward me, and view me well, and you shall see, it is worse with me than you imagine.

*{and be astonished}* At the sight of my great miseries. Others read it, *and ye will be astonished*. You will see cause enough to make you so, see on chap. 17:8. His friends had seen him all this while, but they had not thoroughly considered of his miseries, else they would have been astonished before now.

*{and lay your hand upon your mouth}* You will see so much cause of pity and silence, that you will be ashamed to speak any more so harshly against me as you have done, See chap. 40:4. & 29:9. Judg. 18:19. Prov. 30:32. Mich. 7:16. you will then be content to be silent, while I speak to vent my sorrows, and clear myself. For the word, Hand, see on chap. 15:23. For the word, Mouth, see on chap. 15:5.

**V. 6. ***{Even when I remember, I am afraid, and trembling taketh hold on my flesh}* When I consider my former prosperity from which I am fallen, and the greatness of my misery, whereunto I am fallen, and your hardheartedness toward me, together with the prosperous estate of many a wicked man in the mean while, I cannot choose but tremble.

*{Even when}* So אם is translated. Psal. 63:6.

*{I remember}* When I seriously think of the things that have befallen me in such an extraordinary way.

*{I am afraid}* I am frighted as men are with troubles that come suddenly, and unexpectedly upon them. See on chap. 4:5.

*{and trembling}* As his mind was affrighted, so his body trembled. A great fear seized on him, that made his whole body shake.

*{taketh hold on}* See on chap. 18:20.

*{my flesh}* See on chap. 14:22. Heb. *my flesh hath taken hold on trembling*. See the like, chap. 18:20. Look on the notes there.

**V. 7. ***{Wherefore do the wicked live, become old, yea, are mighty in power?}* Job now goes from his admiration to his confutation of Zophar's oration in the former chapter. And herein 1. he shows that wicked men do prosper in the world, from this verse to verse 17. 2. that yet oftentimes they meet with troubles; from thence to verse 22. 3. that it comes from God's unsearchable decree, that some wicked men live and die comfortably, others miserably, from thence to verse 27. 4. removing the question from the general to the particular, he shows his own condition to be not unlike to many wicked men's in regard of troubles from thence to verse 32. 5. he answers what Zophar said in the end of the former chapter, concerning the violent death of ungodly men, verse 32,33. 6. He concludes all with a declaration of the vanity of their consolations, verse 34. In the first part, he sets out the prosperity of the wicked, in their own flourishing estate in this verse, in the prosperity of their children, verse 8. in the safety of their families, verse 9. in the increase of their cattle, verse 10. in plenty and thriving of their children, verse 11. in their pleasures and delights, verse 12. in their wealthy life, and quiet death, verse 13. 2. He sets out their impiety, which follows their prosperity, verse 14,15. 3. He shows his own dislike of their courses notwithstanding their prosperity, verse 16. In this verse he begins to overthrow Zophar's assertion of the short prosperity of wicked men in this world, and their sudden downfall, chap. 20:5,8. by the experience of all ages, and the testimonies of sense and reason. As if he had said, that which you say, O Zophar, of the short prosperity, and sudden destruction of wicked men is easily confuted by the experience of all ages. Do not we see that they live in health and strength, and lengthen their days to the utmost course of nature, and live in plenty all their days? This cannot stand with your assertion.

*{Wherefore}* Some take it to be an expostulation with God about the prosperity of wicked men, when he himself was in misery, like that, Jer. 12:1. Others that he would know a reason of Zophar, why the ungodly prosper so. But the truth is, it is an argument drawn from experience to confute Zophar's position. How can it be so, when we daily see the contrary?

*{Do the wicked}* See on chap. 3:17.

*{live}* To wit, in health, and strength. And Broughton renders it, *are lively*. So 1 King. 1:25. God save King Adonijah. Heb. *Let King Adonijah live*. To wit, in health and strength, 1 Sam. 25:6.

*{become old}* Live long upon the earth. He does not hereby intend to set out the infirmities of old age, as the word is used, Psal. 6:7. but the strength of those wicked men, that makes them outlive many others. As Dan. 7:9.

*{yea}* Lest any man should think they might live long in health, and yet in a low and mean condition, he adds this as a greater matter, yea they live in an higher fashion, than many better men. For the word, see on chap. 16:4. on the word, *Also*.

*{are mighty}* Or, *prevail*. Have more power than other men, and more store.

*{in power}* Or, *in wealth*. For these two ordinarily go together. See on chap. 15:29. see Psal. 17:14. & 73:12. Jer. 12:1. Hab. 1:16.

**V. 8. ***{Their seed is established in their sight with them, and their off-spring before their eyes}* As Zophar had in the former chapter particularly in several branches labored to show, that the prosperity of the wicked is very short, so Job here does elegantly describe the continuance of it in many particulars the better to convince Zophar of his error. And he begins with their children, which next after their own health, and wealth are the greatest comfort to them in the world. And it is likely, that he also has relation to Eliphaz his words, chap. 5:25. and tells him that the comfort of a numerous and rich posterity, which he promised to him upon repentance, is often bestowed upon wicked men.

*{Their seed}* Their children which come of their seed, Gen. 9:9. Some take it here for their children only, and bring their children's children under the word off-spring. Others take it of the seed sown in their fields, and the other word off-spring of plentiful crops. But the former interpretation, of their posterity, is better.

*{is established}* They shall not only prosper themselves, and live long, but also their posterity.

*{in their sight}* They shall see their children and children's children thriving in the world, and living prosperously in their families. They shall behold this with great delight. Heb. *before their faces*. For the word, Face, see on chap. 14:20. upon the word, *Countenance*.

*{with them}* Living as long as they do, and none of them, or few of them dying before them. Or, they prosper as much as themselves.

*{and their off-spring}* See on chap. 5:25. It may be either taken for posterity in general, or for children's children.

*{before their eyes}* In their sight, as before. The thing is doubled for greater assurance. As chap. 14:10,11. For the word, eyes, see on chap. 15:12. & 18:3.

**V. 9. ***{Their houses are safe from fear, neither is the rod of God upon them}* In this verse is set out the great immunity of these wicked oppressors, that not they only, but all theirs are free from those afflictions that befall better men.

*{Their houses}* See on chap. 15:28.

*{are safe}* Heb. *are peace*. That is, in a most peaceable, and safe condition, 1 Sam. 25:6. 2 Sam. 20:9. Isa. 41:3. Job 5:24.

*{from fear}* They are so quiet, that they fear not any harm to befall them from abroad to disturb their quiet. Some read it, *their houses have peace without fear*. Others, *in their houses there is peace from fear*. Others, *their houses are in peace without fear*. See on Chapter 15:21.

*{neither is the rod}* By God's rod is meant affliction sent from God. See on chap. 9:34.

*{of God}* See on chap. 4:9. & 11:5.

*{upon them}* Upon the oppressors themselves. Or, upon their household and family.

**V. 10. ***{Their Bull gendereth, and faileth not, their cow calveth and casteth not her calf}* As they have comfort in themselves, and their family, so likewise great increase in their cattle, which makes them to grow rich, and wealthy.

*{their bull}* Heb. *His bull*. Every oppressor's for the most part.

*{gendereth}* Heb. *makes to pass*. To wit, his seed, of which the calf is formed.

*{and faileth not}* Heb. *and casteth not away*. Loses not his seed at any time. Or, *and loseth not*. That is, he is so lusty, that he never refuses the cows. So the word is translated, Levit. 16:11. Ezek. 16:45.

*{their cow}* It comes from a word, that signifies to be fruitful, and it signifies a young cow. Such use to be most fruitful.

*{calveth}* Heb. *sendeth forth*. And the word imports safety, and deliverance.

*{and casteth not her calf}* Loses not her calf. Never comes before her time, nor brings forth a dead calf. A great increase must needs follow, where the bulls are never faint, nor the cows never miscarry, Gen. 31:38. Exod. 23:26.

**V. 11. ***{They send forth their little ones like a flock, and their children dance}* As they have some children grown, and prospering, verse 8. so they have little ones still coming on for their comfort at home, when their elder children are gone abroad.

*{They send forth}* They beget many children. Or, they send them abroad into the streets, or fields, that men may see their store. Others take it of the cattle, the bull and cow spoken of, verse 10. for the word see on chap. 14:20.

*{their little ones}* See on chap. 19:17. upon those words children.

*{like a flock}* Abundantly, so that they grow numerous, like a flock of sheep. Or, their greater cattle spoken of, verse 10. multiply like sheep, which is seldom seen, for the lesser cattle increase most, Psal. 107:41. Sheep bring forth more abundantly than greater cattle. Psal. 144:13. Ezek. 36:37,38.

*{and their children dance}* Their little ones being brought up at home like flocks do cheerfully and merrily skip up and down. This is a great delight to parents.

**V. 12. ***{They take the timbrel and harp; and rejoice at the sound of the Organ}* Some understand this verse of the children, whom they bring up with great cost to singing, playing on instruments, and dancing. Others of the parents themselves rejoicing in their prosperity, and solacing themselves with all kind of music. Others of both, the children playing on musical instruments, and the parents singing thereunto. Music is not here condemned, but their security, who lived merrily, and regarded not God, Amos 6:5.

*{They take}* The instrument into their hands to make music, Others read it, *They lift up*. To wit, their voice with the timbrel. They sing in consort with them that play. For the word see on verse 3.

*{the timbrel}* It is esteemed to be a wooden instrument with parchment at both ends, to be beaten on with sticks, much like our drums or tabors.

*{and harp}* An instrument of wood hollow with strings to be played upon.

*{and rejoice}* Take delight and pleasure in that kind of music.

*{at the sound}* See on chap. 15:21.

*{of the organ}* An instrument with many pipes like that which we now call organs. It comes from a word that signifies love, sport, delight. And it seems to be a very pleasing instrument. Such men used on feast days for mirth, Psal. 81:2,3.

**V. 13. ***{They spend their days in wealth, and in a moment go down to the grave}* They live long in an uninterrupted prosperity to the very minute of their death. They have no losses in their life, nor long sicknesses to bring them to their end. For Job did not intend here to show their punishment, but their pleasures lasting all their life, as appears by the words before and after.

*{They spend}* They finish or consume all their days in pleasure. Others read it, *They make their dayes old*. They live long in mirth.

*{their days}* All the time of their life, see on chap. 14:14.

*{in wealth}* Or, *in mirth*; Heb. *in good*. The Hebrew word טוב is used to express many things. 1. it signifies, that which is right and just, 2 Sam. 15:3. 2. that which is profitable, Deut. 6:11. & 30:5. 3. that which is pleasing, 2 Sam. 19:27. & 35.5. 4. that which is full or complete, Gen. 15:15. & 25:8. 5. that which is joyful or delightful. As festival times, 1 Sam. 25:8. Here it is taken in the second sense for riches, or in the fifth for mirth.

*{and in a moment}* They lie not sick, nor in pain as others, but are soon gone: in the twinkling of an eye, 1 Cor. 15:52. A moment is the least division of time. They die very easily without that sense of pain, which other men feel, Psal. 73:19.

*{they go down}* For graves are in the earth, and thither men's dead bodies descend, which walked upon the earth alive. It is also a descent from life to death.

*{to the grave}* See on chap. 7:9.

**V. 14. ***{Therefore they say unto God, Depart from us; for we desire not the knowledge of thy ways}* Now lest any man should think, that Job should speak of such wicked men, as had some eminent parts in them, he shows plainly in this verse and the next, that he speaks of men most notoriously wicked against God, which nevertheless lived in plenty and died easily. He sets out their wickedness, 1. by their rejecting God's acquaintance in this verse. 2. by disgracing his service in the next. For the first in this verse, They were open contemners of God's majesty, and did in effect bid him be gone, neither would they at any time suffer themselves to be taught, and instructed by him.

*{Therefore}* Because they prosper so, they despise God. So this particle is translated, Job 35:16.

*{they say}* It is likely, they would not be so gross, as to speak thus in their mouths, but in their hearts and ways, living as if they desired to have no acquaintance with God. Psal. 14:1. & 39:1. For the word see on chap. 16:1.

*{unto God}* See on chap. 8:3,5.

*{Depart from us}* If they be called upon at any time to know God, they utterly refuse it and stop the mouths of their own consciences, chap. 22:17.

*{for}* So this particle is translated, chap. 17:10.

*{we desire not}* We take no pleasure in walking in your ways, and therefore care not for the knowledge of them. They are accused of an affected ignorance, and despising of knowledge, when they might have it.

*{the knowledge of thy ways}* The understanding of your precepts, and of those things you would have us to do. He does not mean those ways God walks in, but those in which he would have us to walk. For the word way, see on chap. 17:9.

**V. 15. ***{What is the Almighty, that we should serve him? and what profit should we have, if we pray unto him?}* They think it a vain thing for them to trouble their heads with matters of religion, and lay bands of strictness upon themselves, who now live so much at liberty, and wallow in all worldly pleasures in their sinful courses. See the like expressions, Exod. 5:2. Mal. 3:14. chap. 35:3.

*{what is}* What is there in him, more than in others, that we should addict ourselves to his service? We see nothing extraordinary in him deserving so much respect. The like phrase is found, chap. 7:17. Psal. 8:4. Cant. 5:9.

*{the Almighty}* Some derive it from a word that signifies to destroy and lay all waste, Isa. 13:6. Joel 1:15. And they translate it, *The Almighty*. Others from a word, that signifies sufficiency. One who has sufficiency of all good things to give to others, Gen. 28:3. And they would have it translated, *All-sufficient*. Others from a word that signifies a dug, as if God did as it were out of his breasts nourish all creatures in the world, Gen. 49:25.

*{that we should serve him}* The word עבד signifies 1. to worship God, Deut. 6:13. 2. to serve men, Gen. 15:13,14. 3. to labor or work, Exod. 20:9. 4. to till the ground, Gen. 4:2. 5. to make one to serve, or serve ourselves of him, Ezek. 34:27. Here it is taken in the first sense for worshipping God.

*{and what profit should we have}* No benefit will come to us by praying to him.

*{if we pray unto him}* Heb. *if we meet him*. To wit, with our prayers, Jer. 7:16.

**V. 16. ***{Lo their good is not in their hand; the counsel of the wicked is far from me}* Here Job speaks in his own person that before spake in the person of wicked men, and shows his detestation of their impiety. As if he had said, I cannot but admire at the foolish security of these men, who contemn God, as if they had no farther need of his help, but had their happiness in their own hand. But they are deceived. All is in God's hand, who can take away their wealth, when he please. And therefore I will follow none of their wicked devices.

*{Lo}* This uses to bring in some strange thing beyond men's expectation. See on chap. 1:12. Such would be the sudden poverty of these ungodly men, when it came.

*{their good}* Their wealth. They can keep it no longer than God gives leave. They may fail sooner than they rose. For the word see on verse 13.

*{is not in their hand}* It is not in their power to keep their great riches. For the word see on chap. 15:23.

*{the counsel}* I am so far from envying their prosperity, that I cannot approve of their course of life for all their wealth, chap. 22:18. & 10:3. & 18:7. Psal. 1:1. In all these places counsel is put for a course of life, for wicked men as well as good, guide their lives by counsel good or bad.

*{of the wicked}* See on chap. 3:17.

*{is far from me}* I could never approve of it. It was not far from him in place, but in affection: it was abominable to him.

**V. 17. ***{How oft is the candle of the wicked put out? and how oft cometh their destruction upon them? God distributeth sorrows in his anger}* Some take these words, and all the rest to the end of verse 21. as a continued description of the prosperity of wicked men, and read all the verses by interrogation. Have you seen wicked men or their children often punished? It is very rare. But this crosses the former verse wherein he began to speak of the ruin of ungodly men. And it crosses verse 22. &c. wherein he shows that God in his wisdom lets one wicked man live prosperously all his days, and brings another to an untimely end. These words therefore are rather spoken by way of admiration. See on chap. 14:14.
