As we have seen many wicked men live in plenty, till they die, so oftentimes God brings them to untimely ends, and lets them feel much want and sorrow before they die.

*{How oft}* See on chap. 13:23.

*{is the candle}* Or, *lamp*. Some understand it of the riches of the oppressor, which shine for a time, but afterwards are taken away. Others of a violent death of the wicked man, when his life is taken away before it be finished by the course of nature, as a candle is put out before it be done. This agrees best with the destruction following in this verse.

*{of the wicked}* See on chap. 3:17.

*{put out}* See on chap. 18:5. & 6:4.

*{and how oft cometh}* For lively actions attributed to lifeless things, see on chap. 1:19. And for the word see on chap. 15:21.

*{their destruction upon them}* See on chap. 18:12.

*{God}* This is not in the original, but added to fill up the sense, See on chap. 20:23.

*{distributeth}* He gives to the wicked man his portion of sorrows, as parents give their children their portion of goods. The meaning is, he punishes ungodly men according to their merits, chap. 20:29.

*{sorrows}* The word signifies such sorrows, as women feel in their labor, which hold them like bands.

*{in his anger}* See on chap. 4:9. & 16:9. & 14:13.

**V. 18. ***{They are as stubble before the wind, and as chaff, that the storm carrieth away}* When God begins to deal with them in anger, they are no more able to stand before him, than chaff before a violent wind. They are soon blown away out of their place.

*{They are as stubble}* This similitude is often used in Scripture to set out the sudden destruction of ungodly men, chap. 27:21. Psal. 1:4. Isa. 17:13. & 29:5. Hos. 13:3.

*{before}* Heb. *at the face*. For the word, Face, see on chap. 14:20. upon the word, *Countenance*.

*{of the wind}* See on chap. 15:2. upon the words, *Vain knowledge*.

*{and as chaff}* Which is lighter than stubble, and sooner blown away.

*{that the storm}* It comes from a word, that signifies to make an end of a thing. A wind may leave something, but this sweeps all away before it like a whirlwind.

*{carrieth away}* Heb. *stealeth away*. That is, takes away suddenly, as a thief does men's goods, lest he should be taken himself. So Christ's coming to judgment is compared to the sudden and unexpected coming of a thief in the night, 1 Thes. 5:2.

**V. 19. ***{God layeth up his iniquity for his children: he rewardeth him, and he shall know it}* In this verse he shows that the punishment of the cruel oppressor shall be so sharp, that his children shall feel the smart of it. And this was proportionable to his great oppression, whereby he had undone other men and their posterity.

*{God}* See on chap. 4:9. & 11:5.

*{layeth up}* In the treasures of his justice to be produced in due time. He deals with the children, as he did with the parents. He does not presently punish them, but watches a time, when they think their condition most prosperous, that he may the more terrify them.

*{his iniquity}* The punishment of his iniquity. Others read it, *his riches*. Or, *his strength*. That is, his violence, who by his strength got other men's riches. God will keep this his cruel dealing upon record to punish his children for it with temporal punishments, that the world may see, how much God hates oppression. For the word, see on chap. 18:7.

*{for his children}* See on chap. 14:21. Either he will punish both him and his children; or if he suffer him to go to his grave in peace, he will ruin his posterity.

*{he rewardeth him}* The word שלם properly signifies paying of a debt. God by his justice owes them punishment for their oppression, and he will pay it. Sometimes it signifies to give a good reward for things well done, as Ruth 2:12. 1 Sam. 24:19. Sometimes an evil reward for evil deeds, verse 31. of this chapter. Judg. 1:7. 2 Sam. 3:39. Jer. 32:18. Some understand it of the punishment of his sons falling out in his days. Others, of his own destruction.

*{and he shall know it}* Or, *so that he shall know it*. He shall feel God's hand upon himself for his oppression. Or, he shall take notice, that God has plagued his children for it.

**V. 20. ***{His eyes shall see his destruction, and he shall drink of the wrath of the Almighty}* Lest any man should think, because he had said in the former verse, that God lays up the iniquity of the wicked man for his children, that he should escape himself, he adds, he shall see God's judgments lighting upon himself to destruction. And sets out that destruction by an elegant similitude, wherein God's wrath that shall light upon ungodly men is compared to a poisoned cup, which destroys them that drink it. See the like Psal. 75:8. Jer. 25:15. Rev. 14:10.

*{His eyes}* See on chap. 20:9.

*{shall see}* He shall feel by experience God's wrath in his destruction. The eye being the most noble sense is put sometimes for the other senses. For the word, see on chap. 19:27.

*{his destruction}* The Hebrew word here used is found no where else in the Old Testament. And therefore the sense must be helped by the coherence. And what else then destruction can be expected by him, that drinks of the wrath of the Almighty?

*{and he shall drink}* He shall not taste, but have plenty of it poured upon him and into him. Lest any one should think, he should but look upon evil, and not feel it, he adds, he shall drink of it. For the word see on chap. 6:4. & 15:16.

*{of the wrath}* It comes from a word, that signifies heat. For anger makes a man's passion boil within his breast, and heats his blood, and burns and consumes others.

*{Of the Almighty}* See on verse 15.

**V. 21. ***{For what pleasure hath he in his house after him, when the number of his months is cut off in the midst?}* A reason is here rendered why God puts off the punishment of ungodly men till after their death to lay it all upon their posterity. Because else they would live merrily all their days, and not regard what befell their posterity, when they are gone out of the world, and have no knowledge of worldly things, chap. 14:21. & 24:20.

*{For what pleasure hath he}* He has not, nor can have any content or delight in things done after his death.

*{in his house}* In his posterity, who are left in his family. For the word, see on chap. 15:28.

*{after him}* After his death. While he lives he may take much comfort in the safety and prosperity of his children: but when he is dead he can have no comfort therein, though his death should enrich them, nor be grieved at their ruin, although his fall should be the cause of it.

*{when the number of his months}* That is, his months numbered by God. When God takes him out of the world at the time, that God has set for that purpose. See on chap. 16:22. & 15:20. & 14:5.

*{is cut off in the midst}* To wit, from the rest of the months, which he might have lived by the course of nature. A metaphor taken from weavers, who cut off the web from the threads remaining, Isa. 38:12.

**V. 22. ***{Shall any teach God knowledge? seeing he judgeth those that are high}* It might be objected, that it is not always thus, neither does God always deal thus roughly with ungodly men, but sometimes more mildly with them. Job answers, that God, whose wisdom is far beyond ours, proceeds diversly in his dealings with ungodly men. Some he permits to live long, and in abundance, and to die in peace. Others he takes away with heavy and bitter judgment. Yet we that are low both in place and understanding, must not take upon us to censure God's proceedings, who is high in both.

*{Shall any teach}* Any man or angel. Or, any such as use to teach others. The nominative case included in the verb, as Gen. 50:26. They embalmed Joseph, that is, such as used to embalm dead men, verse 2. of that chapter. So here. Is there any so skillful a teacher, that will undertake to teach God? Men that disallow of God's proceedings, take upon them to be God's teachers, Isa. 40:13. & 45:9. Rom. 11:34. 1 Cor. 2:16.

*{God}* See on chap. 8:3,5.

*{knowledge}* Undertake to teach him, what to do, and how he should govern the world.

*{seeing he judgeth}* A judge should know what becomes him to do in all cases. You disparage God's wisdom by censuring his judiciary proceedings, who governs and judges all things.

*{those that are high}* We that are low must not censure God, who has the highest authority, and judges those that are higher than we, chap. 34:13,17,18,19. Gen. 18:25. Rom. 3:5,6. If he be fit to govern angels, that dwell on high in heaven, much more to govern men here below, as he does, verse 23, &c. Eccles. 5:8.

**V. 23. ***{One dieth in his full strength, being wholly at ease and quiet}* Now he comes to set out by examples God's various dealings with ungodly men. So that it has gone well with the one of them, and ill with the other, both in life and death. And yet both of them alike bad.

*{One}* Heb. *This man*, as if he had pointed to some one eminent wicked man whose prosperous life, and quiet death their eyes had been witnesses of. See the like expression, chap. 12:28. & 19:26.

*{dieth}* His prosperity continues without interruption; till he die. He never feels crosses, nor losses, nor sicknesses all his life long.

*{in his full strength}* Heb. *in his very perfection*. Or, *in the strength of his perfection*. In the highest degree of earthly felicity.

*{being wholly at ease}* Prosperous in estate, and healthful in his body, and peaceable in mind.

*{and quiet}* See on chap. 16:12.

**V. 24. ***{His breasts are full of milk, and his bones are moistened with marrow}* An allegorical description of the highest prosperity. He is like a fat cow, that gives store of milk. He abounds in health and wealth, in goods of body and estate. He is fat, rich, and well liking. He has store of riches, and pleasures.

*{his breasts}* Or, *milk pails*. So it may either set out plenty of strength or riches.

*{are full}* Himself has abundance of health and strength, or his cows give abundance of milk to fill the pails. For the word, see on chap. 15:2.

*{of milk}* He is as healthful or prosperous, as a cow, whose dugs are full of milk. Or literally, his cows yield abundance of milk, and so enrich him.

*{and his bones}* See on chap. 20:11.

*{are moistened}* Heb. *watered*. There is an elegancy in the original, His bones is watered. That is, every one of them. Not a bone wants moisture.

*{with marrow}* The word imports outward fatness, as well as inward moisture. For where the bones are full of marrow, there the flesh grows fat. It may be understood of a thriving body, or estate, or both. In such a flourishing condition many a wicked man continues, while he dies.

**V. 25. ***{And another dieth in the bitterness of his soul, and never eateth with pleasure}* As some wicked men live comfortably, and die quietly, so some others die full of sorrow and heaviness, having never had any smack or taste of the pleasures and commodities of this life.

*{And another dieth}* Heb. *and this*. As if he pointed to another wicked man, who lived and died in anguish, and misery. See on verse 23.

*{in the bitterness of his soule}* Heb. *with a bitter soul*. In a sad condition of spirit, and full of sorrow. Having been afflicted all his life, he dies with extraordinary pangs. Bitter things are displeasing to the palate, so are sorrows to the mind, chap. 3:20. 2 King. 4:27. For the word, soul, see on chap. 14:22. & 7:11. & 10:1.

*{and never eateth with pleasure}* Or, *never enjoyeth any good*. He was so full of troubles, all his life long, that he could never make one quiet meal, or take any content in what he did eat. Or, *never eateth of good*. He affords not himself any part of any good thing he enjoys. So ב is taken, Prov. 13:16. For the word, Eateth, see on chap. 15:34. upon the word, *Consume*. For the word, Pleasure, see on verse 13. of this chapter upon the word, *Wealth*. The same words which are here used in the original, are there used also.

**V. 26. ***{They shall lie down alike in the dust, and the worms shall cover them}* Though there be great unlikeliness, and inequality between these ungodly men in this life, yet death makes them equal. They lie both of them in the grave, and are clothed with worms, chap. 5:18,19.

*{they shall lie down}* See on chap. 14:12.

*{alike in the dust}* See on chap. 17:16. & 14:8. & 16:25. & 20:11. & 10:9. A periphrasis of the grave.

*{and the worms}* See on chap. 17:14.

*{shall cover them}* It may be the one of them, who lived and died in plenty, had curious apparel, while he lived, but now in the grave the worms will crawl on him, and cover him, till they have eaten him up, Isa. 14:11.

**V. 27. ***{Behold I know your thoughts, and the devices which ye wrongfully imagine against me}* When Job had thus in general confuted the opinion of his friends, of the unhappy life and death of wicked men, as if they alone, and always were subject to such calamities, he now particularly denies, what his friends did tacitly infer thereupon against him, because of his miseries, and against his children because of their violent death, verse 27,28. I know, that out of your disputes about God's proceedings with ungodly men your aim is to conclude both me, and my children to be wicked men. But it will appear by my former answer, that you wrong us, and misunderstand God's dealing with the ungodly, which is various, as I have showed unto you.

*{Behold}* What I say is a certain truth, and shows God's wonderful proceedings to be far above men's reach. See on chap. 1:12.

*{I know your thoughts}* He does not mean, that he knew their thoughts certainly, and immediately, for that is God's prerogative; but by their discourse he could guess at their intentions. So a wise man can do, Prov. 20:5.

*{and the devices}* The original מזמה word sometimes signifies thoughts in general, or purposes, as chap. 17:11. Sometimes evil thoughts and devices to hurt others. As Prov. 12:1. & 14:17. And sometimes subtle thoughts, whereby men wisely foresee things to come, as Prov. 1:4. & 3:21. Here it is taken for evil devices to hurt Job.

*{which ye wrongfully imagine}* Which you conceive against me injuriously to take away my good name, and comforts, as thieves take away men's goods violently, and unjustly, Zeph. 3:4.

*{against me}* Or, *concerning me*.

**V. 28. ***{For ye say, where is the house of the prince? and where are the dwelling places of the wicked?}* Some take these words as spoken of wicked men in general, as if Job had said, You think you have confuted my opinion sufficiently in bidding me to show, where are the habitations of former tyrants and ungodly men. And if I cannot, then surely God has destroyed them by his judgments, and will do so with all other ungodly persons. But it is rather to be understood more particularly of Job and his children, whom they deemed to be wicked men, because of God's judgments upon them. So in the former verse, he charges them with mischief imagined against himself.

*{For ye say}* Or, *Because ye say*. Or, *When ye say*. Or, *How ye say*. For the word אמר see on chap. 16:1. It is likely that Job intends, they thought so, and gathered it out of some former passages of theirs, for these very words are no where to be found before.

*{where is the house of the prince}* Some understand of Job's own house, and the words following of his children's. Others of his eldest son's house, which was blown down, chap. 1:18,19. and the words following of the younger brethren's houses. How ere it be, it cannot be understood of their material houses, for none was destroyed, but the eldest brother's. But it must be meant of their family, and princely port, which was now gone. For the word בית an house, see chap. 15:28. For נדיב in the primary signification of it imports, a liberal or bountiful person. And secondarily because princes should be such, it signifies a prince. So here.

*{and where are the dwelling places of the wicked}* Heb. *the tent of the dwelling places of the wicked*. Their curious tents fit for many to inhabit. For אהל a tent, see on chap. 5:24. & 11:14. upon the word, Tabernacle: For, The wicked, see on chap. 3:17.

**V. 29. ***{Have ye not asked them, that go by the way? and do ye not know their tokens?}* Here Job begins to answer to the words attributed to his friends in the two former verses, and confutes them by the testimony of passengers.

*{Have ye not asked}* Sure you should have asked. They could have informed you. Or, *cannot ye ask*? you might easily be resolved of the truth of what I have said, if you pleased but to ask.

*{them that go by the way}* Such as in their travels have observed such things, and seen the flourishing estate of many a wicked man, and are skillful by their observations of God's proceedings with such. Or, every mean man, that goes by the wayside suddenly taken up at all peradventure could inform you of this truth. And therefore it is a wonder, that such wise men as you, should be ignorant of it. For עבר see on chap. 14:20. For דרך See on chap. 16:22. & 17:9. But here it is taken properly for a traveler's way, and not figuratively, as in those places.

*{and do ye not know their tokens}* Some take it as a metaphor from travelers, who use to set up marks by the way to inform others, that they might not err, where the way divided itself into several parts. Others from the notes that travelers use to take in their journeys of things remarkable. Some understand it of such tokens, as travelers could give of the remainders of Job's house, and his children's. Others of those examples they could give of rich wicked men, who lived and died in peace, as Job had affirmed, and his friends denied. They could name persons, times, and places to confute you. So he overthrows what Zophar had said, Chap. 20:7,8,9.

**V. 30. ***{That the wicked is reserved to the day of destruction; they shall be brought forth to the day of wrath}* In this verse, and so forward to the last, he tells what those things were passengers used to observe and report to others concerning the prosperous life, and quiet death of many ungodly men. To wit, that they are not punished here according to their merits, but shall be in the world to come, Prov. 16:4. yet the whole verse may better be read thus. That at the day of destruction the wicked is kept back, and they are carried out in the day of wrath. This translation suits well with the following verse. And the sense is, that wicked men of all others are least subject to outward calamities, and that they are often spared, when the testimonies of God's wrath are rife, and almost every where to be seen upon the earth.

*{That the wicked is reserved}* Or, *kept back*. That is, preserved, and kept out of common calamities. So the word is used, 1 Sam. 25:39. Or, *spared*. As 2 King. 5:20.

*{To the day}* Or, *in the day*. So this particle is used, Zech. 7:3. for the word, see on chap. 14:14.

*{of destruction}* See on chap. 18:12.

*{they shall be brought forth}* Or, *carried out*. As it is used, vers. 32. and chap. 20:19.

*{to the day of wrath}* Heb. *of wraths*. Or, *In the day of wrath*. As before: It may be in the plural to signify extreme wrath, that is heavy, such as God in his severest wrath uses to pour upon men in general, and manifest judgments. It comes from a word, that signifies to pass or go beyond one's bounds. Not that God in anger ever goes beyond his: but to signify God's fierce anger, such as man's is, when he is transported beyond all bounds and limits of reason.

**V. 31. ***{Who shall declare his way to his face? and who shall repay him, what he hath done?}* Neither is it any marvel that the wicked man escapes often, when others are afflicted. His riches and power are so great, that no man dare reprove him to his face for his sins, or punish him for the injuries which he has done. It may be, many have cause to grumble at him for private injuries, but they dare not outwardly express it.

*{who shall}* None dare tell him of his faults. All are afraid of him. None dare appear as a witness against him in judgment, or sit on the bench to condemn him. Here he contradicts what Zophar had said, chap. 20:27. the earth shall rise up against him.

*{declare}* Set his wicked life before him so plainly, that he might see his ungodliness in their words, as in a glass. So much the word imports, see on chap. 15:18.

*{his way}* His wicked course of life, see on chap. 16:22. & 17:9.

*{to his face}* Many may mutter behind his back, but his countenance is so terrible, that no man dare come into his presence to tell him his faults. See on chap. 14:20. upon the word *Countenance*.

*{and who shall repay him}* God lets him alone here in his wickedness, and no man dare stir him.

*{what he hath done}* See on chap. 14:9. upon the word *Bring forth*. Who dare punish him for his wicked actions. Others read it thus, *And he doth what he list, and who shall repay him?*

**V. 32. ***{Yet shall he be brought to the grave, and shall remain in the tomb}* This verse may be better read thus, *Yea he is brought to the grave, and remaineth in the tomb*. Wherein you say, if God spare a wicked man all his life long, yet he will be sure to bring him to his end by some exemplary judgment, it is far otherwise, for many a wicked man lives plentifully, dies quietly, and is buried honorably.

*{yet shall he be brought}* He shall be carried forth in pomp to be buried in a stately fashion, and not brought forth to the block or the gallows as he did deserve. For the word see on verse 30. and chap. 19:19.

*{to the grave}* Heb. *to the graves*. That is, to some one of the graves, see on chap. 7:9. & 16:1.

*{and shall remain}* Heb. *shall watch*. That is, he shall abide as quietly in the grave, as a watchman, that stands still in his watchtower, and looks about him. Others understand it thus, he shall regard nothing but his tomb. That is, he shall forget all things in the world, and feel no pain in his body. See on chap. 14:22. Others thus, if he had sense, he would watch over his tomb, lest any man should rob him of his quiet estate there. Others thus. Some other shall be set to watch over him, when he is dead, that no man offer any violence to his dead carcass, so far was he from a violent death. The first sense is the plainest, and suits well with the verse following.

*{in the tomb}* Heb. *over the heap*. The word properly signifies a shock of corn, in which many ears are gathered together like stones in an heap, chap. 5:26. So graves use to have a heap of earth laid on the top of them, much like to a shock of corn in the field, or an heap in a barn.

**V. 33. ***{The clods of the valley shall be sweet unto him, and every man shall draw after him, as there are innumerable before him}* There is no difference between the ungodly man and others in regard of the body in life, death, after death, while it lies in the grave. He lives as plentifully, dies as honorably, sleeps as quietly in his grave, as the best of men do, chap. 24:24.

*{The clods}* The mould that fills up the grave.

*{of the valley}* Where graves are made for the most part. For the word see on chap. 20:17.

*{shall be sweet unto him}* An action of a living man attributed to a dead; to show how free the wicked is from all trouble in the grave. Even as free as a man that rests quietly in his bed, and fears no storms. In his life he felt troubles, now he feels none.

*{and every man shall draw after him}* It is not peculiar to him alone to die; others also must draw out their days toward the grave. Or, *he shall draw every man after him*, intimating that there is as great a necessity for other men to die, as if the dead man were alive, and should draw them with cords after him to the grave. Or impersonally, *every man shall be drawn after him*. Not intimating by whom, as chap. 3:3.

*{as there are innumerable before him}* Heb. *and there is no number before him*. That is, of those that went before him to the grave. Many go before him to the grave, and many follow him. Thus predecessors and successors are set out, chap. 18:20. Eccles. 4:16.

**V. 34. ***{How then comfort ye me in vain, seeing in your answers there remaineth falsehood?}* He concludes now that as he has confuted their disputations, so he may justly reject their consolations. For their disputations were full of falsehood, and their consolations such as could no way assuage his grief, chap. 16:2. They sought to condemn him for a wicked man, because of his afflictions, which was a false ground: and persuaded him to repent of his ill-led life, which was not the right way to comfort him.

*{How then}* Seeing I have disproved your tenets, why do you still proceed to deal thus with me?

*{comfort ye me}* Do you my friends seek to cheer me up in such a false way?

*{in vain}* With empty words, or without solid grounds. Afford me such comfort, as will not stand, or abide, Or, why do you discourage me instead of encouraging me?

*{seeing in your answers}* In your discourses by way of answer to my arguments.

*{there remaineth}* As I have proved your arguments to be false, so you have not taken off my objections against them: and therefore I must still judge them to be so for ought you have said to the contrary.

*{falsehood}* There is no truth in what you have said. Or, *Transgression*. You have gone beyond the rule of truth, and equity. Or *Prevarication*. You have dealt doubly with God, and with me. You have undertaken to clear his justice, and have limited his authority, as if he might not prosper wicked men here, and punish them only in another world, chap. 13:7. You have made me believe, you came to comfort me, and you have altogether discouraged me, Numb. 23:11.

## CHAPTER 22

**Verse 1. ***{Then Eliphaz the Temanite answered and said}* This is Eliphaz his third and last conflict with Job, where in those things which he indirectly charged him with before, now he charges home, and particularly upon him, and so shows plainly that in all those harsh speeches against ungodly men before he aimed at Job himself, though he did not express so much before: yet being puzzled with Job's arguments, like a bad disputant, he repeats what he had said before in substance, chap. 4. & 5. & 15. yea his spirit is come so far as to put Job in hope of prosperity upon repentance which he did not do, chap. 15. Eliphaz always begins, either because he was esteemed wisest, and so the rest gave place to him: or else because they would keep their turns as they did in the first conflict, and no man speak twice together, that so every one might be heard in his course, or because he was more ancient than the rest, as seems to be intimated by his modest speech, chap. 15:10. Eliphaz not only repeats his former grounds, and grows more bitter, as the rest also did in their second conflict with Job, but he only directly accuses Job of a wicked life, and rips it up in particulars, as appears ver. 5. of this chapter, &c. and by their double disputes before, and Bildad's third, chap. 25. The nearest they came to him, was to strike at him in general in those evils they threaten to wicked men, as chap. 4:8. &c. or by gathering ill conclusions out of his passionate speeches now, which were nothing to his former life, as chap. 11:4. &c. or by grating upon his children, as chap. 8:4. But now Eliphaz falls directly upon himself, and reckons him among wicked men. In this speech of his, 1. note a reprehension of Job to verse 21. 2. an exhortation to repentance from thence to the end of the chapter. In the reprehension, 1. he labors to cut off all hope of pleading with God from Job to vers. 5. 2. he enumerates the gross sins, which he supposed him guilty of, from thence to vers. 12. 3. he charges him with certain absurdity gathered out of his former speeches, from thence to verse 15. 4. he endeavors to confute him out of his own arguments, chap. 21:14,15. and that from vers. 15. of this chapter, to verse 21.

*{Then}* See on chap. 4:1.

*{Eliphaz the Temanite}* See on chap. 2:11.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16:1.

**V. 2. ***{Can a man be profitable unto God, as he that is wise may be profitable to himself?}* What means this Job, that you stand so much upon your own uprightness; You would have men to think, that God is greatly beholding to you for your integrity and justice, so that he may seem to have done you wrong in dealing thus hardly with you. But you are deceived, if you suppose that God can reap any benefit or commodity at man's hand. Nay doubtless, for if any man do well and wisely, the good and profit redounds to himself.

*{Can}* So the future is translated, chap. 8:11. and the meaning is, Certainly he cannot profit God, chap. 35:6,7,8. Psal. 16:2,3. Luke 17:10.

*{a man}* Though he be never so strong or stout, for so much the derivation of the Hebrew word imports, see on chap. 14:10.

*{be profitable}* Add any thing to God to make him better or happier. No man can do it, for God is perfectly holy and happy.

*{unto God}* See on chap. 8:3,5.

*{as he that is wise}* Or, *but he that is wise*. So the particle כי is translated chap. 9:18. And then the sense may be, Though a man can do no good to God by his good actions, yet it is not in vain to do good, for a wise man may much benefit himself by his discreet carriage Deut. 10:13. Prov. 9:12. The word that here is translated wise, sometimes signifies prosperous, for good success uses to follow men of prudent carriage.

*{may be profitable unto himself}* See before on this verse, for the word is the same, that was before. Others read the latter end of the verse thus. *Or if it may be profitable doth his good successe depend thereon?* But the former reading is better, as answering best to the scope of the place, and agreeing best with that which follows in the next verse.

**V. 3. ***{Is it any pleasure to the Almighty, that thou art righteous? or is it gain to him, that thou makest thy ways perfect?}* Can your virtue and godliness (if you can prove yourself a godly man, which sure you cannot) do God any pleasure? will it bring him any joy or delight which before he wanted? or will any gain thereby arise to the Almighty, who in himself and of himself is most happy and perfect? Sure it cannot.

*{Is it any pleasure}* The word חפץ sometimes imports liking of a thing, and taking content in it, Psal. 16:3. Sometimes it imports an addition of joy and comfort to one, that had none before, chap. 21:21. So it is taken here. Your righteousness cannot add any joy to God. He wants none.

*{to the Almighty}* See on chap. 8:3,5. & 15:25.

*{that thou art righteous}* See on chap. 9:20.

*{or is it gain to him}* The word signifies profit or increase of wealth, such as tradesmen get by labor, and merchants by travel. God can have no wealth added to him, who owes all already, Psal. 24:1.

*{that thou makest perfect}* That is, sincere, and upright; for no man's ways on earth are answerable to God's law, see on chapter 1:1.

*{thy ways}* See on chap. 16:22. & 17:9.

**V. 4. ***{Will he reprove thee for fear of thee? will he enter with thee into judgment?}* As your righteousness cannot add any good to him, so your wickedness cannot hurt him, that he should contend with you, and take you away, as corrupt judges do malefactors, lest they should accuse them, or do them any harm. Others read it thus, *Would he reprove thee for thy religion? would he come into judgement with thee?* Here can come no good to God, if you were a righteous man, but you are far from it. God would not deal thus harshly with you, if you did fear him. But you have given him just cause to proceed so severely with you, because you have led a most ungodly life, vers. 5. Thus he removes the false cause and sets down the true cause, as he conceives of Job's afflictions. And this interpretation agrees well with the preceding and following sentence, and the words will bear it.

*{will reprove thee}* Really by laying such sore afflictions upon you. So the word is used for real rebukes or strokes, Psal. 39:11.

*{for fear of thee}* As you seem to intimate, chap. 7:12. Or, *for thy religion*. See on chap. 1:1. & 4:6. & 15:4.

*{will he enter with thee}* Heb. *come*. See on chap. 15:21. Psal. 143:2.

*{into judgment}* The Hebrew word משפט signifies 1. the law by which judgment is to be given, and so God's word, which is the supreme law, Psal. 19:9. 2. the suit or case to be tried in judgment, Numb. 27:5. 3. the sentence passed in judgment, 2 King. 25:6. 4. the crime or cause of that sentence, Jer. 49:12. 5. the punishment inflicted by virtue of that sentence, 2 King. 20:40. 6. justice which ought to be in every sentence, Psal. 146:7. 7. the instrument of executing the sentence, or judgment, Ezek. 14:21. 8. the judicial law of God, Mal. 4:4. 9. wisdom, and discretion, which are very needful in judgment: Isa. 30:18. 10. moderation, which is very requisite in judgment, Jer. 10:24. 11. righteous promises, Psal. 119:176. 12. the right, custom, or manner of doing a thing, which requires judgment, Judg. 13:12. 13. the form or show of a person, or thing, 2 King. 1:7. 14. right or title to a thing, Deut. 18:3. 15. a pattern according to which a thing is to be made, Exod. 26:30. Here it is taken in the fifth sense for the punishment. God would not have punished you so severely if you had been a godly man. And then it is same, which was expressed in other words in the beginning of the verse, and shows Eliphaz his confidence, though he were in an error, As if he had said, I am sure God would not have dealt thus with you, if you had been pious.

**V. 5. ***{Is not thy wickedness great? and thine iniquities infinite?}* Here Eliphaz sets out Job's sins in general in this verse, and in particular his deeds of cruelty and pride, verse 6,7,8,9. and shows that these are the causes of his misery, vers. 6,10,11. In this verse Eliphaz seems to speak to this purpose, What should I need to use many words, O Job, to convince you of the justness of your sufferings, when as the very matter itself shows your wickedness to have been so great, as that you did never cease to commit sin? Marvel not therefore at the greatness and durableness of your afflictions, for it is but answerable to your sins, which have been great and endless.

*{Is not}* He cunningly takes it for granted, as if Job had confessed it. You cannot deny but are convinced in your soul, that your wickedness is very great, although you set a good face upon the matter before us.

*{thy wickedness}* See on chap. 20:11.

*{great}* The word sometimes signifies much, but here it signifies great, for he accuses Job of great sins in the verses following. And the multitude of his sins charged on him in the end of this verse.

*{and thine iniquities}* See on chap. 7:21.

*{infinite}* Heb. *There is no end of thine iniquities*. Whereby is not only intended, that he was guilty of many sins, but also that he would have made no end of oppressing others, if God had not laid this heavy affliction upon him. For the word see on chap. 16:3.

**V. 6. ***{For thou hast taken a pledge from thy brother for nought, and stripped the naked of their clothing}* It seems in the fourth chapter that Eliphaz had a little better opinion of Job, than now he has. For there he conceives, that he made some show of piety, though he might dissemble: but here he makes him an open profane wicked man. In this verse he taxes him with extreme cruelty both to kindred, and poor, whom men use most of all to pity. But he was so far from clothing them, that he took away their clothing from them.

*{For}* These particulars, which now I shall repeat, will prove your sins to be very great.

*{thou hast taken a pledge from}* Taken his goods to pawn, or apparel, as follows in this verse, Exod. 22:26,27. Deut. 24:6,10.

*{thy brother}* See on chap. 19:13.

*{for nought}* See on chap. 1:9. Here lay the first fault. It was not a sin to take a pledge, nor to take it of a brother, but to take it without a just cause, where nothing was due, or else the debt was paid, and yet the pawn detained.

*{and stripped the naked of their clothing}* Heb. *and strip off the clothing of the naked*. Spoiled them, taken all away from them, clothes and all, and left them like a beast without a skin. For so much the word imports. For בגד a garment see on chap. 16:15. upon the word *Deceitfully*. You have taken away the garments of poor men for debt, and left them naked. Those that are now naked were made so by you. Or, Those that had but mean apparel, which could scarce cover their nakedness, and so were naked in comparison of others, you have stripped off that poor habit. So unmerciful and cruel were you to the poor. So some interpret that place, Deut. 28:48. This is the second sin to take away the poor man's garment, and lay him open to cold and nakedness.

**V. 7. ***{Thou hast not given water to the weary to drink, and thou hast withholden bread from the hungry}* Now follows the third sin of cruelty, not affording to such as needed it, the meanest drink or meat.

*{Thou hast not given drink}* You would not afford the poor a draught of drink.

*{water}* You would not give so much as a cup of cold water, which was ready at hand, easy to be gotten, and of little cost, or none at all, much less a cup of wine. So chap. 14:4. Not one, that is, not so much as one. For the word see on chap. 14:9.

*{to the weary}* To him that being weary with labor or travel, was very thirsty, and ready to perish for want of it, to whom it had been very acceptable, and not costly to you, Prov. 25:25. Isa. 21:14.

*{thou hast withholden}* You had it to spare, but would not give it, see on chap. 20:13. upon the word *Keep*.

*{bread}* You would not part so much as with a bit of bread. For the word see on chap. 15:23.

*{from the hungry}* From him, that was ready to starve for want of it, as men in a famine are wont to do. So much the word imports. And this argued extreme covetousness and cruelty to let men perish for want of a little relief, Isa. 58:7.

**V. 8. ***{But as for the mighty man, he had the earth, and the honourable man dwelt in it}* Some interpret these words as an accusation of pride in Job, who should be meant to be this honorable man, who by his might had rooted out his poor neighbors, that he might have all to himself. But rather Eliphaz accuses Job of gross partiality, who destroyed the poor every where, whom by his might he could oppress, and spared none, but such as he dared not meddle withal for their might and greatness. And this is the fourth fault here objected to Job. It agrees well with the foregoing and following verses. And it argued cruelty also, for he would have destroyed the rich and great ones also, if he had dared.

*{But}* So this particle is translated, chap. 14:10.

*{as for the mighty man}* Heb. *the man of arm*. That is, a man of strength or might, who is rich and able to defend himself against you. So a man of lips is a man full of talk, chap. 11:2. Riches are gotten by the arm by laboring or fighting, and by the strength of the arm men oppress others, and take away their goods, chap. 35:9. & 38:15. For the word, Man, see on chap. 1:1. The word זרוע Arm is taken, 1. for the member of the body, which we so call, Psal. 18:34. 2. for strength which is exercised by the arm, or wealth gotten thereby, Psal. 37:17. So it is taken here.

*{he had the earth}* Or, *he had the land*. Heb. *the earth (or, land) was his*. You did oppress weak ones, and spare the strong and wealthy. For the word, see on chap. 9:24. & 15:19.

*{and the honourable man}* Heb. *eminent or accepted for countenance*. He that had strength or wealth, or other outward endowments, Lev. 19:15. 2 King. 3:1. Isa. 3:3. & 9:15. Deut. 16:19. For the word נשא see on chap. 21:3. upon the word, *Suffer*. For Countenance, see on chap. 14:20.

*{dwelt in it}* Lived quietly there, not molested by you, as meaner men were. The word ישב signifies, 1. to sit down, as chap. 2. 8. 13. 9. to abide, or remain, or endure, Psal. 102:12. 3. to dwell in a place, Gen. 13:12. 4. to sit in state on a throne, 1 King. 1:17. Here it is taken in the third sense, as it is translated. See Psal. 37:3.

**V. 9. ***{Thou hast sent widows away empty, and the armes of the fatherless have been broken}* Here are the two last sins which are charged upon Job, unmercifulness to widows, and oppression of orphans: two crying sins, Exod. 22:22. Deut. 27:11. Psal. 94:6. Some understand this of denying justice to widows, and oppressing the fatherless in judgment. But it may better be understood of Job's unmerciful and unkind carriage toward them in his own dealings with them, as the rest of the crimes objected are.

*{Thou hast sent away}* See on chap. 14:20.

*{widows}* Such as have lost their husbands, who should provide for them. It comes from a word that signifies to be dumb, for widows are sad and silent.

*{empty}* Without giving them any relief if they were poor. Or, because they had no husbands to defend them, you have taken away their goods and turned them out of doors with nothing to maintain them, Ruth 1:21. Luke 1:53. The word ריקם signifies, 1. empty, as 1 Sam. 6:3. 2. without a cause; Psal. 25:3. Here it is used in the first sense, as the translation shows.

*{and the armes}* The strength wherewith they defend themselves, 2 Chron. 32:8. you have taken away all their means, and left them poor, because their parents were gone, who should defend them. See on vers. 8.

*{of the fatherless}* Now by the widows and fatherless he may mean all miserable and helpless persons, as well as those that have lost parents or husbands: so the word is used, chap. 6:27. Lam. 5:3.

*{have been broken}* By you, you have taken away all help from them. The verb singular with a noun plural intimates the breaking of each arm, or both arms, and rendering them utterly helpless. For the word see on chap. 19:2.

**V. 10. ***{Therefore snares are round about thee, and sudden fear troubleth thee}* Now Eliphaz sets out the miseries, that he conceived would befall Job for those sins, which he had laid to his charge. And that by four similitudes: two in this verse, and two in the next. The first is taken from a beast, that has snares laid for it on every side by the hunter, so that it can hardly escape. The second from a man frighted with some terrible judgment suddenly and unexpectedly seizing on him, which he sees no way to resist, & third from a man in danger in the dark night, that knows not which way to turn to avoid it, but may fall into a pit, or the enemies' hand, while he flies for safety. A fourth from a flood or deluge covering living creatures, and drowning them without distinction or mercy, Isa. 24:17,18. Jer. 48:43,44. Lam. 3:47.

*{Therefore}* Because of these forementioned sins.

*{snares}* Troubles brought upon you by the cunning devices of men, that lie in wait to destroy you, or brought upon you by God's own hand. See on chap. 18:8,9,10. & 19:6.

*{are round about thee}* So that you are in danger, which way soever you turn yourself, and no way likely to escape.

*{and sudden fear}* Some terrible object coming unexpected, and presently overthrowing you, Prov. 3:25. For the word, Fear, see on chap. 15:21.

*{troubleth thee}* See on chap. 4:5. & 11:6.

**V. 11. ***{Or darkness that thou canst not see, and abundance of waters cover thee}* The other two similitudes to set out the depth of those miseries, which would befall Job for his sins, as Eliphaz thought, are set down in this verse.

*{or darkness}* Some great miseries and trouble will seize upon you, Isa. 8:22. For the word, see on chap. 15:22,23.

*{that thou canst not see}* So that you cannot discern any comfort at all, or way of escaping out of it. For the word, see on chap. 19:27.

*{and abundance}* That word properly signifies a multitude of troubles coming suddenly and furiously upon him, as waters in a flood, so that he could not escape.

*{of waters}* Of troubles. So the word is used, Psal. 32:6. & 18:16. & 66:12. & 69:1. Isa. 43:2. For the word, see on chap. 14:9.

*{cover thee}* All over, so that you shall be drowned in them, and utterly destroyed. Here is a gradation in this verse and the former. It may be, a man may escape snares, and not be taken, troubled with sudden fears, and not ruined, run away in the dark, and scape an enemy, but there is no way to escape a flood, when the waters suddenly overwhelm a man. So that it appears, that Eliphaz gives Job as a lost man past all hope of recovery.

**V. 12. ***{Is not God in the height of heaven? and behold the height of the stars, how high they are}* Here he begins to charge Job with absurdities gathered out of some speeches of his. Belike you think that God cannot take notice of your sins to punish them, because he is so far remote from the earth, and as it were so shut up in heaven that he cannot discern things done here below. And then the words are spoken by Eliphaz, but in the person of Job. And this interpretation agrees well with the two following verses. Yet they may be spoken by Eliphaz in his own person checking Job for not making the right use of God's great majesty in heaven to be afraid of sinning against him, but rather encouraging himself thereby to sin, as if God were therefore so far absent, that he could not take notice of men's iniquities here to punish them. This he charges Job with in the next verse.

*{Is not}* Sure he is.

*{God}* See on chap. 4:9. & 11:5.

*{in the height of heaven}* In the highest heaven where the angels are, and above these visible heavens, where the stars are. See on chap. 11:8. & 9:8.

*{and behold}* See on chap. 19:27.

*{the height of the stars}* You see how high they are: but God is much higher. Heb. *the head of the stars*. God is above the highest of them. The head of a man is the highest part of his body. See on chap. 19:9. & 20:6.

*{how high they are}* As being in the middle heaven the highest things that our eyes can reach, Obad. verse 4. yet God is higher, in the third and highest heaven.

**V. 13. ***{And thou sayest, How doth God know? can he judge through the dark cloud?}* He reproves Job as if he denied God's providence, and affirmed that God could not see things done here upon earth. Peradventure because he had spoken so much before of God's prospering wicked men, Eliphaz thought that Job conceived that God did not see their wicked ways, which if he did, he would not so prosper them.

*{And}* Or, *Therefore*. You make this wicked conclusion out of God's high glory in heaven, to deny his providence on earth. So this particle is translated, chap. 35:16.

*{thou sayest}* You think so, as I gather out of your confused speeches of God's providence. For the word, see on chap. 16:1.

*{How}* How can it be that God should know what is done here below? So מה is translated, chap. 25:4. Or, *what*. He knows nothing at all of things done here below.

*{doth God}* See on chap. 3:5.

*{know}* Perceive or take notice of things done here.

*{can he judge}* Can he discern things done on earth, that he may punish men for their ill deeds? Sure he cannot. Thus Eliphaz would make Job to speak as atheists do, Psal. 10:12. & 73:11. & 94:7.

*{through the dark cloud}* We cannot see through the clouds to discern God's glory in heaven: how then can he see us through them, they are so dark?

**V. 14. ***{Thick clouds are a covering to him, that he seeth not, and he walketh in the circuit of heaven}* There are so many impediments of sight between God and us, and such dark clouds, that God cannot discern through them, what we do, or if he could, yet he will not, as having enough to do, and content enough in heaven above.

*{Thick clouds}* Heb. clouds. Thick is added as included in the derivation of that name, which in the original is taken from a word signifying thickness. And indeed they are the thickest and darkest part of heaven by reason of the waters lodged in them.

*{are a covering to him}* Heb. *an hiding place*. But it is well expressed by a cover. An hiding place keeps others from seeing us, a cover keep us from seeing other things. The meaning is the clouds hinder God from seeing men's sins. A wicked speech, and falsely fathered upon Job.

*{that he seeth not}* See on chap. 19:27.

*{and he walketh}* He finds enough to do in heaven, and needs not meddle with earthly things below. Or, he has so much pleasure there, that he takes no care of us here. The word imports a constant employment, Chap. 1. 7. & 2. 2.

*{in the circuit}* The word properly signifies a circle made to keep things within compass, Isa. 44. 13. So God made a circle for the earth, Isa. 40. 22, and for the sea to keep it within its bounds, Prov. 8. 27. So for the heaven here, which is conceived to be circular, and spherical. Some understand it of guiding the angels in the highest heaven. Others of ordering the stars in the middle heaven. Others of a circle to keep God in, so that he can meddle with nothing below, as atheists imagine.

*{of heaven}* See on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

**V. 15. ***{Hast thou marked the old way, which wicked men have trodden?}* Some understand this verse (wherein Eliphaz confutes that atheistical opinion he had attributed to Job vers. 13.) in general thus. If you O Job had but observed God's constant dealing with the ungodly, you would have perceived, that God did ever take notice of ungodly men, and destroyed them for their sins. But it may better be understood of those ungodly men that lived before the flood, and were destroyed in the flood for their wickedness, vers. 16. And so Broughton reads it, *Hast thou marked the way of the old world*? It was sufficient to mention this deluge being fresh in memory, whereby it appears that Job lived before the deliverance out of Egypt, because he mentions the creation, and the flood, but not that deliverance, which had he known it, would have afforded him an excellent argument to prove that godly men might be in great affliction, as the Israelites were in Egypt, and his friends a plausible argument, that God uses to destroy wicked men for their sins, as he did the Egyptians in the Red Sea. Eliphaz his argument from the flood stands thus, God did then destroy all those ungodly men of the old world for their wicked courses. Therefore though he be in heaven, yet he takes notice of men's sins here to punish them.

*{Hast thou marked}* See on Chap. 7. 12, 20. & 10. 14.

*{the old way}* Heb. *the way of old*: that is, of the ungodly men before the flood, how wickedly they lived. Have you observed their course, and what became of them? For the word Way, see on Chap. 16. 22. For the word עולם It comes from a word that signifies to hide, because the ages past or to come are hidden from us. And 1. it signifies a time past, or of old, Isa. 57. 11. Jer. 2. 20. & 28. 8. 2. A time to come, and lasting long, Numb. 10. 8. For ever there, is for a long time: for now these trumpets there mentioned are of no use. 3. Eternity properly so called, without beginning or ending, Psal. 41. 13. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{which wicked men}* Heb. *mortals of iniquity*. For Mortals, see on Chap. 19. 19. For Iniquity, see on Chap. 15. 35.

*{have trodden}* Wherein they have constantly walked, and made paths to be seen by others by their often treading them.

**V. 16. ***{Which were cut down out of time, whose foundation was overflown with a flood.}* Having set down the sins of the old world in the former verse, now he sets down their destruction.

*{Which were cut down}* Or, *which grew full of wrinkles*. Either because old age crept on them before they were aware; or else because their bodies being drowned in the flood the flesh consumed, and the skin wrinkled. The word is used only here, and Chap. 16. 8.

*{out of time}* Heb. *and not time*. That is, before their time. By the course of nature they might have lived longer, had not the flood drowned them, Psal. 55. 23. & 102. 24. Eccl. 7. 17. Job 15. 22.

*{whose foundation}* Whose life was soon gone: or whose wealth and prosperity upon which they built themselves, and which made them carry themselves so arrogantly, and seemed to be so firmly founded, as if it could never be removed: or the earth upon which they trod, which upheld them as a foundation. For upon all these was the flood poured, and they were overflown, or overthrown by it. Heb. *a flood was poured upon their foundation*.

*{was overflown}* All overcovered, so that they and all the beasts and birds out of the ark died, Gen. 7. 10, 20, 21.

*{with a flood}* Heb. *a river*. All the world was like one great river having plenty of water, and running swiftly, and overflowing all.

**V. 17. ***{Which said unto God, Depart from us: and what can the Almighty do for them?}* They were grown so wicked, that they cast off all fear of God and true religion, and banished God out of the world, and therefore deserved to be destroyed by the flood.

*{Which said unto God, Depart from us}* These are the same words, that Job had spoken of ungodly men, Chap. 21. 14. See the interpretation of them all there. Thus they think to cut Job's throat with his own sword, and confute him with his own words. But they bring them to a far other end. Job spoke them as an effect of the prosperity of ungodly men in themselves breeding security, and impiety in them. Eliphaz brings them here as a necessary cause, why God should always destroy ungodly men by some notable judgment, though they prospered for a time.

*{and what can the Almighty do for them}* Some interpret these words as spoken by Eliphaz, expressing the thoughts of the ungodly ones before the flood. And then the sense is, they thought God could do no good for them, and therefore they would not serve him, but follow their own lusts. And the words had been plainer if thus translated, *and asked, what the Almighty could do for them*. Others interpret these words as if Eliphaz did express his own thoughts concerning God's justice in sending the flood upon them, and read it thus, *and what should the Almighty do unto them?* This may agree with the words before. What should he do with them that had cast him off, but drown them with a flood? But it agrees not well with the following words, yet he filled their houses with good things. For then the sense should be, He might justly have destroyed them, but he did not: Like Psal. 78. 38. But this is not true here, for God did destroy them with a flood. Others take it as Eliphaz his aggravation of their sin what cause had they to forsake God who never did them hurt, but had done them much good, as follows, vers. 18. what cause had they to complain of God? See the like, Isa. 5. 4. Jer. 2. 31. Mic. 6. 3. And they read it thus, *and what had the Almighty done to them?*

The first interpretation is the best, and the sense this; They forsook God upon an opinion, that he could do them no good, yet they had received many good things from him. This answers well to Chap. 21. 15.

*{and what}* See on vers. 13.

*{can}* See on Chap. 8. 11.

*{the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 3. & 15. 25.

*{do for them}* What good could he do for such wicked men? Or, *do unto them*. What evil should he do to them? No misery was bad enough for them.

**V. 18. ***{Yet he filled their houses with good things: but the counsel of the wicked is far from me.}* Eliphaz does two things in this verse, first he clears God against that false accusation laid upon him by the wicked world before the flood, that he could do them no good, and shows that they had no cause to say so, for he had done them much good. Secondly, he secretly taxes Job of hypocrisy for affirming that he hated the ways of wicked men, Chap. 21. 16. and yet continued in them, as he supposed, adding that himself might say so much better than Job, who had really forsaken their wicked ways and courses: and did foretell their destruction, whereas Job had flattered them with hope of continuance of their prosperity to their dying day.

*{Yet}* So this particle is translated, Psal. 119. 109, 110.

*{he filled}* God gave them abundance of worldly goods, Act. 14. 17. He stuffed their houses up to the top with all manner of good things, Prov. 1. 13. He accuses them of great ingratitude, that contrary to the custom of men would speak ill of God, who had done them so much good. And it may be he casts Job's former prosperity and unthankfulness, (as he conceived) in his teeth. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 2.

*{their houses}* Their dwelling places, for there men use to keep their store about them. See on Chap. 15. 28.

*{with good things}* Heb. with good. That is, with riches, household stuff, food, and other necessaries, which are good, if well used. For the word see on Chap. 21. 13. upon the word *Wealth*.

*{but}* So the word is translated, Chap. 14. 10. Or, *therefore*. Far be it therefore from me, that I should once harbor in my breast any one thought of these profane persons, for whom God has done so much, and yet they cast him off.

*{the counsel of the wicked is far from me}* Then was he a blessed man, Psal. 1. 1. These words Job had spoken after a long repetition of the prosperity of ungodly men, affirming that for all their wealth he abhorred their ways. See the exposition of all these words, Chap. 21. 16.

**V. 19. ***{The righteous see it and are glad: and the innocent laugh them to scorn.}* Some interpret these words in general of the joy that good men have in all ages, when they see the ungodly of the world consumed by the judgments of God. Others particularly of Noah and his sons rejoicing when they saw the wicked of the old world drowned, and themselves preserved. And these latter read the words thus, The righteous saw it and were glad, and the innocent laughed them to scorn.

*{The righteous}* See on Chap. 17. 9.

*{see it}* See on Chap. 19. 27. upon the word *Behold*.

*{and are glad}* See on Chap. 21. 12. upon the word *Rejoice*. They are not glad of the destruction of men considered in itself, but that God gets glory, and his people ease by their destruction, Psal. 58. 10. & 107. 42. Others read it, *Let the righteous see it, and be glad*.

*{and the innocent}* See on Chap. 17. 8.

*{laugh them to scorn}* Derides their vain confidence, when he sees their miserable end. Others read it, *And let the innocent laugh them to scorn*.

**V. 20. ***{Whereas our substance is not cut down; but the remnant of them the fire consumeth.}* These words are variously interpreted. Piscator takes them to be spoken in the person of Noah, whom he takes to be the innocent man mentioned, verse 19. and adds Saying in the beginning of this verse. As if Noah coming out of the ark should say, God had preserved him and his, and in his wrath destroyed the ungodly. Others take it to be spoken of Noah, but not in his person, and that Eliphaz reckons himself among them that escaped the flood, because he counted himself godly as Noah was. Others conceive, that Eliphaz looked towards Sodom, and acknowledged God's mercy in sparing the Edomites, and not destroying them with fire and brimstone as he had done the Sodomites. Others take it for a prophecy of the preservation of the godly to the end of the world and the destruction of sinners then by fire. But it may better be interpreted of other preservations of the godly, and destruction of the wicked in Eliphaz and Job's time, and peradventure of the continuance of Eliphaz and his companions, family and goods, when Job's children and goods were strangely destroyed; and so it may well depend on the former words, and make way for them that follow, as if he had said, You know well O Job, how God destroyed the ungodly of the old world with a flood, and yet spared Noah's family: and you as well as we have seen many families of ungodly men destroyed, yea your own, when ours and such other godly men's are preserved. Therefore now it is high time that you quickly return to God by repentance lest he destroy you, as he has done your children, vers. 21.

*{Whereas}* Or rather, *Though*. As it is translated, Judg. 13. 16. Though we be spared, yet the ungodly are destroyed continually.

*{our substance}* Our life, which makes us to subsist and stand up in the world. Or, our estate which upholds men's lives.

*{is not cut down}* See on Chap. 4. 7. upon the word *Cut off, and on Chap*. 15. 28. upon the word *Desolate*.

*{but}* Or, *yet*. See on vers. 18. of this chapter.

*{the remnant of them}* Of such wicked men who are like those ungodly ones who were drowned in the flood. God does not only destroy ungodly men, but their seed also. Thus the word is taken for posterity, 2 Sam. 21. 2. Or, *their excellency*, see on Chap. 4. 21. And by their excellency may be meant their wealth, and honor, wherein they excelled others, and which made them more regarded than others.

*{the fire}* For the various significations of the word, see on Chap. 18. 5. Here it may be taken literally, for no doubt but Job and Eliphaz had seen many wicked men, and their goods destroyed by fire. And Job lost some of his cattle by fire from heaven, which it is likely Eliphaz here aimed at. Or it may be understood of some consuming judgment which God in his anger sent upon wicked men, as Chap. 20. 26. Psal. 78. 21.

*{consumeth}* Or, *hath consumed*. For he speaks of things already done. For the word see on Chap. 15. 34. on the word *Consume*.

**V. 21. ***{Acquaint now thyself with him, and be at peace: thereby good shall come unto thee.}* Eliphaz having endeavored to terrify Job from evil ways by threatenings before, now he labors by promises to persuade him to be reconciled to God by true repentance. And first he shows him the way in this verse, and the next, and secondly the benefits of the way. And that 1. In general, restitution of prosperity and comfort, vers. 23. 2. In particular. 1. Outward wealth, verse 24. 25. 2. Inward comfort, vers. 26, 27, 28. 3. Means to do good to others, vers. 29, 30. In this verse he secretly intimates that Job had been estranged from God by a wicked life, and so had found little peace. And therefore persuades him to alter his course, that it might go well with him.

*{Acquaint thyself}* This word הסכן in this conjugation is used but twice besides in the Scripture. In the one place, Psal. 139. 3. it signifies knowledge of a thing. In the other, Numb. 22. 30. it signifies use and customable converse with one. Here it may be a persuasion of Job to get more knowledge of God, or to walk more carefully in his ways.

*{now}* Now you hear what mischiefs have befallen ungodly men, for want of acquaintance with God. Or, *I pray thee*, speaking gently to him to mollify his former sharpness and make his counsel go down the better. So physicians sweeten bitter pills; see on Chap. 13. 18.

*{with him}* To wit, with the Almighty, vers. 23. So the antecedent is taken out of the following words, Psal. 87. 1, 2. & 116. 1.

*{and be at peace}* So shall you be at peace, see the like phrase, Psal. 37. 3. The meaning is, This is your way to recover your former prosperity, see on Chap. 5. 24. & 9. 4. & 12. 6. Or, you shall be in a safe condition: so the word is used, Chap. 21. 9. Others take it for an exhortation to reconciliation to God. Reconcile yourself to him.

*{thereby}* Heb. *by them*. That is, by acquaintance with God, and restoration of your peace and safety. Upon these will follow riches.

*{good}* Prosperity, Riches. See on Chap. 21. 13. upon the word *Wealth*.

*{shall come unto thee}* From God. He shall make you prosper. For the word see on Chap. 15. 21.

**V. 22. ***{Receive I pray thee, the law from his mouth, and lay up his words in thine heart.}* Job might object and say, How shall I know how to get and keep acquaintance with God? Eliphaz answers, By laying up God's law in your heart.

*{Receive}* Learn it of him. The master gives, when he teaches: the scholars receive, when they learn. See on Chap. 15. 12. on the word *Carry away*.

*{I pray thee}* See on vers. 21. of this chapter, upon the word *Now*.

*{the law}* Some understand it of Moses' law, which they conceive, was then newly written. But it is more likely that Job lived before Moses; see on vers. 15. of this chapter, and on Chap. 1. 1. By the law therefore must be meant such instructions in matter of faith and life, as God was pleased to communicate by revelation to the patriarchs, and they by tradition to their children. It comes from a word that signifies to rain, or shoot, and metaphorically to teach. For good precepts are dropped in by little and little like rain, and fastened in the heart, as an arrow shot into the flesh. The word is sometimes used for any instruction, though by man, but here for such as comes from God originally.

*{from his mouth}* As godly men have taught you, and as it came originally from God. See on Chap. 15. 5.

*{and lay up}* Heb. *put*. Not only receive instruction from God, but keep it safe, as treasure to be used upon all occasions.

*{his words}* Such as godly men have communicated to you from God.

*{in thine heart}* Psal. 119. 11. Luk. 2. 19, 51. For the word see on Chap. 15. 12.

**V. 23. ***{If thou return to the Almighty, thou shalt be built up, thou shalt put away iniquity far from thy tabernacles.}* If you being acquainted with God, and receiving, and keeping his law within you shall also return unto him by true repentance, you shall recover your former prosperity: and if you leave your wicked ways, you shall grow rich, verse 24.

*{If thou return}* See on Chap. 15. 13. upon the word *Turnest*. Here it is understood of repentance, which is a returning from sin to God.

*{to the Almighty}* See on Chap. 3. 3, 5.

*{thou shalt be built up}* The ruins of your former prosperity shall be repaired, and your estate more wealthy and prosperous than it was. A metaphor from repairing or building houses, Mal. 3. 15. Are set up. Heb. *Built*. Others understand it of having many children again, as the word is used, Gen. 16. 2. & 30. 3. Ruth. 4. 11. For the word see on Chap. 20. 19.

*{thou shalt put away far}* Or, *if thou put away far, &c*. Then you shall get gold, vers. 24. It is not enough to remove away iniquity, but it must be so far alienated from our affections, that we entertain no thought of returning to it, Chap. 11. 14.

*{iniquity}* See on Chap. 13. 7. on the word *Wickedly*.

*{from thy tabernacles}* See on Chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14.

**V. 24. ***{Then shalt thou lay up gold as dust, and the gold of Ophir, as the stones of the brooks.}* You have been a man much addicted to gold, and used much indirect means to get it, vers. 6, 7. If you will return to God, you shall have plenty of it, which you may take comfort in, as coming from God's blessing.

*{Then}* When you have repented of your misled life, and reformed it. So this particle is translated, Chapter 13. 12.

*{shalt thou lay up}* Heb. lay up, without, *shalt*: the imperative for the future, as Psal. 37. 3.

*{gold}* Golden coin or riches. The word בצר signifies 1. A defense, ver. 25. 2. Gold, Chap. 36. 19. because it is the worldly man's defense. So here.

*{as dust}* In abundance as the dust of the earth. Or, *above the dust*, that is, more than the dust of the earth. An hyperbolical expression of great wealth. So על is used Chap. 23. 2. Or, *upon the dust*. As it is translated, Psal. 24. 2. Or, *beside the dust*. As Psal. 1. 3. By or beside the rivers of water. For the word Dust, see on Chap. 14. 8.

*{and the gold of Ophir}* That comes from Ophir, 1 King. 9. 27, 28. & 10. 11. & 22. 48. Thence used to come excellent gold, and therefore the word is put for choice gold here. For the word Gold see before it is not in the original.

*{as the stones}* Heb. *in the rock*.

*{of the brooks}* Or, *of the valleys*. See on Chap. 20. 17. This is diversely interpreted. Some render the sense of it thus, You need not build castles on hills to secure your wealth: it shall be safe enough in the valleys, and low places. But this argues safety rather than plenty. Others thus, You shall have such plenty of gold, that you may cover houses and palaces with it, if you will. But it is rather a preferring of Job's future condition upon his repentance far above his former prosperity. You shall have far more riches than ever you had, as much or more than the dust of the streets, and stones of the brooks or valleys, 2 Chron. 1. 15. & 9. 27.

**V. 25. ***{Yea the Almighty shall be thy defence, and thou shalt have plenty of silver.}* As you shall attain to plenty of riches, so you shall keep them safe, for God will not take them away from you any more, but he himself will defend you, and increase your store daily.

*{Yea}* God who is better than all gold, will defend you: This is a greater favor than to have store of gold. Thus this particle is used, Chap. 5. 19.

*{the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{shall be thy defence}* Heb. *defences*. This is, strong defense, stronger than many castles. See on vers. 24. Or, *thy gold*. Your best treasure, in which you shall take most delight, as vers. 26. His favor and blessing shall be better to you, than all the treasures of the world.

*{and thou shalt have plenty of silver}* Heb. *silver of strengths*. That is, strong silver, which has power to defend you against all your adversaries. For money is the sinews of war. The word for Silver comes from a word that signifies to desire, because all men desire money, and it can help them to all worldly comforts they do desire. 1. כסף signifies silver in the ore, heap or wedge, as Hag. 2. 8. 2. When it is joined with words of number, it signifies silver coin, or money made of silver, Gen. 23. 16. So here. For the word Plenty, or of strength, some understand by it store of money, because the more a man has, the stronger his estate is, and the more likely his continuance. But it may be understood of money simply without respect of store, and so it answers well to the beginning of the verse which speaks of safety, whereas the former verse spoke of plenty.

**V. 26. ***{For then shalt thou have thy delight in the Almighty, and shalt lift up thy face unto God.}* Eliphaz and his companions had before plied Job with temporal promises: now he brings spiritual ones, which neither he, nor any of his fellows had done before. And this is the highest of spiritual favors to enjoy God.

*{For}* A proof of that which went before. God who gives you plenty of comfort in himself, will not deny you the creature for your content and delight.

*{then}* There is in this particle both an elegancy, and an opposition. Then when you have reformed your ways, and not till then. Then you may make account to find that comfort in God, which you never did find in him before and till then, it is in vain to think of it. Thus he sets out the good that comes to men by godliness, and puts all ungodly men out of hope to attain it.

*{shalt thou have thy delight}* Heb. *shalt thou delight thy selfe*. You shall not only be sustained by God, as others are, but find special delight, and singular pleasure in him.

*{in the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{and shalt lift up}* See on Chap. 21. 3.

*{thy face}* See on chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*. Your countenance, which is now cast down with grief, and fear, by reason of your afflictions, you shall then lift up with boldness, and comfort, even to God himself, chap. 11. 15.

*{unto God}* See on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

**V. 27. ***{Thou shalt make thy prayer unto him, and he shall hear thee, and thou shalt pay thy vows.}* In this verse and that which follows are set out the benefits, which the godly obtain of God by faithful prayer, to wit, audience, granting their desires, establishing their purposes, prosperity in their ways.

*{Thou shalt make thy prayer unto him}* The word signifies to pour out many words in prayer, and make many supplications. Yet God will hear them all.

*{and he shall hear thee}* That is, when you pray then God will hear you. See the like phrase in the notes on chap. 14. 11. For the word Hear, see on chap. 13. 17.

*{and thou shalt pay thy vows}* God shall give you those favors or that freedom, which you did ask of him, when you made vows unto him in the time of your distress. So shall you have cause to pay your vows with cheerfulness. His purpose is not here to call on Job to perform his vows, when God should prosper him, but to assure him that God would grant all his desires upon his repentance. He mentions vows, because godly men used then to join vows to their prayers, Gen. 28. 20. Psal. 66. 13, 14.

**V. 28. ***{Thou shalt also decree a thing, and it shall be established unto thee; and the light shall shine upon thy ways.}* God will not only hear your prayers, and give you the things you pray for, but whatsoever else you speak of, or purpose to go about, God will give it good success.

*{Thou shalt also decree}* The Heb. word גזר signifies, 1. To cut a thing into two pieces. As 1 King. 3. 25. 2. To decide or determine a thing. To separate it from another, and resolve.

*{A thing}* Which is to be done. As here, a word, or a decree. That is, anything whatsoever you determine to be done, shall be done.

*{and it shall be established}* See on chap. 14. 12. It shall be firm, or effected; for then are purposes and words established, when they are brought to action, Isa. 7. 7. & 8. 10. & 14. 24. 46. 10. Numb. 30. 4.

*{unto thee}* You shall but speak the word and it shall come to pass. So ready is God to hear and help you. Or, *For thee*. God will presently effect it for your good.

*{and the light}* Good success shall attend upon all your actions, which shall bring joy unto you. God's favor and blessing shall be upon all your endeavors. See on Chap. 17. 12.

*{shall shine}* God shall prosper your endeavors as the Sun shining upon the earth ripens the fruits thereof. His blessing shall come forth upon you like the morning Sun, see on Chap. 18. 5.

*{upon thy ways}* See on Chap. 17. 9. All your actions shall prosper.

**V. 29. ***{When men are cast down, then thou shalt say, There is lifting up: and he shall save the humble person.}* In the conclusion of this chapter Eliphaz assures Job, that if he repent, and leave off murmuring against God, he shall not only be free of that dejected condition, in which now he was, but also be exalted when others are cast down, and prevail with God for mercy for others also.

*{When men are cast down}* When God shall throw down wicked and ungodly men. Or, *When they are cast down*. That is, your ways, vers. 28. Or, *Though thy wayes be cast down*. Though now you be in a low condition, yet you shall be very high. So כי is translated, Psal. 44. 19.

*{then}* See on Chap. [24…].

*{thou shalt say}* See on Chap. 16. 1. You shall be confident of recovery, and exaltation. Or, you shall pray confidently to God for it, and make no doubt of being heard, and he shall answer you accordingly, and deliver, and exalt you.

*{there is a lifting up}* You shall confidently expect it in your misery. Or, you shall be delivered, and then may boldly say, you are exalted. See other significations of the word, Chap. 20. 25.

*{and he shall save}* God spoken of vers. 26, 27. He that will not regard the proud will save the humble, Jam. 4. 6. 1 Pet. 5. 5. He will deliver them out of their misery and make them prosper.

*{the humble person}* Heb. *him that is low of eyes*. It is likely he means Job himself who was now in a low condition. And misery makes men ashamed, and to look downward, whereas prosperity makes men take courage, and look upward. He assures Job, that upon his submission God would restore him to a great height of prosperity.

**V. 30. ***{He shall deliver the island of the innocent: and it is delivered by the pureness of thine hands.}* Moreover if you cleanse yourself from iniquity, and walk uprightly, God will deliver your neighbors amongst whom you live, upon your prayers from the dangers threatened against them for their sins. So you shall not only do good to yourself, but to others also.

*{He shall deliver the island of the innocent}* The man in a low condition upon repentance shall not only creep out of misery himself, but also by his prayer delivers all his neighbors. Or, *the innocent shall deliver the island*. The sense is the same. Or, *he shall deliver him that is not innocent*. So good men by their prayers sometimes turn away God's judgments from the wicked and ungodly, Jer. 5. 1. Gen. 18. 32. Exod. 32. 11, 14. For the word, Innocent, see on Chap. 17. 8.

*{and it is delivered}* T*he* island, Or, He. That is, the innocent man.

*{by the pureness}* It signifies an exact cleanness, such as is in gold purged from dross, or in garments cleansed with soap. Such is in a good man, that makes conscience of avoiding all kinds of sin.

*{of thine hands}* Of your works. God will reward your good actions not only with doing good to you, but to others also for your sake. For the word Hands, see on Chap. 16. 17. For the change of the person, *of thine hands*, before he spoke in the third person: see the like, Chap. 8. 20, 21. And it is likely Eliphaz did it of purpose here to make Job understand that he meant him, as it is very probable, that Bildad did there. Others understand it of lifting up the hands in prayer for others. You shall deliver them by your prayers holding up pure hands, 1 Tim. 2. 8. And then it answers well to the former verse. And so Job by God's own appointment did deliver his three friends from God's anger conceived against them, chap. 42. 7, 8, 9.

## CHAPTER 23

**V. 1. ***{Then Job answered and said,}* In this chapter and the next Job answers Eliphaz his large oration. In this chapter he defends himself against Eliphaz, who had in the former chapter charged him with many grievous offenses. In the next he confutes his opinion of God's certain ruining wicked men in this world. This chapter (though many interpret it in favor of Job, as if he only cited his friends to God's judgment-seat out of confidence of relief there) is spent in charging God to deal too hardly with him, as appears all the chapter over, where he never charges his friends with anything, but charges God all along. 1. With laying upon him things that he could not sufficiently complain of, vers. 2. 2. In denying him audience, to vers. 10. 3. In punishing him as he does wicked men, though he knew him to be godly, and should find him so in the end. From vers. 10. to vers. 13. 4. In being inexorable, and not leaving off to afflict him, vers. 13, 14. Then he shows the greatness of his fear, and the grounds of it in the three last verses of the chapter.

*{Then}* See on chap. 4. 1.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Even to day is my complaint bitter: my stroke is heavier then my groaning}* As if Job had said, You are deceived, O Eliphaz, if you think, that your words can make me hold my peace. You deal very unequally with me, who interpret my complaints to be rebellion against God, as Bildad also objected rage unto me (chap. 18. 4.) seeing my plague is greater than can be expressed by any sighs or words. You do rather hereby enforce me to show how greatly my mind is troubled, and distracted; as also to renew those my wonted complaints which cannot any way be answerable to the greatness, and grievousness of my misery; neither do I complain so much, as I have cause; much less am I to be blamed for excess in my complainings.

*{Even}* Notwithstanding your words my sorrows are not allayed. I have as much cause to complain now as ever. Or, Notwithstanding my words, whereby I have showed my just cause of complaining, yet you count my just complaints rebellious against God to this very day. For the word see on chap. 16. 4.

*{to day}* At this very time. For the word, see on chap. 14. 14. & 15. 23.

*{is my complaint}* See on chap. 21. 4.
