*{bitter}* I complain of heavy afflictions, which are very bitter unto me: and no wonder then if I complain bitterly. It is bitter to me, that feel what I complain of, and to you, that cannot endure to hear it, nor will not pity me. See on chap. 13. 26. Or, *is rebellion*. That is, you account it so notwithstanding my many protestations, and justifications, because you still misinterpret my words, and gather wrong conclusions out of them.

*{and my stroke}* Heb. *my hand*. That is, the stroke which God's hand has given me, or the plague which he has inflicted upon me. See on chap. 17. 13.

*{is heavier}* More troublesome, for weighty things are troublesome to the body. See on Chap. 6. 3.

*{then}* So this word signifies. See the notes on Chap. 22. 24. You think me very impatient, and that I complain more than I have cause; But you are deceived. I have reason to complain yet more heavily. Or, *upon my*. The more I complain and sigh, the more I am smitten.

*{my groaning}* Or, *my sighing*. Chap. 3. 24. He intimates how cruel his friends were to him, that would not allow him so much as to sigh in his extreme pain: but counted him rebellious against God for so doing.

**V. 3. ***{O that I knew where I might find him! that I might come even to his seat!}* He speaks of God here after the manner of men. Judges appoint certain places, where to sit in judgment to hear all causes, where all men, that are wronged may come and plead for themselves and be righted, 1 Sam. 7. 16, 17. So Job here trusting to the equity of his cause desires to appear before God, making no doubt but he could clear himself before God's judgment-seat. And so he might against his friends, and the things they laid to his charge. But Job's fault here is, that in his eagerness to clear himself, he forgets to charge his friends, and reflects too much upon the Judge in his complaints, as denying him audience, and handling him too harshly.

*{O that}* Heb. *who will give*. The ordinary form of wishing among the Hebrews intimating their own inability to accomplish it, and great desire of it, so that they would accept of it of any one that could give it. See on chap. 6. 8. & 11. 5.

*{I knew}* That someone would acquaint me, where God is to be found.

*{where I might find}* See on Chap. 17. 10.

*{Him}* God. He doubted not, but his friends knew well enough, whom he meant. See on chap. 20. 23.

*{that I might come}* Or, *then would I come*. Or, *go*. As the word signifies, Jon. 1. 3. An argument of a much distempered spirit in a great affliction. I would spare for no labor, but go on to the remote parts of the world, that I might plead my cause with him, chap. 13. 3. & 31. 35. For the word see on chap. 15. 21.

*{even to his seat}* It comes from a word, that signifies to prepare. I would not be afraid to go to his very throne, or seat of judgment prepared for him: Too bold a speech for a mortal creature, and Job is reproved by God for it, chap. 38. 2. & 40. 2.

**V. 4. ***{I would order my cause before him, and fill my mouth with arguments.}* In this verse and the next Job sets out the manner of proceeding in judgment. The plaintiff brings forth his bill, and the defendant his answer.

*{I would order}* See on chap. 13. 18.

*{my cause}* Heb. *the judgement*. See on chap. 22. 4.

*{before him}* Heb. *to his face*. That is, in his presence. I would show what hard measure I endure. For the word, Face, see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word, *Countenance*.

*{and fill}* I would have arguments enough to maintain that I am harshly entreated. For the word, see on chap. 15. 2.

*{my mouth}* See on chap. 15. 5.

*{with arguments}* Whereby to prove, that I am wronged. Arguments on both sides pleaded in judgment come under this word. See on chap. 13. 6. on the word, *Reasoning*.

**V. 5. ***{I would know the words, which he would answer me, and understand what he would say unto me.}* I desire much to know what reason God would give, why he handles me more sharply than other men which yet I cannot imagine what it should be.

*{I would know}* Or, *I should know*.

*{the words}* Which he would produce.

*{which he would answer me}* See on chap. 16. 1.

*{and understand}* Or, *consider*.

*{what he would say unto me}* For the word, Say, see on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 6. ***{Will he plead against me with his great power? No, but he would put strength in me.}* I confess if God should use his almighty and invincible power against me, he would easily ruin me: but I am confident he will not so deal with me, but will bring forth his arguments against me, and let me plead with him in his court of justice, and then I doubt not but to clear myself.

*{Will he plead against me}* Heb. *Will he plead with me*. For they that plead with men in law, plead against them. For the word, Plead, see on chap. 13. 3, 6.

*{with his great power}* With his almighty power. For the word, Great, see on chap. 22. 5.

*{No, but he would put strength in me}* It may better be read thus. no, but he would produce arguments against me. He would proceed with me in a legal, not in a violent way. This best agrees with Job's scope going on in a way of pleading. So ב is used for against, chap. 19. 19.

**V. 7. ***{There the righteous might dispute with him: so should I be delivered for ever from my judge.}* He shows his great confidence of clearing himself, if he might but have a free trial before God.

*{There}* In such a judgment, wherein God does not terrify me with his power, as corrupt judges often do innocent men, chap. 9. 34, 35. & 13. 21, 22.

*{the righteous might dispute with him}* Or, *I being righteous might dispute with him*. Or, *I should be found righteous, if I did dispute with him*. He makes no question of proving himself righteous before God, that is, not a hypocrite, not one that should be so sorely afflicted more than other men. For the word Righteous, or Upright, see on Chap. 1. 1. For the word Dispute, or Plead, see on Chap. 15. 3.

*{so should I be delivered}* Pronounced innocent, that now am handled like the nocent, being subject to many plagues.

*{for ever}* The word signifies victory, and sometimes eternity, which gets the victory over all time. I should never be questioned more by God, nor thus afflicted as a guilty person.

*{from my judge}* From God's condemning sentence. In the agony of his spirit he looks upon God in the beginning of the verse, as his accuser, in the end of it, as his Judge. See the like, Psal. 143. 2.

**V. 8. ***{Behold, I go forward, but he is not there; and backward, but I cannot perceive him.}* Job had a while intermitted his desire, vers. 3. of finding God to show his confidence of speeding well in that trial. Now he resumes it, but withal shows, that his desire is to no purpose, for God will not offer a trial, and he knows not where to find him. He may seek for him all the world over, East, West, North, and South, and yet miss him. This is the sum of this verse and the next.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12. Or, *If*. See on Chap. 9. 11.

*{I go}* For this word, see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Passeth*.

*{forward}* That is, Eastward. The world in itself has no forepart, nor back part: but man is considered as setting his face to the rising Sun, which is the eye of the world. And then Forward is Eastward, Backward is Westward, Left hand is Northward, Right hand is Southward. Some take it for Eastward in respect of the Holy Land: but Job could not mean so, for it was not in request in his time, and he was more eastward than it, Chap. 1. 3. and must look westward towards it, but eastward to the rising Sun.

*{but he is not there}* Job knew that God is in all the world, but his meaning is, he will not appear to me there, that I may reason my case with him. Heb. *and not he*. I find him not there, but seek him in vain, and lose my labor.

*{and backward}* That is, Westward. When a man's face is to the rising Sun, the West is behind him.

*{but I cannot perceive him}* Heb. *understand him*. That is, I cannot so see him, or perceive him, that I may reason with him.

**V. 9. ***{On the left hand, where he doth work, but I cannot behold him: he hideth himself on the right hand, that I cannot see him.}* It is true that God may be seen in his works in the Northern parts of the world, which is more inhabited than the South. Because more temperate, but I cannot find him there to reason with him: and I have less hope to find him in the hot countries of the South, which are less inhabited, and God's works less seen in them. See more on Chap. 9. 9. on the chambers of the South.

*{on the left hand}* Northward, See on vers. 8.

*{where he doth work}* More than in the southern part of the world, as before in this verse.

*{but}* See on Chap. 21. 1.

*{I cannot}* See on Chap. 22. 2.

*{behold him}* So as I may have conference with him.

*{he hideth himself}* He works not so much in those parts, as in other parts of the world. It were in vain for me to travel thither to seek him, for he will not be found of me there, but will as it were of purpose hide himself from me.

*{on the right hand}* In the Southern parts of the world. See on vers. 8. The word ימין signifies 1. The right hand, as Jer. 22. 24. 2. Strength which as it is most seen in the use of the hands, so especially in the right hand, which in most men is readiest for use, Exod. 15. 6. 3. Swearing, covenanting; for the one is done by lifting up the hand to God, Gen. 14. 22. and the other by joining hands together, Prov. 11. 21. Now because men do this for the most part with the right hand, therefore that act is set out by that word, Psal. 144. 8. 4. It signifies the right side, 2 Sam. 24. 5. 5. The southern part of the world, Cant. 4. 16. So here.

*{that}* See on Chap. 13. 13. where this particle is so translated.

*{I cannot}* See on Chap. 22. 2.

*{see him}* See on Chap. 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold*.

**V. 10. ***{But he knoweth the way that I take; when he hath tried me, I shall come forth as gold.}* God knows that I have carried myself uprightly, and if I could get audience of him, I should come clear from the bar as gold does out of the fire.

*{But}* So it is translated, Chap. 9. 16. Though I know not where to find God, yet he knows where to find me, and what my ways have been.

*{he knoweth}* He perfectly understands it, which no man does.

*{the way}* The course of life, See on Chap. 17. 9.

*{that I take}* Heb. *that is with me*.

*{when he hath tried me}* Not by his afflictions, for that he had abundantly done, but in a judiciary way. When he has heard what I can say for myself. This interpretation agrees with the scope of the whole chapter.

*{I shall come forth}* Now I lie under hard censures of men, and God seems to think hardly of me by laying such a load of sorrows on me. If I could but get a fair hearing, I should come out of this obloquy. For the word, see on Chap. 20. 25.

*{as gold}* No dross of an ill-led life will cleave to me. I shall be freed from all scandals objected to me, and glitter like pure gold coming out of the fire. My good name shall be precious. I shall appear loyal to God; and righteous to men.

**V. 11. ***{My foot hath held his steps, his way have I kept and not declined.}* Eliphaz had in the former chapter, verse 22, &c. given Job good counsel to acquaint himself with God and learn of him and obey him. Now in this verse and the next, Job shows that he had so done, and kept close to God's ways and followed him foot by foot, and so carefully obeyed him, that he had not at any time turned away from his precepts: but made great account of his words.

*{My foot}* I have walked in them, I have constantly followed his steps.

*{hath held}* Or, *hath laid hold on*. It is the proper action of the hand to lay hold on, because it signifies keeping a thing fast, and not parting with it, it may be applied to constant walking after God; chap. 17. 9. See on the notes there.

*{his steps}* Some take it for those that God has appointed for man to walk in them. Others for the ways that lead to God. But the word is not so used elsewhere, but signifies God's own footsteps. I have imitated God, and followed him step by step in those things which I ought to follow him.

*{his way}* The way in which he has appointed me to walk in. So it is taken, chap. 21. 14.

*{have I kept}* Observed, and walked in it. For the word, see on chap. 7. 12, 20. & 10. 14.

*{and not declined}* Not gone aside to the right hand nor to the left, in excess or defect, Josh. 1. 7. Psal. 18. 21. & 44. 17, 18.

**V. 12. ***{Neither have I gone back from the commandment of his lips, I have esteemed the words of his mouth more then my necessary food.}* Here Job shows by these several branches the great care he had to keep God's commandments and goes in a gradation. First he chose that way. 2. He kept to it, and went on in it. 3. He went not aside out of it. vers. 11. 4. He went not backward at any time. 5. He was more careful to do God's will, than to look after his bodily food. The sum of this verse is; I have taken so great delight in those ordinances, which our forefathers receiving from his mouth have delivered unto us, that laying them up in the treasure-house of mine heart, I have preferred them before whatsoever was requisite, and necessary for the maintenance and preservation of this present life.

*{neither have I gone back}* The word signifies a quite leaving or forsaking a thing. But Job by a Meiosis means that he had carefully observed all God's precepts. As if he had said; They that see my sad condition would judge that I had cast off all piety. But it is not so. I have made conscience of every good duty.

*{from the commandment of his lips}* From those precepts, which God has made known to me, as men make theirs known by their words, which come out of their lips. For the word see on chap. 15. 6.

*{I have esteemed}* Heb. *I have hid or laid up*. Because those things we esteem highly, as gold, silver, jewels, we hide, lest any should take them away from us. So did Job hide God's word in his heart, that he might not lose it. Or, as housekeepers lay up provision for their family to bring forth in time of need. Psal. 119. 11. Prov. 2. 1. & 7. 1. Job kept God's words in his memory.

*{the words of his mouth}* The same in sense with the commandment of his lips, which was expounded before in this verse. For Mouth see on chap. 15. 5.

*{more then my necessary food}* Or, *my appointed portion*. Men cannot want daily food, and therefore treasure it up. I took more pains to lay up God's word in my heart, than men do to lay up in store provision for the belly, Prov. 30. 8. & 31. 15. Gen. 47. 22.

**V. 13. ***{But he is in one mind, and who can turn him? and what his soule desireth even that he doth.}* Job in this verse and that which follows, shows much human infirmity, and is therefore afterwards justly blamed both by Elihu, and by God himself. As if he had said, Although my conscience tells me, that I am innocent from such great offenses, as use to bring such heavy judgments upon men, yet God will not be moved from his resolution of dealing rigorously with me.

*{But he is in one mind}* He is resolved to lay a load of afflictions upon me, and continues in his purpose.

*{and who can turn him}* None can alter his resolution by power or persuasion, chap. 9. 12, 13. & 11. 10. For the word see on chap. 15. 13. Some read it thus, *if he be against one, who can turn him?* That is, God has conceived an implacable anger against me, and will not leave afflicting me, till I die.

*{and what his soule}* That is, himself. Spoken of God after the manner of men. Lev. 16. 30. Isa. 1. 14. For the word see on chap. 14. 22.

*{desireth}* Whatsoever pleases him, or whatsoever he will have done. There is no dissuading of him.

*{even that he doth}* He will certainly have his will, and bring it to pass, Psal. 115. 3. For the word see on chap. 14. 9.

**V. 14. ***{For he performeth the thing that is appointed for me: and many such things are with him.}* We see and feel by experience, that God will not be moved from what he intends to do: and therefore I have cause to expect many more such afflictions from him, rather than any relaxation of these pains.

*{For}* It will appear in the end, that God will have his will, when he has brought all the misery upon me, with which he intended to afflict me. Or, *Surely*. As it is translated, chap. 8. 6.

*{he performeth}* No man can hinder him from performing the uttermost of his will upon me.

*{the thing that is appointed for me}* He will send all those plagues on me that he has resolved to send, and will not cease afflicting me till all be done. The same word was used for appointed or necessary food, vers. 12.

*{and many such things are with him}* He has not yet showed all the evil he intended to me. He has many more plagues yet to bring upon me. This caused the following fear in Job, as he confesses in the next verse.

**V. 15. ***{Therefore am I troubled at his presence; when I consider, I am afraid of him.}* And this is it which does trouble and amaze me greatly so often as I look upon God's proceedings with me, and likewise consider with myself both the greatness and also the long continuance of my miseries, and see no likelihood of an end. I have cause then to fear that God will go on to use his great power to afflict me more and more till I die.

*{Therefore}* I am not troubled with the conscience of an ill-led life, as you uncharitably conceive of me; but frightened by God's mighty power manifested in afflicting me more than others. I am troubled at my present calamities, and afraid of fiercer.

*{am I troubled}* See on chap. 21. 6. I am so troubled that I would run away from it, if I could. For the word signifies haste, as well as fear or trouble.

*{at his presence}* Heb. *at his face*. Or, *because of him*. Or, *for fear of him*. For the word Face see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*.

*{when I consider}* When I weigh in my mind all God’s harsh dealing with me.

*{I am afraid of him}* See on chap. 22. 10.

**V. 16. ***{For God maketh my heart soft, and the Almighty troubleth me.}* For my heart fails me, and I am so troubled with the many miseries God has laid upon me, and the uncertainty of the end of them, that I have no strength left to bear them, 1 Sam. 28. 20.

*{For}* Heb. *And*. But it is translated, For, chap. 17. 10. And so it may here. You marvel that I am so dejected. You should not wonder at it, for God has cast me down.

*{God}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{maketh soft}* Makes me so afraid and dejected, that like soft wax I am not able to bear his heavy blows of affliction, but if they continue they will break me to pieces. So that Job here does not by a soft heart mean a penitent or patient one, but one worn with afflictions, and able to bear no more. It was broken with sorrows. As vers. 17. It may be he meant, that God had taken away strength within, and yet laid on load without.

*{my heart}* See on chap. 15. 12.

*{and the Almighty}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{troubleth me}* See on verse 15. of this chapter.

**V. 17. ***{Because I was not cut off before the darkness, neither hath he covered the darkness from my face.}* Here he shows the cause of his so grievous fear, because there appeared no issue out of his troubles, which as he uses, he compares to darkness. As if he had said, I have just cause indeed to fear and tremble, because God has brought such great troubles upon me, out of which I see no way of escaping.

*{Because I was cut off}* Destroyed by death, which is God's executioner to cut off men's lives.

*{before}* Heb. *From the face of*. So that these troubles might not have found me out, nor I seen or felt them, which I should not have done if God had killed me before. Others understand it thus, Because these troubles have not yet made an end of me, but I must live to suffer more. And this he speaks by way of wonder and admiration, that his poor carcass could endure so much misery, and not sink under it. See the like complaint, chap. 3. 20. & 10. 18.

*{the darkness}* The afflictions that now are upon me. I wish I had died, before they had come upon me. For Darkness, see on chap. 15. 22, 23.

*{neither hath he covered}* God has not quite kept the darkness of affliction from me as a thing covered, that I might not see evil or feel sorrow. Heb. *and he hath, &c*. But the negative particle is to be taken out of the former part of the verse, as it is Psal. 1. 5. And so to be read as before. Some read it affirmatively, and understand a keeping of him from death, which would have ended all his sorrows. As chap. 3. 23. & 6. 8. So death is called darkness, chap. 14. 23. But the former interpretation is better.

*{the darkness}* These great afflictions which are tedious to me, as a long dark night to a man that cannot sleep. See before on this verse.

*{from my face}* From me, that I might not have felt these sorrows. For the word Face, see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*.

## CHAPTER 24

**Verse 1. ***{Why seeing times are not hidden from the Almighty do they that know him not, see his days?}* In this other part of Job's answer in this chapter he pulls down the foundation, which his friends had laid, concluding him to be a wicked man, because so many and great troubles fell on him, as befell none but ungodly men. The words should rather be read thus, Why are not times hidden from the Almighty seeing they that know him do not see his dayes? It would make a man not well instructed in God's providence think, that God takes no notice of things done here, nor regards them, seeing the best of his servants could never observe that he punishes many ungodly men according to their deeds in this world. So that either Job shows what worldly men would conclude out of God's indulgence to wicked men here; or else he speaks in a passion or strong temptation, as doubting for the present of God's providence in punishing sinners, though elsewhere, when he was in a better mood, freely and fully confessed by him. See the like passion in godly men upon the like occasion, Psal. 73. 2. &c. Jer. 12. 1. Hab. 1. 12. &c. And this suits well with that which follows, wherein he repeats innumerable gross wickedness committed by ungodly men at which God winks, and punishes them not in this world.

*{why are not}* It seems so, and ungodly men are ready to think so.

*{times}* The wicked actions that ungodly men do in their times, as follows vers. 12. &c.

*{hidden}* Unknown, as things hidden or covered are unseen of us.

*{from the Almighty}* from God. Or, *by the Almighty*. So that good men cannot see him execute judgments on the wicked, nor know not when he will, as follows in this verse. For the word Almighty see on chap. 8. 3, 5. For מן By, see vers. 10. 14.

*{seeing}* So ו is translated, chap. 19. 28.

*{they that know him}* They that fear him, and are best acquainted with him.

*{do not see}* Cannot behold them. If it had been so general a truth, as you O Eliphaz do affirm, that God uses to punish ungodly men here in this world always, I and other godly men should have observed it before now.

*{his days}* The punishments which he had laid on ungodly men for their sins in the time appointed by him: chap. 20. 28. So the day wherein God pours out his wrath is called His day. Isa. 2. 12. & 13. 6. Jer. 46. 10. Joel 2. 11. For the word Day see on chap. 14. 14.

**V. 2. ***{Some remove the land-marks; they violently take away flocks, and feed thereon.}* Here Job begins to give a reason, why many may think that God does not guide the world, and makes a large catalogue of gross sinners not punished by him in this world: And first he gives instance in great oppressors to verse 14. In this verse he begins with thieves and robbers, and such as remove landmarks. As if he should say, What is it, that these men dare not do? For not regarding the landmarks, which are set for the dividing of one man's land from another, and in keeping whereof no small part of the public peace and tranquility consists, they encroach upon other men's grounds, and they feed on the herds of cattle, and flocks of sheep, which they have also violently, and wrongfully taken from others.

*{Some remove}* Some is not in the original, but added to make the sense plain. Or, *They remove*. That is, the wicked spoken of verse 6. So the antecedent follows the relative. Psal. 87. 1, 2. They remove the bounds for their own advantage to get away their lands from other men. This is often condemned in Scripture. Deut. 19. 14. & 27. 17. Prov. 22. 28. & 23. 10. Or, *Some touch*. For landmarks were counted sacred, and it was esteemed a fault so much as to touch them.

*{the land marks}* Heb. *The bounds*. These were set to determine all controversies between neighbor and neighbor about the limits of their lands. And therefore men anciently durst not touch them, much less remove them.

*{they violently take away}* See on chap. 20. 19.

*{flocks}* Heb. *a flock*. It signifies a herd of sheep and goats. It comes from a word that signifies to set in order. Cattle of the flock are kept in order, and not suffered to stray as other cattle do.

*{and feed thereon}* Nourish themselves and their families on other men's cattle. Or, feed them, that is, those cattle they have taken from others, they do not make away privily as thieves use to do lest they should be apprehended, but feed them openly in their own ground, or in the lands taken away from the owners of those cattle, or others, to show that they are not ashamed of what they have done, nor fear no punishment for it. The word רעה signifies 1. to feed others, men or beasts. Gen. 29. 7. & 48. 15. 2. To feed men, or rule over them. Psal. 78. 72. 3. To feed upon or eat up, as Jer. 22. 22. 4. To wander or remove from place to place, as sheep go about for food, and flocks are removed from pasture to pasture: Numb. 14. 33. 5. To be a companion to another, for such feed together like sheep. Prov. 13. 20. Here it may be taken in the first sense, for feeding them, or in the third sense to feed upon them.

**V. 3. ***{They drive away the ass of the fatherless, they take the widows ox for a pledge.}* Yea they are so much delighted with tyranny and oppression, that they spare not the most miserable and helpless among men, but seek to ruin them.

*{they drive away}* Take them from the owners for their own use.

*{the ass}* The asses. The singular for the plural, as Gen. 1. 20. Fowl for fowls. Or, the only ass of the fatherless, who had no more. So the sin is aggravated like David's, 2 Sam. 12. 3. Or, one ass from each of those fatherless people. An ass was a great loss to one of them, who needed it much to fetch home provision.

*{of the fatherless}* So impudent and cruel were they, that they would not spare poor orphans. See on chap. 22. 9.

*{they take for a pledge}* See on chap. 22. 6.

*{the ox}* Wherewith she must till her ground, and being so necessary for her ought not to be kept from her for a pawn, Exod. 22. 26, 27. Deut. 24. 6, 10, 11, 12, 13, 17.

*{of the widow}* See on chap. 22. 9.

**V. 4. ***{They turn the needy out of the way: the poor of the earth hide themselves together.}* Some understand this verse of the pride of these wicked men, who cannot endure to see a poor man in the way with them, and make them afraid to come in their sight. But the words foregoing and following require rather, that it should be understood of their oppression, which was so grievous, that the poor were forced to hide themselves out of their way, lest when they had taken their cattle, they should take them also for slaves.

*{they turn}* The fear of them causes the needy to go out of those ways they should walk in.

*{the needy}* It comes from a word that signifies to desire, for poor men's wants make them always desirous of supply.

*{out of the way}* See on chap. 17. 9. It is sometimes taken for a course of life, but here it is taken literally for a way that men walk in about their necessary affairs. Yet the poor durst not walk there for fear of the rich oppressors.

*{and the poor}* The word signifies such as are afflicted, and thereby impoverished.

*{of the earth}* Or, *of the land*. See on chap. 9. 24.

*{hide themselves}* They have been so roughly dealt withal by them, that they are afraid to appear in their presence, Prov. 28. 28.

*{together}* Being all in one condition they may hide themselves in one place to condole with one another. Or, *alike*. None has more hope to escape the fury of these unmerciful men, than others, notwithstanding any relation or other bands. So the word is translated, chap. 21. 26.

**V. 5. ***{Behold as wild asses in the desert, go they forth to their work rising betimes for a prey: the wilderness yieldeth food for them, and for their children.}* He sets out the cruelty of these oppressors, and the spoil they make in the world by the similitude of the wild ass, a swift and untamable creature, as if he had said, these are rather to be accounted wild and savage beasts than men: not unlike to the wild asses, which keep in the wilderness, early in the morning they go forth unto the spoil, as if this were the trade and occupation, whereby they must needs get their living. Fields and tillage they have none, but the wilderness and desert places, in which they practice this their pillage, are to them instead of great lands and possessions; out of which they gather no small revenues, and furnish themselves and theirs with all necessary provisions for life. So these oppressors live in this world as in a wilderness, making a spoil of all they meet withal.

*{Behold}* See on chap. 1. 12. Men may well look upon their cruelty with admiration.

*{as}* This is not in the original, and therefore is added in lesser letters to make the words plainer. See the like chap. 7. 9.

*{wild asses}* These are they that do all the spoil: the tame ones are fed by men, and kept in order.

*{in the desert}* For that is the place of wild asses: tame ones are in pastures among other cattle.

*{go they forth}* Out of their thickets where they lay hid all night. For the word see on chap. 20. 25.

*{to their work}* Wild beasts go not forth to work, but tame ones. Yet they go as constantly out to their prey every day as the tame ones do to their work. Heb. *in their work*. That is, to be employed in their work. One verb included in another, see on chapter 15. 8.

*{rising betimes}* As men that would thrive rise early in the morning to follow their work, so do wild asses to get a prey. This sets out the diligence of oppressors to watch all advantages to make a prey of others, chap. 7. 21.

*{for a prey}* The word טרף signifies 1. a prey gotten by wild beasts or thieves, Psal. 76. 4. 2. food, because the beast's food is their prey, which they get by violence. So it is translated Meat, Mal. 3. 10. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{the wilderness yieldeth food}* Though the wilderness be very barren, yet the wild ass can pick out sustenance there for him and his young ones. So oppressors can pick maintenance for them and theirs out of the throats of the poor. For the word Food, see on chap. 15. 23.

*{for them}* Heb. *for him*. For every one of these asses. So violence yields maintenance to every oppressor.

*{and for their children}* Or, *to him for his children*. To the ass for his young ones: to the oppressor for him and for his family, for the word is, for the young men. So the word is translated, 1 Sam. 21. 5.

**V. 6. ***{They reap every one his corn in the field: and they gather the vintage of the wicked.}* In the time of harvest they reap down other men's corn and by force carry it away, and take their vintage away also, like ungodly men, as they are, and so deprive them of the benefit of all their toils and labors past, and the hope of meat or drink in the winter following.

*{They reap}* The oppressors spoken of before.

*{every one his corn}* Every one the corn of another man laying some title to it, as if it were his. Or, it may be read, *his corne*; without every one. And then the sense may be, the servants of the oppressor fetch meat for their master out of other men's fields. Or, *its corne*. That is, the field's, as follows. Some divide the Heb. word thus. בלי לו *They reap the corne that is not his.* Which is not their master's. The word properly signifies provender for cattle. See on Chap. 6. 5. Heb. *mingled corne*, Or, *dredge*.

*{in the field}* They stay not, till the owners have reaped it, but prevent them.

*{and they gather}* They take it as boldly, as if it were their own.

*{the vintage}* Heb. *the vineyard*. That is, the grapes, that grow there.

*{of the wicked}* Of him whom they count wicked. Or, *of him that is as bad as themselves*. They spare neither good nor bad: Others read it, *the wicked gather the vintage*. You need not marvel they deal so unjustly, as to gather other men's crops, for they are wicked, and make no conscience of wronging any man. A verb plural after a noun singular is common in Heb.

**V. 7. ***{They cause the naked to lodge without clothing, that they have no covering in the cold.}* Those that come in their hands they not only spoil of their cattle and crops, but also of their garments, and so lay them open to the pinching cold, that they can take no rest.

*{they cause to lodge}* They take away their garments, and so make them lie naked all night. This was an aggravation of their cruelty, for the nights were colder than the days, neither could they then work to keep them warm.

*{the naked}* Such as they have made naked.

*{without clothing}* Without bedclothes, such as men use to lay on them in the night. These pawns men ought not to keep all night, Deut. 24. 13. But these unconscionable men make no scruple of breaking God's commandments. It may be read, *They cause them to lodge naked*; because they have no clothing. So, Him, in the Heb. is translated, Them, verse 5. And he means, that the poor, whom he had before spoiled of their cattle, now he spoils of their clothes. And מבלי is translated, Because not, Lam. 1. 4. Exod. 14. 11. So verse 8. of this chap. For want of a shelter, that is, because they had no shelter.

*{that they have no covering}* They have taken away all their clothes, so that nothing at all is left to cover them, and keep them warm.

*{in the cold}* This is a second aggravation. In the hot Summer nights they might better spare their bedclothes, but in the cold Winter, besides the trouble and pain, it might endanger their health and lives.

**V. 8. ***{They are wet with the showers of the mountains, and embrace the rock for want of a shelter.}* He goes on to show the progress of the malice of the oppressors and sufferings of the oppressed, which having their clothes taken away, dare not go abroad about their business, nor keep their houses, lest they should be dragged to prison by the oppressors, but go like conies or wild beasts to lodge in holes in the rocks, where the water falls on them so abundantly and violently from the mountains, that they are in danger of drowning, or catching their death by cold.

*{They are wet}* Being driven out of their houses they lie in holes, where they cannot lie dry for the rain.

*{with the showers}* With the abundance of water; that runs down the mountains after showers of rain, and falls into their lurking places.

*{of the mountains}* Which are over their heads.

*{and embrace the rock}* Make much of it, and are glad they may be hidden there from their oppressors. See the like phrase, Lam. 4. 5. They go into dark holes and are glad to lie on the cold stones instead of a warm bed.

*{for want of a shelter}* Because they know not where else to be safe.

**V. 9. ***{They pluck the fatherless from the breast, and take a pledge from the poor.}* Lo the former outrages committed abroad suffice them not, but they are as cruel within doors: where to the mother's great grief they violently snatch her infant from her breast, and strip the poor of their raiment.

*{They pluck}* Not the oppressed, vers. 8. but the oppressors spoken of before. For the word, see on Chap. 20. 19.

*{the fatherless}* See on Chap. 22. 9. But here it is meant of sucking children, whose fathers are newly dead, which is a double grief to the mother to lose the husband, and have the child violently taken from her.

*{from the breast}* From sucking the breast, that the mother being a slave may go to her work. They allow her not time to give her child suck. Or make her wean it before it be fit, that she may be freer for her slavish work. Or, they take it before it be weaned, to bring it up or sell it for a slave. Others read it, *from the prey, or destruction*. They had gotten all his goods before, and left him nothing, and now they seize upon his person to make him a slave. Others, *out of mischievousnesse*. They are so spiteful, that they will not spare the fatherless.

*{and take a pledge}* See on vers. 3. and Chap. 22. 6.

*{of the poor}* See on vers. 4. They take some necessary thing away which they cannot spare. See more on Chap. 22. 6. some read, *they take that which is on the poor for a pledge*. That is, they take away their raiment, vers. 10. and leave them naked by day, as they had done by night before. And this argues more cruelty, and impudence than the former. For men might take notice of this, which knew not what they wanted in the night. Others thus, *they take the poor for a pledge*. As the widow's ox before, vers. 3. And this was the height of cruelty and impudence, not to be content with their cattle, and clothes, but to force them by hard labor (having seized on their persons) to redeem themselves.

**V. 10. ***{They cause him to go naked without clothing, and they take away the sheaf from the hungry.}* Some understand this verse and the two verses following of their progress against those, whom they had oppressed before. Others of detaining the wages of such as they hired to do their work, and denying them necessary provisions in time of their labor in the oppressor's service.

*{They cause him to go naked}* Him whom they have oppressed before, they now oppress more, and take away his clothes. Or, they make the hireling to go naked, in that they will not pay him his wages, that he may buy him clothes.

*{without clothing}* The word was used for bedclothes vers. 7. here for wearing clothes in the day.

*{and they take away}* See on Chap. 22. 3.

*{the sheaf}* That small quantity of corn, which the poor man has in his ground, or has leased. This was great cruelty to oppress the poor, 2 Sam. 12. 3.

*{from the hungry}* From him that was ready to starve. This was extreme cruelty. See on Chap. 22. 7. Others read it thus, *And those that carried their sheaves they made to go away hungry*. That is, their laborers in harvest. Either they deprived them of their food they should then have had of them, contrary to Deut. 25. 4. Or, kept back their wages, so that they could not buy themselves victuals; contrary to Levit. 19. 13. Jam. 5. 4. Or, it may be they should have paid them in corn in exchange for their labor, for it is likely that money was scant in Job's time. But this they did not, and so exposed them and theirs to starving.

**V. 11. ***{Which make oil within their walls, and tread their wine-presses, and suffer thirst?}* Some interpret this verse of poor men, who having but little oil or wine to make, yet for fear of these oppressors dare not do it openly in the vineyard, but do it privately within their own walls, yet have it taken away by cruel oppressors, so that they cannot quench their thirst with their own oil or wine. Others understand it of the laborers, who made oil and wine for these oppressors within their walls, yet might not taste of it.

*{Which make oil}* This word is nowhere else used, and coming from a word, which signifies oil is fitly translated making oil.

*{within their walls}* Which oppressed men make oil within their own walls for their own use, or within the walls of the oppressors for theirs. Or, *within their ranks*. That is, between the olive trees, that grow in ranks, between which the olives ordinarily were pressed. Or, between the olives laid on heaps on each side to be cast into the press. Or, between the bounds set up in order to make a press to press the olives.

*{and tread their wine presses}* To press the wine out of the grapes, Isa. 63. 3.

*{and suffer thirst}* If it be meant of the wine-presses of the oppressors, then the meaning is, that they would not suffer the poor laborers, that trod out their wine to drink of it, contrary to Deut. 25. 4. If of their own wine-presses, then the sense is that the oppressors took all away, and would not let them drink of that small store of wine, they had left.

**V. 12. ***{Men groan from out of the city, and the soule of the wounded cryeth out: yet God layeth not folly to them.}* These cruel men not only oppress the men in the country, where few can behold or relieve them, but also are grown so impudent, that openly in the midst of cities where many look on, and laws should be in force most of all to restrain them, they do so grievously persecute the inhabitants that they cry out of their pain to God for justice against them, yet he calls them not to account for their cruelty.

*{Men groan}* That is, such of the citizens, as are oppressed by them. For the word Men, see on Chap. 19. 19. For the groaning of the oppressed, see Exod. 2. 24. & 6. 5. Ezek. 30. 24.

*{from out of the city}* Such as dwell in the city groan. Or, their complaints are heard out of the city, which they are forced to leave, and come to bewail their miserable estate in the country. Thus these oppressors wrong city and country. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 28.

*{and the soul}* The person himself, for the soul cannot groan, yet it imports an earnest and hearty groaning coming as it were from the soul, as if they were dying and giving up the ghost. See Ezek. 30. 24. For the word see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{of the wounded}* The word signifies one deadly wounded, as it is translated, Ezek. 30. 24. And such send out heavy groans. This sets out the greatness of the oppression.

*{cryeth out}* This also shows the greatness of the oppression, for men cry out aloud and earnestly in great pressures. See on Chap. 19. 7.

*{yet}* So it is translated, Psal. 119. 110.

*{God}* See on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{layeth not folly to them}* See on chap. 1. 22. He does not handle them as malefactors, nor call them to account for their foolish, and sinful actions done against all reason and equity.

**V. 13. ***{They are of those that rebel against the light; They know not the ways thereof, nor abide in the paths thereof.}* Having before set out the open iniquities of oppressors, now he comes to set out the secret works of darkness, and the horrible wickednesses, which some commit in secret unknown to the world, yet not hid from God, nor punished by him.

*{They}* Those wicked oppressors spoken of before besides their violence known to the world, commit many horrible sins in secret. Or besides those open oppressors, there are some that commit horrible sins in secret. God knows all this, but punishes neither.

*{They are of those that rebel}* That fight against the light, as rebels do against lawful authority.

*{against the light}* Some understand it of spiritual light. They sin against the light of nature and Scripture. But the words following enumerating the great sins they commit in the night, shows plainly that it is meant of the light of the day, as is expressed plainly, verse 14. And the sense is, they cannot abide the daylight, no more than rebels can lawful authority: and if it were in their power, they would pull the Sun out of heaven, that it might be always night, that they might sin unseen. For the word, see on Chap. 17. 12.

*{they know not}* They do not acquaint themselves with it. They are mere strangers to it. They love not to walk in it.

*{the ways thereof}* The ways of the light. They love to do their business by night, and not by day, lest their sins should be discovered, 1 Thess. 5. 7. Eph. 5. 11, 12. Or, *his wayes*, That is, God's ways, who was mentioned, vers. 12. They are unacquainted with God's word the rule of life. For the word, see Chap. 16. 22.

*{nor abide}* Are not constant in them. If they stumble on a good action, they quickly give it over: For the word, see on Chap. 22. 8.

*{in the paths thereof}* Of the light. Or, *in his pathes*. That is, God's; as before.

**V. 14. ***{The murderer rising with the light, killeth the poor and needy and in the night is as a thief.}* He sets out the indefatigable study and discovery and observation of fit times by wicked men to spoil and rob others. In the daytime they either bide in their habitations, or lie skulking in woods and byways, but early in the morning they set out to rob those that go forth in the first light of the day to do their business, and at night they watch such as travel late, to make a booty of them.

*{The murderer}* He that kills men without authority, contrary to the sixth commandment, where the same word is used in the original.

*{rising}* Out of his bed or hiding place. For the word see on chap. 14. 12.

*{with the light}* Heb. *at the light*. That is, as soon as the daylight appears, at daybreak before it be too light to discover him, Mic. 2. 1. Psal. 10. 8. As honest men rise early to go about the works of their calling, so he to rob and kill. For the word, Day, see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{killeth}* Those that oppose him, or that he thinks can discover him.

*{the poor and needy}* See on verse 4.

*{and in the night}* When the light is past, and the dark comes again, he goes to seek his prey, as the wild beasts do, Psal. 104. 20, 21.

*{is as a thief}* That is, is a very thief indeed. So as sometimes in Scripture language, sets not out the similitude, but the thing itself. They would be angry, if they were called thieves, but they are so indeed. See the like phrase, Hos. 4. 4. & 5. 10. Joh. 1. 14.

**V. 15. ***{The eye also of the adulterer waiteth for the twilight, saying, No eye shall see me, and disguiseth his face.}* As he had before set out the secret ways of the murderer, so now of the adulterer, who never thinks himself safe enough, but gets him a double cover, the one of the dark night, the other of a vizard, that he may not be discovered.

*{The eye}* The adulterer himself uses his eye to that purpose. For the word, see on Chap. 20. 9.

*{also}* He is as watchful of his opportunities as the murderer.

*{of the adulterer}* Of him that desires to defile his neighbor's bed. It is the same word, which is used in the seventh commandment.

*{waiteth for}* Expects it, longs till it come, or observes and takes notice of it as soon as it comes, that he may execute his filthy desires. For the word, see on Chap. 7. 12, 20. & 10. 14.

*{for the twilight}* Not in the morning, when the day comes on, but in the evening, when it begins to be dark, and the night coming on affords him a large time to satisfy his lust, Prov. 7. 9, 10.

*{saying}* In his heart; not with his mouth to himself, nor to others, Psal. 10. 11.

*{No eye shall see me}* My adultery shall not be discovered, but be kept secret. See the like phrase, Chap. 7. 8.

*{and disguiseth his face}* Heb. *and he putteth his face in a secret place*. Hides it with his cloak or with a vizard, that he may not be discovered. So whores were wont to hide their faces, Gen. 38. 14. For the word, Face, see on Chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*.

**V. 16. ***{In the darke they dig through houses, which they had marked for themselves in the day time: they know not the light.}* From describing the course of murderers, and adulterers he goes on to set out the extravagant courses of thieves and robbers, who spare for no labor by day or night to enrich themselves by the spoils of others. Or, it may be understood of adulterers, as shall be showed in the particulars.

*{in the darke}* In the night: for either they dare not, or are ashamed to do it in the day. For the word, see on Chap. 14. 22, 23.

*{they dig through}* Heb. *he digs through*. The thief, or murderer, vers. 14. Or, rather the adulterer, verse 15. which is the immediate coherence.

*{houses}* To come at his prey, enemy, or strumpet. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 28.

*{which they had marked for themselves in the day time}* The thieves or murderers go abroad in the daytime to see, where are houses likely to afford good booty, and easiest to be broken up, or enemies easiest to come at, and in the night they divide themselves every one to the place designed for him in the day. Or, the adulterer and adulteress confer together in the day, and appoint some house to meet in at night, and the adulterer takes care for a secret passage. The digging through walls that are near for adultery, and secret passages unknown to the husband, are no new things, as appears by the ancient heathen poets, who describe such privy meetings of such lewd persons for such sinful purposes.

*{they know not the light}* They cannot abide it, but wish for night still to execute their wicked designs, which if done in the day would either be prevented or discovered. See on verse 13.

**V. 17. ***{For the morning is unto them even as the shadow of death: if one know them, they are in the terrors of the shadow of death.}* In this verse, a reason is given why such wicked persons make haste to dispatch their unlawful designs in the night, because they think they are quite undone, if the morning light discover them.

*{For the morning is unto them even as the shadow of death}* Or, *For as soone as it is morning, the shadow of death comes upon them*. If those murderers or thieves come too late or tarry too long about their evil exploits, and the daylight appear before they have finished them, then are they in a deadly fear, lest they should be discovered, and brought to condign punishment. Or, if the adulterer and adulteress lie too long in bed, they are in a sad condition, fearing discovery and punishment. The morning is to such malefactors horrible, and fearful as a dark grove to a passenger, who fears death every step he treads. Or, They think death approaching, as a man does, whose sight decays. Or, as by the shadow is discerned the approaching of the substance, so they think death at hand. For Morning, see on Chap. 7. 18. For the shadow of death, see on Chap. 3. 5.

*{if one know them}* If any man spy them about their wicked enterprises when the day peeps. Or, *if they can discerne one another*. As this phrase is used, Ruth 3. 14. At break of day such persons are ashamed to look upon one another, and afraid lest others should take them in their wickedness, and discover them.

*{they are in the terrors of the shadow of death}* As full of fears as men who discern death standing before them to lay hold presently on them. See on verse 11.

**V. 18. ***{He is swift as the waters, their portion is cursed in the earth; he beholdeth not the way of the vineyards.}* When he has robbed, murdered, and committed adultery, till the earth be weary of him, then he turns pirate, and does what mischief he can at sea or on great rivers.

*{He is swift}* He gets him swift boats or ships to spoil such as trade upon the waters.

*{as the waters}* Heb. *upon the waters*: upon the Sea or Rivers. They that read it, *as the waters*, give this sense, When he is like to be taken in his wickedness in the morning, he flees away swiftly like water in the river to escape punishment. Others understand it of his flying by sea. But it is best interpreted of piracy. For Water, see on Chap. 14. 9.

*{their portion is cursed in the earth}* They have carried themselves so ill on the land, that they look for no portion there, but all men curse, and therefore they will come there no more. Or, They dwell in desolate places near the sea, which are barren, as if they were cursed, that so they may be ready to rob on the waters upon all occasions. So barren ground is cursed ground, Gen. 3. 17. fruitful ground blessed, Gen. 27. 27. Others understand this and the words following of the destruction of wicked men by God's judgments on them and their possessions. But then should Job cross all he had said before, and yield up the bucklers to his enemies, and say as they said. It is more likely therefore, that he goes on to set out the prosperous life, and quiet death of men so bad, that all the world hates them. For the word Portion, see on Chap. 20. 29. For Cursed, see on Chap. 3. 1. For Earth, on Chap. 9. 24.

*{He beholdeth not the way of the Vineyards}* He did heretofore live by husbandry and planting, but now he has got an easier trade, and looks after it no more. Or, *he turneth not, &c*. So the word is used, Psal. 25. 16. Others interpret it of never enjoying a fruitful land more, but living in poverty, which is contrary to the scope of the place, as was noted before. For the word Beholdest, see on chap. 5. 1. upon the word *Turn*. For the word Way, on chap. 17. 9. Others understand it of keeping himself in desert places, and not walking in those ways, where men use to travel to their work, lest he should be taken.

**V. 19. ***{Drought and heat consume the snow-waters: so doth the grave those which have sinned.}* As the snow-waters are easily and quickly consumed by drought and heat, so wicked men continue prosperously in their sins all their life long, and then die a natural death quickly and easily, and are not taken away violently by God's judgments. Such a like affirmed by Job, chap. 21. 13, 32. Psal. 23. 4.

*{Drought}* It properly signifies the drought of the ground, which speedily drinks up the snow-waters melted by the heat of the sun.

*{and heat}* Heat will melt ice, and much more snow.

*{consume}* Heb. *snatch away*; quickly take away, as thieves do other men's goods lest they should be taken.

*{the snow-waters}* The snow melted into water, which is easily dissolved, and suddenly gone.

*{so doth the grave}* See on chap. 7. 9.

*{those which have sinned}* Gross sinners die as easily as other men. The word signifies to err and miss the mark. See on chap. 5. 24. But here it points at oppressors and great malefactors. The whole verse may be read thus, *In the drought and heat they rob, and in the snow-waters: they sin to the grave.* That is, they rob in all weathers, summer and winter, and never give over till they die. The prepositions *in* and *to* are not in the original, but such are often omitted.

**V. 20. ***{The womb shall forget him, the worm shall feed sweetly on him, he shall be no more remembered, and wickedness shall be broken as a tree.}* He goes on to describe the easiness of the death of the wicked man, as shall be showed in the interpretation of the several parts of the verse.

*{The womb shall forget him}* His mother that bare him, shall not be much afflicted at his death, because he died so easily, free from the torments and violences that others endure, when they go out of the world. The word Womb in the original comes from a word that signifies affection, for mothers affect their children tenderly whom they have borne in the womb. Prov. 31. 2. And they are much afflicted at their painful death and would not forget it, but speak often of it.

*{the worms shall feed sweetly on him}* See on chap. 17. 14. He shall no more feel the worm eating his carcass than other men do. See the like expression of lying quietly in the grave, chapter 21. 33.

*{He shall be no more remembered}* Had he died by some memorable judgment of God for his wickedness, he would have been talked of longer, but dying a natural and easy death, as others do, he is soon forgotten as well as they, verse 24. Psal. 31. 12.

*{and wickedness}* See on chap. 13. 7. upon the word *Wickedly*. But here by wickedness is meant the wicked man, or oppressor spoken of before. As Psal. 107. 42. So vanity for a vain man, chap. 35. 13.

*{shall be broken as a tree}* The wicked oppressor shall die as easily as a rotten tree is broken down. Or, all his wickedness shall as easily be forgotten as the pieces of a rotten tree molder away. For a Tree see on chap. 14. 7.

**V. 21. ***{He evil entreateth the barren that beareth not: and doth not good to the widow.}* He looks back to other ungodly actions of this cruel oppressor before he died, and shows that after he had robbed the husband, and murdered him, he deals harshly with his wife now left a widow, and sometimes barren, and having neither husband, nor child to relieve her.

*{He evil entreateth}* Heb. *He breaketh*: So the word is used, Jer. 11. 16. Psal. 2. 9. Or, *He feedeth upon*, Or, *destroyeth*. For the word, see on verse 2.

*{the barren}* Such were dejected enough of themselves, barrenness being esteemed a curse in those times, yet this cruel man adds to her affliction, 2 Sam. 1. 26.

*{that beareth not}* That brings not forth children.

*{and doth not good}* That is, does much hurt. For he intends to set out the cruelty of oppressors to such as have none to defend them. This manner of speaking is usual in Scripture, as in the third commandment, God will not hold him guiltless, that takes his name in vain, that is, he will hold him very guilty. See the like, Psal. 51. 17.

*{to the widow}* See on chap. 22. 9.

**V. 22. ***{He draweth also the mighty with his power; he riseth up, and no man is sure of life.}* While the oppressor was alive, he went on from one degree of cruelty to another. And when he had made a prey of the poor, and helpless, then he sets upon the rich and mighty, and never leaves persecuting of them, till he have them in danger of their lives.

*{He draweth also}* To wit, into his power and danger, as a fowler draws fowls into the net. Psal. 10. 9.

*{the mighty}* The strongest or richest of men are not able to stand out against him.

*{with his power}* He is stronger and richer than they, and so prevails against them.

*{he riseth up}* To oppress others. When he sets himself against them, he ruins them. So enemies in battle are said to rise up against men, Psal. 18. 39. & 44. 5. For the word see on chapter 14. 12.

*{and no man is sure of life}* Heb. *and he is not sure of life.* Though he be strong, yet he is not able to stand up against the oppressor, who not only takes his wealth away, but also endangers his life, Deut. 28. 66. For the word Sure see on chap. 15. 22. upon the word *Believeth*.

**V. 23. ***{Though it be given him to be in safety wherein he resteth, yet his eyes are upon their ways.}* Job goes on to set out the prosperities of these wicked men contrary to the opinion of his friends, showing that although they got their goods so wickedly, and God was not ignorant of it, yet he gave them a peaceful settled estate till their death.

*{Though}* This word as it is not in the original, so it may well be left out, and the words read thus, *It is given him to be in safety, wherein he resteth, though his eyes are upon their wayes.*

*{it be given him}* Or, *he gives it him.* That is, God, often understood in this book, whensoever anything is spoken belonging to him. God makes them thus to prosper, though they be extreme wicked. For the word see on chap. 14. 4. upon the word *Bring*.

*{to be in safety}* God has bestowed upon them all things needful for preservation and maintenance plentifully.

*{wherein he resteth}* So that he fears no danger, but lives as he list.

*{yet his eyes are upon his ways}* Or, *though his eyes are upon their wayes.* That is, Although God sees all their wickedness, yet he makes them prosper. Or, *and his eyes are upon their wayes.* God's eyes seem to be continually fixed upon them, so that he blesses all their labors, and suffers them to want nothing. Ez. 5. 5. Psal. 34. 15. Deut. 11. 12. For the word Eyes see on chap. 15. 15. upon the word *Sight*. For the word Ways see on chap. 17. 9.

**V. 24. ***{They are exalted for a little while, but are gone and brought low, they are taken out of the way as all other, and cut off, as the tops of the ears of corn.}* Here Job shows that he did not intend to speak of any judgments of God, that cut off wicked men before their time, but to show that both in life and death they fared like other men. Man's life is not long, yet all that time they lived they prospered, and lived as long as others, and died in a full old age, and easily, as corn cut down when it is ripe.

*{They are exalted}* They grow rich and are promoted to great honor.

*{for a little while}* For that small time they live upon the earth.

*{but are gone}* Heb. *and are not.* To wit, here upon the face of the earth among men, chap. 3. 16. The beginning of the verse may be read thus, *They are exalted, and within a little while they are not.* That is, they live in prosperity, and die easily without any long or lingering sickness.

*{and brought low}* As low as death.

*{they are taken out of the way}* Heb. *They are shut up.* To wit, in the grave.

*{as all other}* They die and lie quietly in the grave, as other men do, chap. 3. 14. & 21. 33. Eccles. 2. 16.

*{and cut off}* But he speaks of a natural death; see on chapter 14. 2.

*{as the tops}* Heb. *as the head.* See on chap. 19. 9.

*{of the ears of corn}* This was that hard word in the original, that cost the lives of many Ephraimites, they could not say *Shibboleth*. Judg. 12. 6. By the tops of the ears of corn may be meant the ears themselves which grow on the top of the straw. See the like phrase, chap. 22. 12. about the height of the stars.

**V. 25. ***{And if it be not so now, who will make me a liar, and make my speech nothing worth?}* In the conclusion Job by his earnestness shows his confidence in the equity of his cause, which he pleaded that it was so just and so fully laid open and proved by him, that no man could confute what he said.

*{And if it be not so}* If this be not a truth, which I have contended for, that such as oppress others, live prosperously, and die easily.

*{now who will make me a liar}* If any one of you think he can confute me, let him come forth, and do it, Or, *where? who is he that will make me a lyer?* See the words so used, chap. 9. 24. As if Job had looked round about him for an antagonist. By making him a liar, he means disproving what he had said, and convincing him of untruth.

*{and make my speech nothing worth}* Heb. *and bring my speech to nothing.* So the Dutch translation reads it *also*. That is, show that my reasons are nothing worth, and do not prove that for which I brought them.

## CHAPTER 25

**Verse 1. ***{Then answered Bildad the Shuhite and said}* Bildad is very brief in this last reply upon Job. Either he thought Job obstinate, and would not spend many more words upon him; or else he was convinced that Job was in the right, and therefore gives no answer to his dispute chap. 24. but only to his complaint against God, chap. 23. and seeks to convince him by arguments taken from God's majesty, and the heavenly creatures, as Eliphaz had done before, chap. 4. 17. &c.

*{Then}* See on chap. 4. 1.

*{Bildad the Shuhite}* See on chap. 2. 11.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Dominion and fear are with him, he maketh peace in his high places.}* As if he had said, surely Job you do forget God's supreme authority and power, which might strike awful fear of him into all creatures, as it does to the mighty angels, whom together with the other heavenly bodies he rules so powerfully and peaceably, that they have neither cause nor will to complain of his proceedings. How dare you then being a weak sorry man call the great God to give an account to you of his judgments? As he did, chap. 23. 3, 4.

*{Dominion}* The supreme authority over all things in heaven and earth. Heb. *To rule.* It is his due to rule, yours to be subject.

*{and fear}* That is, majesty which may strike a fear and terror into others: for God fears none. For the word see on chap. 15. 21. Upon, A dreadful sound.

*{are with him}* With God who is often spoken of without naming in this book, the great things mentioned making manifest at first sight, who is spoken of. He has the highest authority, and majesty answerable.

*{he maketh}* See on chapter 14. 9. upon the word *Bringeth forth*.

*{peace}* Concord or agreement. Or, *prosperity and tranquility*.

*{in his high places}* In the heavens both visible and invisible, which are clearly his. Men can lay some title to the earth, but no color of title to heaven. Some read this latter clause thus, *He worketh in peace in his high places*, No angel in heaven dare complain of his proceedings, and how dare you O Job call him to account? Others read the whole verse thus; *The dominion and power which is with him, maketh peace in his high places.* He has so much authority and majesty, that no angel in heaven among those many millions dare question him. And though there be thousands of stars, yet he keeps them all from clashing one against another, and rules the whole heaven in peace and happiness. For the word see on chap. 16. 19.

**V. 3. ***{Is there any number of his armies? and upon whom doth not his light arise?}* It is in vain for you poor man to think of contending with God, who has innumerable armies ready to fight for him, if any man oppose him. Yea you have cause to submit to him, who gives light to you, and to all living creatures.

*{Is there any number}* They are innumerable.

*{of his armies}* All creatures in heaven and earth are God's soldiers, but here are intended more especially the innumerable company of angels, or rather stars in heaven, as appears by what went before and follows after. These are all ready to fight at God's command, and for his honor, 2 King. 19. 35. Judg. 5. 20.

*{and upon whom doth not his light arise}* There is no living creature, that enjoys the light of the sun, but is beholding to God for it. For it is light given by him to the sun. For Light see on chap. 17. 12. For Arise see on chap. 14. 12.

**V. 4. ***{How then can man be justified with God? or how can he be clean that is born of a woman?}* Not only God's majesty, but also your own impurity should make you afraid to desire to plead with God. For he is just and pure, you are sinful and impure, and that by your very birth.

*{How then can}* It is not possible. If you will contend with God you must needs go by the worst, for he is always in the right.

*{man}* A miserable creature whose sins bring diseases and death upon him. See on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{be justified}* See on chap. 4. 17. & 9. 20.

*{with God}* Some understand it before God, if he refer himself to God's judgment, he must needs pronounce him sinful. Others, *in comparison of God*. Though he may be excellent in comparison of other men, yet he is very unjust *in comparison of God*. But the whole scope shows that Bildad meant, if he will contend with God, as if God had done him some wrong in afflicting him more than was fitting. This was Job's motion, chap. 23. 3, 4. of which Bildad here shows the inequality, and injustice, and unprofitableness. For the word, God, see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{or how can he be clean that is born of a woman}* See on chap. 14. 4. & 15. 14. &c.

**V. 5. ***{Behold even to the moon, and it shineth not; yea the stars are not pure in his sight.}* You think the moon and stars are glorious creatures: and so they are indeed. Yet if God should contend with them, he would find spots and blemishes in them: so will he find sins in you worthy of his corrections, notwithstanding your vain confidence in your own uprightness.

*{Behold}* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{even to the moon}* If you consider all the glorious things in the world beginning with earthly things, as gold, silver, jewels, and then look above these to the moon and stars, those heavenly lights, which are clearer than they, you will find, that God can spy blemishes in them. He mentions the moon either because that is the lowest of those heavenly lights, or else it may be this conference held while night, and Bildad might with his finger point up to the moon, when he could not see the sun. So Piscator conceives that David made the eighth Psalm in the night, and therefore mentions not the sun, Psal. 8. 3. So Elihu is conceived to speak according to the present occasion, chap. 37. 21. and Piscator thinks Christ saw a vine in his passage from the house to the garden, and took occasion from thence to use that similitude, Joh. 15. 1. The word in the original is conceived to come from a word, that signifies to cool or refresh, because the moon cools those things in the night, which the sun overheats in the day. And for the same reason God is pleased to give an intercourse of fair weather and foul, summer and winter.

*{and it shineth not}* Though it shine clear to us, yet God can see darkness in it.

*{yea the stars}* Those glorious lights that are thought to be higher than the moon, and farther from the earth.

*{are not pure}* Free from blemishes and spots. See on chap. 14. 4. & 15. 14.

*{in his sight}* He discerns obscurity in them, though we cannot, Heb. *in his eyes.* See on chap. 15. 15.

**V. 6. ***{How much less man, that is a worm? and the son of man, which is a worm?}* In the conclusion Bildad lays man as low as he can, showing that he is so far below the stars, that he is as base as the worms, which are the vilest creatures, trod upon by men, yea he is a very worm.

*{How much less}* See on chap. 4. 19.

*{man}* See on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{that is a worm}* That is, a vile creature like a worm. So the word of similitude, As, is left out, chap. 7. 7. See on chap. 17. 14. The word properly signifies such little worms as breed in flesh or cheese, or other food, Exod. 16. 24.

*{and the son}* This shows that this baseness comes by our birth, and is common to all men. For the word, see on chapter 14. 14.

*{of man}* See on chap. 14. 1.

*{which is a worm}* The word in the original differs from the former, and signifies both great and small worms. And it is doubled to assure us of man's baseness.

## CHAPTER 26

**Verse 1. ***{But Job answered and said,}* Job offended with Bildad's speech, as having said nothing to the controversy, ironically invades him, and whereas Bildad had spoken of God's great power and wisdom, he shows that he could speak more highly of God than Bildad had done, and therefore his speech, as it was impertinent, so also needless. His complaint against Bildad is in the four first verses. His description of God's magnificence in the rest of the chapter.

*{But}* Heb. *And.* It is used 1. as a word of connection, as Prov. 2. 9. 2. as a word of opposition, Prov. 1. 25. 3. as a word of assimilation, Job 16. 21. 4. as a word of augmentation, Job 30. 9. 5. it gives a reason of a thing, Job 11. 4. 6. it infers one thing from another Job 27. 12. And accordingly it is translated, *And, or But, or As, or Yea, or For, or Then*. Here it is used in the second sense, for, But. And Job opposes his answer to Bildad's reply.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{How hast thou helped him, that is without power? How savest thou the arm that hath no strength?}* In this verse and those two that follow, there is an admiration of Bildad's proceeding, but ironical, and so the more emphatical. Some understand it of God, as if Job had said, O Bildad, how stoutly have you stood up in defense of God, as if he were not able to defend himself? You have played the cunning advocate, where there was no need. Others interpret it as having relation to Job himself, as if he had said, You are one of those, that take upon you to comfort me in this my sad condition, but you have altogether discouraged me.

*{How}* You have not well performed the task you have taken in hand.

*{hast thou helped}* By pleading for God, or advising me. God appears never the more just, nor I am never the more comforted for anything you have said.

*{him that is without power}* You think God is mightily beholding to you for pleading for him: but he can defend himself without your help. Or, you think I am much engaged to you for upholding me in my weak condition by wholesome counsel; but I see no such thing.

*{How savest thou}* What freedom or deliverance gets such a one by your words?

*{the arm}* Him that is weak: for the strength of man lies in his arms. You think belike that God is weak and needs your help to defend him. Or, though I be very weak, yet I have no help from you at all. See on chap. 22. 9.

*{that hath no strength}* Him that is unable to defend himself.

**V. 3. ***{How hast thou counselled him that hath no wisdom? and how hast thou plentifully declared the thing as it is?}* God has wisdom enough and needs not your counsel, neither have you spoken truly of the thing. Or, my ignorance is no whit informed by your discourse, neither have you spoken truly of my condition.

*{How hast thou counselled}* You speak as if you were of God's privy counsel, and could direct him how to guide the world. Or, you have given me uncomfortable counsel in my sad condition.

*{him that hath no wisdom}* You carry yourself in this case as if God had not wisdom enough to guide the world without your direction. Or, you think me a fool, and have given counsel altogether unprofitable for me.

*{and how hast thou plentifully declared}* You think that you have so wisely and plentifully handled the cause, that all must needs be of your mind, but you are deceived.

*{the thing as it is}* The solid truth: the word signifies, 1. essence or being, or the substance of a thing. Or, *that which is*, as chap. 11. 6. & 30. 22. From this תושיה comes the Greek word for essence *οὐσία*, 2. working or operation, which is an effect of essence. Things must be before they can work, and are to small purpose, if they work not. So Isa. 28. 29. 3. wisdom which is one of the most excellent things which are, or have a being, Prov. 2. 7. & 3. 21. Here it is taken in the first sense, for that which is, as it is translated, yet some take it in the third sense, and read it thus, *and hast declared wisdome abundantly.* You think you have made a very wise oration, and couched much learning in few words: but it is not so. Others thus, *and how hast thou made him to know wisdome abundantly?* Which last reading is very answerable to the former part of the verse, you speak so highly, as if you were able to teach God abundance of wisdom, how to govern the world.

**V. 4. ***{To whom hast thou uttered words? and whose spirit came from thee?}* Whom think you that you have spoken to? If to God, he is wise enough, and needs not your teaching. If to me, I know these things as well as you yourself, and you might have spared your pains. You conceive O Bildad that you have spoken high things revealed to you in an extraordinary way by God's spirit; but these are things commonly spoken of by other men; as well as by you.

*{To whom hast thou uttered words}* Whom have you gone about to inform? For, Uttered, see on chap. 15. 18. upon the word, *Told*.

*{and whose spirit}* Or, *whose breath.* Chap. 32. 8.

*{came from thee}* Or, *came out of thee.* Do you think that God's spirit spoke by you, in an extraordinary manner? Surely no. For the word, see on chap. 20. 25. Others understand it thus, Whose sad soul was recovered out of his dumps by your speeches? Others thus, Who received their souls from you, that you should make yourself equal with God, as if you could instruct him? Thus God convinces Job of folly by setting his great works before him, chap. 38. Others read it thus, *whose spirit admired thee.* For the spirit goes as it were out of itself after those things it admires. You think in the pride of your heart, that men will admire at your rare sayings: but there is no such cause.

**V. 5. ***{Dead things are formed from under the waters, and the inhabitants thereof.}* Lest any man should think that Job did in his former ironical speech deride those great things of God, which Bildad had spoken of the former chapter. And that Bildad might know, that Job understood these things as well as he, Job now makes a more majestical discourse of God's infinite power and knowledge than Bildad had done before. As if he had said, you have spoken of things visible, but I will show how God's providence reaches to those things, that no man can see how they are formed.

*{Dead things}* Or, *Livelesse things.* It comes from רפא which signifies to heal or make strong. And רפאים signifies 1. giants, which are the strongest men on earth, Deut. 2. 11. 2. Dead or lifeless things, which have no strength at all, Psal. 88. 10. So here, these dead things some take to be minerals and jewels and pearls, which are taken out of the bottoms of rivers or sea-waters, or of mountains from under springs. Others for reeds, and some trees, which take root under the waters, and grow out of them. Others for the spawn of fishes, which bring forth great increase.

*{are formed}* The word חול signifies, 1. to bring forth children out of the womb, when they have their right form, chap. 39. 1. 2. because children are brought forth with much pain and outcries both of the mother and child, it signifies to be in anguish, groan grievously or mourn, like a woman in travail, as Deut. 2. 29. Here it is taken in the first sense. Such things are formed in the bottom of the waters or under them as in a womb, and by God's providence brought forth to light, as children are.

*{from under the waters}* Either in the earth in the bottom of the waters, or in the lowest part of the waters. For Waters, see on chap. 14. 9.

*{and the inhabitants thereof}* That is, and under the inhabitants thereof, which are the fishes. Or; *with the inhabitants thereof*, The fishes are strangely formed by God's power in the waters, which one would think should scatter their spawn quite away, and not enliven it. Or, *which are the inhabitants thereof*, if it be meant of production of fishes from the spawn, or of dead bodies of men drowned, which may be called the inhabitants of the sea. So some interpret this verse. Or, *and under the neighbours thereof.* That is, under the mountains near the sea.

**V. 6. ***{Hell is naked before him, and destruction hath no covering.}* Not only things in the bottom of the earth and of the seas are uncovered before God, but also those that are in hell, and as it were clean destroyed to us; are clearly discerned by him.

*{Hell}* Or, *The grave.* Some understand it of the lowest parts of the earth: but those were spoken of in the last verse. This may therefore reach as low as hell, for God knows what is done there as well as in the grave. For the word, see on chap. 7. 9. upon the word *Grave*.

*{is naked}* As plainly discerned by God, as a body by us that has no garment to cover it, Psal. 139. 8. Prov. 15. 11. Heb. 4. 13.

*{before him}* In his sight. That is, in God's sight. The things spoken show whom he meant, though he named him not, so that his friends could not choose but understand of whom he spoke.

*{and destruction hath no covering}* It is the custom of the Scripture for greater assurance to repeat things in other words negatively, which it had before delivered affirmatively. So here. Hell is called destruction, because it does forever ruin all those that come into it. So the Devil is called Abaddon (the same word, that is here used) Rev. 9. 11. because he brings all his followers to eternal destruction in hellfire. And that is naked, which has no covering to keep it from the eyes of men. The sum is, God knows all, that is done in hell, which is covered from our sight, but not from God's.
