**V. 7. ***{He stretcheth out the north over the empty place, and hangeth the earth upon nothing.}* Having showed God's great power and wisdom by his working in and knowledge of those things, that are clean hid from men, now he sets it out further by his upholding the mighty frame of heaven and earth. You see the starry heaven, yet you see no solid body near it to uphold so great a fabric. You see the weighty earth compassed round about by the heavens, yet you see no pillars to uphold it. All this sets out the mighty power and wisdom of God.

*{He stretcheth out}* So the word is translated, Prov. 1. 24. Sometimes it signifies declining or bowing down, as Psal. 102. 11.

*{the north}* That part of the heaven where the north pole is seen, under which Job lived, and the south part was thought then not inhabited. See on chap. 9. 9. by a figure of the part for the whole, instancing in that part which he saw he may mean the whole heavens.

*{over the empty place}* Over the air, which seems to us empty, and is so in respect of any strength is in it to bear up such a frame.

*{and hangeth the earth upon nothing}* The earth is upheld only by God's almighty power, having no foundation at all, but hanging like a ball in the midst of the heavens, Psal. 104. 5. Heb. 1. 3. Whereas man cannot spread a curtain without some solid thing to uphold it. For the word, Earth, see on chap. 9. 24.

**V. 8. ***{He bindeth up the waters in his thick clouds, and the cloud is not rent under them.}* From the framing of heaven and earth, he proceeds to the course of God's daily providence. And first sets out his wisdom and power in causing thin clouds vanishing like smoke by a strange art to hold mighty floods of waters, as appears by many deluges in the world after a few days' rain. The water no doubt is thicker and heavier than the cloud, that holds it, yet the cloud is not cleft asunder with the weight of it, but keeps it as it were a strong man in a cobweb, till God please to give order for it to drop upon the earth by little and little to make it fruitful.

*{He bindeth up}* He keeps the waters in clouds as in vessels, so that they break not all through to overwhelm the earth. Therefore the clouds are compared to bottles, chap. 38. 37. The word signifies keeping them in by main strength, as things tied up in sacks, or sewed up, are kept from falling out.

*{the waters}* The rainwaters, or waters above the firmament, Gen. 1. 7. For the word, see on chap. 14. 9.

*{in his}* The clouds are called God's, 1. because as he causes the sun to draw up moist vapors from the earth, so he turns them into rainwater, and prepares the clouds to hold them. 2. Because God's incomprehensibleness in Scripture is set out by dwelling in and covering himself with the clouds, which no man can see through, verse 9. Psal. 18. 11. & 97. 2. 3. Because his majesty and state is set out by riding on the clouds, as a king or emperor on a stately horse, Est. 6. 8, 11. 10. 1.

*{thick clouds}* The word signifies any kind of clouds thick or thin. It is derived from a word that signifies thickness, because the clouds are the thickest part of the air, yet far thinner than the water which they hold. It should rather have been translated, *in his clouds.* As it is expounded in the end of the verse by another Heb. word. For the thinner the cloud is, the more strange is the thing. As 1. that waters should be kept in, whose nature is to flow abroad. 2. that they should not quite be restrained, but let down by degrees to water the earth. 3. that thin air should hold water thicker than itself. 4. that the weight of the water or violence of the wind driving the clouds up and down should not rend them, and let down all the waters in them to drown the earth, Gen. 7. 11. These are all strange things, and would be esteemed miracles, and might serve to convince atheists, if they were not ordinary.

*{and the cloud}* Which upholds these waters.

*{is not rent}* Or, *cleft into two pieces*, that all the water might run out.

*{under them}* Under the waters by the weight of them, which is so great, that they might easily break through a thin cloud and drown the earth. Yet ordinarily God lets them out so as they do no harm, but good, although sometimes in judgment he drowns men, and beasts with them. And the wonderful power and mercy of God is seen in keeping them in with clouds, as he does the sea with sand, that the whole world, and all the creatures in it do not perish in another flood, Jer. 5. 22.

**V. 9. ***{He holdeth back the face of his throne, and spreadeth his cloud upon it.}* Here God's incomprehensibleness is set out, as was hinted in the former verse. He has a glorious throne indeed in the highest heaven, but the clouds coming between keep us from the sight of it.

*{He holdeth back}* Heb. *He layeth hold on.* To wit, that he may hide it, as follows in the end of the verse.

*{the face}* The visible demonstration of it, as a man is known by his face. For the word, see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word, *Countenance*.

*{of his throne}* The highest heaven, which is his throne, Isa. 66. 1. It cannot be meant of the air, though that sometimes be called heaven, for the clouds cannot keep the sight of that from us, a great part whereof is below the clouds. They do indeed sometimes keep the sight of the starry heaven from us, and of the highest heaven always. Christ was covered with one from his apostles' sight, when he went up to heaven, Act. 1. 9.

*{and spreadeth his cloud upon it}* To cover it from the sight of men upon earth. As the clouds keep us from seeing God's throne in the highest heavens, so man's understanding is too shallow to comprehend God's essence.

**V. 10. ***{He hath compassed the waters with bounds, until the day and night come to an end.}* As before he had showed God's power in ordering the heavens, so now in bounding the seas: and that both in regard of place and time, that they may not go beyond their limits to the end of the world.

*{He hath compassed}* He has as it were with a pair of compasses drawn a circle about the sea, that it may not pass to hurt the earth. For the word, see on chap. 22. 14. upon the word *Circuit*.

*{the waters}* Heb. *the face of the waters.* So the face of the earth is put for the earth, Gen. 1. 29. For the upper part of it lies open to view like a man's face. For the word, Waters, see on chap. 24. 9.

*{with bounds}* The word חק signifies 1. a decree or statute, Mal. 4. 4. 2. the thing decreed, as a bound of place, chapter 38. 10. a decreed time, chapter 14. 13. appointed food, chapter 23. 12. Here it is taken for a bounding place to the sea, or a bounding time, as follows in the verse, within which as within a circled wall the sea is kept fast, chap. 38. 8, 10, 11. Psal. 104. 9. Prov. 8. 29. Jer. 5. 22.

*{until}* This word does not prove, as some would have it, that afterwards the sea shall overflow the land again, but that it shall not before, what it shall do afterwards is not revealed to us. So the word is used, Psal. 110. 1.

*{the day}* Heb. *light.* See on chap. 24. 13, 16.

*{and night}* Heb. *with darknesse.* As light is put for day, chapter 24. 13, 16.

*{come to an end}* Heb. *untill the consummation of light with darknesse.* That is, to the end of the world, which cannot stand without the vicissitude of day and night, Genes. 8. 22. Jerem. 31. 35, 36.

**V. 11. ***{The pillars of heaven tremble, and are astonished at his reproof.}* So great is the power of almighty God, that when he shows any sign of indignation, the strong heavens, and those things that seem to uphold them are likely to totter and fall down.

*{The pillars}* Some understand hereby the heavens themselves upheld by the strength God has put into them at first, as with pillars. As the pillar of a cloud, Exod. 14. 19. for the cloud itself. Others understand it of the highest mountains, which seem by their height to uphold the very heavens.

*{of heaven}* Of the starry or airy heaven. See on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

*{tremble}* Show their weakness, as a man afraid shows his by shaking. Or, the mountains shake in an earthquake, Exod. 19. 18. Psal. 114. 4.

*{and are astonished}* In danger of subversion, as a man trembling at some great object of fear knows not whither to go for help, but looks for present ruin.

*{at his reproof}* As a slave chidden by a prince trembles, and is aghast, so the pillars of heaven at the signs of God's indignation. Such as understand it of the heavens themselves interpret it of the thunder, whereby God seems to show his anger to the heavens, and to make them as it were to tremble, 2 Sam. 22. 8, 9. Psal. 18. 13, 14, 15. Psal. 104. 7. For God by the thunder speaks so loud, that stout men are terrified, Exod. 9. 28. Psal. 29. 3, &c. they who understand it of the mountains, interpret it of earthquakes which make them to shake, and sometimes to molder away, as if God were angry with them. Some put, until, in the beginning of the verse out of the former verse, and interpret both of restraining the sea, until the world end, when both heavens, and mountains shall be strangely altered, as if God were angry with them, 2 Pet. 3. 10, 12.

**V. 12. ***{He divideth the sea with his power, and by his understanding he smiteth through the proud.}* God's great power and wisdom is here further set out by raising storms in the sea, and quieting them again at his pleasure, which is too great a work for any creature to do.

*{He divideth}* Or, *He moveth.* Or, he causes a storm to come upon the sea, wherein it seems to be divided by the wind, part of it flying up into the air from the rest, and it lies as it were in ridges; the top of one wave far from another, Isa. 51. 15. Jer. 31. 35.

*{the sea}* See on chap. 14. 11.

*{with his power}* God is so strong that he can make the sea tempestuous.

*{and by his understanding}* As he is powerful enough to raise storms in the sea, so he is wise enough to lay them again, Psalm 107. 25, 29.

*{he smiteth through}* Wounds them as it were, so that they are not able to rise. Psal. 18. 38. That is, he allays the tempest, and makes the sea as quiet as a man mortally wounded.

*{the proud}* The proud sea, for in a tempest it seems to be proud, and swells as if it would overrun all the world. Heb. *pride.* Some understand it of proud men, which is nothing at all to the coherence, which rather refers it to the swelling of the sea, chap. 38. 11. Some understand the whole verse of making tempests, and interpret the smiting through the waves to be the same with dividing the sea before with the wind. Others conceive the dividing of the waters at first into seas and rivers to be here meant. Others understand it of dividing some arms of the sea from the body of it by earthquakes, filling some channels with the fall of hills. Others think all is spoken of calming the sea, and translate quieting for dividing, as Jer. 31. 2. it is, causing to rest. And so Jer. 50. 34. Others understand it of dividing the Red Sea for the children of Israel to pass through it. And by the last word they understand Egypt or the Egyptians then and there drowned, as it is taken, Psal. 87. 4. & 89. 10. Isa. 51. 9. In the two last places dividing the sea, and destroying the Egyptians are joined together. Others by Rahab understand Pharaoh, for both he and the Egyptians proudly insulted over Israel, as the sea at last did over them. Psal. 136. 13, 14, 15. This would stand well with the words, but that Job is conceived to be before that division of the sea. See on chapter 22. 15. At least if this were known to Job, it was likely to be by a relation of a thing new done before Moses writ it.

**V. 13. ***{By his spirit he hath garnished the heavens; his hand hath formed the crooked serpent.}* And that I may knit up my speech with the most excellent work among all others, this is he which has adorned the heavens with such wonderful and unspeakable beauty: and those circles, which like winding serpents fetch a compass round about the heavens, are framed with his own hands.

*{By his spirit}* By the Holy Ghost, who wrought in the creation, Gen. 1. 2. For the word, see on chap. 15. 2.

*{he hath garnished the heavens}* He has not only created them, but also adorned them beautiful to the eye. That is, he has garnished them with stars, as a palace is adorned with stately pictures. Others understand it of clearing the sky from dark clouds in sunshiny days, which is a beautiful sight. Others of lightnings, and fiery meteors, which at some times give light in the air. For Heavens, see on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

*{his hand}* His power has made it. For the word see on chapter 15. 23.

*{hath framed}* See on verse 5. upon the word, *Formed*; yet here it differs a little, as having no relation to forming in a dark place, like the womb, but only implying the making of them by God as truly as children are formed by him in their mother's belly. Others read it *wounded*, as Isa. 51. 9. where Rahab also is mentioned as here verse 12. by which they mean Egypt, and by the serpent Pharaoh.

*{The crooked serpent}* The several circles of heaven or orbs, which are involved one within another, as a serpent crooks or bows himself, and lies round, in several circles one within another. Others interpret it of a constellation, or company of stars called the dragon. Others of the whales or great sea fish, called sea-dragons, Isa. 27. 1. And they say that Job concludes his speech with two great and wonderful works of God in two extremes of the world, the stars in heaven, and huge fish in the sea. The Heb. is word for word, *the serpent the bar.* If it be taken for the circles of heaven, then they are like a serpent and bar in their roundness, as before, and smoothness. If of the constellation called the Dragon, the stars there it is likely are like a serpent, or bar at full length. So also are the whales long. If of the fiery meteor called *Draco volans*, the flying dragon, as some would have it, that also is long like a serpent and bar of a door. Thus Job labors many ways to set out God's infinite wisdom and power by higher expressions, than Bildad had done in the former chapter, and more largely.

**V. 14. ***{Lo these are parts of his ways, but how little a portion is heard of him? but the thunder of his power who can understand?}* Among God's infinite and innumerable works these are to be reckoned. But these are but small parts of them. For who is able to comprehend in his mind, much less to unfold in words the number, greatness, and admirableness of them? So he acknowledges the great wisdom, power and majesty of God, which shines forth abundantly in his glorious works, so as it might astonish the beholders. And withal implies his own and other men's slender ability to apprehend them according to the worth of them.

*{Lo}* Or, *Behold.* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{these are parts}* These works of God, which [have] repeated are indeed parcels of God's glorious works. Others interpret it *the ends.* That is, extremities or utmost parts of them. But there are much more glorious things to be spoken of God, if we could penetrate further into his works.

*{of his ways}* God's ways are taken sometimes for those ways whereby he draws near to us in his word and works, Isa. 55. 8. Sometimes for the ways wherein he would have us come to him, Psalm 119. 33. Here it is taken in the first sense for God's works, whereby he comes to us, or makes himself known to us. Some understand it of tempests, which all see to be God's ways, for men cannot bring them. So it answers to the thunder in the words following. So God's coming is set out. Psal. 18. 9. &c. And his way, Nah. 1. 3.

*{but how little}* That which has been spoken is but very little in comparison of what might be said, if we did fully understand, or were able fully to express God's proceedings. chap. 4. 12.

*{a portion}* Heb. *a word, or thing.* See on chap. 19. 28. upon the word *Matter*.

*{is heard}* In that which I have spoken now. Or, In all men's discourses. All that ever the wisest men could speak of God's ways falls infinitely short of the height of them.

*{of him}* Of God. This word shows that the scope of Job's speech was not so much to make us understand God's works, as to bring us to know God by them, and as it were to see him in them, as a builder in a stately house. For ב, of, see on chap. 7. 11.

*{but the thunder of his power}* Some understand it literally of the thunder, which wonderfully sets out God's power and frightens men. See on verse 11. upon the word *Reproof*. See also chap. 37. 2. Others of God's most glorious works, thundering in men's ears the great wisdom and power of God all the world over, and that more loud than the thundering of the captains in war, chapter 39. 25. Others of the setting out of the greatness of God's works in such a glorious manner as becomes them. So men talk of thundering eloquence which amazes the hearers, when great things are set out in high expressions. For the word Power see on chap. 9. 4. upon the word *Strength*.

*{who can understand}* No man can. It passes the capacity of the wisest men in the world to understand all God's wonderful works. Thus Job purges himself from the ignorance of God's ways, and profane contempt of God's works so often objected by his friends.

## CHAPTER 27

**Verse 1. ***{Moreover Job continued his parable and said}* Job having answered Bildad now stays a while expecting that Zophar should take his course. But he either hopeless of reclaiming Job, or confounded with his arguments, or having a better opinion of him by reason of his high expressions in setting out God's infinite wisdom and power, though far short of the things themselves, yet far beyond what himself and his companions had spoken of that argument, held his peace, and gives up the bucklers. So do the rest of his companions discouraged by him. For which Elihu was angry with them all, chap. 32. 1, 2. because they condemned him without a cause, yet he fails not to show Job his faults. When Job sees that his friends were silent, he goes on the more confidently to maintain his cause.

*{Moreover}* The word is And. Here it is translated moreover, to put us in mind of some space between this and Job's answer to Bildad.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{continued}* Heb. *added to lift up.* He spoke again, and more freely and boldly than before, to maintain the truth and his own integrity, seeing no man opposed him, and his adversaries were conquered. To Add in Scripture language signifies to do the same thing again, and to do more in that kind than was done before. So to add to sin is to sin again, and to sin more grievously, Judg. 3. 12. & 4. 1. & 10. 6. And for God to add to appear to Samuel, is to appear again, and declare his mind more fully to him, 1 Sam. 3. 8. For lifting up of a parable it may be meant of his speaking or lifting up words into the mouth, as Ascending into the heart is thinking, Jer. 7. 31. Or it may be spoken in regard of the voice. Job being now more fearless might speak in a louder tone to express his confidence. Or, in regard of the matter, for he that speaks in parables, or choice sentences speaks in a higher style, than they that speak in plain words. This phrase of lifting up a parable is used, Numb. 23. 7. For the word נשא see on chap. 21. 3. upon the word *Suffer*.

*{His parable}* It is derived from a word that signifies to rule. 1. Because they rule in speech, and illustrate our sentences, as rulers are eminent in a nation. 2. Because other speeches are tried by them, as by rulers and judges. 3. Because they come from kings and eminent persons, as Solomon's Proverbs. 4. Because they have a throne in all men's minds, and no man dares to contradict them. The word also from which it is derived signifies to be like. 1. because many parables are similitudes and liken one thing to another. 2. because they are characters of good men's manners, and express them lively as the wax does the seal stamped on it. By the word is meant, 1. Principles in divinity commonly believed of all, and used in common speech by them. Ezek. 18. 2. 2. Witty sentences spoken by wise men of great note, as Prov. 1. 1. 3. Riddles or hard sayings requiring labor for the understanding of them. Psal. 49. 4. & 78. 2. 4. Similitudes, Ezek. 18. 2. Here it may be used in any or all of these senses, for all are to be found in this chapter and the next.

*{and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{As God liveth, who hath taken away my judgment, and the Almighty, who hath vexed my soule.}* In this speech of Job, 1. He clears his own innocency further, to verse 11. of this chapter. 2. He acknowledges that God's judgments light on ungodly men sometimes in this world, but not always. This lasts to the end of the chapter. 3. He sets out God's infinite wisdom, which goes beyond the wisest projects of men, in the next chapter. In the clearing of his innocency, 1. He professes it to verse 7. 2. He proves it by his just confidence, which he could not have, if he were an ungodly man, or a hypocrite. For the clearing of his own innocency, 1. He does it by oath, and calling God to witness that he speaks nothing but the truth, verse 2, 3, 4. 2. By the unjustness of the act, if he should pronounce himself wicked, whom he knew not to be so, verse 5. 3. By self-condemnation which would follow thereupon, verse 6. In his solemn oath, 1. He calls God to witness, verse 2. 2. He professes his constancy in upholding his integrity to death, verse 3. 3. His uprightness, verse 4. For the first, The sum of this verse is, as if Job should have said, My friends I hear, what your opinion is of me, and I confess, that almighty God does at this time so deal with me, oppressing this my languishing soul with such extreme misery, that he seems to have debarred me from all right of defending, and maintaining my own innocency against you.

*{As God liveth}* The usual form of an oath among the people of God. 1 Sam. 25. 34. 2 Sam. 2. 27. For God see on chap. 8. 3, 5. For the word Liveth see on chap. 19. 25. When we speak of men's lives we mean at present, when we speak of God's life, we mean forever. Job's intention is to show that as sure as God is the living God forever, so certainly he will not forsake his integrity, nor speak falsely. He takes an oath here. 1. Because he speaks of a weighty matter. 2. Because his arguments being spent, he had no refuge left but to appeal to God. 3. Because else they might think he spoke ambitiously rather than truly of himself in the following chapter. 4. That he might keep himself within compass of truth speaking upon his oath.

*{who hath taken away my judgment}* If it be meant of the sentence of judgment, then the sense is, that God would not hear his cause, though often desired to do it, nor clear him although he were a godly man, and unjustly condemned by his friends. If it be meant of execution, then the sense is, That God would not take away from him those heavy judgments, which he uses to lay upon ungodly men, and which his enemies used as arguments to prove him to be such a one, and would not think any better of him, till they were removed. So he accuses God to have no regard of his innocency. And this passionate speech Elihu taxes him withal, chap. 34. 5. For the word Judgment, see on chap. 22. 4. For Taking away see on chap. 12. 20. upon the word *Removeth*.

*{and the Almighty}* And as sure as the Almighty lives forever. For the word see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{who hath vexed}* Heb. *who hath made bitter.* Who has laid great plagues upon me to the grief of my soul, which are as displeasing to me, as bitter things to the palate, Ruth, 1. 20.

*{my soul}* See on chap. 14. 22.

**V. 3. ***{All the while my breath is in me, and the spirit of God is in my nostrils}* Now he sets out the time how long this oath should bind him, to wit, as long as he lived.

*{All the while}* Heb. *That all the while.* That is, I swear, I will never alter my judgment of my own integrity while I live. Or, *Surely all the while.* So כי is translated, chap. 28. 1.

*{my breath is in me}* So long as I live. An elegant description of life, for as soon as man lives, he breathes, Gen. 2. 7. And when his breath fails, he dies, 1 King. 17. 17. Psal. 146. 4. Isa. 2. 22.

*{and the spirit}* The breath which God has given me, as appears by what went before, and what follows in this verse. For the word see on chap. 15. 2. upon Vain knowledge.

*{of God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{is in my nostrils}* See on chap. 14. 13. upon the word *Wrath*. While my breath goes in and out of my nostrils, I will maintain my integrity.

**V. 4. ***{My lips shall not speak wickedness, nor my tongue utter deceit}* How ill soever you judge of me, yet I will not speak contrary to that which I have said, and so do wickedly in betraying the truth, as you have done, chap. 13. 7. I will not deny my integrity.

*{My lips shall not}* Heb. *If my lips shall.* The reddition is left out, Then let me be counted a liar, or, Let God punish me severely for it. Another form of swearing, see on chap. 1. 11. So that here is a double oath. The first in v. 2. The second here. For the word see on chap. 16. 6.

*{speak wickedness}* I will not confess myself to be a wicked and deceitful man, as you charge me, for then I should speak untruly. This interpretation suits well with the words before and after. For Wickedness see on chap. 13. 7.

*{nor my tongue}* See on chap. 15. 5.

*{utter}* The word הגה signifies sometimes to meditate, as Psal. 1. 2. sometimes to speak, Psal. 37. 30. sometimes to sigh and mourn like doves, Isa. 59. 11. For men should meditate of what they intend to speak, and speak what they have thought on, and mournings come from sad thoughts. Here it is taken in the second sense for speaking and revealing the thoughts of our minds to others.

*{deceit}* See on chap. 13. 7.

**V. 5. ***{God forbid that I should justify you: till I die, I will not remove my integrity from me.}* You condemn me as a wicked man, because the hand of God is upon me. I cannot subscribe to this opinion of yours. I will never confess so long as I live, that God accounts me for a wicked man, notwithstanding these heavy things inflicted upon me by him.

*{God forbid}* Heb. *Let it be a common or profane thing unto me*. Such things might not be touched, 1 Sam. 24. 6.

*{that}* So אם is translated, 1 Sam. 24. 6. Others make it a new oath, If I do justify you, let God condemn me.

*{I should justify you}* In this case, or think you are in the right when you condemn me. For Job had nothing against their persons nor lives, but only against their carriage in this business, and their rash censuring of him. For the word see on chap. 4. 17. & 9. 20.

*{till I die}* Till I breathe out my last. So the word signifies, see on chap. 13. 19. Till, does not here intimate that Job would do it after death, but only affirm, that he would never do it, while he lived. See the like. 2 Sam. 6. 23. Isa. 22. 14.

*{I will not remove}* See on chap. 12. 20.

*{my integrity from me}* I will not confess myself guilty of those crimes of which you accuse me.

**V. 6. ***{My righteousness I hold fast, and will not let it goe: my heart shall not reproach me so long as I live.}* As in the last verse, he had showed it to be a most unjust act, if he should confess himself a wicked man contrary to his knowledge, so here he shows, that his conscience would perpetually check him for it.

*{my righteousness}* I will maintain that I have kept a good conscience toward God and man. Or, I know my cause is righteous, and that I am in the right, and you in the wrong. And to this I will stick.

*{I hold fast}* Heb. *I have held fast.* You see that I have stood close to it, and maintained it against you all, and put you to silence, so that none of you has a word to say against it. For the word see on chap. 18. 9.

*{and will not let it go}* As I have hitherto defended it against you, so I will against all others, that shall question it. Heb. *I will not let it cease.*

*{my heart}* See on chap. 17. 11.

*{shall not reproach me}* Shall not have occasion to cast it in my teeth to my disgrace, that I have betrayed my own innocency.

*{so long as I live}* Heb. *from my dayes.* My conscience shall clear me from a wicked life led by me in time past. Or rather, it shall have no cause, nor give way to confess me a wicked man, for time to come so long as I live.

**V. 7. ***{Let mine enemy be as the wicked, and he that riseth up against me, as the unrighteous.}* You conceive that I have spoken in the behalf of the wicked, as though they should always escape unpunished. But you are deceived, and I am so confident, that howsoever wicked men may thrive in the world, and die in peace and plenty, that if I were disposed to wish hurt and damage to my enemies, I would not desire any greater mischief should befall them, than befalls ungodly men after their prosperity. So much do I hate iniquity, though you please to reckon me among wicked men.

*{Let mine enemy be as the wicked.}* A popular manner of speaking, wherein, when men express an abomination of a thing, they wish it to their enemies, taking it for granted, that the power of malice is so great, that no man can express it in the wish of any particular evil. See the like phrase, 2 Sam. 18. 32. 1 Sam. 25. 26. Dan. 4. 19. There is no good so great, but men would wish it to their friends, nor no evil so great, but men would wish it to their enemies. For the word wicked, see on chap. 3. 17.

*{and he that riseth up against me}* The same thing doubled in other words to express his earnestness. A periphrasis describing an enemy by his hostile action. For the word see on chap. 14. 12.

*{as the unrighteous}* Let him feel those miseries that unrighteous men shall one day feel.

**V. 8. ***{For what is the hope of the hypocrite, though he hath gained, when God taketh away his soule?}* You conceive me to be a hypocrite. If it were so, though I might in health, and prosperity flatter myself, yet when God shall bring me to death, which I know not how soon it may be, I could not die with any comfort.

*{For}* Or, *Surely.* As it is translated, chap. 28. 1. For it gives no reason of the former verse, but affords an argument to prove Job to be no hypocrite.

*{what is}* He can have no hope at all, for he has nothing to ground it upon.

*{the hope}* How can he hope for any good in death, or what is the good he hopes for then? It may be in his height, he had some high thoughts, but his [heart] droops, when God warns him before his tribunal seat by death. Then he sees that all his hopes are vain. But I have hope in death, chap. 13. 14, 15, 16. For the word see on chap. 5. 16. & 11. 18. & 14. 7.

*{of the hypocrite}* See on chap. 8. 13.

*{though he hath gained}* Though he be never so rich, yet all will fail him in death. Or, *when he hath been covetous.* All that he has gotten by his covetous courses, he must lose, when he dies. Or, *that he should bring it about.* That he should be able to continue in his prosperity. He cannot be so mad as to think, that he can outlive death. For the word see on chap. 22. 3.

*{when God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{taketh away}* Heb. *makes to wander.* To wit, his soul out of his body by death, either natural or violent. All his gain will do him no good, when he dies. Matth. 16. 26. Luk. 12. 20.

*{his soule}* See on chap. 14. 22.

**V. 9. ***{Will God hear his cry when trouble cometh upon him?}* In this verse and the next Job proves, that a hypocrite though never so rich can have no sure hope in God in trouble or death. And that by three effects of hope. 1. It makes man confident, that God will hear his prayers in this verse. 2. It affords him delight in God, when he wants it in the world. 3. It makes him with boldness in his lowest estate constantly to seek to God for help, verse 10. This a hypocrite cannot do.

*{will God}* Surely he cannot think that God will hear him. For the word God, see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{hear}* So hear it, that he grant, what he asks, for otherwise there is no good to us in hearing the words of our prayers, for God hears them speak, to whom he denies their requests. For the word see on chap. 13. 17.

*{his cry}* His prayer. Yet it argues earnestness in prayer withal, for men cry aloud when they are in great distress and need sudden help. God will not hear the earnest prayer of the hypocrite in his misery: but he will hear mine. chap. 12. 4. Therefore I am no hypocrite, whose prayers God will not hear. Psal. 18. 41. & 109. 7. Prov. 1. 28. Isa. 1. 15. Jer. 14. 12. Ezek. 8. 18. Mic. 3. 4. Joh. 9. 31.

*{when trouble}* Not want, but anguish. Things that straiten and vex him. This is more than want of good things; see on chap. 5. 19.

*{cometh upon him}* See on chap. 15. 21. And for living actions attributed to dead things, see on chap. 1. 19.

**V. 10. ***{Will he delight himself in the Almighty? will he always call upon God?}* A godly man can find matter of delight in God in time of adversity, and call upon him for delivery with some confidence, having had experience of his favor before: but a wicked man, or a hypocrite cannot.

*{will he delight himself}* Will he or can he find comfort in God, when the world affords him none? See on chap. 22. 26. Others interpret it of God's hearing his prayers, and granting his requests, and so giving him matter of delight. Which agrees well with the words before and after.

*{in the Almighty}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{will he always}* Heb. *in every time.* It may be he may formally call on God in time of prosperity; but can he seriously do it in time of adversity?

*{call upon}* Pray to God. See on chap. 13. 22.

*{God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

**V. 11. ***{I will teach you by the hand of God: that which is with the Almighty will I not conceal.}* Some interpreters knit these words to the former, and read them thus, *I teach you being under the hand of the Almighty; and what I am toward the Almighty, I do not conceal from you.* As if Job had said, I have showed, that a hypocrite dare not seek to God for help in trouble. I dare and do do it in this great affliction, therefore I am no hypocrite, and your sentence against me is unjust, verse 12. But as the words are read, they are a preface to persuade them to attend to what he had to say in the residue of this chapter, and in the chapter following, wherein he shows, that though he be no wicked man notwithstanding his afflictions, yet God does often cut off wicked men by temporal judgments, although not always, and that they shall perish in the end. And that God's various proceedings are above the reach of human understanding.

*{I will teach you}* See on chap. 6. 24. & 8. 10.

*{by the hand of God}* By God's help. Or rather, *of the hand of God.* Concerning God's actions wherein you are very ignorant, for you measure them by a wrong rule. You conceive, that whom he prospers he loves, and whom he grievously afflicts he hates. But you are deceived, for he prospers or afflicts good men or bad, as he please himself. For he has men's prosperous estates, or cross in his own hand to dispose of, as he pleases. So ב is translated of Exod. 12. 43, 44, 45. Psal. 87. 3. For the word Hand, see on chap. 15. 23. For God see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{that which is with the Almighty}* What course he uses to take. How he prospers or afflicts, as he please himself, and is not tied to any constant law or rule in his external proceedings. For the Almighty see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{I will not conceal}* The same thing repeated in other words for certainty; as chap. 14. 10. 11. yet not without some emphasis, I will not hide, but by all means make it known. A meiosis, whereby more is intended, than is spoken. See the like on chap. 17. 4. upon, Not exalt. And on chap. 15. 18. where see for the word also upon the word *Hid*.

**V. 12. ***{Behold all ye yourselves have seen it, why then are ye thus altogether vain?}* Whereas I said that I would teach you God's proceedings, I doubt not but you know them well, or at least should do, seeing you brag so much of your observations. But I will call those things to your remembrance, which it may be you have forgotten that you have seen God afflict good men as well as bad. And therefore your conclusion is most vain, that God's love may be known by prosperity, or his hatred by adversity, Eccles. 9. 1. yet I deny not, but that God does many times lay heavy judgments upon the ungodly, as I will now show, and make plain. Neither did I deny it before: but showed it not to be God's perpetual way of proceeding.

*{Behold}* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{all ye yourselves}* See on chap. 13. 4.

*{have seen it}* You have seen examples of God's various proceedings in the world.

*{why then}* Heb. *and why is this?* To wit, that you go against your own observation.

*{are ye thus altogether vain}* Heb. *are ye vain in vanity?* Why do you make such most vain conclusions, that have nothing to uphold them?

**V. 13. ***{This is the portion of a wicked man with God, and the heritage of oppressors, which they shall receive of the Almighty.}* Some take these words to be a repetition of what his friends had said before; but they are rather a concession of the truth thereof so far, that God does sometimes destroy ungodly men by outward judgments, and ruin their posterity after them. Yet so as that this could make nothing against Job, who could depend upon God in these miseries, which hypocrites could not do, verse 6, 8. The words are almost the same with those of Zophar, chap. 20. 29. See the exposition of them there. Only a word or two of those words, which differ in the original.

*{with God}* Which God lays up by him to inflict upon ungodly men in time, as parents lay up portions for children. For God, see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{of oppressors}* See on chap. 15. 20.

*{which they shall receive}* See on chap. 15. 12. upon, Carry thee away.

*{of the Almighty}* See on chap. 8. 5.

**V. 14. ***{If his children be multiplied, it is for the sword: and his off-spring shall not be satisfied with bread.}* He shows 1. that if the wicked rich man does escape God's judgments, while he lives, yet his posterity meets with them, when he is gone, if not before, verse 15, 16, 17, 18. 2. that he often meets with them in his lifetime, and especially at his death, from thence to the end of the chapter. The judgments of God upon his children are 1. the sword and famine, in this verse, 2. hatred of men, so that the world shall be glad to be rid of them, verse 15. 3. loss of their parents' wealth, verse 16, 17. loss of their parents' habitation, verse 18.

*{if his children}* The children of the rich wicked man, and oppressor, verse 13. For the word, see on chap. 14. 21. upon the word, *Sons*.

*{be multiplied}* If he have many children; as wicked rich men often have.

*{it is for the sword}* God gives them over to be slain by the enemy in battles, or by some murderer, Hos. 9. 13. For the word, see on chap. 15. 22. & 19. 20.

*{and his off spring}* See on chap. 5. 25. & 21. 8.

*{shall not be satisfied}* That is, shall be starved for want of bread. If the sword kill them not, the famine will. Meiosis, more meant than spoken, for men may not be satisfied with bread, and yet live. But death by famine is here intended. See the like manner of speaking on chap. 14. 10, 11. & 15. 18. For the word, see on chap. 7. 4. & 10. 15. upon the word, *Full*.

*{with bread}* With food. They shall not have enough to keep them alive. If men have bread enough, they can live, though they want other food. But if they cannot get that, they quickly perish. For the word, see on chap. 15. 23.

**V. 15. ***{Those that remain of him shall be buried in death: and his widows shall not weep.}* If it happen that any of his remain having escaped the sword and famine, so that they die by no violent death, but are brought to the grave like other men, yet not one of their widows shall bewail her husband, but shall rather be glad, that she is thus rid of him, and delivered from him.

*{those that remain of him}* Those of his children or nephews, which escape the two former judgments mentioned, verse 14. For the word, see on chap. 20. 21.

*{shall be buried in death}* That is, they shall be presently and privately buried, without any funeral pomp, such as usually is conferred upon rich men, when they die. This best agrees with what follows in the verse. They had carried themselves so ill, that their widows would bestow neither cost nor tears at their funerals. Some understand it of the plague, which is a most deadly disease. But the former interpretation is better.

*{and his widows}* The widows of every one of them. Others conceive, that each of them had many wives, as rich men then used to have, but none of them would weep for their husbands' death. For the word, see on chap. 22. 9.

*{shall not weep}* At their funerals, or for their death, Psal. 78. 64. Either they were not left to mourn for them, or rather, their husbands were so unkind to them, and hateful to the public, that they thought them not worthy of lamentation, but rather were glad they were rid of them. Meiosis; as verse 14. on the word *Satisfied*. If they that are nearest to them, be glad they are gone out of the world, much more others. This is a judgment, to die without being lamented, Jer. 22. 18. Eccles. 12. 5.

**V. 16. ***{Though he heap up silver, as the dust, and prepare raiment as the clay.}* He sets out in this verse and the next the fruitless labor of the ungodly man in heaping up abundance of riches by oppressing others to enrich his posterity, which yet shall never enjoy it. But God will give it to better men.

*{Though}* See on chap. 8. 4. & 11. 14.

*{he heap up}* He get great quantity of it.

*{silver}* See on chap. 22. 25.

*{as the dust}* A proverbial speech to set out abundance, as there is store of dust in a heap. For the word, see on chap. 14. 8. upon the word, *Ground*.

*{and prepare}* Provide it by his labor and toil, or unjust dealing.

*{raiment}* Choice of apparel, whereby rich men express their pride.

*{as the clay}* In abundance, as there is store of clay. Or, though he have so much, that he make no more account of it, than of the dirt under his feet. For the word, see on chap. 15. 12.

**V. 17. ***{He may prepare it, but the just shall put it on, and the innocent shall divide the silver.}* I grant indeed he may get and provide these things, but neither he nor any of his shall enjoy them, but some other man of a more honest and upright conversation. His garments shall come into the just man's hands, who shall also have a great portion of his treasure, Prov. 28. 8. Eccles. 2. 26.

*{He may prepare it}* See on verse 16. Broughton reads it elegantly, *well may he prepare it.* God will suffer him to toil to get it, but not to enjoy it.

*{but the just}* The good man. He that is not ungodly. See on chap. 17. 9.

*{shall put it on}* Shall wear it, and reap the benefit and comfort of it. Meiosis, See on chap. 14. 10, 11. and 15. 18.

*{and the innocent}* See on chap. 17. 8.

*{shall divide}* Shall have his portion of his treasure, as if he were his child, though he be nothing at all of kin to him. See on chap. 21. 17. upon the word *Distributeth*. And on chap. 20. 29. on the word, *Portion*.

*{the silver}* See on chap. 22. 25.

**V. 18. ***{He buildeth his house as a moth, and as a booth, that the keeper maketh.}* As he shall lose his treasure, and apparel, so likewise his house shall suddenly come to utter ruin.

*{He shall build his house as a moth}* As a moth prepares himself a lodging in some stately garment thinking to abide there forever, but either eats himself out of house and home, or is brushed out of it: so the oppressor builds him a stately palace, thinking to live in it all his days, and leave it to his posterity after him, but either he works himself out of it, or is put out of it by others: Deodate reads it thus, *He shall build, but his house shall be as a moth.* Not having relation to the moth's building her house, but to the sudden destruction of the moth which is quickly crushed with a finger, Psal. 39. 11. The same thing is set out by the similitude of a spider's web, Job 8. 14. and by many other similitudes, Psal. 58. 7, 8, 9. Others read it thus, *He shall build his house as Arcturus.* So this word is translated, chapter 9. 9. And their meaning is, that he should build him a stately palace, as if he were in heaven among the stars, but it shall quickly fall. For the word, Build, chap. 20. 19. For House, see on chap. 15. 28.

*{and as a booth which the keeper maketh}* He sets out the sudden and irrecoverable ruin or loss of the oppressor's house by another similitude taken from a booth made by a watchman in the orchard or field, of a slight building to last for a while till the fruit which he watches be ripe, and then it drops down, when winter comes. Isa. 1. 8. Lam. 2. 6. For the word, Keeper, see on chap. 7. 20. upon the word, *Preserver*. For the word, Maketh, see on chap. 14. 6. upon the word, *Bring forth*.

**V. 19. ***{The rich man shall lie down, but he shall not be gathered: he openeth his eyes, and he is not.}* The translators seem to understand it, of lying down in death, and their meaning to be, that when the rich man dies he shall not be buried; and that he dies suddenly in the opening of an eye, like the twinkling of an eye, 1 Cor. 15. 52. Others that interpret it of death, read the latter part of the verse thus, *when a man opens his eyes, he shall not be,* That is, when another man looks to find him alive, he shall find him dead and gone. As chap. 7. 8, 10. and 20. 9. Psal. 37. 10, 35. Others that favor this interpretation read the whole verse thus, *The rich man shall lie down, and it shall not be gathered, he openeth his eyes, and it is not.* That is, when he dies, his wealth spoken of verse 16. shall not be gathered to himself, he cannot carry it away with him, and if he could look for any comfort in it, he would be deceived. See the like spoken of a dead man, chap. 14. 21, 22. Or, *it shall not be gathered to his children*; if he could open his eyes, he would see them poor enough for all his riches. Others conceive these words to be meant of falling into poverty, altering only the end of the verse thus, *and there shall be none.* And thus they interpret it, He shall be so poor, that he shall lie in the streets, and no man shall pity him so as to receive him into his house: but all men shall abhor him for his former cruelty. Yea he shall look round about him in this extremity for help, but shall find none to relieve him. Others interpret the words of his lying down to sleep, translating them thus, *When the rich man lieth down, it is not taken away, when he openeth his eyes, it is not.* That is to say, when he goes to bed he has all his wealth about him, but when he wakes it is all gone. Like that, Ruth 1. 21. His goods would unexpectedly be taken away by fire or thieves, or like evils before the morning.

*{The rich man shall lie down}* For the word Lying down, see on chap. 14. 12.

*{but he shall not be gathered}* To his fathers: He shall not have the honor of burial, 2 King. 22. 20. Jer. 8. 2. Or, *it shall not be taken away.* That is, his wealth. So this word is translated, Gen. 30. 23. The reason of this double signification is, because things gathered to some are taken away from others.

*{he openeth his eyes}* If he could open his eyes after death; Or, lying in the streets he looks about him for help. Or, when he wakes from his sleep in the morning. For the word, Eyes, see on chap. 15. 15. upon the word, *Sight*.

*{and he is not}* He is not alive. Dead men, who converse not with others are accounted as having no being. Or, *it is not.* That is, his wealth. All is gone within the compass of one short night.

**V. 20. ***{Terrors take hold on him as waters, a tempest stealeth him away in the night.}* Those troubles which now follow, some conceive to befall the wicked man the father of the children mentioned, verse 14. in his life. Others, at his death. They are set out by similitudes taken from a deluge of waters, or storm of wind, verse 20, 21. Which come suddenly, prevail irresistibly, overturn all in their way. 2. He shows the vanity of all ways of escape plotted by this rich oppressor, verse 22. 3. His infamous departure, verse 23.

*{terrors}* Fears of want, or of a violent death, as chap. 18. 11, 14. & 15. 21.

*{take hold on him}* It is singular to note out, that everything would terrify him, or affright him. They take such hold on him, that they will not let him go, but accompany him to his grave.

*{as waters}* Abundantly, so that they overflow him, as waters falling from a high hill, or rainwaters causing a deluge, and drowning towns. Sore afflictions are compared to waters, chap. 22. 11. Psal. 32. 6. But here abundance of terrors is signified. As abundance of other things elsewhere, Isa. 11. 9. Psal. 88. 17. Am. 5. 24.

*{a tempest}* A sudden great affliction. See on chap. 21. 18. upon the word, *Storm*.

*{stealeth him away}* Carries him away by violence, and unexpectedly, as thieves do stolen goods, chap. 1. 17. & 21. 18. Where see also for the word.

*{in the night}* When other men are asleep, and lie quietly in their beds, and when he cannot see any way of escaping, as he might in the day. God sends many heavy judgments on his enemies in the dark night, that they may be the more terrible and unavoidable. As Exod. 12. 29. 2 King. 19. 35. Isa. 15. 1. Dan. 5. 30.

**V. 21. ***{The east wind carryeth him away, and he departeth: and as a storm, hurleth him out of his place}* The former verse may fitly be a similitude taken from a storm at sea, which makes the water rage: this from a whirlwind at land, which carries all away before it.

*{the east wind}* Some violent affliction like the east wind, which blows furiously, Ezek. 27. 26. Jon. 4. 8. Hos. 13. 15. For the word, see on chap. 15. 2.

*{carryeth him away}* Into poverty, or captivity, or death, Hos. 1. 6. For the word see on chap. 21. 3. upon the word, *Suffer*.

*{and he departeth}* Out of his present condition of health, wealth, or life. See on chap. 14. 20. upon the word, *Passeth*.

*{and as a storm hurleth him}* Takes him away like a whirlwind suddenly in spite of his teeth.

*{out of his place}* Out of his habitation, where he lived plentifully before, chap. 8. 18. For the word see on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 9.

**V. 22. ***{For God shall cast upon him, and not spare: he would fain flee out of his hand.}* The sum of the verse is, that when wicked men, that have oppressed others, find God laying heavy load upon them, they plot all ways to escape his judgments, but in vain.

*{For}* So it is translated, chap. 18. 8. Or, *When.* As it is translated, Zech. 12. 2.

*{God}* This is added to supply the sense, and therefore written in smaller letters to show from whom these afflictions come. Others understand it of the storm or east wind spoken of before, which will bring many miseries upon him.

*{shall cast upon him}* He shall send afflictions upon him before mentioned, or such like, as it were darts from heaven, which no man could do, chap. 20. 23.

*{and not spare}* He in his cruel oppressions spared none, and now God will not spare him, but send one storm of affliction after another, till he be destroyed, as he oppressed others, till he ruined them. Like for like. See on chap. 16. 13.

*{hee would fain flee}* He has a great desire and uses all means to escape the storm, but cannot. Or, *he flees apace.* Heb. *in fleeing he flees.* He makes haste to escape God's hand, as men do that run apace from one place to another to escape an enemy, and stay in none, but are still pursued, till they be taken and slain. He made others flee for fear of him before (see on chap. 24. 4.) now God makes him flee. For the word, see on Job 14. 2.

*{out of his hand}* Or *because of his hand.* He would escape the plague, that God's hand inflicts upon him, if he could, but he is still pursued by God's hand, till he perish. So the word is used, Exod. 9. 3. & 24. 11. For the word see on chap. 15. 23: others read it, *he flees clean away from his place.* To escape God's judgments, if he might. He is driven out of house and home. So יד is translated, Deut. 23. 12. Isa. 56. 5.

**V. 23. ***{Men shall clap their hand at him, and shall hiss him out of his place.}* And this is yet a great deal the more miserable, that he being thus cast down from his former happy and flourishing estate, all men shall be so far from taking pity of him, that on the contrary part they shall laugh and rejoice at his downfall, hissing and clapping their hands at him.

*{Men shall}* Every one that sees his ruin shall do it, Heb. *He shall clap.* That is, hands shall be clapped at him, to wit, by all that see his downfall. Or, those men that clap their hands shall clap them at him. See for both these ways on chap. 3. 3. upon the word, It was said. This argues great infamy and disgrace. For no men are thus used by all sorts of people, but such as are odious to all (as wolves to the flock) and thought unfit to converse with any man. Others read it, *God shall, &c.* That is, God shall show himself glad of their ruin, as men do by clapping of hands. But the former being more literal, and arguing a public disgrace, is better. Others read, *It shall.* That is, the storm or east wind spoken of before shall prosecute him, till it have driven him clean out of his place.

*{clap their hands}* For joy that they are delivered from his oppressions, which they testify by that outward gesture, Lam. 2. 15. Ezek. 25. 6.

*{at him}* At the ungodly oppressor spoken of before. They shall be glad of his destruction.

*{and shall hiss him}* In sign of detestation of him for his former cruelties, 1 King. 9. 8. Lam. 2. 15. Zeph. 2. 15. Jer. 25. 9. Mic. 6. 16.

*{out of his place}* Out of his town, country, or city, where he dwells. Or out of the world. They shall never leave hissing at him, till they be quite rid of him. Others understand it of the hissers. They shall every man out of his place of several habitation, where he dwells, hiss at him. The former sense argues a deadly, this a general detestation. The former agrees best with verse 20. &c. For the word, see on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 9.

## CHAPTER 28

**Verse 1. ***{Surely, there is a vein for the silver, and a place for gold, where they fine it.}* Job having in the former chapter showed, that God does sometimes in this world punish ungodly men to show his justice, and terrify others, now lest his friends should object, why does he not do so always, or why does he afflict godly men as much, to make the world believe, that they are as bad? he sets out God's wonderful wisdom, which carries things far above the reach of the wisest men. Herein, 1. he demonstrates, what admirable things God had given men wisdom to find out, to verse 12. 2. How that part of wisdom, whereby a reason of God's various dealing with men of one sort, either good or bad, in prospering or afflicting them should be given, is too high for any creature to conceive or reveal, to verse 28. 3. that man therefore must not censure others to be good or bad according to their prosperity or afflictions, but rather labor to keep himself close to the rule of God's word, and to be content with what is there revealed to him, and to judge of others according to their lives not according to their outward estates. verse 28. For the first he lets pass worldly things abroad, which may be thought to argue no great wisdom, because they are obvious to every eye, and instances in corn and minerals, which are formed under the earth, and brought forth by that wisdom God has given to man. And 1. he sets out the principal metals to be digged out of the earth, verse 1, 2. which are silver, and gold, verse 1. iron and brass, verse 2. And 2. he shows the hardness of the work, verse 3. &c. to verse 12.

*{Surely}* The word commonly signifies, For. But here being no reason given of what was spoken before, but a new matter handled, it is well translated, *Surely*. So it is translated chap. 8. 6.

*{there is a vein}* Or, *a mine.* Heb. *a comming forth.* That is, either a place where it puts forth itself into veins, or a way to bring it out of the earth. Man's wit can find a way to bring hidden things out of the earth, but not to know a reason of God's judgments. He instances in this mining business rather than in astronomy, or navigation, because a man's eyes may guide him in these latter, but not in the former. He can see the stars at first to guide him in those, but may dig long ere he see a gold or silver mine. Also it is harder to deal with earth, and rocks to dig up metals, than to look on stars, or sail in seas.

*{for the silver}* See on chap. 22. 25.

*{and a place}* See on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 9.

*{for the gold}* Which is of greater price than silver.

*{where they fine it}* This translation seems to look to the gold finer's shop. But all Job's discourse speaks of things underground. Others read it, *from whence they fine it.* That is, from whence they take it to refine. One verb included in another. See on chap. 15. 15. & 16. 11. & 18. 8. But it may best be read, *which they fine.* Intimating that gold and silver, which now glister so much, did not so at first, but were buried low in the earth, and there lay buried in rubbish, till by the wisdom given to man by God they were taken out from thence and refined.

**V. 2. ***{Iron is taken out of the earth, and brass is molten out of the stone}* Having spoken before of the two nobler metals silver and gold, now he speaks of the production of the two meaner, iron and brass.

*{Iron}* See on chap. 19. 24.

*{is taken}* See on chap. 15. 12. upon the word, *Carry away*.

*{out of the earth}* Heb. *out of the dust.* It is by miners digged out of the earth, as other metals are. See on chap. 14. 8. upon the word, *Ground*.

*{and brass}* A metal finer than iron, yet not so fine as silver.

*{is molten}* The dross in it must be got out by fire. Or, *which is molten.* It is taken out of the earth, and then refined by fire; as was said of gold, verse 1.

*{out of the stone}* That is, out of the ore, which is like a stone. For the word see on chap. 14. 19. It may be read, *and the stone which is melted into brasse.*

**V. 3. ***{He setteth an end to darkness, and searcheth out all perfection: the stones of darkness, and the shadow of death.}* The difficulty of the work requiring much art and labor, to bring forth these metals out of the earth, is set out in this verse, and many verses following. 1. The workmen must strive with darkness, in this verse. 2. With water, verse 4. 3. With fire, verse 5. 4. With stones and dust, verse 6. 5. With strange and uncouth ways in the earth, verse 7, 8. 6. With rocks and mountains, verse 9. 7. The fruit of his labor is set out, verse 10, 11. In this verse is set out the art of man in digging holes in the earth, and bringing light into them to discern the metals for which he digs.

*{He setteth an end to darkness}* The earth being dark in the bowels of it, when the miners have digged deep, they bring lights down into it to find out those metals, that else would lie hid there forever. Others understand it not of the lights carried down by the workman, but of the metals brought forth to light. They lay in darkness before: but now there is an end of that darkness, and the workman brings them forth into the light to be seen by men, who else should never have beheld them. Some understand this He to be God: but it appears by the opposition, verse 12. that it is meant of the miner, who does all these things, as shall be showed in the notes. And it sets out God's glory the more, that the most curious artist, that can do such great matters, yet is not able to give an account of God's judgments. For the word End, see on chap. 16. 3. For the word Darkness, on chap. 15. 23.

*{and searcheth out all perfection}* He never leaves digging and searching till he have found out the most excellent and perfect metals.

*{the stones of darkness, and the shadow of death}* Or, *and of the shadow of death.* That is, he rests not till he find out not only the metals spoken of before, but also precious stones, which lie low in the earth hid in darkness, where is nothing but a deadly shade. Places under the earth, are as it were consecrated to darkness, left to devour dead men's carcasses, and are pictures of hell the kingdom of darkness. The darkness there is so terrible, that it may fright men to death, and to some workmen the dark damps under the earth have proved deadly. For the word Stones see on chap. 14. 19. For the Shadow of death, on chap. 3. 5. All intimates the hardness of coming by them overcome by man's art, and industry.

**V. 4. ***{The flood breaketh out from the inhabitant; even the waters forgotten of the foot: they are dried up, they are gone away from men}* When men are digging for metals, waters arise out of springs below in the ground, where there was no suspicion of them, as of the swelling of rivers on earth. Yet they come in so fast as the workmen are forced to leave the place, till they have got the water out again. These waters drown the mines, and hinder the work. Yet such art has God given to man, and courage withal, that they leave not off the work, but empty the waters by buckets, pumps, wheels, and such like devices, and then go on afresh, till they have finished their work.

*{The flood}* See on chap. 20. 17.

*{breaketh out}* The word signifies a violent breaking forth of the water ready to drown all the workmen. See on chap. 16. 14.

*{from the inhabitant}* Some understand it of the channel where it ran before, but the water rather dwelt in it, than the channel in the water. Also Job speaks of water underground, where no channel appears. Broughton reads it, *from the spring.* Perhaps he brings גר from נגר losing נ. From the place whence it flows. Others read it, *so that there is no abider.* That is, there is no workman that dare abide there to go on with his work, till the water be drawn out. Thus this particle is used, verse 11. Jer. 48. 2. Ezek. 16. 41. And this seems to be the best sense, and to agree best with the scope of the place.

*{even the waters}* These words are added, because the adjective following is plural. And so much is understood in the word Flood, which consists of many waters.

*{forgotten of the foot}* Never trod in by any man, as being underground, and as far remote from the knowledge of men, as things clean forgotten by them. Broughton reads it, *unkenned of any foot.*

*{they are dried up}* Heb. *they are drawn up, or diminished.* To wit, by such means, as the workmen use for that purpose.

*{they are gone away}* Or, *they are removed.* By the art, and labor of the workmen.

*{from men}* From those workmen. The pits are cleared, so that they may go to work again. Or, *by men.* That is, by the workmen's pains. So מן is translated, Jer. 10. 14. For the word Man see on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

**V. 5. ***{As for the earth, out of it cometh bread: and under it is turned up, as it were fire.}* Sometime it comes to pass, that the upper part of the ground is fruitful, and brings forth corn to feed men, yet underneath, where the workmen look not for it, may be brimstone or coal mines, which may easily of its own accord, or by the strokes of their iron instruments, or falling of their lights take fire, and hinder the miners' work. Which yet they can quench or empty, and go on with their work. Broughton reads it thus. *A ground out of which groweth food, is underneath changed as fire.* It may be understood barely of digging up brimstone or coals out of the earth, though not fired, nor hindering the workmen.

*{As for the earth}* See on chap. 9. 24.

*{out of it cometh bread}* Corn of which men make bread comes out of the earth. Bread-corn as we call it; and as it is translated, Isa. 28. 28. For coming out see on chap. 20. 25. For Bread on chap. 15. 23.

*{and under it is turned up}* Brimstone or other fiery matter brought to light by the miners. Or, *and under it is changed.* There is other matter underneath, far different from corn. So the word is used, chap. 20. 14. Or, it takes fire underneath, as was hinted before.

*{as it were fire}* Some understand it of gold, or precious stones like fire in color. Others of the lamps of the workmen, which makes the earth seem to be on fire. Others of brimstone or coals, which have fire in them. Others of the firing of the mines, which sometimes happens. For the word see on chap. 18. 5.

**V. 6. ***{The stones of it are the place of Sapphires: and it hath dust of gold.}* Some conceive that as water and fire were set out before as impediments of the miners' labor, so stones and dust, here. If he will get sapphires, he must cut through stones for it, and if he will have gold, he must dig up great store of rubbish, which may endanger to choke him. Others conceive that the goodness of the ground is commended, which instead of common stones brings forth sapphires, and other precious stones, and gold instead of dust, and that in abundance, chap. 22. 24.

*{The stones of it}* See on chap. 14. 19.

*{are the place}* See on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 29.

*{of sapphires}* Of precious stones. The sapphire an excellent stone is put for the rest. This stone is mentioned, verse 16. Also Exod. 24. 10. & 28. 18. Cant. 5. 14. see on those places.

*{and it hath dust of Gold}* Or, *oare of gold.* See for Dust, on chap. 14. 8. upon the word *Ground*.

**V. 7. ***{There is a path which no fowl knoweth, and which the vulture's eye hath not seen.}* The difficulty of this mining work is here set out by the inaccessibleness of the place to those birds of prey, that fly high to seek food on the tops of rocks and mountains, where men use not to come: and that in this verse. And of wild beasts which range through deserts and woods for prey, where men dwell not, verse 8. Yet art leads men to these underground places to find gold, where no quick-sighted bird nor beast can come.

*{There is a path}* The way that leads down to the gold mines. This shows the uncertainty of the way, hardness to effect the work, and labor of men to overcome both. For the word see on chap. 18. 10.

*{which no fowl}* The word properly signifies a ravening fowl, which searches all places for food, even the most inaccessible, and which God has furnished with most piercing eyes to spy out their prey afar off: yet can they not see metals underground.

*{and which the vulture's eye hath not seen}* The vulture's eye is used proverbially for a sharp and quick sight. Whether the word signify a vulture or no, is doubtful, and therefore some render it *a kite*, others a pie. It is thought the Jews themselves now understand not aright those ancient names of fowls. However, the sense is, that this path to the mines is not seen by the most quick-sighted fowls. For the word Eye, see on chap. 15. 15. upon the word *Sight*.

**V. 8. ***{The lions whelps have not trodden it; nor the fierce lion passed by it.}* As in the former verse he had showed, that no birds of prey; so here, that no beasts of prey can find out these hidden metals.

*{the lions whelps}* Heb. *The sons of the lion.* For Sons see on chap. 14. 21. Or, *The sons or children of pride*, as it is translated, chap. 41. 34. But it seems by what follows to be meant here of proud beasts, such such as young lions are.

*{have not trodden it}* Have not found out that way. They use not to travel underground where these metals are, although they travel in many desert and uncouth places.

*{nor the fierce lion}* See on chap. 4. 10.

*{passed by it}* He may go over it, but not come near it, so as to take notice of it.

**V. 9. ***{He putteth forth his hand upon the rock, he overturneth the mountains by the roots.}* In these unknown ways as before our miner met with water and fire to hinder his work, so here the hard rocks offer themselves as bulwarks to defend the gold and silver mines. A man would think that this should discourage him. Also hills cover them, but he sets upon rocks, and by indefatigable pains digs to the bottom of hills, and fetches out the ore from thence.

*{he putteth forth}* He puts his hand forth to smite on the rocks, and to dig through them. For the word see on chap. 14. 20.

*{his hand}* See on chap. 15. 23.

*{upon the rock}* Or, *flint.* The word signifies the hardest kind of rocks. Nothing can discourage him. He can find out a way to work on hardest rocks.

*{he overturneth the mountains by the roots}* Or rather, *he turneth it up at the roots of the mountains.* That is, he digs to the bottom of the mountains, if the vein go so deep, and fetches out gold and silver from thence. So the same word is translated turning up, verse 5. By the root of the mountains here is meant the bottom of them, which lies low within the earth, as the root of a tree does.

**V. 10. ***{He cutteth out rivers among the rocks, and his eye seeth every precious thing.}* In this verse and the next he sets out the profit, which our miner gets by his great art, and labor. As he works in the rocks, streams unlooked for break out, which he cunningly turns another way, and so gets the precious stones that are under them.

*{he cutteth out rivers}* He cuts the rocks, not the rivers. But by cutting the rocks he makes way for store of water to pass like rivers. So we say, to grind meal, that is, to grind corn, and turn it into meal. See the like on chap. 22. 6. On stripping the naked. Some understand it of bringing water from rivers through rocks to move his engines, or wash his metals: but it is best to understand it of waters breaking out of those rocks, in which he digs for gold, verse 9.

*{among the rocks}* Where men would least of all look for waters.

*{and his eye}* See on chap. 15. 15. on the word, *Sight*.

*{seeth}* He sees, and obtains those precious things, which no man saw before. For the word, see on chap. 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold*.

*{every precious thing}* All rare minerals and metals of great price, and the choicest stones, whereof are made the finest jewels. Prov. 1. 13.

**V. 11. ***{He bindeth the floods from overflowing, and the thing that is hid, bringeth he to light.}* As in the former verse, he turns away some streams, that would have hindered his work, so here he hinders others from coming into his mines, and so he gets the precious things hidden in the earth.

*{He bindeth}* Water cannot be bound, but the meaning is that he restrains it from coming into the mines, as a man bound cannot go whither he would.

*{the floods}* The waters under the ground, that would hinder him in his work, and deprive him of the fruit of it.

*{from overflowing}* So that they may not overflow his pits. He keeps them back by dams, and other artificial devices. So מן is used to express a negation, verse 4. Heb. *from weeping.* For in those underground waters, the spring sends them out by drops for want of a free channel, they soak out of the earth, as tears drop from the eye, or water from a still.

*{and}* Having thus kept his mine clear he gets such precious things, as lie hid there.

*{the thing that is hid}* The metals, which before lay as it were buried in the earth, and concealed from the sight of men.

*{bringeth he to light}* He brings it forth to be seen by the light of the sun. For the word, Bringeth, see on chap. 14. 2. and 20. 25. upon the word, *Cometh forth, or out*. For the word, Light, see on chap. 24. 13, 16.

**V. 12. ***{But where shall wisdom be found? and where is the place of understanding?}* In the former verse he had showed how far man's wisdom could reach, namely so far, that it could fetch out gold, silver, precious stones, out of the bowels of the earth, and make them as useful to man, as if the earth had brought them forth like corn. In this verse he puts a period to human wisdom, and applies all to his present purpose, showing that man's wit, that is so pregnant in other things, is not able to find a reason of God's proceedings in his various dispensations, prospering some good and bad, and crossing others of both sorts. And therefore his friends had no reason to condemn him for a wicked man because of his afflictions, unless they would take upon them to be gods; for to know God's intent in his various dealings with men of the same condition, is too great a work for the wisest man in the world.

*{But}* So this particle is translated, chap. 26. 1. As if he had said, many very hidden things had man's wit found out, so that one would think nothing were too hard for him, yet he cannot find out the true reason of all God's proceedings, 1. because there is no place, in which man may find it, to verse 15. 2. he has no price to buy it, to verse 20. 3. no power in any creature to attain it, to verse 23. 4. God only knows it, to verse 28.

*{where}* Heb. *from whence.* Where is that vein out of which you may dig this wisdom? it is farther off, and harder to find, than minerals in the bottom of hills, or pearls in the bottom of the sea.

*{shall wisdom be found}* Some read it, *that wisedom.* That is, wisdom to discern the reason of God's proceedings with the sons of men. Others, *wisedom it self.* For all man's wisdom in searching the most secret corners of the earth is but folly in comparison of God's wise proceedings in his dispensations towards men. That wit which can reach the former, falls far short of attaining the grounds of the latter. For the word, Found, see on chap. 17. 10.

*{and where is the place}* See on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 21.

*{of understanding}* Where this understanding of the grounds of God's proceedings may be found.

**V. 13. ***{Man knoweth not the price thereof, neither is it found in the land of the living.}* It is a vain thing for man to think he has attained to it, for God only has set it in order, and no man on earth can reach it.

*{Man}* See on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{knoweth not}* Among all his rare inventions he cannot find out this.

*{the price thereof}* It is invaluable. Mercer, by the price understands the place of it, for those things which we know not whence they come, we can set no price upon, because we know not the use, excellency, or value of them. The word is translated, Order Exod. 40. 4, 23. And so it may here. God has so disposed it, that no man knows the order of his proceedings. And this agrees well with the verse before, and after, which speak of the hardness of finding it out. But the price and value of it is distinctly handled, verse 15, &c.

*{neither is it found}* See on chap. 17. 10.

*{in the land}* See on chap. 9. 24. on the word, *Earth*.

*{of the living}* Of such as live here on earth, much less among those that lie dead in the grave. By the living is meant men living in the world, for no man would look for it among the beasts. So Eve is called the mother of all living, Gen. 3. 20. yet was she not the mother of beasts. Thus the land of the living is taken, Psal. 27. 13. & 116. 9. & 142. 5. Isa. 38. 11. & 53. 8. Jer. 11. 19. The grave is the the land of the dead, or of forgetfulness, Psal. 88. 11. 12.

**V. 14. ***{The depth saith, It is not in me: and the sea saith, It is not with me.}* As he had showed before, that this wisdom is not to be found among men upon earth, so now he intimates unto us, that if a man could safely travel into the depths of sea or land, and converse with them or the creatures in them, if they could speak they would tell him, that his labor were lost, if he sought there to understand the cause and manner of God's administrations.

*{The depth}* The word signifies any deep gulf of earth or water, where men cannot find the bottom, Gen. 1. 2.

*{saith}* If it could speak it would say so. It is as sure, as if it did tell the searcher, that he sought in vain. For living actions attributed to lifeless creatures, see on chap. 1. 19. For the word, see on chap. 16. 1.

*{it is not in me}* I cannot resolve you of the reasons of God's doing.

*{and the sea saith}* That bulk of waters distinct from rivers, commonly so called. See on chap. 14. 11. Some take it for the travelers by sea, or fishes in the sea, which can no more inform us in this point, than men upon earth.

*{it is not with me}* Neither can I resolve you.

**V. 15. ***{It cannot be gotten for gold, neither shall silver be weighed for the price thereof.}* If any should object, If this wisdom cannot be found anywhere, yet it may be gotten for money. What cannot money do? The answer is ready. That this wisdom is of so great price, that all the treasures in the world cannot purchase it. This he does illustrate by an enumeration of those things which are of greatest account in the world, yet not of sufficient value for this purchase: as gold, silver, precious stones, and jewels.

*{It cannot be gotten for gold}* Heb. *Fine gold shall not be given for it.* Men may offer it, but it will not be taken, and therefore it is in vain to think of giving it. See the like Act. 8. 18, 19, 20. The meaning is, it cannot be bought, or gotten: for the purest gold in the world. For the word Gotten, see on chap. 14. 4. upon the word *Bring*. The word Gold in Heb. *is only shut up*; But it signifies gold here, because silver follows, and it is commonly joined with gold in other places, as 1 King. 6. 20, 21. where it is translated Pure gold, for men use to store up the purest gold, and so it is meant here, that no gold though never so pure, can be compared with this wisdom, Prov. 3. 15. & 8. 10. 19. & 16. 16.

*{nor silver}* If gold cannot purchase it, much less silver being of lesser value. For the word see on chap. 22. 25.

*{be weighed}* In the balance. For they were wont in those days to weigh their silver. See on chap. 6. 2.

*{for the price thereof}* It cannot be gotten for any price.

**V. 16. ***{It cannot be valued with the gold of Ophir, with the precious Onyx; or the Sapphire.}* It is of more value than all the wealth of the Indies. None of the things here named are worthy to be compared with it.

*{It cannot be valued}* Heb. *laid on the ground.* As goods were wont to be, when men lived by exchange, and are yet in some parts where they lay a heap of salt, against a heap of other things to be changed for it, that buyer and seller may see they be of equal worth, Lam. 4. 2.

*{with the gold}* Some take it for a chain of gold, or some other precious thing made of gold. Others for choice gold. Broughton conceives it to be the name of gold in Ophir, and not a Heb. word.

*{of Ophir}* See on chap. 22. 24.

*{with the precious Onyx}* For the Onyx see on Exod. 28. 20. For precious, see on verse 10. of this chapter.

*{Or the Sapphire}* See on Exod. 28. 18.

**V. 17. ***{The gold and the crystal cannot equal it, and the exchange of it shall not be for jewels of fine gold}* To show the worth of this high wisdom he goes on to instance in other precious things of great account in some countries, yet such as cannot purchase this wisdom.

*{The gold}* Which is counted the chiefest of metals.

*{and the crystal}* The word is nowhere used but here. It has his name from purity and clearness, in the original. Some therefore guess it to be crystal, others to be a diamond. But in these names of metals and jewels, or at least in many of them the Jewish writers are as much to seek, as in the names of many fowls, see on verse 7.

*{cannot equal it}* Heb. *cannot be set in order against it.* See on verse 13. 16.

*{and the exchange of it}* It will not be changed for these things. They would not be a sufficient recompense or restitution for it: So the word is used. chap. 15. 31. & 20. 18.
