*{shall not be for jewels of fine gold}* Or, *vessels of fine gold*. Of the most solid gold, which has been most thoroughly purged by the fire, and so is become harder than other gold less purified. Others take it to be the name of a place, whence pure gold came, as Ophir was: and they think this *Phaz* in the original to be the same with U*phaz*, a place famous for gold, and mentioned Jer. 10. 9. Dan. 10. 5. Broughton translates it *Phez-gold*, and would have it to come from Fesse in Barbary. The names differ not much: and it is likely, that Jewish writers are to seek in these several names of gold, as well as of fowls and precious stones.

**V. 18. ***{No mention shall be made of coral, or of pearls: for the price of wisdom is above rubies.}* If we should speak what might be said of this wisdom, if men understood it thoroughly, these precious things here mentioned should not once be named, they are so vile in comparison of it.

*{No mention shall be made}* These things though excellent in themselves, yet deserve never to be mentioned, but clean forgotten, when we talk of this high wisdom. For the word see on chap. 24. 20.

*{of Coral}* Or, *Ramoth.* This and the following word are not well understood now. This word comes from a word, that signifies to be high, and it may be the translators render it *coral*, because that is said to grow on rocks.

*{or of pearls}* The word is used nowhere else, and therefore translated pearls in general.

*{for the price}* It comes from a word, that signifies to draw out, as the price of a thing draws it out from the shop or market to the buyer. Others read it *a little wisdom*, because they say the word signifies a little sled, without wheels, such a one as husbandmen draw their seed-corn in, which will not hold much. So some read Psal. 126. 6. *Bearing a draught of seed.*

*{of wisdom}* See on verse 12.

*{is above rubies}* A precious stone of a red color, as appears, Lam. 4. 7.

**V. 19. ***{The Topaz of Ethiopia shall not equal it, neither shall it be valued with pure gold.}* The topaz a precious stone which men travel as far as Ethiopia to fetch (and therefore must needs be of great price) and the gold that is oftenest purified (and therefore most pure) are not comparable to this wisdom.

*{The Topaz}* See on Exod. 28. 17.

*{of Ethiopia}* So it is likely to be meant here, though interpreters acknowledge another Cush nearer to the land of Canaan.

*{shall not equal it}* See on verse 13. 17.

*{neither shall it be valued}* See on verse 16.

*{with pure gold}* With the purest gold, which is most free from dross. For Pure see on chap. 14. 4. For Gold, see on verse 16. of this chapter.

**V. 20. ***{Whence then cometh wisdom? and where is the place of understanding?}* The question is propounded in this verse and resolved. 1. Negatively, where it is not, v. 21. 22. 2. Affirmatively, where it is, verse 23. For the question in this verse, it is as if he had said, Be it therefore, that men, who are so painful and diligent in seeking and searching after the things before rehearsed, be it I say, that they are desirous, and that they apply their whole study, and labor to attain unto this wisdom, yet whence will they fetch it? or in what place or country will they come to the knowledge of it? The verse is the same with verse 12. all save one word. See the interpretation there.

*{cometh}* See on chap. 15. 21. & 1. 19.

**V. 21. ***{Seeing it is hid from the eyes of all living, and kept close from the fowles of the air.}* It is hard to find the place of it, because none now alive can find it, neither men, nor other creatures, in this verse, nor those that did live before, and are now dead, have attained to the full knowledge of it, verse 22. As if Job had said, If they look for it beneath among men, who can dig deepest, no man that lives, has ever seen it: if on high, no bird, that can fly highest, ever set eyes on it.

*{Seeing}* It has been showed before, verse 13. that no men could attain to it, nor birds attain to things easier to be had, verse 7. where is it then to be found?

*{it is hid}* It cannot be found out by any man, being beyond the reach of the quickest sight, and so concealed, that none can attain it.

*{from the eyes}* See on chap. 15. 15. upon the word *Sight*.

*{of all living}* See on verse 13.

*{and kept close}* Another word of the same signification to show, that it is so sure hidden, that no man can possibly find it. So much may be seen on chap. 14. 10, 11.

*{from the fowles of the ayre}* Heb. *of heaven.* See on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

**V. 22. ***{Destruction, and death say, We have heard the fame thereof with our ears.}* Lastly, if we shall go down into the bowels of the earth or to hell, and there inquire for this wisdom, and demand whether they can tell us any tidings of it; they will not deny that the fame and bruit of it, which sounds in all places, has also pierced thither: but further information can they not give us.

*{Destruction}* See on chap. 26. 6.

*{and death say}* Either he means it of hell and the grave, attributing speech to them, as to other like things, verse 14. or else such as did live on earth before, but now were in hell, and the grave.

*{We have heard the fame thereof with our ears}* This argues a great fame of God's wisdom in his various dealings with the sons of men. We have heard it, and heard the fame of it, and that with our ears. See the like, Psal. 44. 1. Obad. 1. 1. But yet a bare fame without knowing the particular reasons of God's proceedings. For the word Heard, see on chap. 13. 17. For the word Fame, in Heb. *it is a hearing*. For things that are famous are everywhere talked of, and heard. For the word Ears, see on chap. 15. 21.

**V. 13. ***{God understandeth the way thereof, and he knoweth the place thereof.}* Some conceive these words to be the words of hell and death, as if they should answer to him, that should inquire of them concerning this wisdom, that they had heard of it indeed, but God only had the perfect knowledge of it. But they may well be Job's own words, as if he had said to his friends, Doubtless which way soever men shall turn themselves, be they never so witty and laborious, they must needs confess this (which if you had thoroughly considered, you would not so rashly and unadvisedly because of the greatness of my misery have condemned the whole course of my life) to wit, that God has reserved to himself the deep reasons of his various proceedings with men. That God only knows this, he affirms in this verse, and proves it from his powerful providence 1. set out, verse 24. 2. enlarged by effects in ordering wind and water, rain and thunder, verse 25, 26. 3. he concludes it more particularly, verse 27.

*{God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5. He means, God alone knows it, as appears by the opposition to what went before. None knows it but God.

*{understandeth}* Perfectly knows.

*{the way thereof}* Not the way how to attain to it, but the way of God's wise acting and disposing of the estates and conditions of men in the world. For Job went not about to teach his friends how to find out this mystery, but blamed them, that they took upon them the knowledge of that which God only knew, and as if they had been of God's privy counsel, condemned him for a wicked man, because of his afflictions, whereas God sometimes afflicts good men extraordinarily for reasons known to himself only, and which man's wisdom cannot reach. For the word see on chap. 17. 9.

*{and he knoweth the place thereof}* Not where men may find it, but where is it. To wit, in himself only, where no creature can reach to the height of it. For the word, Place, see on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 26.

**V. 24. ***{For he looketh to the ends of the earth, and seeth under the whole heaven.}* For God is not so narrow-sighted, as men are; but though he be higher than the highest heavens, yet he not only knows all that is in heaven, but also all things on earth, yea those things that are done in the remotest parts of the world, and farthest off from the view of men, or anywhere under the whole cope of heaven.

*{For he looketh}* He knows them as well, as men do things, which they see with their eyes.

*{to the ends of the earth}* There is nothing in the whole course of nature, which he does not understand, Prov. 15. 3. Zech. 4. 10. For the word, Ends, see on chap. 26. 14. upon the word, *Parts*. For the word, Earth, see on chap. 9. 24.

*{and seeth}* See on chap. 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold*.

*{under the whole heaven}* Whatsoever is or is done under the whole heaven. Or, *under all the heavens.* That is, under all the climates of heaven, or all the world over.

**V. 25. ***{To make the weight for the wind, and he weigheth the waters by measure.}* These words are rather to be understood of the works of God's daily providence, than of the settling things at the first, because he says, God looks over all the world to do it. God keeps the winds and waters in order, so that they cannot increase or be diminished, as they will themselves, but must blow and flow when and as he please, and no otherwise.

*{To make}* See on chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *Bring forth*.

*{the weight}* To keep the wind so in order, as if it were weighed, that it may blow just so much as God will, and no more: so long as he will, and no longer: where he will, and nowhere else.

*{for the wind}* Which is very light, yet God can weigh it: For the word, see on chap. 15. 2. upon Vain knowledge.

*{and he weigheth}* Or, *ordereth.* He keeps them so in order, as if they were weighed out.

*{the waters}* Of the sea and rivers, so that they keep within their channels, or overflow, as, when, and where he please. For the word, see on chap. 14. 9.

*{by measure}* So that they keep a just decorum with his will. Or, *by weight.* For we do not use to weigh by measure.

**V. 26. ***{When he made a decree for the rain, and a way for the lightning of the thunder.}* It appears that God by his providence looks over all the world also by sending rain, and thunder, when, and where, as much, and as little as he please.

*{When he made}* Or *when he maketh.* As on verse 25. For Job speaks not of God's works of creation, but of providence. For the word, see on chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *Bring forth*.

*{a decree}* Or, *a statute.* When he guides it as exactly, as if it were ordered by a statute law: So that it rains no more, nor less than he will. He numbers the showers, and drops of rain. Or, *a bound.* For the word see on chap. 26. 10.

*{for the rain}* So that it should rain upon one city, and not upon another. Am. 4. 7.

*{and a way}* A certain course appointed by God, when, how long, how terribly it should lighten and thunder. For the word, see on chap. 17. 9.

*{for the lightning}* It comes from a word, that signifies to see, because lightning is very bright and may be seen far, Mat. 24. 27. And also it is seen before the thunder be heard.

*{of the thunder}* Heb. *of the voices.* The word signifies a sound or voice in general, but here it is put for the thunder, which is the loudest sound of all, and therefore called the voice of God, Psal. 29. 3. And it is called voices in the plural number, because for the most part many thunderclaps come after one another. It is called the lightning of the thunder, because it is caused by the thunder, as sparks by knocking flints together. The lightning indeed appears first, because the sight is quicker than the hearing, and the eye can see sooner and farther than the ear can hear. Yet the thunder goes before it in nature and time, as the cause before the effect. Others read, *the lightning and the thunder.* Extending God's providence immediately to both.

**V. 27. ***{Then did he see it, and declare it, he prepared it, yea and searched it out.}* Or, *Then doth he see it, and number it, and prepare it, and search it out.* For as it was observed before, Job speaks not of the use of God's wisdom in the works of creation, but of providence. And this heap of words shows how well known, familiar, intimate, and proper this wisdom is to God: even as things are to men, which they have always in their sight, and count daily, and prepare, or order, and search into to know the depth of them.

*{Then}* In the course of his daily providence, when he orders wind, and water, rain, and thunder.

*{doth he see it}* He has it present with him, and employs it in doing things above human reason. And therefore no wonder that you cannot tell the cause of my afflictions, who are to seek in these things. For the word, see on chap. 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold*.

*{and number it}* He comprehends it, and possesses it all, as may appear by these wise, and mighty actions of his, the reason whereof we cannot comprehend. So the word is translated, chap. 14. 16.

*{and prepare it}* He has it as ready to use in those daily wonderful works of his providence, as men have arms or money prepared and laid up for war.

*{and search it out}* He understands it, as perfectly, yea more perfectly, than men do those things, into which they have thoroughly searched. And that this is the true meaning of the text, and that it is not to be interpreted of wisdom which God makes known to men, but of that wisdom, which he keeps unto himself containing the reasons of his actions, which he would not have men to know, appears, by that which went before. For Job would never have blamed his friends for taking upon them to know God's revealed will, which they ought to know. But he blames them for diving into God's secrets, and presuming to know the secret causes of his afflictions, which God had not revealed to them. It appears also by the opposition in the verse following, whereas God does in a manner forbid men to seek after the secret causes of his proceedings, and in all their ways to follow the rule of his will revealed in his word.

**V. 28. ***{And unto man he said, Behold the fear of the Lord, that is wisdom, and to depart from evil is understanding.}* Now Job accommodates what he had said before to his present purpose, and answers a tacit objection. What is there no wisdom then communicated to man by God? Does God keep it all to himself? No: so, says Job. But he has forbidden man to inquire after the secret causes of the works of his providence towards all creatures, especially towards men, and commanded him to seek after, and be content with, and count it wisdom enough for him to know God's revealed will, and to guide his life according to it. Thus Job concluded his speech, as Solomon does his Ecclesiastes, Eccl. 12. 13. and sets out a wise man, as God sets him out, chap. 1. 1. See like places, Deut. 4. 6. & 29. 29. Psal. 111. 10. Prov. 1. 7. & 9. 10.

*{And}* Or rather, *But.* As it is translated to the same purpose, Deut. 29. 29. Let not man be discontented, that he knows not the ways of my secret providence; let him be content with that knowledge, which I am pleased to reveal to him.

*{unto man}* Broughton reads it, *to Adam*, to wit, at the beginning: but we have showed before, that Job speaks not here of things done in the beginning, but in the course of God's ordinary providence. For the word, see on chap. 14. 1.

*{He}* God, spoken of in the former verses. Job delivers not the golden sentence in his own words, but in God's, that his friends might the more be affected with it.

*{said}* In divers ages he revealed this to his servants.

*{behold}* Take notice of it as of a thing of great excellency, and my special will, that you let my secret judgments alone, and study to live according to my law. For the word, see on chap. 1. 12.

*{the fear}* See on chap. 15. 4. & 1. 1.

*{of the Lord}* Of God, who is the foundation or pillar, that upholds all things, according to the derivation of the word in the original: and therefore is worthy to be feared of all men.

*{that is wisdom}* That is the wisdom that God requires of man, and wherewithal he must be content, and not dive into God's secrets.

*{and to depart from evil}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{is understanding}* It is the best and most fit understanding of man. If he can get but so much knowledge, as to learn to worship God aright, and depart from sin, he is a happy man. But here is more intended. So to depart from evil, that he do good, for both are required, and he that truly fears God, will be so wise, as to show his care of both these duties, and not busy himself to dive into God's secrets.

## CHAPTER 29

**Verse 1. ***{Moreover Job continued his parable and said,}* Job having now the second time made some intermission in his speech to see if his friends would give any answer, and finding them altogether silent, now goes on, and in this his last speech fully sets out his integrity, which he had but glanced at in his former discourses, and excuses his impatience by his great fall from so great prosperity to so great misery without any gross wickedness committed by him. And 1. he sets out his former prosperity in this chap. 2. his present misery, chap. 30. 3. his constant piety, chap. 3[...]. For the words of this verse they are all the same, that were in the beginning of his former speech, chap. 27. 1. See the exposition of them there.

**V. 2. ***{O that I were as in months past, as in the days, when God preserved me.}* In this verse Job wishes for the recovery of his former prosperity, the greatness whereof he sets out in the following verses to the end of the chapter.

*{O that I were}* Heb. *Who will give me.* The Heb. form of wishing. See on chap. 6. 8. & 11. 5.

*{as in months past}* As in the former time of my life, when I lived in prosperity, and greatness. Man's time in Scripture is sometime measured by years, sometime by months, and sometimes by days. The word months in the original comes from the moon, as it does in English, because months are measured by the course of the moon. The other word signifies either time past, which was before us, or that which is before us in place, or in our sight. Here Job speaks of his times of prosperity formerly.

*{as in the days}* The same thing intended, which was meant by months before, and it shows Job's great desire of his former prosperity by this reduplication of the thing, though in other words. For the word see on chap. 14. 14. & 15. 32.

*{when God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{preserved me}* Kept me from all evil, and all mine also. This the devil observed, chap. 1. 10. For the word, see on chap. 7. 12, 20. & 10. 14.

**V. 3. ***{When his candle shined upon my head, and when by his light I walked through darkness.}* God's preservation of him, he sets out, 1. by his blessing on his domestical affairs, to verse 7. 2. political, to the end of the chap. On his domestical affairs, 1. in general, verse 3. 4. 2. in particular, verse 5. 6. In general in this verse by a similitude. In the next verse literally. The similitude in this verse is taken from God's care of the world in general. He prospered me in all my affairs, and directed me, as he does the world by the sun in the day, and by the moon in the night. Others take it for an allusion to the cloud and pillar of fire, that guided the Israelites in the wilderness, the one by day, and the other by night. But it is doubtful, whether Job knew that or no. He is thought to be more ancient. See on chap. 1. 1. & 22. 15.

*{When his candle}* Or, *lampe*, Psal. 132. 17. & 18. 28. By God's candle he means his favor and blessing, which prospered him in all he went about. For the word, see on chap. 18. 6.

*{shined}* Gave me light and direction, how to proceed in all my affairs, and gave me good success. Others read it, *When he made his candle to shine.* That is, when God himself directed and prospered me by his favor.

*{upon my head}* Or, *over my head.* An allusion to the sun and moon, which God has placed above us to give us light. Or, as some think, to the carrying of a lamp or torch, which is lifted up, that they which follow may see the better. For the word, see on chap. 19. 9.

*{and when by his light}* By his favor and direction. Heb. *at his light.* That is, by the benefit of it. For the word, see on chapter 18. 5. Others read, *and when by the light of it.* That is, of God's candle.

*{I walked}* I went on in a constant course of prosperity. For the word see on chap. 14. 20. on the word *Passeth*.

*{through darkness}* Not through troubles, for Job had none in those days, chap. 1. 10. but through dangers on all sides, into which other men fell, but he escaped them. Or, through doubtful businesses and difficult, which he could never have waded through by his own reason, and understanding. For the word, see on chapter 15. 22, 23.

**V. 4. ***{As I was in the days of my youth, when the secret of God was upon my Tabernacle.}* Here he particularly sets out his former prosperity in regard of his domestical affairs in plain terms. I would I were, as I was in the prime and flower of my youth, when God did so friendly and familiarly intermeddle with my affairs.

*{as I was in the days}* That is, in the time. Here man's time is set out by days, as verse 2. by months. For days see on chapter 14. 14.

*{of my youth}* Some read it, *of my winter*, that is, that I were at rest again from these pains, as a soldier in his winter quarters. Others, *of my Autumne*: that is, of my former prosperity, for in autumn many fruits of trees and of the earth are ripe, and in their prime. Others, as in the translation, *of my youth.* And some understand it, as I was in the prime, and strength of my days. And others, of the youth or prime of his estate and prosperity. And this must needs be the sense, however the word be brought to it, directly, or by consequence. This agrees well with the words foregoing, and following, wherein it appears, that Job wished for more than rest and quietness.

*{when the secret}* When God in a familiar and secret manner guided me and my family, and made all my affairs to prosper. For the word see on chap. 15. 8.

*{of God}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{was upon my tabernacle}* See on chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14.

**V. 5. ***{When the Almighty was yet with me, when my children were about me.}* Here begins the description of God's blessing in particular on Job's family, 1. in regard of his children and servants, in this verse; 2. of his wealth, verse 6. When God showed himself constantly favorable to me, and gave me store of children and servants to attend on me.

*{When the Almighty}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{was yet}* Continued constantly to bless me, and had not given over to prosper me, and my family.

*{with me}* To help me, and keep me from troubles. In such a case God's people think him present with them: but when he crosses them, then they look upon him as absent by reason of the weakness of their faith. Rom. 8. 31. Psal. 10. 1.

*{when my children}* Or, *when my servants.* Heb. *when my young men.* The word signifies either children, who live with their parents, while they are young, or servants, who for the most part are young also.

*{were about me}* When I had abundance of children, and servants attending on me every one in his place ready to serve me. This was indeed a great temporal blessing. Psal. 128. 3.

**V. 6. ***{When I washed my steps with butter, and the rocks poured me out rivers of oil.}* A hyperbolical expression of that store of provisions, which Job had. As if butter had flowed in streams, and the rocks had poured out store of oil to him. See the like expressions, Deut. 32. 13. & 33. 24. Gen. 49. 11. Psal. 81. 16. Job 20. 17. By these two is meant all manner of riches. He had such store of cattle, that he had abundance of milk and butter like rivers: and such store of olive trees, and other trees, that he had oil, and other liquors made of fruits as plentiful, as if the rocks had poured it out to him in streams.

*{when I washed}* When I had such plenty of butter, that if I would or needed, I could have used it as water to wash my feet withal. Not that Job used to do so, for he was never so dainty, proud, nor prodigal. This he mentions, because the riches of the ancients consisted most in cattle. Thence came *pecunia* money in Latin of *pecus* cattle, and money was stamped with a lamb on it, and called קשיטה a lamb. See Gen. 33. 19. in the margin. Now butter was the best profit of cattle, and therefore Job sets out his riches by the abundance he had of it.

*{My steps}* Heb. *my goings.* That is, his feet the instruments of going. He had store enough to have washed his feet; or in every place where he went, his cattle were so plentiful, that he might have washed every step in butter.

*{with butter}* Heb. *in butter.* Which increases the signification. A little butter will serve to wash one's feet withal, but much is needful to wash them in. For the word see on chap. 20. 17.

*{and the rock poured me out rivers of oil}* Olive trees grew in stony places in Syria and Arabia: and Job had such plenty of oil, as if every stone had afforded him oil. It may be read, *and there was with me a rock melted into rivers of oyle.* See on chapter 28. 2. The last word signifies both oil and fatness, for oil is of a fatty nature.

**V. 7. ***{When I went out to the gate, through the city, when I prepared my seat in the street.}* In this verse, and so forward to the end of the chapter Job sets out God's blessing upon him in his political affairs. And 1. he shows how much honor he had in his passage to the judgment seat, verse 7, 8. 2. What respect he had there, verse 9, 10, 11. 3. The cause of it, verse 12. &c. to verse 18. 4. His confident persuasion of the continuance of his prosperity. verse 18, 19, 20. 5. The grounds of it from thence to the end of the chapter. In the first part, 1. he shows when and where this honor was done unto him: in his passage to the gate, in this verse. 2. By whom, By young and old. verse 8. For the first, It is as if Job had said, As I was happy at home, so likewise abroad; I had much honor whensoever I went along the streets toward the public place of judicature, where I had a seat in the common place provided for me. So that I was not only happy in my stock in the fields abroad, (verse 7.) but also I had great respect in the city.

*{When I went out}* Out of my house toward the judgment seat. For the word see on chap. 20. 25. upon the word, *Cometh out*.

*{to the gate}* To the place of judicature to sit as a judge. See on chap. 5. 4. Drusius reads it, *when I went out of the gate.* Or, *from the gate.* To wit, to go home. I had done justice there, and the people honored me all the way homeward. Broughton reads it, *when I went out of door to the city.* That is, out of the door of my house about my business. But שער signifies rather the gate of a city than the door of a house, as it is often used. Neh. 3. For, went out, see on chap. 20. 25. on the word, *Cometh out*.

*{through the city}* It seems his house was within the city, and his passage from thence to the place of judgment, as it was ordinary, so observable.

*{when I prepared}* When I had it made ready for me.

*{my seat}* He means not an ordinary place, but a seat of judgment, whereon he used to sit to judge the causes of the city. Hereby it appears that Job was a public magistrate.

*{in the street}* Near the gate of the city, in an open place, where all comers in, and goers out might see and hear.

**V. 8. ***{The young men saw me, and hid themselves: and the aged arose, and stood up.}* In this verse he sets out the great reverence that was showed to him by all sorts of persons, although in a different way, according to their differing ages.

*{The young men}* He means not those of his family, as verse 5. but such as met him in the streets.

*{saw me}* Going toward the judgment seat. For the word see on chap. 19. 27. upon the word *Behold*.

*{and hid themselves}* Either for reverence of my person, or for fear I should espy something amiss in them.

*{and the aged}* See on chap. 12. 12.

*{arose}* In token of honor, and to show reverence unto me. Lev. 19. 32. For the word see on chap. 14. 12.

*{and stood up}* They would not sit down again, till I was gone by, so much they did honor me.

**V. 9. ***{The Princes refrained talking, and laid their hand on their mouth.}* The honor that Job had in his way to the judgment seat was set out in the two former verses, his honor there is set out verse 9, 10, 11. And that 1. from the principal men called princes, verse 9. 2. From the middle sort of the city called nobles, verse 10. 3. From the meaner sort, as well as the higher, verse 11. In this verse he shows how the principal men of the city brake off their communication, as soon as he came in presence, and voluntarily kept silence, till he had done speaking.

*{The Princes}* The chief of their companies or societies in the cities, or they that had most authority in the city.

*{refrained talking}* Heb. *restrained themselves in words.* They would not speak at all, while Job was speaking, nor interrupt him, that they might hear all he had to say, out of respect to his wisdom or dignity. And this was not done by mean men only, but by personages of principal quality, so much respect had they to Job. See on Job 4. 2.

*{and laid their hand on their mouth}* See on chap. 21. 5. The word for hand signifies the palm of the hand, not the finger or the fist. And it imports a full stopping of the mouth with the hand open, that no words may come out of it.

**V. 10. ***{The Nobles held their peace, and their tongue cleaved to the roof of their mouth.}* The nobles following the examples of the princes were silent, while Job spoke, and would by no means interrupt him. Such a general reverence was given to Job by all that heard him, verse 21, 22.

*{The Nobles}* It comes from a word that signifies, Before, because nobles are eyed and looked upon by all men. Here is meant the lower officers of the city, as by princes are meant the higher in the former verse.

*{held their peace}* Heb. *The voice of the nobles was hid.* That is, suppressed, not heard. They were ashamed to speak in my presence, and hid their voice, as men ashamed hide themselves. For the word Voice, see on chap. 15. 21. upon the word *Sound*.

*{and their tongue cleaved to the roof of their mouth}* That is, they were as still as if they could not speak. For if the tongue be not at liberty, it cannot utter words. So this phrase is used to set out an extreme silence, Psal. 137. 6. Ezek. 3. 26. as it is used to set out extreme thirst. Lam. 4. 4. For the word Tongue, see on chap. 15. 5.

**V. 11. ***{When the ear heard me, then it blessed me, and when the eye saw me, it gave witness to me.}* All people were glad when I was on the bench to do justice: They that heard my sentence praised me, and they that saw my just proceedings gave an ample testimony of my upright dealing.

*{When the ear}* See on chap. 13. 17.

*{heard me}* See on chap. 13. 17.

*{then it blessed me}* It commended my just proceedings and wished me well. That is, the man that heard my sentence, for the ear cannot bless. See the like of the eyes, Luk. 10. 23. Not the eyes only, but the men were blessed, who saw those things, Christ speaks of there.

*{and when the eye}* See on chap. 15. 15. upon the word *Sight*.

*{saw me}* See on chap. 19. 27. upon the word *Behold*.

*{it gave witness to me}* The man that saw me (as before in this verse, upon the Ear) either upon the bench, who though he could not come to hear me, yet was confident, I would do right: or such as met me in the streets did testify as much of me.

**V. 12. ***{Because I delivered the poor, that cried, and the fatherless, and him that had none to help him.}* Now Job lays down the grounds of that great good opinion the people generally had of him, to wit, not for his wealth and greatness, but his sincere administration of justice, which was seen, 1. In relieving the poor, that were wronged, to verse 17. 2. In pulling down proud oppressors, verse 17. The first is set out in elegant words, with a particular deciphering of the needy persons relieved by him. As 1. the afflicted and fatherless, in this verse. 2. Such as were ready to perish, and widows, verse 13. 3. The cause of his so doing, verse 14. 4. His helping the blind and lame, verse 15. 5. The poor, and that with a careful inquiry into their cause, verse 16. that he might right them, not for any gain, that could be looked for from them, but out of conscience; all which testifies, that Job by many witnesses might be cleared from the unjust imputations laid upon him by his friends.

*{Because}* For this reason others spoke well of me. Or, *That.* They bare witness, that I did carry myself thus uprightly, and would do it still, if they were called thereunto.

*{I delivered}* I freed the poor from those, who by their wealth and might would have oppressed them.

*{the poor}* The word signifies a man afflicted or oppressed, I delivered him out of the hand of him, that would oppress him, Psal. 72. 12.

*{that cried}* Who being oppressed by an enemy too strong for him, cried unto me to do him justice. For the word see on chap. 19. 7.

*{and the fatherless}* See on chap. 22. 9.

*{and him}* And any man that complained to me of wrong done him, who had none else that would adventure to right him. Or, *even him.* That is, such a one as not only had lost his parents, but also had no friends in the world, that would take his part, Eccles. 4. 1.

*{that had none to help him}* I did pass sentence on his side according to right, when others would not, and stood to him, when none durst be seen to assist him.

**V. 13. ***{The blessing of him that was ready to perish, came upon me: and I caused the widows heart to sing for joy.}* Whereas others were so far from assisting people ready to perish for want of their help, that they rather insulted over them so much the more, and were therefore hated by those miserable creatures, I took pity of them, and freed them from the danger of death approaching, and therefore had many good prayers, and well wishes from them.

*{The blessing}* See on verse 11.

*{of him that was ready to perish}* By their wants, and men's unmercifulness, Prov. 31. 6. They must have perished, if I had not relieved them. It may be understood of such as were unjustly accused of great crimes, which might endanger their whole estate or lives, and must needs perish, if Job had not by a just sentence relieved them against the will, and power of others. For the word see on chap. 4. 7.

*{came upon me}* I relieved them, and carried myself so, that they had just cause to commend me, and wish me well. Or, I was praised, and prayed for by them. Or, I had the benefit of their prayers, and God blessed me extraordinarily for relieving them. For the word see on chap. 15. 21.

*{and I caused the widows heart to sing for joy}* I delivered her from her enemy, and relieved her in her necessity, and gave her great cause of hearty joy. For the Widow see on chapter 22. 9. Such are more sad, as wanting husbands to assist, and comfort them. But Job gave them cause of joy. For Heart, see on chap. 17. 11. He sets the widows so free, and out of danger of their oppressors, that their hearts were so full of joy, that they could not keep it in, but it burst out in singing.

**V. 14. ***{I put on righteousness, and it clothed me: my judgment was as a robe and a Diadem}* Lest any should object, that Job was a popular man, and sought the applause of the people by siding with the weakest, were their cause right or wrong, and so got credit by unlawful means, now he lays down the fountain of those good offices which he did to others, even to the poorest which was a high account, which he had of justice, and upright dealing. It may be other men thought it an honor to them to wear robes and diadems, tokens of public magistracy: but he accounted doing of justice his greatest ornament.

*{I put on}* I covered me all over with it as with a garment. I favored no unjust way. For the word see on chap. 8. 22.

*{righteousness}* Justice and upright dealing in all my sentences. For the word see on chap. 8. 13.

*{and it clothed me}* It covered me all over; so that no man could see any injustice in me in any of my public affairs. The same word in the original with the former. Heb. *I put on righteousnesse, and it put on me.* I was never parted from it, nor it from me in all my sentences of judgment.

*{my judgment}* My just proceeding in judgment in careful searching out the right, and faithful pronouncing sentence accordingly, see on chap. 8. 3. & 22. 4.

*{was a robe}* Or, *a coat.* It is translated a mantle, chap. 1. 20. & 2. 12. which was an ordinary garment. And then Job's meaning is, that his justice was publicly taken notice of by all, his righteous proceedings being as visible as the coat on his back. They who translate it Robe, mean that it was an ornament to him, as the robe was to the king or priest. But that is rather set out in the next word. This word comes from a root, that in the original signifies to deal perfidiously, as also does בגד a garment. See on chap. 6. 15. upon the word, *Dealt deceitfully*. So God by our garments puts us in mind of our falling from him. Before man fell, he needed no garments.

*{and a diadem}* Not only a covering of the head, but an ornament to it, surrounding the temples, as the word imports. Such did kings and priests wear. His meaning is, that his just proceedings were a great honor to him.

**V. 15. ***{I was eyes to the blind, and feet was I to the lame.}* Having in the former verse cleared himself from ambitious popularity, and showed his integrity in judging causes, now he returns to show how he was many ways helpful to such as were oppressed.

*{I was}* He did not take state on him, and put off poor suitors to his servants for direction, but he himself directed them.

*{eyes}* He does not say, an eye, but eyes, in the plural, to intimate, that he gave them sufficient, and full directions, how to manage their causes for the best advantage of their right. For the word see on chap. 15. 15. on the word *Sight*.

*{to the blind}* To such as knew not how to manage their own law-business; for such he speaks of here.

*{and feet was I to the lame.}* If they wanted means or countenance to follow their just cause I supplied it. So that no man wanted an advocate to direct him, or plead for him in Job's court in a just cause. He would do it himself.

**V. 16. ***{I was a Father to the poor: and the cause, which I knew not, I searched out.}* In this verse, he shows his great affection to the poor, and his great care they might have no wrong.

*{I was a Father to the poor}* I was a father careful to protect them from all injuries. See the word of similitude left out, chap. 7. 7. The word for father and for poor come both from one root in the Hebrew, אבה which signifies to wish well to, or desire, for parents wish well to their children, and desire their good, and poor men desire to enjoy those things they want. For the word Father, see on chap. 15. 10. For the word Poor see on chap. 24. 4. upon the word *Needy*.

*{and the cause}* The matter of contention, especially between rich men oppressing, and poor men oppressed.

*{which I knew not}* This may be understood two ways. Either that he was careful to inquire into difficult causes, which he could not easily understand the right, that so he might pass a just sentence: or that he was not only ready to receive causes that were brought to him, but also inquired abroad, what poor men were oppressed by those that were mighty, who either were ashamed or durst not complain, and these he animated to prosecute their right, and helped them to it, who otherwise would have been undone. Others read it, *Of him whom I knew not.* Intimating that he was ready to right such men, as he had no acquaintance withal, out of pure love to justice.

*{I sought out}* I endeavored to know who were wronged, or how I might right them. Deut. 13. 14. Prov. 25. 2. & 29. 7.

**V. 17. ***{And I brake the jaws of the wicked, and plucked the spoil out of his teeth.}* Here he shows that though wicked men by their wealth and might sought to oppress and ruin others, like wild beasts taking the prey in their teeth ready to devour it, yet he did so keep them under, that they could not oppress others, and forced them to make restitution to those whom they had formerly oppressed.

*{And I brake the jaws of the wicked}* Heb. *the jaw teeth or the grinders*. A phrase often used in Scripture to show the cruelty of wicked oppressors disappointed, Psal. 3. 7. & 58. 6. For the word Wicked, see on chap. 13. 7. on the word, *Wickedly*.

*{And plucked the spoil out of his teeth}* I made him restore those things he had wrongfully taken from others. There are but two ways to keep wild beasts from devouring, to break their jaws, or to take the prey out of their teeth, 1 Sam. 17. 35. Am. 3. 12. Job did hinder some from wronging others, and made other wicked men to make restitution. The Hebrew word, for, Pluck, is cast. For the word, Spoil, see on chap. 24. 5. For the word, Teeth, see on chap. 16. 9.

**V. 18. ***{Then I said, I shall die in my nest, and shall multiply my days, as the sand.}* Job having had so much authority, and prosperity, and so many good wishes from others by reason of his upright carriage, and the good he had done to them, persuades himself, that he should still enjoy a comfortable life, and in the end find a quiet and comfortable death. Which he professes in this verse. And sets it out by the similitude of a tree, verse 19. and of a bow, verse 20. But he now finds it far otherwise, and that troubles him.

*{Then}* See on chap. 4. 2. It not only intimates the time, when Job spoke it, to wit, in his prosperity, but also the reason why. Because of his upright carriage, and general good opinion, that all men had of him. So this particle is used to infer a reason of what follows, chap. 21. 34.

*{I said}* I thought so, I was fully persuaded in my mind, that my condition would not alter. So the word is used, Psal. 30. 6. & 39. 1. For the word itself, see on chap. 16. 1.

*{I shall die}* Heb. *I shall breath out.* Or, *give up the Ghost.* That is, I shall not perish for want, or be slain by enemies, but shall quietly in peace give up my spirit to God. So did Abraham, Gen. 25. 8. For the word, see on chap. 13. 19.

*{in my nest}* In my house in a prosperous estate, among my children, in the midst of my family, as a bird dies in his nest through old age, and is not devoured before by the hawks or kites. As man's house is here called a nest, so the bird's nest is called a house, Psal. 84. 3.

*{and shall multiply}* I shall live many years.

*{my days}* The days of my life here on earth. For the word, see on chap. 14. 14.

*{as the sand}* I shall live as many comfortable years, as there shall be sands on the seashore. That is, I shall live long in a plentiful condition. A hyperbolical expression in Scripture to set out a great multitude, Gen. 22. 17. & 32. 12. & 41. 49.

**V. 19. ***{My root was spread out by the waters, and the dew lay all night upon my branch.}* I conceived my prosperity would last, because I found myself like a tree watered from the earth below, and from the heaven above, and daily flourishing more, and more.

*{My root}* For the similitude from a root, and the word itself, see on chap. 18. 16.

*{was spread out by the waters}* Heb. *opened to the waters.* A tree planted near a riverside, where the water in time of drought may continually refresh it, brings forth abundance of fruit, Psal. 1. 3. Jer. 17. 7. so Job's honor and riches increased daily. The word פתח is used sometimes for opening the mouth, or beginning to speak, as chap. 1. sometimes for opening a scabbard by pulling the sword out of it, Psal. 37. 14. 3. of opening sacks by untying them, as Gen. 44. 11. 4. for the earth opening itself to devour men, as Num. 16. 32. or to bring forth corn. Isa. 45. 8. or for opening it by plowing, as Isa. 28. 24. 5. for engraving on brass or stones, which is a kind of opening of them, Exod. 28. 9. 6. for budding or blossoming of trees, whereby they seem to open themselves, Cant. 7. 12. 7. of opening a gate or door, as Isa. 26. 2. Here it may be taken in the fourth sense for the earth giving way to the water of the river to pass to the root of the tree to make it fruitful. For the word, Waters, see on chap. 14. 9.

*{and the dew}* As the water gives strength to the root to enable the tree to bring forth much fruit, so the dew falling from heaven, and lying on the branches makes the fruit swell, and helps to ripen it, Hos. 14. 5. So Job by God's blessing got store of wealth, and enjoyed the comfort of it. The word in the original has his name from covering, because the dew covers the branches of trees, and the earth also.

*{lay all night}* The dew uses to arise in the night, and abide till the heat of the sun dry it up. Hereby is intended the continuance of Job's prosperity in all his ways.

*{upon my branch}* Or, *upon my harvest.* See on chap. 18. 16.

**V. 20. ***{My glory was fresh in me, and my bow was renewed in my hand.}* Some read the words thus, *My glory shall be fresh in me, and my bow shall be renewed in my hand.* And they take it for a confident persuasion, that Job had of the continuance of his prosperity, like that verse 18. But the former translation is better, which makes it a reason of that confidence, verse 18. Because not only his honor, and strength did not decrease, but also increase daily: and therefore was likely to continue. So he goes on with the similitude of the tree, which being watered from earth and heaven, continues green, and flourishing. As chap. 14. 7, 8, 9. Isa. 40. 31.

*{My glory}* The honor and respect which I have from others. It comes from a word that signifies to be heavy or weighty; for honor adds weight to men's persons, and makes them of greater account than others. For the word, see on chap. 19. 9.

*{was fresh}* Heb. *new.* I had every day more respect from men than other.

*{in me}* Heb. *with me.* It was from others, not within himself.

*{and my bow}* My strength by access of a multitude of friends continually to uphold me, Gen. 49. 24. 1 Sam. 2. 4.

*{was renewed}* Heb. *did change.* To wit, itself, or its strength. See on chap. 14. 7. As an archer in the wars, when his bow breaks, takes a new one, and goes on stoutly in the battle, so Job's prosperous condition grew every day stronger than other.

*{in my hand}* So that I could use it as a means to preserve me from my enemies, as an archer does his bow in his hand. For the word, see on chap. 14. 23.

**V. 21. ***{Unto me men gave ear, and waited, and kept silence at my counsel.}* Job goes on to the end of the chapter to show the grounds of his persuasion of the continuance of his prosperity. These are taken from that great respect, that men gave to his words, expressed in divers particulars. The sum of this verse is, as if Job should have said. I did not without cause judge, that my prosperity would continue, because whensoever I spoke, every one heard me with great attention: every one waited to see the issue of my speech, and agreed to me, when I had spoken.

*{Unto me men gave ear}* They kept silence, when I spoke, and no man interrupted me but all hearkened to me, as conceiving great weight to be in all my words. See the verse 9, 10, 11. For the word, see on chap. 13. 17. upon the word, *Hear*.

*{and waited}* Attended expecting what would be my resolution in counsel or judgment, as if they had all depended upon my words.

*{and kept silence}* Replied not again, but consented to what I said. As follows verse 22.

*{at my counsel}* As soon as they heard, what I had concluded, they were satisfied.

**V. 22. ***{After my words they spake not again, and my speech dropped upon them.}* As they hearkened to my speech with patience, and waited to know my resolution, and consented to it when they heard it, so no man was so bold, as to reply, but all were pleased with my words, as herbs and flowers with the dew of heaven.

*{after my words}* After I had done speaking.

*{they spake not again}* Either because they had a persuasion, that I spoke oracles, or else, that I spoke so fully, that nothing needed to be added to persuade men to believe it, or so truly, that they had nothing to object against it. This word is used for not doing a thing again, 1 Sam. 26. 8. 2 Sam. 20. 10.

*{and my speech dropped upon them}* Or, *When.* Or, *thus my speech dropped upon them.* He shows the cause, why they were thus silent and attentive, because his speech was as pleasing to them, as dew to the grass, Deut. 32. 2.

**V. 23. ***{And they waited for me, as for the rain, and they opened their mouth wide, as for the latter rain.}* The content men took in my counsels and sentences, was the cause, that they longed in doubtful matters to hear my resolution, as the dry earth or husbandman longs for rain.

*{And they waited for me}* See on verse 21.

*{as for the rain}* That is, for the former rain, when men have sowed their grounds, which they long for to bring up their seed, Psal. 72. 6. It is opposed to the latter rain in this verse. See Joel, 2. 23.

*{and they opened their mouth wide}* Not to speak, for they kept silence to hear Job: but as the earth chaps, and for drought opens as it were the mouth of it wide, that the rain may fully satisfy it, so were they as desirous to hear Job's resolution of their doubts, Psal. 63. 1. & 65. 9, 10. & 119. 131. Prov. 30. 16. Or, as men, that open their mouths wide to fill them with desirable food, Psal. 81. 10. For the word, Mouth, see on chap. 15. 5.

*{as for the latter rain}* Some take it for the rain before the harvest to make the corn swell. Others for the rain after harvest to bring up grass again for the cattle, which dries away in the heat of harvest, Deut. 11. 14. Jer. 5. 24. Jam. 5. 7. Prov. 26. 1. Zech. 10. 1.

**V. 24. ***{If I laughed on them, they believed it not, and the light of my countenance they cast not down.}* It often comes to pass, that when men in authority carry themselves familiarly with their inferiors, they are contemned, but it was not so with me, but if it happened that in my pleasure I uttered anything merrily, and somewhat more freely than I was accustomed, yet no man thereat took offense, neither needed I therefore to cast down my countenance, as if I had forgot myself.

*{If I laughed on them}* The word שחק in Scripture language, signifies, 1. slighting a person or thing, chap. 39. 7. 2. it is put for smiling upon one in goodwill as men do at feasts upon one another, Eccl. 10. 19. 3. for jesting or being in sport, Prov. 29. 9. 4. for deriding or laughing to scorn, Psal. 2. 4. Here it is taken in the third sense for jesting with them, for Job did not use to slight or deride men.

*{they believed it not}* My countenance, and carriage was so grave at other times, that when I smiled on them, or jested with them, they could not believe, that I was in jest, but thought some serious matter was hid under my smiling countenance, and cheerful words: or they would not believe that I would be so familiar with them, and were overjoyed at it, Luk. 24. 41. See on Job 39. 14. For the word, see on chap. 15. 22.

*{and the light of my countenance they cast not down}* They did not show any dislike of my cheerfulness, as not becoming my place and gravity. Or, *yet the light of my countenance they cast not down.* That is, they did not contemn me because of this familiar carriage, which often breeds contempt of superiors in inferiors: Though they thought there was more in it than jest, yet they durst not think I dissembled, or wronged my place by light carriage: so great an opinion had they of my wisdom and goodness, that they durst not censure me, though they understood not the reason of my actions. So ו is translated, Psal. 119. 110. For the word, Light, see on chap. 18. 5. For, Countenance, chap. 14. 20.

*{cast not down}* In Heb. is, *made not to fall.* They made me not hang down my countenance for shame or grief, Gen. 4. 5. For men ashamed or grieved let their countenance fall, whereas men pleased or cheered lift up their heads, Psal. 3. 2.

**V. 25. ***{I chose out their way, and sat chief, and dwelt as a king in the army, as one that comforteth the mourners.}* Here he concludes with showing what great respect he had from all sorts of men being accounted as an emperor among them, and yet that he used this authority to the good of the people, and so was both feared, and loved of them. As if he had said, If there were any weighty business in hand, I taught them what way and course was best for them to take, sitting among them in the chiefest, and most honorable place, as a king in the midst of his army: and look what counsel, and advice soever I gave them, it was as acceptable unto them, as the speech of him, that comforts those, who are in any distress and misery.

*{I chose out their way}* I directed them, how to bring their lawful affairs to pass. For, Ways, see on chap. 16. 22.

*{and sat chief}* And had the uppermost place, and chief command in all their weighty actions. For the word Sat, see on chap. 22. 6. on the word, *Dwelt*. For, Chief, see on chap. 19. 9. upon the word, *Head*. Some read the beginning of the verse thus.

*{If I chose their way, I sat chief}* That is, whensoever I went to them, they set me above all others, and showed greatest signs of reverence to me.

*{and dwelt as a King in the army}* I was as much feared and honored, as if a king had come among them with a great army, though I came without any force: This shows the reverent account they had of Job, and that constantly, and continually.

*{as one that comforteth the mourners}* Though I was thus reverenced by them, yet they loved me also as men in trouble love those, that come to visit them. An allusion to the charitable manner of those countries, where they used to come to comfort their friends in heaviness, chap. 2. 11. & 42. 11. Or to a king, who speaks comfortably to his soldiers after a loss in battle, or aught else that may grieve them: and therefore is worthily beloved of them, 2 Sam. 19. 7. For, mourning, see on chap. 14. 22.

## CHAPTER 30

**Verse 1. ***{But now they that are younger then I, have me in derision, whose fathers I have disdained to have set with the dogs of my flock.}* As in the former chapter Job had showed the great height to which he had attained in his prosperity, so here he pathetically sets out the extreme misery to which he was now brought, which doubtless was so much the heavier to him, as a fall from a hill hurts a man more than lying on the ground. Here is set out this depth of his misery, 1. by the verbal injuries he received from others, to verse 10. 2. by real ones, to verse 15. 3. by describing his own grief and pain by reason of his sores, to verse 19. 4. By God's rough handling of him, to verse 24. 5. He concludes all with a lamentation of his undeserved misery, as he conceives, and that to the end of the chapter. For the 1. he aggravates the sorrow conceived at their scorn and scoffs by the vileness of the persons. A generous spirit cannot endure to be scoffed at by any, but least of all by base persons. The sum of this verse is, as if Job had said, Such a one was I within these few days as I have told you. But good God, how are all things changed with me, and what a great and sudden alteration has followed? For lo I am become a laughingstock to those, who in years come very far behind me, and whose fathers I would not have once vouchsafed to have in the same estimation, that I had my dogs, which were set to keep my flocks.

*{But}* So it is translated, chap. 26. 1. And very fitly here, for in the whole chap. he opposes his present misery to his former prosperity.

*{now}* This he could not utter without much grief of heart, and dejection of spirit, finding so great an alteration in his condition.

*{they that are younger then I}* Heb. *they that are smaller of dayes then I.* That is, who have not lived so long as I. No doubt it was a great grief to Job that had been honored so much by the ancient, now to be contemned by his youngers; especially himself in likelihood being grown in years. It is not likely that Job meant any of his three friends, that now reasoned with him; as some learned interpreters conceive, for doubtless Job had not so mean an opinion of any of their pedigrees as to think their parents worthy to be set below his dogs. It is more likely that these affronts were offered by others; either before his friends came to comfort him, or while they sat still by him, chap. 2. 13. Or it may be these several conferences might be on several days; and these scoffs by others might come between. The word צעיר sometimes signifies small in condition, low or vile; as, chap. 14. 21. sometimes in number, that is few; as here; Fewer of days. Sometimes alone without mention of days it signifies younger, as Gen. 19. 31. & 48. 14. As on the contrary great is put for old, Job 32. 9. And translated, eldest, 1 Sam. 17. 28. For, Days, see on chap. 14. 14. & 15. 23.

*{have me in derision}* See on chap. 29. 24. upon the word, *Laughed*.

*{I would have disdained to have set with the dogs of my flock}* Whose fathers lived so poorly, that my dogs that kept my sheep fared better; and therefore I might well have a very low account of them. This suits well with their hard fare, verse 3, 4. It was the harder for Job, who had lived so plentifully to suffer such scorn from persons of so beggarly a breed. Job did not despise any for their poverty, but shows their very low condition, that now derided him. To take the words severally. Contemning or disdaining here may be taken for counting them utterly unfit for the meanest office in Job's many employments. For Fathers, see on chap. 15. 10.

*{to have set with the dogs}* To have trusted as my dogs that kept my flocks from the wolves. Or to have made my dog-keepers, or feeders of my dogs, which was a very mean office.

*{of my flock}* The word properly signifies a flock of sheep or goats; not of greater cattle; which they were more unworthy to be trusted withal as being of greater price. See on chap. 21. 11.

**V. 2. ***{Yea, whereto might the strength of their hands profit me, in whom old age was perished?}* Lest any man should think Job proud, or that out of an inhumane contempt he had slighted or loathed their fathers, here he gives a reason of it, their very old age, which made them more fit to be relieved, than employed by him.

*{Yea}* See on chap. 16. 4. upon the word, *Also*.

*{whereto might the strength of their hands profit me}* Some understand these words of those young ones that derided Job, and interpret the words thus, They were young indeed, and strong and lusty, but so idle, that I could have gotten no good by employing them, but rather loss. Others understand them of their fathers mentioned before, who were now so old, that they had no strength to do Job any service, if he should employ them. For the word, Hand, see on chap. 15. 23.

*{in whom old age was perished}* Some understand this also of the young men. They were so idle and beggarly, that they could not live to old age. Others of the fathers, that they got no experience in their life, but were fools in their old age. Or, rather they lived so long till they were children again, and had neither strength nor wit to do me any service. They had lost the strength of young men, and the wisdom of old men.

**V. 3. ***{For want and famine they were solitary: fleeing into the wilderness in former time desolate and waste.}* The baseness of those men is set out in this verse and the next, by their flying from the company of other men to dwell in wildernesses, and by their poor food.

*{For want}* They had no provisions necessary for their bodies, but wanted all things.

*{and famine}* Want of food: not arising out of scarcity, but out of their own idleness, or God's judgment upon them, in a time, when Job and others had enough.

*{They were solitary}* Not without children, as the word is used, Isa. 49. 21. But flying the company of men being ashamed to live among them for want of apparel and other necessaries, lest they should be derided. This agrees with what follows, and argues extreme poverty. For the word, see on chap. 15. 34. Or, they were dark as the night, as the word seems to import, chap. 3. 7.

*{fleeing into the wilderness}* Seeking waste places to dwell in as hated of all men, verse 5. and counted unworthy to live among them. Or, of their own accord for shame or fear avoiding the sight of other men. The word signifies a dry place, where no man would dwell for want of water, that could dwell elsewhere. Broughton translates it, *The unwatery land.* So it is expounded, Psal. 63. 1.

*{in former time}* Heb. *yesternight.* As it is translated, Gen. 19. 34. Or *yesterday*, as 2 King. 9. 26. That is, lately. If they be rich, or in any better condition now, it is not long since they were thus beggarly, or since the places were laid waste.

*{desolate}* Having no inhabitants there, and fitter for beasts to dwell in, than for men, chap. 38. 26.

*{and waste}* Like a land utterly ruined by the enemy that has no provision left in it, Zeph. 1. 15. This word, and the former come both from one root in the original, and signify an utter devastation.

**V. 4. ***{Who cut up mallows by the bushes, and juniper roots for their meat.}* As in the former verse he had set out their beggarly condition by their habitation, so here by their miserable life feeding on bitter and unsavory, yea and unwholesome meats, which no man would eat, if he were not ready to starve for hunger.

*{who cut up}* To wit, to eat, and for their daily food, as follows in the verse. For the word, see on chap. 8. 12.

*{mallows}* Salt herbs of an unsavory taste growing in salt places and barren, chap. 39. 6. Jer. 17. 6. for it comes from a word, that signifies salt in the original. A food, that poor people were forced to eat having not means to provide better.

*{by the bushes}* Or, *by the shrubs.* It seems they use to grow in such desert places.

*{and juniper roots}* For Root, see on chap. 5. 3. & 14. 8. Rithmah, Num. 33. 18. had its name from רתם a juniper. It seems many grew there. So we read of the city of palm trees, Deuteron. 34. 3.

*{for their meat}* They were forced to make meat of hard roots of juniper in those desert places. Others read it, *to warm them.* As the same word is used, Isa. 47. 14. And it is manifest that coals of juniper give a great heat, Psal. 120. 4. But what need they such firing living in woods, and having plenty of the trees themselves to burn? For the word, see on chap. 15. 23, upon the word, *Bread*.

**V. 5. ***{They were driven forth from among men, (they cried after them, as after a thief.)}* Here is laid down the reason, why these people fled into the wilderness. Either they were banished for their misdeeds; or ashamed to tarry there because of the general clamor of men against them.

*{They were driven forth from among men}* Heb. *From the midst.* To wit, of the city or of men gathered together in it. They could not abide there with any safety. They were beggarly or unfaithful, unfit to live among other men, that were laborious. See on verse 4. For the word, see on chap. 20. 25. upon the word, *Body*.

*{they cried after them, as after a thief}* Thieves are hateful to all men, and hooted and shouted at in all places. So were these base people: which made him fly from the company of men.

**V. 6. ***{To dwell in the clifts of the valleys, in caves of the earth, and of the rocks.}* He had before set out their coarse fare, now in this and the next he sets out their coarse lodging, and sorry abiding or dwelling, proportionable to their fare.

*{To dwell}* This depends upon the beginning of the former verse. They were drawn out from the company of men, and so forced to dwell in solitary places.

*{in the clifts of the valleys}* Or, *of the rivers.* They durst not abide in towns or cities, but sought out cities to hide themselves in, which either were made by earthquakes, or by inundation of water in rocks, or low valleys. The word clift, comes from a word that signifies, to break. See on chap. 13. 25. And it may import holes made by floods or winds, or their own labor in digging. For the word, Valleys, see on chap. 20. 17. on the word, *Rivers*.

*{in caves of the earth}* Heb. *in holes of dust.* As if they had been made by digging. So חורים is translated holes, Isa. 42. 22. And networks, Isa. 19. 9. because they are full of holes. And baskets made like network, Gen. 40. 16. And dung or excrements, which go out of the draught, 2 King. 18. 27. For the word, Earth, or Dust, see on chap. 14. 8. on the word, *Ground*.

*{and of the rocks}* Some rocks are hollow, and might afford shelter for such miserable creatures, chap. 24. 8.

**V. 7. ***{Among the bushes they brayed, under the nettles they were gathered together.}* Here he shows what great wants, and inconveniences these contemptible people endured here in their cold lodgings.

*{among the bushes}* See on verse 4.

*{they brayed}* They made a noise like a wild ass for hunger, cold, or other inconveniences: for the wild ass does not bray when he is full, and nothing troubles him, chapter 6. 5. & 24. 12.

*{under the nettles they were gathered together}* They were all in the same miserable condition, and had no better place to meet in, than where nettles, and stinking weeds grew. So the word is used for Abiding, 1 Sam. 26. 19. Or, *They were all joined to the nettles seeking shelter there*. So it is Putting in, 1 Sam. 2. 36. Others read it, *They were scabbed or stung*. To wit, with nettles, which made them roar so, in the beginning of the verse.

**V. 8. ***{They were children of fools, yea children of base men, they were viler then the earth.}* They come of contemptible parents, men of no note for any knowledge or goodness, but more abject, than the very earth upon which they tread.

*{They were children}* See on chap. 14. 11.

*{of fools}* Of men that had no understanding in them. Hence Nabal had his name. 1 Sam. 25. 25.

*{yea}* See on chap. 19. 4. on the word *Also*.

*{children of base men}* Heb. *of men without name.* That is, of obscure and mean men; for such men's names use not to be taken notice of, whereas names of men famous for high birth and parts are noted while they they live, and chronicled when they die. So Gen. 6. 4. men of name in the original, are called men of renown in the translation.

*{They were viler then the earth}* The earth is low in place, and vile in men's account, as trodden upon by the foot of every beast. This shows what a base account men had formerly of those that now stand up to deride Job. For the earth see on chap. 9. 24. Others read it, *They were smitten out of the earth.* That is, beaten out of it, and and driven out of the land by violence, see on verse 5.

**V. 9. ***{And now I am their song, yea I am their by-word.}* How vile they were that derided him, Job had showed at large in the former verses; now he shows how basely they used him. If they make themselves merry by art, they make songs of my miseries: if they talk pleasantly, they make sport of them. A thing too common in the world for mean men to make sport of great men's ruin.

*{And now}* In this time of my misery. They durst not carry themselves so to me in my prosperity.

*{I am their song}* I am the matter of their song. They make themselves music with my miseries. See the like complaint, Psal. 69. 12. Lam. 3. 14, 63. Ezek. 33. 32. The word properly signifies playing on a stringed instrument. It seems to vex Job the more in his misery, they not only made songs of him, but also played them upon instruments.

*{yea I am their by-word}* Heb. *I am to them for a word.* That is, I am common in their mouths, they talk of little else, but of my fall. They speak of it with disgrace, and make of me a contemptuous proverb, chap. 17. 6.

**V. 10. ***{They abhor me, they flee far from me, and spare not to spit in my face.}* He now complains that he was unto these mockers, as a loathsome thing, which they could not endure to look upon, but thought worthy of the greatest contempt and despite.

*{They abhor me}* See on chap. 9. 16. & 19. 19.

*{They flee far from me}* They will not come nigh me, my sores are so loathsome. See on chap. 21. 16.

*{and spare not to spit in my face}* Heb. *and withhold not spittle from my face.* This was a sign of the greatest disgrace that could be put upon a man, Numb. 12. 14. Isa. 50. 6. Deut. 25. 9. Some think it is meant figuratively, that they did greatly contemn Job. But why might not these scoffers actually spit in Job's face, as well as the Jews in Christ's? Mat. 26. 67. This must needs be very bitter to Job, that he who was wont to be honored by princes, should now be contemned by beggars. For Face see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*.

**V. 11. ***{Because he hath loosed my cord, and afflicted me, they have also let loose the bridle before me.}* Job here looks higher than at those who despise him, and sets down the cause why these young beggarly persons contemned him, to wit, God's afflicting him, and so taking away that awe, and reverence they had of him before. Which they perceiving, take a licentious boldness to disgrace him in the vilest manner, and run riot, and are carried against him without all reason, God having loosed the bands of his authority over them.

*{because he hath loosed my cord}* Because God has done it. God is often understood in this book of Job, taking it for granted, that his friends knew whom he meant, so verse 19. By loosing the cord, is meant, taking away that reverence they formerly had to him, which bound them to respect him. See on chap. 12. 18. Others understand of taking away his riches, whereby he kept others in awe; and could shoot arrows of displeasure against them, if they offended him, chap. 29. verse 20. But now his string was loosed, and though they abused him, he could not shoot at them. For the word Loosed, see on chap. 12. 18. and on chap. 29. 19. on the word *Spread*.

*{and afflicted me}* He shows how God had made him contemptible, to wit, by laying more afflictions on him, than on other men, by reason whereof they thought, God hated him, and they might contemn him. For the various significations of the word see on chapter 16. 1.

*{they have also}* Or, *therefore they have.* For the cause of their contempt is here set down. So the particle is translated, chap. 35. 16. They, that is, those, who scoffed at Job.

*{let loose}* Or, *cast out.* To wit, the bridle out of their mouths. For the word see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Sendest him away*.

*{the bridle}* That authority whereby I kept them in awe, as a horse is kept in by a bridle, Psal. 32. 9. Others understand it of the bridle of their mouths or tongues, as Psal. 39. 1. & 141. 3. So that they now durst speak any evil of me, or to me, who before being kept in by my authority, as with a bridle durst not do it.

*{before me}* Heb. *from my face.* They did deride me to my face, so that I could not see any authority I had over them, or any respect they had to me. For the word Face see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word *Countenance*.

**V. 12. ***{Upon my right hand rise the youth, they push away my feet, and they raise up against me the ways of their destruction.}* The reins being now let loose he shows how these people carried themselves toward him. Those youths that hid themselves before, and could not endure the gravity of my countenance, now come boldly into my presence, offer violence to me, and lay siege to me as enemies to a city.

*{Upon my right hand}* Where I used to have most strength. They see, that I have no strength in me left to resist them, and therefore insult over me. For the word see on chap. 23. 9.

*{rise}* Up against me to scoff at me, and abuse me. For the word see on chap. 14. 12.

*{the youth}* Heb. *the blossome.* Children in youth are like blossoms in the trees. Hence also the young birds in the nest have their name in the original. He intimates that not only young men did abuse him, as verse 1. but also boys. Broughton fitly calls them Springgalls.

*{they push away my feet}* As wrestlers use to trip up men's heels to lay them on the ground, so they seek to ruin me. Or, It may be meant literally of affronts offered to his sore and weak body. For the word see on chap. 14. 20. upon Sendest him away.

*{and they raise up against me the ways of their destruction}* As enemies that besiege a city, raise banks and heaps before it, that they may scale the walls and enter to destroy it, so do they seek all means of my disgrace, and ruin, see on chap. 19. 12. For destruction, see on chap. 18. 12. Broughton reads the end of the verse thus, *and they cast upon me the causes of their woe.* That is, they do me abundance of mischief, or, They think and say that all their miseries were brought upon them by my means, and now they will be revenged on me.

**V. 13. ***{They mar my path, they set forward my calamity; they have no helper.}* Here he shows how they did altogether hinder him of all ways of comforts and escaping, and prospered in all their evil courses against him, being strong enough of themselves to do him mischief.

*{They mar my path}* They disturb all ways and courses that I can think of either to get any comfort or delivery. They have cut off all way of succor from me. I know not what to speak, nor what to do with any freedom. For Path, see on chap. 18. 10. upon the word *Way*.

*{they set forward my calamity}* I am miserable enough in regard of my losses and sores: but they make me more miserable by their taunts, and unkind usage of me. Or, All their plots against me prevail to increase my miseries, whereas none of mine can prevail to ease me. Or, They count it profitable to them to vex me, so great is their malice against me.

*{they have no helper}* They are grown so immodest, that though no man of any fashion give them countenance, yet they vex me. Or, Such as have none to help them in their wants, yet scoff at me in my miseries. Or, they need no man to egg them on to abuse me, they are forward enough to do it of themselves.

**V. 14. ***{They came upon me as a wide breaking in of waters: in the desolation they rolled themselves upon me.}* Some conceive that Job goes on with the similitude of an army besieging a city, who when they have made a great breach go in, in abundance, and lay all desolate. Others take it for a similitude taken from the sea, or some great river, which having found a breach in the banks overruns the country, and drowns all that comes in the way.

*{The came upon me}* They set upon me all together with great violence.

*{as a wide breaking in of waters}* As the water when it has made a great breach runs in amain, so as no man can stop it. Hence Pharez had his name in the original, Gen. 38. 29.

*{in the desolation}* Heb. *under the desolation.* Or, *instead of desolation.* God having broken the banks of my health, and estate, they take occasion thereby to abuse me, and to ruin me. See verse 3.

*{they rolled themselves upon me}* They took full liberty to abuse me and spoil me as they pleased. And I had no more power to resist them, than men have to resist a flood, or an army breaking in upon them. A man that rolls himself over the grass, presses it all down. So did they labor wholly to suppress Job. Gen. 43. 18. Psal. 37. 5. in the margin.

**V. 15. ***{Terrors are turned upon me: they pursue my soul as the wind, and my welfare passeth away as a cloud.}* The frights and terrors that those adversaries brought upon him, and the ruin of his former prosperity he sets out by two similitudes. The one from a violent wind, that blows down all before it; the other from a cloud that vanishes quite away, and affords no rain at all to refresh the earth.

*{Terrors are turned upon me}* Heb. *is turned.* That is, every kind of terror is come upon me. I want nothing to affright me; for heaven and earth seemed to conspire against him to ruin him, chap. 1. & 15. 21. & 18. 11, 14.

*{they pursue}* They follow me closely with all eagerness, as persecutors do good men, and give me no rest.

*{my soule}* Heb. *my principall one.* Or, *my princesse.* Because the soul is the principal part of man, and rules all the parts and members of the body, and is more eminent than the body, as princes are more eminent than common men. So the masculine is translated a Prince, chap. 21. 28. Others read it *my nobility*, or, *my honour*, or *my bounty.* For all these the word will bear. And they mean, that they had deprived him of his credit and wealth, and all means of exercising bounty to others as formerly, chap. 29.

*{as the wind}* Some understand it of the suddenness of Job's misery coming on him, as the winds blow swiftly. But is better understood of the violent and irresistible manner of Job's troubles seizing upon him, as the wind blows down all that is in the way. For the word Wind, see on chap. 15. 2. upon the word *Vain knowledge*. Terrors made his soul unfit to do her office, and endangered his life by reason of those terrible things which befell him.

*{and my welfare}* My health, wealth, and safety.

*{passeth away}* Is clean gone. See on chap. 15. 19.

*{as a cloud}* Not as swiftly as a cloud, as some would have it, but totally as a cloud vanishing quite away, and affording no rain at all. Job was exalted in wealth and honor, but no more footsteps of it appearing now, than of a cloud that is vanished, chap. 7. 9. Hos. 6. 4. & 13. 3.

**V. 16. ***{And now my soul is poured out upon me, the days of affliction have taken hold upon me.}* In this verse, Job sets out the troubles of his mind. In the 17. verse, the pain of his body, and in the 18. the defiling of his garments. As his mind was troubled in the daytime, so his body was pained in the night season, and his clothes made filthy and troublesome to him. He might have better borne the reproaches of others, if his own soul and body had not been troubled, but this addition made his burden exceeding heavy.

*{And now}* See on verse 1.

*{my soul}* Some understand it of the soul itself: but that was yet in his body. It is therefore better interpreted of the courage and strength of his soul, which was much weakened by his afflictions. For the word, see on chap. 14. 22.

*{is poured out}* The word in Scripture sometimes imports plenty, as Joel 2. 28. But here it imports emptying, for when the wine is poured out, the vessel is empty. So when man's courage, and strength is gone, he has an empty soul. Psal. 42. 4. His soul did as it were melt away for grief.

*{upon me}* Or, *within me.* That is, within my body. Men cannot see how much I am weakened by my troubles, and other men's scoffs: but I feel it. So it is translated, *In me*. Psal. 42. 5.

*{the days}* See on chap. 14. 14.

*{of affliction}* Of trouble or calamity.

*{have taken hold upon me}* Whatsoever strength was left within me, is decayed, and gone out of my soul, so that nothing is left within me to sustain me in my troubles. And withal, affliction has laid so fast hold on me, and continued so many days, to keep me under, that I can by no means get rid of it.

**V. 17. ***{My bones are pierced in me in the night season, and my sinews take no rest.}* Not only my soul is troubled within me, but also my body is so full of pain, that in the nighttime, when other miserable men take rest, and all creatures are wont to be at quiet, then the marrow of my bones rages through intolerable pains, as if one had pierced them through with a dart, and my heart is so overbeared with grief, that my pulses beat all night long so strongly, that they keep me from all rest.
