*{my bones}* So great is my trouble, that it not only has seized upon all my flesh, but also entered within my bones. These uphold the weight of the body, and are the firmest part of it. Every one of them is pierced. For in the original it is a noun plural with a verb singular. For the word see on chap. 20. 11.

*{are pierced}* This phrase in Scripture language intimates a great grief, and such a pain as penetrates through the most inward and solid parts of the body. His pain pierced his flesh, and went into the very bones. Some read it, *he hath pierced my bones.* Meaning God, who had laid this sore affliction upon him, and is oftener mentioned in this book, than named. Others thus, *the night pierceth my bones.* I feel more pain in the night, than in the day, as most sick people do.

*{in me}* Heb. *from above me.* As attributing all his pains to God, who had afflicted him from heaven. Or, *from within me.* By reason of those sores and parts I feel in my body.

*{in the night season}* He aggravates his sorrows from the circumstances of time, intimating that he had no rest at all day nor night, as chap. 7. 3, 14. & 17. 12.

*{and my sinews}* Or, *my pulses*.

*{take no rest}* Heb. *lie not down.* Or, *sleep not*, That is to say, my pulses, which use to move more slowly in the nighttime, when the heat is drawn into the internal parts, now by reason of inflammation beat as fast in the day, and will not let me sleep. For the word, see on chap. 14. 12.

**V. 18. ***{By the great force of my disease is my garment changed: it bindeth me about as the collar of my coat.}* He that before wore gorgeous apparel as an ensign of magistracy, now has so much filth, and corruption running out of his sores, that it fouls and pollutes all his garments, and makes them noisome to others, and hurtful to himself.

*{By the great force of my disease}* My disease is so strong, and has so far prevailed against me, that it has spoiled my apparel. Others understand it of God's great force, for those words, Of my disease, are not in the original. But the first reading is best, because the immediate cause of polluting his garments, was the filth that ran from his sores. For Great see on chap. 22. 25.

*{is my garment changed}* The color of it is changed, and it is made filthy. My garment has as it were put on another garment. Others understand it not of change of the color of his garment, but of often putting on other clothes, as they that have running sores, use to do. But the former sense is better, for it is not likely that Job had either such store of clothes, or good attendance.

*{it bindeth me about}* It compasses me round about, and sticks close unto me. Others read it, *He binds me about*, that is, God compasses me about with sores.

*{as the collar of my coat}* It wrings me as an uneasy collar of a coat wrings a man's neck. They used in those days to wear coats open only at top and bottom. And such a one full of and stiff with filthy matter must needs hurt him, that puts it on, or pulls it off. Broughton reads it elegantly, *at the edge of my coat it girds me.* For Collar, see on chap. 15. 5. on the word, *Mouth*.

**V. 19. ***{He hath cast me into the mire, and I am become like dust and ashes.}* I lie wallowing and tumbling in this most loathsome dirt: and being covered all over with the scales, and scrapings, that fall from my scabs, I am become more like unto the unprofitable dust and ashes than unto a man.

*{He hath cast me into the mire}* He has caused me to be polluted all over by my sores. Or, he has put the greatest disgrace upon me that can be. For what greater disgrace, than to throw a man into the kennel? For the word Cast, see on chap. 6. 24. & 8. 10. For Mire see on chap. 13. 12. upon the word *Clay*.

*{and I am become like dust and ashes}* Foul with the filth of my sores, or contemptible like dust and ashes, which every foot treads on. By these two words, Abraham sets out his own vileness. Gen. 18. 27. For Dust see on chap. 14. 8. on the word *Ground*.

**V. 20. ***{I cry unto thee, and thou dost not hear me: I stand up, and thou regardest me not.}* Here Job expresses that, which troubled him more than his sores or contempt, to wit, that God would not hear his prayers, nor look with favor upon his person.

*{I cry}* I pray earnestly, as a man ready to be drowned, cries aloud for help. For the word see on chap. 19. 7.

*{unto thee}* To you O God, to whom men use to pray in their adversity. Before he spoke of God to his friends, now he speaks to God before them.

*{and thou dost not hear me.}* Or, *but thou dost not hear me.* For he opposes his speech to God's silence. So that particle is translated, verse 1. Hearing is here put for answering, see on chap. 16. 1. upon the word, *Answered*. See the like complaint, Psal. 22. 2.

*{I stand up}* To pray unto you. For standing was a usual gesture in prayer. Jer. 15. 1. This argues constancy in prayer, as the former word did vehemency: and waiting upon God for an answer.

*{and thou regardest me not}* You will not give a comfortable answer, or show any countenance to me. Others read it, *and thou markest me.* You do as it were mark me out, not to help me, but to punish me more grievously, as if you did delight in my misery. This suits well with the words following, as the former with the words foregoing.

**V. 21. ***{Thou art become cruel to me: with thy strong hand thou opposest thyself against me.}* Job here in his impatience speaks irreverently of God, accusing him, that contrary to his nature, and former course of gentle proceeding with him in his prosperity, now he was grown so harsh to him, as that he thought no misery too great for him, that his hand could lay upon him.

*{thou art become}* Hebr. *thou art changed*; or, *turned*, as Isa. 63. 10.

*{cruel to me}* See the like like complaint, Psal. 77. 9. Jerem. 30. 14.

*{with thy strong hand thou opposest thyself against me}* You do like a capital enemy practice all your might upon me to afflict me. Heb. *with the strength of thy hand.* For Hand, see on chap. 15. 23. Thus Job often complains of God, as of an enemy. As chap. 13. 24. & 16. 9.

**V. 22. ***{Thou liftest me up to the wind: thou causest me to ride upon it, and dissolvest my substance.}* Some understand this verse thus, You give affliction such power over me, that like a wind it may take me up, and blow me clean away; others thus, You did lift me up in my former prosperity, and made me to ride on prosperously for a long time, and at length did ruin all my wealth. Which way soever it be, it is set out as an effect of that cruelty, wherewith he charged God in the former verse.

*{Thou liftest me up}* See on chapter 21. 3. upon the word, *Suffer*.

*{to the wind}* See on chap. 15. 2. upon the words, *Vain knowledge*. You do so hurry me with one affliction after another, that I can find no rest.

*{thou causest me to ride upon it}* You do as it were lock me into the saddle. You give the wind such power over me, that I cannot get off the wings of it, till it break my neck: as in the next verse. And chap. 27. 20, 21.

*{and dissolvest my substance}* You cause my body to melt away like wax by the heat of my affliction. For the word, Substance, see on chap. 26. 3. upon the words, *The thing as it is*. Some read it, *and dissolvest my wisdom.* You bring me to my wit's end, like a mariner in a grievous storm, so that I see no way to escape, Psal. 107. 27.

**V. 23. ***{For I know that thou wilt bring me to death, and to the house appointed for all living.}* I see now and perceive by your rough dealing, that you are resolved to bring me to the grave: and no wonder (though I come to it in a sadder way, than many others) for it is that which all must come to in the end one way or another.

*{For}* Here he shows what issue he looks for from these calamities. It appears by your giving me over to this tempest of afflictions, and giving me no ease, that you purpose by them to end my life, for no man can long endure such a condition and live. Some read it *Surely*, and so make it the beginning of a new sentence having no dependence on the former words, but ushering in what follows. So כי is translated, chap. 28. 1.

*{I know}* I am fully so persuaded, See on chap. 19. 25.

*{that thou wilt bring me}* Heb. *that thou wilt make me return.* To dust, from whence man was taken at the first, Genes. 3. 19. Or, *that thou wilt turne me.* You will convert this flesh into dust, Psal. 90. 3. You will put me into a lifeless condition.

*{to death}* To a bodily death by these afflictions.

*{and to the house}* See on chap. 15. 28.

*{appointed}* By God, for, a place for all men to meet in, when they die, and to abide in, as in a house, till the resurrection, chap. 17. 13. That commonly is the grave. The Hebrew word is מועד is used 1. for a set time to do any business, as for the solemn festival times in the Old Testament, Lam. 1. 4. 2. for a place appointed for a public or common meeting. Isa. 14. 13. 3. for the act of meeting in such an appointed place, Exod. 33. 7. Here it is taken in the second sense for a place of meeting.

*{for all living}* For all men, which are the best living creatures on earth. For he speaks not here of beasts, Heb. 9. 27. See on chap. 28. 13.

**V. 24. ***{Howbeit he will not stretch out his hand to the grave, though they cry in his destruction.}* The interpreters seem to understand these words as if Job did thus comfort himself, Although I thus complain, now God is destroying me by these sore afflictions, and make no question, but that by them he will end my days, yet this comfort I have, that my sorrow will end with my life, for God uses not to afflict dead bodies in the grave. Others give this sense of the words, though I cry never so much to him, yet he will not stretch out his hand to keep me from the grave, but will certainly destroy me. Both these interpretations make the words depend on the former. But Broughton carries them on to the following words to set out Job's compassion to others in misery, that others might now pity him. He reads the words thus. *And prayed I not when plague was sent? when hurt came to any, thereupon cryed I not.*

*{Howbeit he will not stretch out his hand}* For Stretching, see on chap. 14. 20. upon the word, Sendest him away: for Hand, see on chap. 15. 23.

*{to the grave}* Heb. *to the heap.* For graves use to be covered with a heap of earth, that it may be known where people are buried. By the grave is understood the persons in the grave. God afflicts living bodies, but not dead, chap. 3. 12, 13.

*{though they cry}* Though sick and dying men cry out for pain. For the word, see on chap. 19. 7.

*{in his destruction}* When God destroys them. Such as die with great pain, complain not in the grave. Some read the end of the verse thus, *is there any cry in his destruction?* Do men complain of any pain in the grave, after that God has taken away their lives? never any yet did.

**V. 25. ***{Did not I weep for him that was in trouble? was not my soul grieved for the poor?}* Here begins the sad conclusion of this chapter, wherein Job shows, 1. what pity he showed to men in misery; in this verse, 2. that though he might hope therefore to escape trouble, yet he was deceived, verse 26. 3. the sad effects of this unexpected calamity: to the end of the chapter. In this verse Job seems to complain in this wise. If I had been hardhearted to others their miseries, it had been no wonder, that neither God nor man pity me now in mine. But God knows that I expressed my compassion to others by weeping for them, that suffered hard things, and that without hypocrisy, for I inwardly lamented the wants of other men.

*{Did not I weep}* Certainly I did. Or in manner of an oath, If I did not do so, let God punish me. See on chap. 1. 11.

*{for him that was in trouble}* Heb. *for him that was hard of day.* That saw many troublesome days. Such affections of sympathizing with others in their misery becomes good men, Psal. 35. 13, 14. Rom. 12. 15.

*{was not my soul}* I was heartily grieved for them, and did not dissemble in my tears. For the word see on chap. 14. 22.

*{grieved for the poor}* Though I were rich, others' wants went to my heart, yet no man pities me now. For Poor see on chap. 24. 4. on the word, *Needy*.

**V. 26. ***{When I looked for good, then evil came unto me: and when I waited for light, there came darkness.}* Having carried myself so tenderly towards men in miseries, I expected according to God's usual proceedings to have enjoyed happy, and sunshiny days: but evil and cloudy ones have overtaken me.

*{When I looked for}* When I hoped for, and expected good days.

*{good}* See on chap. 21. 13. upon the word, *Wealth*.

*{then evil came unto me}* He means no evil of sin, but of sorrow and trouble. For, came, see on chap. 1. 19.

*{and when I waited for light}* When I attended for the continuance of my comfortable condition. For, Light, see on chap. 24. 13. 16. & 18. 5.

*{then came darkness}* Then contrary to my expectation came abundance of trouble upon me.

**V. 27. ***{My bowels boiled, and rested not: the days of affliction prevented me.}* My bowels being inflamed do make a great noise: and this most bitter time stole upon me suddenly, before I had so much as once thought of it.

*{My bowels}* It signifies the inward parts, for as he was clad all over with sores without, so was he all on fire within. For the word, see on chap. 20. 14.

*{boiled}* Worked up, and grew hot within me like a seething pot over a great fire. So strong was his disease upon him.

*{and rested not}* Heb. *and were not silent.* For trouble and pain is compared to a noise; and freedom from it, and allaying of it to silence, Psal. 107. 29. Jon. 1. 11, 12.

*{the days of affliction}* Times of trouble. For, Days, see on chap. 14. 1.

*{prevented me}* Came suddenly and unexpectedly upon me. I thought my prosperity would have continued still, but it proved far otherwise. Others read it, *met me.* That is, came upon me unawares, and took me unprovided. So the word is translated, Deut. 23. 4. Neh. 13. 2.

**V. 28. ***{I went mourning without the sun: I stood up, and I cried in the congregation.}* I showed forth all manner of signs of a man in a sad and mournful condition, my skin is become black, yet not through the heat of the sun: yea of such force is this my affliction, that it drives me even openly in the great meetings, and assemblies of the people, when any such come about me, to utter sorrowful voices not intelligible. This my pain forced from me.

*{I went}* I continued in such a posture, as a man goes on in a journey. For the word see on chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Passeth*.

*{mourning}* Some understand it of inward sorrow of heart, others of outward expressions of sorrow in black apparel. See on chap. 5. 11. Psal. 35. 13. & 38. 6. & 42. 9. & 43. 2.

*{without the sun}* Some read it, *out of the sun.* I was so sad, that I took no pleasure to walk where the sun shined, but sought dark melancholy places, in which I might hide myself and vent my sorrows. Others take בלא חמה for לא בחמה. As chap. 16. 17, על לא חמס for לא על חמס *not for the sun.* Or, *not by the sun.* My great sorrows came not from an external, but from an internal principle. I was not sunburnt, but heart-burnt by reason of my losses, sores, and strong pains. The sun has three names in Hebrew, חמה here to set out the heat of it, Psal. 19. 6. 2. חרס to intimate the drought, which the sunshine brings upon the earth. 3. שמש To show that it is the public servant of God and of the world, as setting out God's glory in the brightness of it, and giving light to the world.

*{I stood up}* It may be he could not sit, or lie down for pain. Or, He had sudden fits of pain coming on him, that made him rise up and roar. The word signifies both rising up and standing. See on chap. 14. 12.

*{and I cried in the congregation}* It is not likely that Job came into the congregation in public in this sad condition, as formerly, because he had not that respect, nor ability of body, which he had before. But it is very likely that many came to see Job thus strangely altered, and that he thought it did not become a man of his gravity, who had spoken so temperately in the public congregation now to roar out before so many, but to bite in his grief, till they were gone: yet so great was his pain, that he could not abstain from shrieking out in their presence. For the word Cried, see on chapter 19. 7.

**V. 29. ***{I am a brother to dragons, and a companion to owls.}* Some make this verse an exposition of the beginning of former. Job sought solitary and shady places to be alone, and desired to shun company, that he might mourn in secret, as dragons and owls do in desert places. Others make it an exposition of the latter part of that verse. As dragons and owls make a hideous and not intelligible noise, when anything troubles them, so did Job cry out more like a beast, than a man in this his pain and misery, as if it were so great, that it were inexpressible in words. See the like similitudes, Psal. 102. 6, 7. Isa. 38. 14. Mich. 1. 8.

*{I am a brother}* I am like them. I sit alone, as they do, and howl as they do. So this word is used to set out a likeness, Gen. 49. 5. Prov. 18. 9. For the word, see on chap. 19. 13.

*{to dragons}* For the word, see on chap. 7. 12. upon the word, *Whale*.

*{and a companion}* I do as they do, Prov. 13. 20. & 28. 7. 24.

*{to owls}* Heb. *to the daughters of the owle.* Some understand it of young owls, that make a great noise, when the old ones are gone from them. But it may well be understood of owls in general, as the sons of men are put for men, and the daughters of Zion for the inhabitants thereof. Some read it, *ostriches*.

**V. 30. ***{My skin is black upon me, and my bones are burnt with heat.}* His forementioned pain, and sorrowful complaints had so wrought upon him, that not only his skin was deformed without, but also his bones dried up within for want of moisture.

*{My skin is black upon me}* So strong is my pain, and my sores send out so much filth, that my skin gathers together in black swellings, Psal. 119. 83. Lam. 4. 8. & 5. 10.

*{and my bones are burnt with heat}* They are withered, and all moisture gone out of them, as out of things burnt with the fire; Psal. 32. 3, 4. & 102. 3. Ezek. 24. 10. For, Bones, see on chap. 20. 11.

**V. 31. ***{My harp also is turned to mourning, and my organ into the voice of them that weep.}* Here is the most sad conclusion of the whole chapter, wherein Job sets out that great change of his estate, from high prosperity to deep misery by a neglect or contrary use of those musical instruments, which formerly he used to express or increase his joy, but now were either neglected, or used to play sad and doleful tunes to stir up or increase sorrow. So that now he minded nothing but heaviness. It is not likely that Job did use any music now, but rather give it quite over to spend his time in sorrow. As the Jews did in Babylon. Psal. 137. 2.

*{My harp also}* See on chap. 21. 12.

*{is turned to mourning}* Heb. *is for mourning.* Whereas before I used to solace myself with playing on the harp, now I weep altogether. The word signifies sadness of heart for some loss or cross showed forth by the countenance, apparel, voice or tears.

*{and my organ}* See on chap. 21. 12.

*{into the voice}* See on chap. 15. 21. upon the word *Sound*.

*{of them that weep}* My mirth is turned all into lamentation, such as is made by them that weep for dead friends, or under some great judgment, Judg. 2. 4, 5.

## CHAPTER 31

**Verse 1. ***{I made a covenant with mine eyes, why then should I think upon a maid?}* In this chap. Job sets out the uprightness of his former life in the time of his prosperity, that he might convince his adversaries, that these extraordinary troubles came not upon him for extraordinary sins, as they said, and that he might draw them off from speaking harshly to him, and move them to pity him for time to come. He instances in many particular duties of God's laws which he was careful to perform. He sets them not down in the same order they are written in the law, but according as they came to his mind, or he had had occasion to practice them. Thus he shows himself to be such a man as he is described, chap. 1. 1. Fearing God and eschewing evil. And 1. he clears himself from fornication, in the four first verses, 2. from injustice in trading, to verse 9. 3. from adultery, to verse 13. 4. from ill dealing with his servants, to verse 16. 5. from unmercifulness to the poor, to verse 21. 6. from wronging orphans, to verse 24. 7. from trusting in riches, to verse 26. 8. from idolatry, to verse 29. 9. from desire of revenge, to verse 32. 10. from denying relief to strangers, verse 22. 11. from hiding his sins, verse 33. 12. from justly provoking others, to verse 38. 13. from detaining other men's lands, or hirelings' wages, to the end of the chapter. Most of these things were objected to him by his friends; so that here he wipes off all the dirt they cast upon him in all their discourses. For the first part. 1. He professes his care to avoid occasions of fornication in this verse. 2. He sets down the reasons of this his care and resolution. 1. loss of good he expected from God, verse 2. 2. fear of punishment, verse 3. 3. God's omniscience who would soon take him tripping, verse 4. For the first in this verse wherein he shows the great care he had to keep himself from fornication by resolving to keep his eyes and heart in order from looking so upon beautiful virgins, that his heart should run after them.

*{I made a covenant}* Heb. *I cut a covenant.* For it was a fashion in making covenants to cut a calf or some other beast in pieces, and to walk between the pieces; intimating, that they desired God so to destroy them, that should break the covenant, Jer. 34. 18. Job did resolve with himself, as firmly, as if he had entered into covenant with his eyes, that they should not let in any object of unclean thoughts into his heart. The word in the original for Covenant comes from a word that signifies to choose, because men choose the parties whom, and the conditions whereupon they enter into covenant.

*{with mine eyes}* All the senses may afford matter of danger to the soul in the case of uncleanness, but especially the eyes beholding beauty, till the soul be set on fire with it, Matth. 5. 28. 2 Pet. 2. 14. Therefore Job chains them especially.

*{why then}* Why should I break this covenant? what good should I get by it? Sure nothing but loss. As verse 2. &c. It is translated *And why*, chap. 7. 21. Or, *How then.* How is it lawful for me to break this covenant?

*{should I think}* Entertain wanton and lascivious thoughts, considering of her beauty, till my heart lust after her, Matt. 5. 28.

*{upon a maid}* A virgin. Not that he gave liberty to his thoughts to lust after married wives, for that he disclaims also, verse 9. but he thus speaks, because maidens in their youth are most beautiful, and therefore the minds of wanton men are carried most after them, and as women grow in years, so their beauty decays. And sure if Job were so careful to shut virgins out of his mind, much more to exclude wives.

**V. 2. ***{For what portion of God is there from above? and what inheritance of the Almighty from on high?}* Some understand these words, as if Job should have said thus, I did forbear all uncleanness, and yet you see that God has laid as heavy judgments upon me, as if I were an adulterer. Others thus, by way of question, What could I have looked for from God, if I had been a fornicator? And they pick the answer out of the next verse. Nothing but destruction and strange punishment. And these take the words, Portion, and Inheritance, catachrestically for evil befalling him, as it is used Chap. 20. 29. whereas ordinarily they signify good things that befall us. But the right is, What good could I have expected from God, if I had been such a person? Nay might I not rather have expected much evil from him? Verse 3.

*{For}* This particle ordinarily signifies *And.* But here it infers a reason, and therefore is well translated, *For,* as also Chap. 11. 4. As if Job had said, I considered with myself, that I could never look for any good from God here or hereafter, if I should have lusted after virgins.

*{what portion}* None at all, that good is. For *Portion,* see on Chapter 20. 29.

*{of God}* See on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5.

*{is there from above}* From God, whose throne is on high in heaven: as follows in this verse. Others might think all well, if they could escape the eyes of men? But Job takes notice of God's majesty, whose eye was always upon him, and this kept him from sin.

*{and what inheritance}* See on Chap. 20. 29.

*{of the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 1, 5.

*{from on high}* From God in heaven, as before in this verse. See Chap. 16. 19.

**V. 3. ***{Is not destruction to the wicked? and a strange punishment to the workers of iniquity?}* Not only the hopelessness of good, but also the certain expectation of strange punishments kept Job from ways of wantonness.

*{is not destruction}* See on Chap. 18. 12.

*{to the wicked}* To all ungodly men, but especially to unclean persons.

*{and a strange punishment}* Such as God does not usually inflict. Isa. 28. 21. Or as men use to inflict upon those whom they count strangers. Obad. verse 12. Or, such as they were not used to, nor did not expect, and therefore it would be strange to them, when it came. Every way it imports extraordinary punishments to be inflicted upon fornicators by God. Others read it, *and alienation.* To wit, from God; he counts them strangers, and bears no good affection to them. Or, from their country and habitation. They shall be banished.

*{to the workers}* To those that make a trade of sin.

*{of iniquity}* Of any sin, but especially of wantonness. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 35. upon the word *Vanity.*

**V. 4. ***{Doth not he see my ways, and count all my steps?}* This verse agrees well with what went before. Wicked adulterers think themselves safe in their dark places, where no man can see their abominations. But Job took notice of God's all-seeing eye, and therefore durst nowhere commit any such folly.

*{doth not he see}* Sure he does. Or, *would he not see?* If I were such a one, would not God take of it, and punish me severely for it? Doubtless he would. For the word *See,* see on Chap. 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold.*

*{my ways}* For the word, see on Chap. 16. 22. & 17. 9. For God seeing our ways, see 2 Chron. 16. 9. Job 34. 21. Prov. 5. 21. & 15. 3. Jer. 32. 19.

*{and count all my steps}* See on Chap. 14. 16.

**V. 5. ***{If I have walked with vanity, or if my foot hath hasted to deceit}* This verse the translators join to that which follows, and it seems to be a contradiction. For if Job did walk deceitfully, how could God find his integrity? For the salving whereof some join this verse to the former, and read it thus, *would not he see my ways, and count all my my steps, if I had walked with vanity, or if my foot had hasted to deceit?* That is, I did not so walk, because I knew God would take notice of it and punish me for it. Others make it an absolute sentence without dependence on what goes before, or follows: but they conceive it to be defective, and supply it thus, *If I have walked with vanity, etc.* then let God punish me for it. A usual form of imprecation. See on Chap. 1. 11. Others make it both absolute and perfect, but read it by way of question, *Have I walked with vanity, or hath my foot hasted to deceit?* That is, surely I have not done so. And this is the best reading.

*{If I have walked}* Or rather *have I walked*? So אם is translated by way of question Chap. 6. 30. & 8. 3. & 17. 2. & 27. 11. & 30. 25. Walking argues a course of life. For the word see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Passes.*

*{with vanity}* That is, vainly, unprofitably, so as I have not been profitable to others. See on Chap. 15. 31.

*{or if my hope}* If I have gone up and down to deceive men.

*{hath hasted}* As walking intimates practice, and constancy, so hasting argues delight and swiftness in deceiving.

*{to deceit}* To defraud men of their goods, and possessions. It appears that he speaks of getting other men's means fraudulently, by the reddition, verse 8. wherein he wishes the loss of his own, if he had wronged others. See on Chap. 15. 35.

**V. 6. ***{Let me be weighed in an even ballance, that God may know mine integrity}* Let the integrity of my life before, and my heavy sorrows now be weighed in an equal balance, and I doubt not but my sorrows will be found heavier than my miscarriages, and God will pronounce me a godly man, and free me from these sufferings.

*{Let me be weighed}* Heb. *Let him weigh me.* To wit, God, as follows in this verse. A metaphor from merchants, that used then to weigh their moneys, to see that they had their full weight, Jer. 32. 10. And Job doubts not, but his integrity will hold weight in God's balance. See on Chap. 6. 2.

*{in an even ballance}* Heb. *in ballances of justice.* That is, just ones. So that my integrity may be laid in the one balance, and my sufferings in the other, and the beam may be kept even. See on Chap. 6. 2. Just balances are required, Lev. 19. 36. Prov. 16. 17. Unjust blamed, Prov. 11. 1. & 20. 23. Hos. 12. 7. Mic. 6. 11. Amos 8. 5. See on Chap. 8. 3. for justice.

*{that God may know}* That he may make it appear to you, who think me a hypocrite, that I am none; and that by taking away these plagues, which make you judge so hardly of me. Then you would perceive that God takes notice of my integrity. Or, *and God shall know.* I am confident then, that God will right me. For *God,* see on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5. For *know,* see on Chap. 15. 25.

*{mine integrity}* My upright dealing with all men, for he speaks here of commerce with others in matters of gain, as was observed on verse 5. See on Chap. 27. 5.

**V. 7. ***{If my step hath turned out of the way, and mine heart walked after mine eyes, and if any blot hath cleaved to my hands}* Job in this verse intimates unto us what conscience he made of upright dealing with all men, insomuch as he would not take one step out of the way of justice, for any object of gain, though never so great, which his eyes presented to him; neither would he suffer any ill-gotten goods like dirt to cleave to his hands to defile them.

*{If my step hath turned out of the way}* If at any time I have gone out of the just way of trading to get gain unrighteously. For *way,* see on Chap. 17. 9.

*{And mine heart walked after mine eyes}* If I did covet other men's goods when I saw them, Josh. 7. 21. Job speaks not here of looking after beauty for wantonness, for that he had spoken of verse 1. and does again verse 9. But of looking on men's goods to covet them, which is called the lust of the eyes, 1 John 2. 16. For *heart* see on Chap. 15. 12. For *walking* see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word, *passes*. For *eyes* see on Chap. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*.

*{And if any blot}* Anything belonging to another man taken away unjustly from him, which were a sin, and so a blemish to me before God. See Chap. 11. 15. Or, *And if any thing*. To wit, which is none of mine, but unlawfully gotten. *{Hath cleaved to my hands}* If I have kept such unlawful goods from the right owners, and so stand still polluted in God's sight. For *Hand* see on Chapter 29. 9.

**V. 8. ***{Then let me sow, and let another eat, yea let my off-spring be rooted out.}*

A fit recompense for such a wickedness. If I have unjustly taken away other men's goods, then let God give my goods to another, and destroy all the fruit of my labors.

*{Then let me sow, and let another eat}* Let that curse fall upon me, which God in his law threatens to ungodly men, that other men enjoy the fruit of my labors, Lev. 26. 16. Deut. 28. 30. etc. For *Eat* see on Chap. 21. 25.

*{Yea let my off-spring be rooted out}* Some understand it of Job's children, as Chap. 31. 8. & 27. 14. But that agrees not with Job's present condition, who had no children left, nor hope of having, for he still expected death. Others therefore interpret it of trees, which he had planted before, and might yet be left, though his children's houses were blown down, and so the end of the verse suits well with the beginning, and the word rooted out will be literally taken for plucking quite up all that he had planted with so much pains.

**V. 9. ***{If mine heart have been deceived by a woman, or if I have laid wait at my neighbours door.}*

As Job had before cleared himself from fornication and covetousness, so now he clears himself from adultery. 1. Denying that he was guilty of it, in this verse. 2. Wishing like measure to himself, if he were guilty, verse 10. 3. Showing what deterred him from it, fear of human punishment, verse 11. of divine, verse 12. In this verse he shows, 1. That he kept a sure guard over his heart, so that the beauty of his neighbor's wife could not pierce it. 2. That he did not as adulterers use to do, watch for a fit opportunity to obtain his desire of his neighbor's wife.

*{If my heart have been deceived by a woman}* If I have so gazed on my neighbor's wife, that I have been enamored of her, and so my heart has been beguiled of chastity, Prov. 7. 7, 21, etc. For *heart* see on Chap. 15. 12. *Deceived* or *Allured.* For strumpets deceive by allurement; Prov. 7. 13, 14.

*{By a woman}* By her persuasions; or, *Concerning a woman.* That is, in way of adultery by lustful thoughts arising out of my own heart. So על is used for concerning, Eccl. 1. 13. By a woman here is meant the wife of another man, for he had spoken of virgins, verse 1. and his own wife could not deceive him in this kind. For the word see on Chap. 2. 9. & 14. 1.

*{Or if I have laid wait at my neighbours door}* If I have watched when his wife came to the door, that I might allure her to folly, or when her husband goes forth, that I might be let in to abuse his wife. See such waitings of adulterers, Chap. 24. 15, 16. He had not in a secret place watched for an occasion to spoil a neighbor's wife of her chastity. So much the word imports, Ps. 10. 8, 9. Prov. 1. 11.

**V. 10. ***{Then let my wife grind unto another; and let others bow down upon her.}*

A sore imprecation, yet such a one as shows, how God oftentimes punishes men for adultery, by giving over their wives to be strumpets. Let my wife, as I have justly deserved, be subject to the lust of another man. Thus God punished David's adultery, 2 Sam. 12. 11.

*{Then let my wife}* For the word see on Chap. 2. 9. *{Grind unto another.}* A clean expression of an unclean act, with allusion to the two millstones one covering the other, as some conceive. So Lam. 5. 13. where our translation reads it, *They took the young men to grinde.* The vulgar reads it, *They did unchastly abuse the young men.* This sense agrees best with the words before and after; yet some interpret it of slavery: let my wife be a slave to another man, and be employed in the meanest act of slavery, grind his corn for him in his mill, Exod. 11. 5. Matt. 24. 41. Judg. 16. 21. Lam. 5. 13.

*{and let others bow down upon her}* Another modest expression of the unclean act of adultery. So the Scripture often does in things not fit to be named. As covering the feet for sending forth the excrements, Judg. 3. 24. And going in to a woman for the act of adultery, Ps. 51. in the title, and many others. Teaching us to think of gross sinful acts, as of things that should not be named, Eph. 5. 3, 12. much less done.

**V. 11. ***{For this is an heinous crime; yea it is an iniquity to be punished by the Judges.}*

He sets out one cause in this verse, that kept him from adultery. He was loath to come to public shame. And he considered that this was a grievous sin, fit to be openly punished by the civil magistrate.

*{For this is}* Or, *For this were.* It would have been a foul fault in me, if I should have done it.

*{an heinous crime}* The word signifies a plotted sin, such a one as natural corruption does not ordinarily suggest, but men must beat their wits to invent, and execute it. And such sins carry much mischief with them. It is used in Scripture to express horrible sins, as incest, Levit. 18. 17. & 20. 14. Prostitution of one's children to whoredom, Levit. 19. 29. Killing of the Levite's concubine by filthiness, Judg. 20. 6.

*{yea it is an iniquity}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{to be punished by the Judges}* To wit, to take notice of, and punish; for adultery is punished with shame among the heathen, and with death by God's law, Gen. 38. 24. Lev. 20. 10. Deut. 22. 22. This word is translated judge, Exod. 21. 22. Some sins there are, that men's laws take no notice of, but adultery is a sin, that wrongs husbands so much, and the public that it deserves punishment of men.

**V. 12. ***{For it is a fire that consumeth to destruction, and would root out all mine increase}* Magistrates are oftentimes very favorable in matters of adultery, but when they fail, God will by his secret judgment like fire consume adulterers, and all they have.

*{For it is a fire}* Or, *For it would be a fire.* As the end of the verse is translated, I forbore adultery, for I knew, if men did not punish me, God would destroy me for it. Here may be some allusion towards burning lusts, Rom. 1. 27. Or toward the destruction of Sodom for their burning lusts, Gen. 1. 19. Or to the first punishment of whoredom, Gen. 38. 24. Or to that lake of fire, into which all adulterers shall be cast at last, Rev. 21. 8. However God's wrath against adulterers is compared to devouring fire, which burns men, cattle, houses, corn, trees, and leaves the adulterer nothing, Prov. 6. 27. See on Chap. 15. 34. & 20. 26.

*{that consumeth}* That devours all he has, as a man does his meat. See on Chap. 21. 25. upon the word, *Eat.*

*{to destruction}* To the destruction of the adulterer in this world, by some great judgment: this agrees best with the words following, yet some interpret it of hell.

*{and would root out}* See on verse 8.

*{all mine increase}* All my corn, and fruits.

**V. 13. ***{If I did despise the cause of my Man-servant, or of my Maid-servant, when they contended with me}*

He proceeds to show, that as he dealt faithfully with others abroad, so he did not like ungodly masters, domineer over his poor servants at home; but laying aside the person and state of a master for a time, he heard all their complaints with patience, and did them right. *If I did despise;* or *if I had despised.* And so it may well be knit to the verse following, *what then shall I do, etc.* Whereas others make it an oath, with the imprecation understood, as Chap. 1. 11. If I have oppressed my servants, let God plague me for it. The word *despise,* signifies an utter abhorring a person, or thing, so that one would not come near it, as Chap. 19. 18. But Job here by a meiosis intends, that he was so far from abhorring them, that he heard them favorably, and did them right.

*{The cause}* Heb. *the judgment.* See on Chap. 22. 4.

*{of my Man-servant}* Of any manservant of mine. It is commonly translated servant, but here manservant, because of the opposition to the maidservants in the following words.

*{or of my Maid-servant}* Heb. *And.* But put for *or,* as Exod. 21. 15, 17. Job was so far from despising both, that he would despise neither, no not them of the weaker sex, which were least able to right themselves.

*{when they contended with me}* Some understand this of suits between him and his servants before other judges. Job durst refer his cause between him and his servants to any man. But it is not likely that Job being so godly and just would not, or could not satisfy his servants himself. Others understand it of contentions between servant and servant brought before him to judge. But it is most likely to be meant of complaints from discontented servants, as if he did not order things aright in his family, which complaints he heard with patience, which many masters would not, and stop their mouths with just answers, which many other masters could not. See on Chap. 13. 3, 6. & 23. 6.

**V. 14. ***{What then shall I do, when God riseth up? And when he visiteth what shall I answer him?}* Lest any man should attribute Job's kind dealing with his servants to human affection, and the facility of his nature, and not to piety, he mentions two excellent motives that stirred him up to be so courteous to his servants: the one from God's judgment in this verse, wherein there is no respect of persons: and servants having the same common master, may be heard before God as well as masters, Col. 4. 1. The other in the next verse from the common condition of birth both in masters and servants, verse 15. He intimates in this verse that the fear of God did keep him from abusing his authority to the injury of the meanest servant.

*{What then shall I do?}* Or, *what then should I do?* To wit, if I had been guilty in this kind, I should not then have known which way to turn myself, when God should call me to account. It is likely this was not Job's thought now only, when he spoke these words, but was in his heart long before, and kept him from oppressing his servants. For the word see on Chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *bring forth.*

*{when God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{riseth up}* When God being as it were awaked with the complaints of my servants against me, shall rise up as it were, that is, set himself to revenge the injuries done to my servants. Ps. [68.]1. Zech. 2. 13. Others understand it of the day of judgment. For the word see on Chap. 14. 12.

*{and when he riseth}* When he shall inquire into my carriage towards my servants, see on Chap. 5. 24. 7. 18.

*{what shall I answer him?}* I shall be no way able to defend myself. My guilt would stop my mouth. For the word see on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 15. ***{Did not he that made me in the womb, make him? and did not one fashion us in the womb?}* I durst not oppress my servant, because though our outward condition in the world differ, yet I considered, that he by nature was as good as I; being made in a woman's womb of the like substance of soul and body, wrought in the same manner, and by the same God.

*{Did not he}* God, mentioned verse 14.

*{that made me}* It is not spoken of our first creation, but of our several formation in the wombs of our mothers, as appears by the words. For the word see on Chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *Bring forth.*

*{in the womb}* For the word see on Chap. 15. 2.

*{make him}* See before in this verse, for the word is the same in the original, as well as in the translation. See the same thing affirmed of our common formation by God, Chap. 34. 19. Prov. 14. 31. & 17. 5. Mal. 2. 10.

*{and did not one}* The same God.

*{fashion}* Heb. *apt,* or *fit us.* Set all the faculties of the soul, and members of the body together in a comely, and profitable proportion. See on Chap. 8. 8.

*{us}* Me, my men, and my maids.

*{in the womb}* For the word see on Chap. 24. 20. Some read the end of the verse thus; *Did he not fashion us in one wombe?* To which interpretation it is hard to give a good sense, and therefore the former is better.

**V. 16. ***{If I have withheld the poor from their desire, or have caused the eyes of the widow to faile}* From hence to verse 21. is set out his singular care to relieve the poor. 1. In not frustrating their desires in this verse. 2. In feeding them, verses 17, 18. 3. In clothing them, verses 19, 20. The sum of this verse is, that he had not disappointed the needy of their desire, nor suffered the poor widows to look for somewhat at his hand, even till their eyes ached, and yet go without it.

*{If I have withheld}* If I have denied them those things which in their need they did desire of me. For the word see on Chap. 22. 7. and on Chap. 20. 13. on the word, *Keep.*

*{the poor}* See on Chap. 5. 16.

*{from their desire}* From the thing desired by them. So faith is put for the doctrine believed, Jude verse 3. and hope for the thing hoped for, Col. 1. 5. Now the thing the poor desire is, how to be relieved by the counsel, wealth, power, and patronage of rich and great men. Job did not object enmity, or multitude, or importunity, or ungratefulness of the poor, but readily condescended to their requests. He did not deny to satisfy their desire in those things, that need made them to crave. For the word see on Chap. 22. 3.

*{or have caused the eyes of the widow to faile}* If I have made them to wait long, and consume their eyes in expectation of some good from me in vain. Or, till they were weary with looking, and then let them go without it. Consuming of the eyes, in Scripture language signifies earnest expectation of a thing with eyes fixed on him, that should grant, till one be quite out of hope, Ps. 69. 3. & 119. 82, 123. Jer. 14. 6. Lam. 4. 17. Yet there may be a meiosis. He was so far from frustrating their desires, that he soon relieved them, when he was once acquainted with their wants. So Ps. 51. 17. Not despising, is highly esteeming. For *causing to fail,* see on Job 9. 22. Where it is translated, destroying. For *eyes,* see on Chap. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*. For the *widow,* see on Chap. 22. 9. God often commends widows to our charitable consideration, because being destitute of husbands they are exposed to many injuries, and if anything amiss befall them, they bear it more heavily than others, bewailing their condition, and the loss of their husbands, under whom they lived safely before. Every light wrong puts them in mind of their former comfort, and renews their grief. Job therefore was extraordinarily careful to comfort and relieve such, when he knew their griefs and wants.

**V. 17. ***{Or have eaten my morsel myself alone, and the Fatherless hath not eaten thereof.}* In this verse he sets out his charity in feeding the poor, negatively: in the next affirmatively. The sum of this verse is, that he had not according to the manner of covetous and churlish persons eaten his meat alone without giving part thereof to the hungry orphans.

*{Or}* See on verse 13.

*{have eaten}* See on Chap. 21. 25.

*{my morsel}* My food. For men cannot eat their bread or meat whole, but by pieces.

*{myself alone}* Without giving part to the poor.

*{and the fatherless}* See on Chap. 22. 9.

*{hath not eaten thereof}* See on Chap. 21. 25.

**V. 18. ***{(For from my youth he was brought up with me as with a Father, and I have guided her from my mother's womb)}* In this verse he proves what he had said in the former words, that the fatherless and the widow fared the better for him, and with him. For he was so tender of them, that he fed them at his table, and guided them in their affairs as if they had been his own children.

*{For}* See on Chap. 19. 25.

*{from my youth}* Since I came to look into the world, and began to be versed in worldly affairs. For the word see on Chap. 13. 26.

*{he}* The fatherless, that was poor, spoken of verse 17.

*{was brought up with me}* I bred him, that had no father to do it.

*{as with a father}* As tenderly as fathers use to breed their children, Chap. 29. 16. For the word see on Chap. 15. 10.

*{and I have guided}* Helped with counsel, and all things needful, as guides direct in the right way.

*{her}* The widow, spoken of verse 16. Others understand it of she-orphans, which are for the most part more helpless than boys.

*{from my mother's womb}* A hyperbolical expression, varying from the former in the beginning of the verse, intimating, that Job had such an inbred desire of doing good to the poor, that it showed itself betimes, as if it had come into the world with him, and waited only for a fitting time to express itself in action. For *womb:* see on Chapter 15. 2. For *Mother,* see on Chapter 17. 14.

**V. 19. ***{If I have seen any perish for want of clothing, or any poor without covering}* In this verse, and the next is set out Job's care of clothing the poor, who was ready upon the very sight of their nakedness to furnish them with apparel.

*{If I have seen}* See on Chapter 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold.*

*{any perish}* Anyone ready to die for cold and nakedness, whom I did not readily relieve. See on Chapter 4. 7. & 29. 13.

*{for want of clothing}* Heb. *Because he had no clothing.* Was destitute of apparel. See on Chap. 24. 7, 10.

*{or any poor}* See on Chap. 24. 4.

*{without covering}* Without apparel to hide his nakedness, and shelter him from the showers of heaven. See on Chapter 24. 7. Some read it, *and have not given clothing to the poore.* Though he had none before, yet I bestowed it on him.

**V. 20. ***{If his loins have not blessed me, and if he were not warmed with the fleece of my sheep}* Job did not stand and look on, and pity them only, but out of his own store provided warm apparel for them, as themselves have thankfully acknowledged.

*{If his loins have not blessed me}* Some take it to be an attribution of speech to speechless things, intimating, what his loins should have done, if they could have spoken. Others, for a verbal acknowledgment of the poor, who thanked him because their loins were clothed, and warmed by him. Others, for a strong and hearty prayer to God to requite Job's kindness; for a great part of man's man's strength is in his loins; as the strength of Behemoth is, Chap. 40. 16. For *Blessing,* see Deut. 24. 13. 2 Sam. 8. 10.

*{and if he were not warmed with the fleece of my sheep}* This expresses the cause why the poor blessed Job. They felt cold till he clothed them, now they joy that they are freed from it. Isa. 44. 16. The fleece is put for woolen garments made of the fleece of Job's sheep, which kept the poor warm.

**V. 21. ***{If I have lift up my hand against the fatherless, when I saw my help in the gate.}* Here Job clears himself from wronging orphans and such as were helpless, which yet he might have done without danger or blame, having by reason of his authority and respect from others, riches, power, and friends, all men ready to assist him, and no man daring to oppose or censure him.

*{If I have lift up my hand against the fatherless}* To kill him, or strike him, or set myself against him in judgment, to bring him to some unjust loss, or undeserved punishment, as the words following seem to import, Isa. 10. 32. Zech. 2. 9. 1 Kings 13. 4. Others understand it of threatening men, or denying them justice when they came to demand it. For *hand,* see on Chap. 15. 23. For *fatherless,* see on Chap. 22. 9.

*{when I saw my help in the gate}* When I knew I had enough in the public place of judicature, which was exercised in the gates, Ruth 4. 1. to assist me in unjustly ruining others. For *See* look on Chap. 19. 27. on the word, *Behold*.

**V. 22. ***{Then let mine arm fall from my shoulder blade, and mine armes be broken from the bone.}* This imprecation may well be referred to all the former passages, from the the 13th verse, but it answers very proportionably to the last in the former verse. He wishes a punishment answerable to the sin mentioned, that if his arm have lifted up his hand to do violence to the poor, it may never more be useful to him. See the like, Ps. 137. 5, 6. Where forgetting Jerusalem, is to be punished with forgetting skill in music, and not mentioning of her, with never speaking more. Job by this imprecation seeks to clear himself from those false imputations of cruelty to the poor, which Eliphaz had fastened on him, Chap. 22. 6, 7.

*{Then let mine arm}* Or rather, *my shoulder*, as it is translated 1 Chron. 15. 15.

*{fall from my shoulder blade}* Let the flesh of my shoulder rot, and fall off from the shoulder bone. This answers well to what follows in this verse.

*{and mine arm}* See on Chap. 22. 8.

*{be broken}* From the word שבר comes our English word breaking to shivers.

*{from the bone}* Or, *from the channell-bone.* The word קנה signifies. 1. A reed. 2 Kings 18. 21. 2. A sweet spice called calamus. Exod. 30. 23. 3. A spear, which is long like a reed. Ps. 68. 30. 4. A stalk of corn, for the same reason, Gen. 41. 5. 5. A long bone in the arm for length somewhat like a reed reaching from the shoulder to the elbow, or rather to the hand, for then it is longer, and more like a reed, and has a joint in the way, as canes have. And it does more fully set out Job's meaning here, who wishes the whole shoulder and arm to rot, if he had been cruel to the poor. 6. An artificial or measuring reed, which had a certain length and breadth. Ezek. 40. 5, 6. 7. A balance, the beam whereof is long and narrow like a reed. Isa. 46. 6. 8. A branch of a candlestick, for the same reason. Exod. 25. 32. Here it is taken in the fifth sense, for the bone in the arm, and the sense is as before, let the flesh of my arm rot, and fall from the bone. A grievous thing to see, for a man to have a shoulder bone, and the bones of his arm whole without any flesh on them.

**V. 23. ***{For destruction from God was a terror to me, and by reason of his highness I could not endure.}* Two reasons are set down why Job did abstain from hurting the poor and fatherless. The one was a fear of punishment from God: the other was an awful reverence of God's majesty.

*{For}* See on Chap. 19. 25.

*{destruction}* I was afraid, that God would send such a judgment upon me, as I mentioned in the former verse, renting my flesh from my bones, which would prove my destruction, Isa. 13. 6. Joel 1. 15. For the word see on Chap. 18. 12.

*{from God}* Such a heavy destruction as God uses to send on oppressors. For the word see on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{was a terror to me}* Though I needed not to fear men, who could have many to assist me, if I had a mind to wrong others, verse 21. Yet I was always afraid of God's judgments. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 21. on the word, *dreadful*.

*{and by reason of his highness}* By reason of his great majesty. Others read it, *by reason of his weight;* or, *the weight of it,* that is, the weight of God's anger, or of the destruction mentioned before. The reason of the different translation is, because the verb from whence this word comes, signifies both to lift up, and to bear a burden. See on Chap. 21. 33. on the word, *suffer.*

*{I could not endure}* Heb. *I could not.* If God should set upon me in a hostile way, for forsaking or oppressing those that were in misery, I could not have endured the punishment he might justly have inflicted upon me. Others read it, *I could not stand.* I were not able to endure his presence, nor uphold my own safety. Others thus, *I could not prevaile.* He would be too hard for me and utterly overthrow me.

**V. 24. ***{If I have made gold my hope, or have said to the fine gold, Thou art my confidence.}* In this verse, and the next, Job frees himself from covetousness, and shows how innocent he was of it, by removing from him two effects of it. 1. Confidence in riches, in this verse. And 2. Placing his joy and delight in them, in the next verse.

*{If I made gold my hope}* If I have not trusted in God alone, but have made an idol of my riches, and trusted in them. So covetousness is called idolatry, Col. 3. 5. Gold the chiefest of metals, and coin, is put here for all riches.

*{or have said}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{to the fine gold}* See on Chap. 28. 16.

*{Thou art my confidence}* See on Chap. 8. 13, 14. & 11. 20.

**V. 25. ***{If I rejoiced, because my wealth was great, and because mine hand had gotten much}* If I have after the manner of those, who wholly bend their minds to heap up riches, leaped for joy to see my substance increased, and have thought them to have been gotten both by my own industry, and for myself alone.

*{If I rejoiced}* In a carnal way, setting all my contentment in temporal riches, and using them only for my own delight and pleasure, Luke 12. 19. & 16. 19. James 5. 5.

*{because my wealth was great}* Because I grew very rich. For *wealth* see on Chap. 15. 29. For *great* see on Chap. 22. 5.

*{and because mine hand had gotten much}* This is the language of ungodly men, attributing their wealth to their own industry, not to God's blessing, as Job does, Chap. 1. 21. Job was not overjoyed because he had gotten much wealth by his labor, or otherwise, Deut. 8. 17, 18. Isa. 10. 10. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23. *Gotten.* Heb. *found.* For *find* see on Chap. 17. 10. *Much.* Rather very much, for the word signifies more than great. See on Chap. 8. 2. upon the word, *Strong.*

**V. 26. ***{If I beheld the Sun when it shined, or the Moon walking in brightness.}* In this verse, and those two that follow, the holy man expresses his detestation of idolatry. And that 1. By avoiding the occasion of it, in this verse. 2. The causes and signs of it, in the next. 3. He sets down the reason of his careful avoiding of it, verse 28. Some understand it of covetousness, that Job was careful not to be corrupted by the sunshine of prosperity, or deceitfulness of riches: but the more received interpretation is the former.

*{If I beheld}* If I stood to gaze upon it, as men do attentively look upon things in which they do delight. For the word, see on Chapter 19. 27.

*{the Sun}* Heb. *the Light.* Which though it may be attributed to any star, yet most properly to the sun, the fountain of light, which gives light to the stars. And the rather is it so to be understood, because it is set before, and opposed to the moon; and it is also called a great light, Ps. 136. 7. And the greater light, Gen. 1. 16. For the word see on Chap. 17. 12.

*{when it shined}* When it appeared in his full glory, and was most like to take up my thoughts.

*{or the Moon}* See on Chap. 25. 5.

*{walking}* Moving and keeping on her constant course. For the word see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word, *passes*.

*{in brightness}* Heb. *Bright,* or *Precious.* For light is a precious thing; and so is the moon in our account, when it shines bright, and when it is at the full, and most adorned with light from the sun, then she walks in her state and glory: and then she looks most beautiful, like shining gold or silver. For the word see on Chap. 28. 10. And the same word is applied to the light, Zech. 14. 6. Piscator joins the verse with the next thus; *If when I beheld the Sun when it shined, or the Moon when it went clear, my heart hath been secretly enticed, so that my hand hath kissed my mouth.* It is likely he did it, because he could see no fault in looking upon the brightness of the sun and moon. No more is there any fault in rejoicing when wealth increases, verse 25. But to rejoice in it, so as wicked men do, who place their greatest happiness in it, is a great fault. So here to look upon the sun and moon, as having a divine power in them, as the heathen did, is questionless a great sin. This is thought to be the ancientest idolatry in the world. The heathen not knowing God, and seeing so great light, comfort, influence, on these lower bodies, in the sun and moon, did gaze upon them as upon gods, and worship them accordingly, as if there were no higher light. But Job kept himself clear from this idolatry, Deut. 17. 3. Jer. 43. 13. 2 King. 21. 3. & 23. 4, 5, 11. Ezek. 8. 16.

**V. 27. ***{And my heart hath been secretly enticed, or my mouth hath kissed my hand}* If I have been deceived so much as privily in my heart, much less openly consented unto idolaters, and after the manner of their superstitious custom, by moving, and putting my hand to my mouth, yielded any worship unto those heavenly bodies.

*{And}* Or, *Or,* as this particle is used, Exod. 21. 15, 17.

*{my heart}* See on Chap. 15. 12.

*{hath been enticed}* If by the sight of the great beauty and brightness of them I have been drawn to attribute a divine power to them in my heart. For the word see on verse 9. on the word, *Deceived.*

*{secretly}* Heb. *in secret.* If I have thought so in my soul, though not expressed it by word or gesture, so as any man might take notice of it. For the word see on Chap. 22. 14. upon the word, *Covering:* and on Chap. 24. 15. on the word, *disguises*.

*{or my mouth hath kissed my hand}* As I have not committed this idolatry inwardly by cogitation, so neither outwardly by adoration. Whereof putting the hand to the mouth seems to be a sign in those days. So kissing the Son, Ps. 2. 12. is a sign of giving divine honor to him. Kissing the calves was the like, Hos. 13. 2. So also kissing Baal, 1 King. 19. 18. Because they could not reach the sun and moon, the heathen used, when they saw them shine bright, and as it were show themselves in their glory, to lift up their hands to them, and then put them to their mouths, by this sign adoring them as gods. If the things they worshiped were nigh them, they kissed them. If out of reach, they put their hands to their mouths, as acknowledging they had their breath and life from them. In the Hebrew it is, *if my hand hath kissed my mouth.* That is, if I put my hand to my mouth. For *Hand* see on Chap. 15. 23. For *mouth* see on Chap. 15. 5.

**V. 28. ***{This also were an iniquity to be punished by the Judge; for I should have denied the God that is above.}* Most men think idolatry to be a light sin, especially growing, and being confirmed by length of time, multitude of idolaters, show of profit, and other specious reasons. But Job counted this a most heinous sin, as by which he should have denied God, and deserved most horrible and fearful punishments.

*{This}* This kind of idolatry.

*{also}* As well as fornication, adultery, cruelty to the poor, mentioned before. For the word see Chap. 16. 4.

*{were an iniquity}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{to be punished by the Judge}* See on verse 11.

*{for I should have denied}* By giving divine honor to the sun and moon, I should have denied it to belong unto God.

*{the God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{that is above}* That is above the earth, and is in heaven. Or rather, that is above the sun and moon, and gives light to them, and life or vigor to all the creatures.

**V. 29. ***{If I rejoiced at the destruction of him that hated me, or lift up myself, when evil found him.}* In this verse, and the two next, he clears himself from malice and desire of revenge. Either entertained within, in this verse; or showed forth in words, verse 30. though he had grievous provocations, verse 31.

*{If I rejoiced}* If I took contentment at the ruin of my adversary, and fed my thoughts with his fall. Some men that are not so bad as to work mischief to their enemies, yet entertain thoughts of joy when they hear they are ruined by others. Job did not so much, Prov. 24. 17, 18.

*{at the destruction of him that hated me}* At the overthrow of my enemy.

*{or lift up myself}* Some take it to be the same with the former. I have not lifted up my spirit to joy. But it is rather a further degree of malice: I have not in the pride of my spirit insulted over my ruined enemy. Broughton renders it, *If I have bestirred me.* And that elegantly, and suitably to the original. I would not suffer my choler to awake upon occasion of his ruin to insult over him.

*{when evil found him}* When he fell into trouble and calamity. A usual phrase in Scripture. So the same word should be translated, Deut. 31. 17. Ps. 116. 3. And it may be understood barely of lighting upon him, as Deut. 19. 5. Or intimates a kind of pursuit of sin to bring wicked men to punishment, as Chap. 3. 22. Like evils hunting the wicked man to destruction, Ps. 140. 11. By evil is meant evil not of sin, but of punishment, as Amos 3. 6. *Find* see on Chap. 17. 10.

**V. 30. ***{Neither have I suffered my mouth to sin by wishing a curse to his soule.}* Much less would I wish his ruin, or destruction myself, who durst not rejoice in it when it was wrought by others without my procuring.

*{Neither have I suffered}* The word in the original is given, that is, given way to, or uttered. So it is often used, as Chap. 9. 18. Ps. 46. 10. Gen. 20. 6. His natural corruption might press him to pray against his enemy, but it could not prevail with him. For the word see on Chap. 14. 4. upon the word, *Bring.*

*{my mouth}* I have not spoken sinful words. Sometimes the mouth is put for the words, as Chap. 15. 4. Sometimes the tongue, Ps. 72. 2. Sometimes the palate, as it is here in the original. For all these are instruments of speaking.

*{to sin}* See on Chap. 5. 24.

*{by wishing a curse to his soule}* Heb. *by asking his life by a curse.* That is, by praying God to lay his curse upon him, which might take away his life. For *soul* see on Chap. 14. 22.

**V. 31. ***{If the men of my Tabernacle said not, O that we had of his flesh! we cannot be satisfied}* Some understand this verse of the desire of Job's servants to eat of the flesh of Job's cattle still, and live always with him who provided so plentifully for them. But this is nothing to the coherence. Others understand it of a desire of revenge in Job's servants, who, because he puts them to so much trouble in entertaining strangers, and gave their meat to them, and made them stay for more, wished they might eat Job's own flesh, and so be rid of that tedious service. But there is no likelihood, that Job, who was so kind to strangers, was so unkind to his own servants. But the best way is to refer the word, His, to Job's enemy mentioned verses 29, 30. As if Job had said, yet I protest, that I wanted not setters on even among my own household servants, who still persuaded me to requite those injuries which I received, with most bitter revenge: nay, their minds were so incensed, that they cried out, that they should never be satisfied on them, no not though they had eaten them up quick. Thus David's men stir him to revenge against Saul, 1 Sam. 24. 4. So does Abishai, 1 Sam. 26. 8. 2 Sam. 16. 9. This interpretation suits well with the coherence, and sets off Job's patience the more, that would not be provoked to be revenged on his adversary, no not by them of his own household.

*{If the men}* Heb. *mortals.* See on Chap. 19. 19.

*{of my tabernacle}* Servants, men and women, that dwelt with me, for then they dwelt in tabernacles, as now in houses. Or if Job and other great men had houses (for else it is not likely that the fall of a tent could kill Job's children, Chap. 1. 19. And it seems he had a princely house, Chap. 21. 28.) They were still called tabernacles, because men first dwelt in such before they had art to build houses, and the meaner sort (very likely) dwelt in such at that time. For the word, *Tabernacle,* see on Chap. 5. 24. & 11. 14.

*{said not}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Oh that we had of his flesh}* For this kind of wishing see on Chap. 6. 8. & 11. 5. For *Flesh,* see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{we cannot be satisfied}* Without his death, or swallowing him quick, Ps. 123. 4. Job 19. 22.

**V. 32. ***{The stranger did not lodge in the street, but I opened my doors to the traveller}* This verse sets out Job's great hospitality, who kept open house for all passengers, and stood at his door to observe who passed by, to invite them to his house. There were not inns then to entertain strangers, as there are few now in the Eastern countries: and therefore strangers must have lain out of doors, exposed to wind and weather, if some good men did not entertain them. This Job used to do like Abraham, Gen. 18. Lot, Gen. 19. and the good old man of Gibeah, Judg. 19. 19, 20. This duty is commended, Rom. 12. 13. Heb. 13. 2. 1 Pet. 4. 9.

*{The stranger}* He that dwelt not there but traveled from home and was destitute of lodging and other necessaries.

*{did not lodge}* Did not abide without in the night; for I entertained him in my own house; as follows in this verse.

*{in the street}* Out of doors, which would have been very troublesome, Gen. 19. 2, 3. For the word see on Chap. 18. 17.

*{but I opened my doors}* I caused them to stand open, that strangers might come if they pleased, or I might the better see them to invite them. For *opening,* see on Chap. 29. 19. upon the word, *Spread out*.

*{to the traveller}* Ready for him to enter in at, without knocking or opening. So ready was Job to entertain strangers. Or *to the way.* The door of his house was made, and set open toward the highway, where most travelers come.

**V. 33. ***{If I covered my transgression as Adam; by hiding mine iniquity in my bosom}* Lest his friends should object, you have highly commended yourself for many virtues, but have you no faults? Yes, says Job, but I have not, as men usually do, labored to conceal them, but freely acknowledged them to God, and heartily craved pardon for them.

*{If I covered}* Or, *have I covered?* Sure I have not. See on verse 5. I have not covered my sin, by concealing, denying, excusing, extenuating, or any other way, but acknowledged it with sorrow of heart.

*{my transgression}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{as Adam}* Who did what he could to hide his sin from God. Gen. 3. 7, 8, 12. Others read it, *after the manner of men.* For men in their natural estate do what they can to hide their sins, Hos. 6. 7. The reason of the difference is, because in the Hebrew tongue Adam is the proper name of the first man and is also a common name to all men.

*{by hiding}* Heb. *to hide:* for things are therefore covered, that they may be hidden, and not seen.

*{mine iniquity}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{in my bosom}* Kept it close, as things hidden in the bosom: Broughton reads it of self-love. Piscator, *because I loved it.* As if Job should assign two causes, that might have moved him to conceal his sin, if he had not feared God. 1. The usual custom of natural men. 2. Love of sin.

**V. 34. ***{Did I fear a great multitude, or did the contempt of families terrify me, that I kept silence, and went out of the door?}* Some knit this verse to the former, as if Job had said, I did acknowledge my fault, when I did wrong, and did not conceal it for fear the multitude should rise up against me, and the basest persons should deride me. This could not make me keep silence, nor hide my head for shame. Others read it thus, *Though I could have terrified a great multitude, yet the most contemptible of the family terrified me, so that I kept silence and went out of door.* And their meaning is, though in my prosperity I could by my great power have kept many in awe, yet I durst not wrong the meanest person in any family; nor would not speak unjustly against any man, nor go out of door to hurt him.

*{Did I fear?}* Or, *Though I could have terrified.* As before. Or, *broken,* as it is translated Chap. 3. 25.

*{a great multitude}* A great company of men, that should have set themselves against me. For *Great,* see on Chap. 22. 5.

*{or did the contempt of families}* The basest person that is most contemned in any family. For *Contempt* see on Chap. 12. 5, 21. *Or yet did the contempt of families.* As before.

*{terrify me}* Though there were nothing in them to make me afraid to use my power to oppress them, yet because I feared God, I durst not do it.

*{that I kept silence}* I durst not speak against them to wrong them.

*{and went not out of the door}* I would not set one foot over the threshold to do the meanest man wrong. For *went out,* see on Chap. 20. 25. on comes out.

**V. 35. ***{O that one would hear me! behold my desire is, that the Almighty would answer me, and that mine adversary had written a book}* Job having propounded so many testimonies of his innocency with imprecation of so many evils to himself, if he had not spoken truth, now in this verse desires a fair trial: and in the two next verses shows how he would carry himself in it. In this verse he desires 1. An equal judge. Secondly that God would give a reason, why he dealt so harshly with him. 3. That any one of his friends who had pleaded vehemently against him, would write down all their charge. And doubts not, but he should come off well. Verses 36, 37.

*{O that one would hear me}* O that any indifferent person had the hearing of the cause between God and me, and my friends and me. Others understand it of God, and read it, *O that he would hear me.* That is, the Almighty; as follows. So the antecedent is gathered out of the words following, Chap. 16. 9. & 18. 13. For such manner of wishing see on Chapter 6. 8. & 11. 5. For *Hear* see on Chapter 13. 17.

*{behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{my desire is}* I am so assured of clearing myself, that I desire nothing more, than to come to trial, whereof guilty men are afraid; others read it, *my signe is*. Meaning the mark or scope, that I aim at, which comes all to one.

*{that the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5. *{would answer me}* He means not, that God would answer his prayers, but answer him in his cause, as Chap. 9. 32. & 13. 22, 23. For the word, see on Chapter 16. 1.

*{and that mine adversary}* Heb. *the man of my contention.* That is, he that contends with me, be it one of my friends here, or any other. For *man,* see on Chap. 1. 1. For *contention,* see on Chap. 20. 16. *{Had written a book}* Had set down his charge against me in writing, as the custom was then, and is still in lawsuits. See on Chapter 19. 23.

**V. 36. ***{Surely I would take it on my shoulder, and bind it as a crown to me}* Here he shows how he would carry himself in the judgment. 1. He would pick matter of commendation out of his enemies' accusation by confuting his falsehoods, as being sure he could not object any gross wickedness truly against him. And that he does in this verse. 2. That he might be sure to be cleared he would lay open all the course of his life before the judge, even such passages, as his adversary knew not. And that he does in verse 37.

*{Surely I would}* Heb. *If I did not,* as in many verses before.

*{take it}* Or *Bear it.* See on Chapter 21. 3. upon the word *suffer.*

*{upon my shoulder}* Not as a burden, but as an honor, as a standard-bearer carries an ensign that all men see it. Isa. 5. 26. & 13. 2.

*{and bind it as a crowne to me}* Account it as a great honor, as a crown is to a king, because I shall have occasion thereby fully to clear myself. The word is crowns. That is, a glorious crown with many golden circles and jewels in it. For *crown* see on Chapter 19. 9.

**V. 37. ***{I would declare unto him the number of my steps, as a prince would I go near unto him.}* That my enemy may know, that I am not ashamed to show my face in such a trial, I am ready to give an account of all my life past. Neither will I stay, till I am cited thither by my adversary, but will come boldly of my own accord. A great testimony of innocency in Job to come uncalled, when guilty persons warned are loath to appear.

*{I would declare}* I would set before him, make a free and a full confession. See on Chap. 15. 18. upon the word, *Told.*

*{unto him}* To my adversary. I would help him make up his book, and tell him many secret passages of my life, which he knows not. Or, to the judge when my adversary can say no more against me, I will recite all the course of my life before the judge to see what he can pick against me, either way argues great confidence of innocency.

*{the number of my steps}* All my words and deeds one by one, as far as I can remember, to see, at which he can take advantage.

*{as a Prince}* I would come before him with a heroical spirit, and an undaunted courage, as a prince that comes to judge rather than to be judged. An argument that he was not guilty of any wicked way. See on Chap. 29. 10. on the word, *Nobles.*

*{would I go near unto him}* I would not keep aloof off from the judgment-seat, as one guilty, but come near to it in confidence as expecting absolution.

**V. 38. ***{If my land cry against me, or that the furrows likewise thereof complain}* Job having heretofore cleared himself from many faults objected by his friends, now proceeds to purge himself of those faults that concern his own possessions, and 1. Of unjust getting of his land, in this verse. 2. Of unrighteous gain of it in the next verse. 3. He adds a suitable imprecation verse 40. 4. The writer adds his testimony of the conclusion of Job's words, verse 40. also.

*{If my land}* The land, which I now possess.

*{cry against me}* An elegant attribution of a cry to a dead thing, to show the sin to be so great, that dead things would complain of it, if they had tongues, though the men oppressed dare not. See the like Gen. 4. 10. Job 16. 18. The sin here condemned is taking away another man's land unjustly by fraud or force: and the land is brought in, as complaining, that it is by violence kept from the right owner, and crying to God for a just revenge on the usurping possessor of it. See the like Hab. 2. 11, 12. If the earth may complain of sin wrought upon it, much more of violence offered to itself in detaining it from the right owner.

*{or that the furrows thereof}* That are made in it by the plow, chap. 39. 10. Psal. 65. 10. Hos. 10. 4.

*{likewise}* Or, *Together* with the rest of the land.

*{complain}* Heb. *weep*. If they complain, that an usurper makes furrows in the ground, who has no power over it, as God's people do, Psal. 129. 3. Others interpret this verse of detaining the laborers' wages, and make the next an exposition of it.

**V. 39. ***{If I have eaten the fruits thereof without money, or have caused the owners thereof to lose their life.}* Some understand this verse as the former of taking away of the land, so all the fruits and benefits of it without satisfaction to the right owner. Others of depriving the laborers of their wages, so that they perished for want of food, while he went away with all the profit of the land without satisfying them for tilling it.

*{If I have eaten}* See on chap. 31. 25.

*{the fruits thereof}* Heb. *the strength thereof*. That is seed fruits, as the natural force of the earth put forth, Gen. 4. 12.

*{without money}* Heb. *without silver*. For that was the ordinary coin. Gold was more rare even in Solomon's time. 1 Kings 10. 21. Some understand it of denying the former owners the price of the ground. Others of denying the laborers their wages. For silver see on chap. 22. 25.

*{or have caused the owners thereof to lose their life.}* Heb. *or have caused the soule of the owners thereof to expire*, or *breath out*, or *grieve*. For all these the word signifies in the original. If I have wronged the former owners, or present laborers in it, so as that for want of their due from me they have perished. Though the land was his, yet the laborers had a right to be paid for their works out of the fruits of it, and were as it were owners in part. Jer. 22. 13. Mal. 3. 5. Jam. 5. 4. For life see on chap. 14. 22. upon the word *soul*.

**V. 40. ***{Let thistles grow instead of wheat, and cockle instead of barley. The words of Job are ended.}* He concludes with an imprecation, which may be applied to many verses before; but it is very suitable to the last. For what is more just than that he who by defrauding others expects great crops of corn, should reap thistles and cockle.

*{let thistles grow}* Heb. *come forth*. For that word see on chap. 20. 25. He means, in his own ground, else it had been no punishment to him at all.

*{instead of wheat}* The worst crop instead of the best kind of corn.

*{and cockle}* A stinking weed, as the original of the word in the Hebrew intimates.

*{instead of barley}* It has its name in the original from a verb that signifies to fright one, so as his hairs stand an end for fear. So the spikes of the barley stand bolt upright.

*{the words of Job are ended}* Here is an end of Job's words spoken in conference with his friends. For he spoke afterwards to God, though not much. chap. 40. 3. & 42. 1.
