And these words are fitly added by the writer, because Job had largely spoken without interruption, and had said all he had to say in his own defense, and seeing none of his friends replied here, he makes an end of the conference. For words see on chap. 19. 28. on the word, *Matter*. For Job see on chap. 1. 1.

## CHAPTER 32

**Verse 1. ***{So these three men ceased to answer Job, because he was righteous in his own eyes.}* The disputation between Job and his friends being now ended, the judgment of it follows. First by Elihu in 6. chapters, and that in four several speeches. The first in Chap. 32. and 33. The second, in chap. 34. The third in chap. 35. The fourth in chap. 36, 37. Secondly by God, who does more fully convince both Job, and his friends. In this chapter he turns his speech to Job's friends: in the next to Job himself. In this chapter is first an historical transition from the former disputation to Elihu's speech. To verse 6. Secondly Elihu's preface to Job's friends, to make them attentive to his following oration. To the end of the chapter. In the former is laid down first the cause why Job's friends left speaking. Verse 1, 2. why Elihu did speak. And that first his anger against Job, verse 2. 2. Against his friends. And that first for condemning Job, whom they could not convince, verse 3. For giving over speaking before they had convinced him, verse 4, 5. 3. Elihu spoke more uprightly, and wisely in this cause, than Job's three friends had done, which appears 1. In that he stops Job's mouth so that though he gave him liberty thrice or four times to speak, yet he had nothing to say for himself. 2. In that God blames him not at all, as he does Job's other friends. For the words. So. Heb. *And.* But thus it is translated also Chap. 2. 13.

*{these three men}* Eliphaz, Bildad, and Zophar, Job had stopped all their mouths, so that they had no more to say against him. For the word men see on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{ceased}* They were very hot, and disputed eagerly before, as worldly men follow their business in the weekdays: but now they were as quiet, as men are on a Sabbath. From this word the Sabbath has his name in the original. They would have no more to do with Job, but leave him to abound in his own sense, who would not be admonished by his friends.

*{to answer}* Heb. *From answering.* See on chap. 16. 1.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{because}* They saw their arguments could do no good on him, and despaired of making him see his sin, and therefore ceased: As they spoke unfitly before, so they gave over now, before they had convinced Job of his evil opinion of God's proceedings with him.

*{he was righteous in his own eyes}* Although they conceived they had said enough to prove him to be a wicked man, yet he still thought himself to be in a good condition. They therefore having no further proof against his solemn protestation, leave him to himself, as a man desperate. He seemed confident that his friends had not confuted him, and that God used him too severely, as an enemy. For the phrase see on chap. 18. 3.

**V. 2. ***{Then was kindled the wrath of Elihu the son of Barachel the Buzite, of the kindred, of Ram: against Job was his wrath kindled, because he justified himself rather then God}* Elihu's anger was kindled, because both Job and his friends had offended against the right order of disputing in this weighty business. For whereas Job should have only defended his own integrity against his friends, he in passion flew out oftentimes to speak unadvisedly of God's proceeding with him, as if it were too harsh. And his friends condemned him for a wicked man because of his extraordinary troubles, although they were not able to prove him to be so.

*{then was kindled the wrath}* Anger is compared unto fire, which is blown up by outward discontenting occasions, as bellows. For wrath see on chap. 4. 9. & 9. 5, 13. & 14. 13.

*{of Elihu}* The name signifies, My God is Jehovah, or My God is the same, Psal. 102. 27. It intimates to us, that his parents, who gave him this name were worshippers of the true God.

*{the son}* See on chap. 14. 21.

*{of Barachel}* This name signifies one whom God has blessed.

*{the Buzite}* Coming from Buz. The name of some one of his predecessors, or an inhabitant of the City of Buz, which being joined with Dedan and Tema. Jer. 25. 23. seems to be a city of Idumea.

*{of the kindred}* Or, *of the family.* That is, of the posterity of Ram in a direct line, for the Scripture uses to mention men's parents in this case, not their kindred in a collateral line, unless they be famous, or something be spoken that concerns them: neither of which is here.

*{of Ram}* This cannot be that Ram, that came of Pharez, Ruth. 4. 19. for it is likely he was not so ancient as Job. Or if he were, he would not leave the Israelites to live among the Edomites. Nor that Aram mentioned, Gen. 22. 21. For then could not Elihu be a Buzite, but must needs come of Kemuel the brother of Buz. Nor is it likely to be Abraham, as the Chaldee paraphrase translates it, who (they say, but without book) was first called Ram, high. Secondly Abram an high father. Thirdly Abraham a father of a great multitude. But it is likely to be the name of some great man well known in these countries then, though not mentioned elsewhere in Scripture. And Elihu's pedigree is more fully set out, than Job's or his three friends, partly to gain more full credit to the story, but principally because he was a young man not so well known, as Job and his friends, and therefore had need to get credit from his ancestors.

*{against Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{was his wrath kindled}* See before on this verse.

*{because he justified}* He thought himself just, or pronounced himself just in his plea. See on chap. 4. 17. & 9. 20.

*{himself}* Heb. *His soul.* See on chap. 14. 22.

*{rather then God}* Elihu was not angry, that Job justified himself against his friends, but that he did it against God in maintaining his own innocence, too much complaining of God's dealing too harshly with him, and desiring to plead with God, as if he did rather afflict him with his power, than in a course of justice. Job had not directly said so much against God, but Elihu gathers it out of the former passages. See the like charge, Chapter 34. 5, 6, 17. & 35. 2. For God see on Chapter 4. 9. & 11. 5.

**V. 3. ***{Also against his three friends was his wrath kindled, because they had found no answer, and yet had condemned Job}* Elihu's anger against Job's friends arises from this ground, that like willful men they did without reason condemn Job for an ungodly man, because of his great afflictions, but could not prove any such thing against him out of the whole course of his life.

*{also against his three friends}* Against Job's three friends. See on verse 1. For friends see on chap. 16. 20.

*{was his wrath kindled}* Elihu's wrath. See on verse 2.

*{because they had found no answer}* Because they could not answer his reasons; and failed in maintaining God's cause against Job, as well as their own. For found see on chap. 17. 10. & 31. 29.

*{and yet had condemned Job}* Though they could not convince him, yet they did condemn him for a wicked man. So much the word signifies. See on chapter 9. 20. & 15. 6. For Job see on chap. 1. 1.

**V. 4. ***{Now Elihu had waited, till Job had spoken, because they were elder then he}* The reason why Elihu forbore to speak so long is set down in this verse, because of his youth. The reason why he speaks now, is set down in the next verse because his elders would speak no more in the cause. So Elihu's modesty is commended, who held his peace, as long as Job or any of his friends had anything to say: yet speaks at length, and that not out of any proud persuasion of his own learning and knowledge, but compelled to speak by necessity.

*{now Elihu}* See on verse 2.

*{had waited till Job had spoken}* Heb. *Had expected Job in words.* Had kept silence, till Job had spoken, and some space of time after, to see, if Job or his friends would speak any more. Piscator reads it, *had with Job expected their word*, Job had made an end, chap. 31. 40. and waited to see what his friends would reply. So did Elihu also. Broughton reads it thus, *waited to speak to Job*. Seeing how ill his friends managed the cause he had a great desire to speak, but out of reverence to their years forbore, till they had quite done. For Job see on chapter 1. 1. For words see on chap. 19. 28. upon the word *Matter*.

*{because they were elder then he}* Heb. *Because they were elder for dayes then he.* Both Job, and his three friends were ancienter than Elihu, and therefore he kept silence, as long as he had any hope that either of them would speak. For Elder see on chap. 12. 10. on the word, *Aged*. For days see on chap. 14.

**V. 5. ***{When Elihu saw that there was no answer in the mouth of these three men, then his wrath was kindled.}* When he perceived that these ancient men had nothing more to answer Job, nor to defend God, but were as men destitute of any arguments to convince Job, he was greatly offended with them.

*{When Elihu}* See on verse 2.

*{saw that there was no answer}* Perceived by their long sitting still, after Job had made an end, that they either would not, or could not say any more in the business. For Saw see on chap. 19. 27. upon the word, *Behold*.

*{in the mouth}* See on chap. 15. 5.

*{of those three men}* See on verse 1.

*{then his wrath was kindled}* At that time and for that cause. See on verse 2.

**V. 6. ***{And Elihu the son of Barachel the Buzite answered, and said, I am young, and ye are very old, wherefore I was afraid, and durst not shew you mine opinion}* Here begins Elihu's preface to Job's three friends which lasts to the end of the chapter. Wherein you have 1. The entrance to verse 15. 2. the scope to verse 17. 3. the further proceeding of it to the end of the chapter. In the first part note 1. The cause of his former silence. verse 6, 7. & 2. the reason of his speaking now. verse 8. &c. to verse 15. The cause of his forbearing before was 1. His youth verse 6. 2. Their age. verse 7. For the first. Such was his modesty, that he was ashamed to utter his mind in the presence of those, who were so much ancienter than himself, lest they should espy his ignorance.

*{And Elihu the son of Barachel the Buzite}* See on verse 2.

*{answered and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

*{I am young}* Heb. *I am few of dayes.* See on chap. 30. 1.

*{and ye are very old}* See on chap. 15. 10.

*{wherefore I was afraid}* He thought his thoughts to be unworthy to be propounded in an assembly of such grave men, who as they were far elder, so he conceived them to be far wiser than himself. I hid my speech as serpents hid themselves, when men approach. So this word is used for a serpent, Mich. 7. 17. Deut. 32. 24.

*{and durst not}* Heb. *and feared.*

*{to shew you}* See on chap. 15. 17.

*{mine opinion}* What I think of the matter you have disputed of with Job all this while: For he speaks not yet to Job but to his friends, as appears verse 12. Chap. 33. 1.

**V. 7. ***{I said, days should speak, and multitude of years should teach wisdom}* I was in hope truly, that you would have brought forth something worthy of these your years, and that I should have received from your mouth wisdom, which you had learned of old age itself: But I saw at length, that I was deceived.

*{I said}* In my heart. I thought so. See on Chap. 16. 1. A common proverb ran in his mind, that wisdom used to attend old age.

*{days}* That is, many days. Not young men, that have lived but awhile, but old men, who have lived long as it follows, such as have lived many years, such can best teach. Chap. 8. 9. For Days, see on Chap. 14. 14. & 15. 23.

*{should speak}* Old men should speak to purpose. Or, *Let dayes speake.* I will not interrupt these old men, but hear them quietly, that I may learn of them.

*{and multitude of years}* Such men as were very old, as you are, verse 6.

*{should teach wisdom}* Should instruct younger men, such as I am. I thought wisdom was entailed upon old age. Heb. *Should make known wisdom.* For wisdom, see on Chap. 28. 12.

**V. 8. ***{But there is a spirit in man, and the inspiration of the Almighty giveth them understanding}* Now he proceeds to show the cause of his speaking. As first, God's giving wisdom to young men sometimes, verse 8. 2. Denying it sometimes to old men, verse 9. 3. The frustrating of his expectation by them, from verse 10. to verse 15. The scope of this verse is, that true wisdom is not gotten with years, but given by God's spirit. Not that wisdom is not ordinarily increased with years (for old men are freer from passions, learn much by use and experience, and by continuance in study add daily to their knowledge) but to show that knowledge is not so tied to age, but that God's spirit gives that measure of it to a young man sometimes, that he gives not to an old man.

*{but there is a spirit}* See on Chapter 15. 2. upon, Vain knowledge.

*{in man}* See on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{and the inspiration}* It is God's work to inspire wisdom into man. His own study cannot get it.

*{of the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{giveth them understanding}* Heb. *Maketh them to understand.* Others read the whole verse thus, *Surely the spirit, that is in man, and the inspiration of the Almighty.*

*{maketh them to understand}* That is, God's spirit inspiring man gives him understanding. Others thus, *Surely there is a spirit in man; but the inspiration of the almighty maketh them to understand.* This last is the best, and the meaning is, that though arts and tongues be good helps to knowledge, and man have a reasonable soul in him, that can reason of things to and fro, yet he cannot attain true wisdom, unless God's spirit enlighten him. Chap. 38. 36. Prov. 1. 6. Eccl. 2. 26. Dan. 1. 17. 2. 21.

**V. 9. ***{Great men are not always wise: neither do the aged understand judgment}* He does not object doting to old men, but shows, that age is not the cause of wisdom, neither is it the old men's privilege to be wise only, but sometimes young men may be wise as well as they: Or old age does not of its own nature bring wisdom with it.

*{great men}* Men in authority, officers, as it is translated, Esth. 1. 8. But here it should rather be men of many years, opposed to young men, or men of few years or days, as verse 8, and Chapter 30. 1. And that agrees well with the end of this verse, where they are called Aged.

*{are not always wise} Alwayes*, is not in the original, but is added by the interpreters to express the sense of the text the better; for some old men are wise, though all be not.

*{neither doe the aged}* See on Chap. 12. 12.

*{understand judgment}* Know always what is right. For judgment, see on Chap. 22. 4. Broughton reads the verse thus, *Men of not great time may be wise: as the old understand the right.* But the former seems more suitable to what went before. verse 6, 7.

**V. 10. ***{Therefore I said hearken to me, I also will shew my opinion}* In this verse you have Elihu's full resolution to speak. 1. Because he had waited as long as any of them had anything to say, and therefore now might speak without immodesty, though he were younger than they, verse 11. 2. Because they had not done the work they undertook to convince Job, and therefore might well give way to another to do it, verse 12. 3. He shows the reason why they could not do it, to wit, lest they should be proud, verse 13. 4. He promises a new way of convincing Job, verse 14. The sum of this verse is; I think fit to entreat you all, and especially you O Job, to hear me patiently. For I think it needful to deliver my opinion, seeing you have spoken nothing to the purpose all this while, nor as becomes men of your years, and experience to speak.

*{Therefore I said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

*{hearken to me}* Heb. *Hear thou me.* You O Job: or every one of you his friends. So God speaks in the singular number in every commandment, though he spoke to thousands, that every one might apply it to himself in particular, Exod. 20. 3, 4, &c. Elihu would have every one of them to hearken to him, as if he had spoken to him only. For the word see on chap. 33. 17.

*{I also will shew mine opinion}* See on verse 6.

**V. 11. ***{Behold I waited for your words; I gave ear to your reasons, whilst you searched out what to say}* Here is an answer to an objection. There is no need that you should speak, when so many wise men have abundantly answered all that Job has objected: And he might be satisfied with what they have said, unless he were obstinate in his opinion. Elihu answers, that he had carefully weighed all they had said for themselves, and for God's glory, and against Job, and found that a further answer was needful; for they had not given satisfaction in the former controversy. It is as if Elihu had said, Surely you cannot rightly any way charge me with misbehavior for dealing with you in this sort, forasmuch as I never went about to interrupt you in your talk; Nay, I did not only listen to your words, but I did also most diligently consider what they might import, still looking when something should come from you, wherewith Job's words might soundly have been answered.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{I waited for your words}* He turns his speech again to Job's friends: I stayed to see what answer you would give to Job's last speech. For Words, see on Chap. 19. 28. on the word *Matter*.

*{I gave ear}* I hearkened diligently.

*{to your reasons}* Heb. *to your understandings*. To the arguments you brought, to see whether they were sound or no: I sought to understand not only what you said, but also what you meant.

*{whilst you searched out}* This shows, that they had kept silence a great while, before Elihu spoke: He gave them much time to study for new matter.

*{what to say}* What further arguments you might use against Job. Heb. *Words.*

**V. 12. ***{Yea I attended unto you; and behold there was none of you that convinced Job, or that answered his words}* But I see, I was deceived in my expectation: For when I had weighed all things which you brought, I found nothing to be spoken of you sufficient to prove Job to be an ungodly man, as you charge him, neither have you well answered his arguments to the contrary, nor convinced him of his unbeseeming speeches of God's carriage toward him.

*{Yea I attended unto you}* I thoroughly weighed your words, as if I had looked into you.

*{and behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{there was none of you}* Every one of you might think himself able to confute Job, but the best of you could not do it.

*{that convinced Job}* That spoke against him to purpose, so as that he might find no just answer for himself. For convince, see on Chap. 15. 3. on the word *reason*. For Job see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{or that answered his words}* That gave a satisfactory answer to his reasons brought to clear himself. He could not deny, but they answered largely; but he counts answers to no purpose, to be no answers. For answer, see on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 13. ***{Lest ye should say, we have found out wisdom: God thrusteth him down, not man}* This I say, lest you should flatter yourselves, as if you had sufficiently convinced Job and he remained obstinate, so that none but God can put him out of his good conceit of himself, and therefore we leave him to God to convince him. Or, lest you should think, that you have now hit the nail on the head, and brought an unanswerable argument to prove Job an ungodly man, because his afflictions are such as no mortal man could inflict upon him, and therefore God, who is most just, and cannot be deceived, has in afflicting him thus heavily passed sentence on our side, that he is a wicked man. This argument seems to be taken out of Job's own confession, Chap. 19. 5, 6.

*{lest ye should say}* For Say, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{we have found out}* See on Chap. 17. 10.

*{wisdom}* See on Chap. 28. 12.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{thrusteth him down}* Drives him from one affliction to another, as the wind does the leaf.

*{and not man}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

**V. 14. ***{Now he hath not directed his words against me; neither will I answer him with your speeches}* In these words Elihu seeks to get Job's good will, that he might the more willingly attend to what he had to say. He intimates therefore to Job's friends, that he would not deal with him as they had done, neither in the manner, nor matter of his speeches. Would not be so harsh in words against him, as they were: For Job had not spoken anything to provoke him, as they conceived he had done against them: Neither would he bring such weak reasons as they had done against Job; for that would do no good; but he would bring milder words, and better arguments; and so it seems he did; for he stopped Job's mouth, and prepared him to submit to God's following discourse.

*{Now he hath not directed}* Not set his words in order, as soldiers in battle array to fight against me. Heb. *ordered*. See on Chap. 13. 18.

*{his words against me}* Or, *his words to me*. He has not disputed against me, but against you; yet seeing you cannot answer him, I will endeavor to do it.

*{neither will I answer him}* See on Chap. 15. 13. & 20. 10. on the words, *Turnest, and Restore*. Here it is put for returning words, that is, answering.

*{with your speeches}* With your arguments, or rather with other as weak as they. I will bring stronger arguments against him, and come better prepared for the combat. It is true, that Elihu had better arguments, yet sometimes he used such as they did, but in milder words, and to better purpose; not to prove Job a wicked man, but to show that he had carried himself irreverently in his speeches toward God.

**V. 15. ***{They were amazed, they answered no more, they left off speaking}* These words are not the writer's, but Elihu's own, turning away his speech from Job's three friends to the auditors (whereof there might be some store at so great a dispute) that he might gird them the more for their ignorance.

*{They were amazed}* They were so confounded with Job's arguments, that though Elihu had rubbed them up, and stayed awhile, to see what they would do, yet dared they not enter into the lists with Job any more. They were so amazed, that they were even broken in their understanding, as it were, and utterly unfit to dispute with Job any more.

*{they answered no more}* They were as mute as fishes. See on Ch. 16. 1.

*{they left off speaking}* Heb. *They removed speeches from themselves.* That is, speech was removed from them. They were as silent as if they had lost their natural faculty of speaking. See the like phrase Chap. 3. 3. It was said, Heb. *one said*.

**V. 16. ***{When I had waited (for they spake not, but stood still and answered no more)}* This verse, and the former may both be read in the present tense, *They are amazed, they answer no more, they leave off speaking. Seeing I have waited, but they have not spoken, but stood still, and answered no more, I will answer, &c.* So the preter tense is put for the present, Psal. 1. 1. And, is translated, Seeing, Chap. 28. 21, 13. But Chap. 23. 6. You see that they will not be provoked to speak, therefore I will.

*{When I had waited}* For the answer of Job's three friends to his last speech.

*{for they spake not}* See on Chap. 13. 22. They deceived my expectation, I could get no words from them.

*{but stood still}* Their tongue stood still: They spoke never a word.

*{and answered no more}* See on Chap. 16. 1. He uses many words to the same purpose to show their great silence, which he much marveled at.

**V. 17. ***{I said I will answer also my part, I also will shew mine opinion}* Here is Elihu's progress in his speech after some silence. In which you have, 1. His resolution to speak again in this verse. 2. His ability, verse 18. 3. His instigation, verse 19, 20. 4. His caution in speaking, verse 21, 22. The sum of this verse is, that Elihu conceives it was now come to his turn to speak, and necessity called him to it, seeing all the rest held their peace.

*{I said}* This is not in the original, and may better be left out, as was showed in the various reading of the former verse.

*{I will answer also}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{my part}* I will take a turn, and supply what was wanting in your answers. See on Chap. 20. 29.

*{I also will shew mine opinion}* See on verse 6.

**V. 18. ***{For I am full of matter, the spirit within me constraineth me}* Though your store be done, yet I come well prepared. I have such store of solid and convincing arguments, that though I would never so fain contain myself, my spirit will not suffer me.

*{For I am full}* I have abundance, I am like a woman with child: I cannot keep it in.

*{of matter}* Heb. *of words*. Of good arguments to convince Job, which you cannot do.

*{the spirit}* It may be understood of God's Spirit, or his own. For spirit, see on Chap. 15. 2. upon Vain knowledge.

*{within me}* Heb. *of my belly*. By the belly is meant the internal parts, where the soul has his mansion. Prov. 20. 27, 30. & 22. 18. Hab. 3. 16. Joh. 7. 38. See more on Chap. 15. 2.

*{constraineth me}* The thoughts of my heart are like wind in my belly. They do so pinch me, that I cannot keep them in. They want room, and press to come out, Psal. 39. 3. Jer. 20. 9.

**V. 19. ***{Behold my belly is as wine, which hath no vent, it is ready to burst like new bottles}* In this verse he sets out the trouble that he had to keep in his thoughts. They were too many to be enclosed in so little room as his breast. In the next he shows the way of easing himself. His trouble he sets out in this verse by a similitude from wine shut up in new bottles, which if it find not a way out, will make a way. He elegantly compares words shut up in the mind desirous to utter them, to wine, the soul to bottles, silence to the stopple, which keeps in the wine, the grief of the mind hereupon to the breaking of the bottles, speech to the opening of them, by taking away the stopple of silence. The sum is, he should be much troubled if he might not have free license to vent his mind fully in this business.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{my belly}* See on Chap. 15. 2.

*{is as wine}* As new wine, which being not thoroughly purged, cannot be shut up in bottles without danger of breaking, Mat. 9. 17.

*{which hath no vent}* Heb. *which is not opened*. Therefore some read it, *my belly is as a bottle of wine. which is not opened:* Fetching the word bottle out of the end of the verse: Others make it two similitudes: one in the beginning of the verse, taken from great vessels of new wine, stopped up before they have done working, which makes the vessels break: The other in the end of the verse, from bottles that are stopped too close, which are burst by the force of the wine shut up in them, and seeking to break prison. Elihu's intent, by one, or both similitudes, was to show what a trouble it was to him to refrain from speaking. For the word see on Chap. 29. 19. upon the word, *Spread out*.

*{it is ready to burst}* Heb. *it will be cleft asunder*. See on Chap. 26. 8. on the word, *Rent*. He shows the peril of not speaking. It would make his mind as useless as a broken bottle. Or, he sets out the necessity of speaking by fullness of thoughts working in his mind, which will break out, do he what he can.

*{like new bottles}* Though new bottles be stronger than old to keep in wine, yet the wine may be so strong that it may break them. So Elihu's desire of speaking was so great, that though he should do all he could to keep in his words, yet they would burst out. Piscator conceives by new bottles to be meant bottles of new wine, because else it seems to cross our Savior's speech, Matth. 9. 17. Who makes old bottles more subject to be broken by the wine, and new ones to hold strongest. As some in the beginning of the verse take wine for bottles, so in the end bottles may be taken for bottles of new wine.

**V. 20. ***{I will speak that I may be refreshed: I will open my lips, and answer}* Therefore will I speak, that I may take breath, and that I may at length freely pour out that I have so long time conceived and concealed in my breast.

*{I will speak}* See on Chap. 13. 22.

*{that I may be refreshed}* Heb. *that I may breath*. That I may not be so straitened as before (in verse 18, 19.) but may have more space to put forth my thoughts, and enlarge myself.

*{I will open my lips}* I will begin to speak. For as a man cannot speak ordinarily without opening his lips, so a dumb man may open his lips, that cannot speak at all. And they that can, may open their lips for other ends, as to eat, drink, or breathe. For open, see on Chap. 29. 19. on the words, *spread out*. For lips, see on Chap. 15. 6.

*{and answer}* Answer Job better than you have done. Or *speak my mind*; for so the word is used for speaking first, and translated so, Chap. 3. 2. See more on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 21. ***{Let me not I pray you accept any mans person, neither let me give flattering titles unto man}* In this verse he sets down the manner how he purposes to carry himself in his ensuing discourse, and gives the reason of his wariness in the next verse. Elihu desires in this verse, that they would not be offended, if without regarding any man's favor or authority, so as to prejudice the truth, he speak his mind freely, and forbear pleasing titles, and flattering proems.

*{Let me not}* Do not expect it of me, nor be offended if I do not. Or *I will not*. So אל is used, Psal. 121. 3, 4.

*{I pray you}* Or, *now*, as that particle is translated, Chap. 5. 1.

*{accept any mans person}* Heb. *accept the face of man*. I will not regard any man in this dispute out of favor, but deal fairly according to the truth of the cause. Many judge amiss, because they look on men's faces, or have respect to kindred, friendship, might, more than to the right, I will not so do. I will neither look upon you as opponents, nor upon Job as an answerer, but indifferently upon the thing in question, and determine also accordingly. This fault of accepting persons Job had taxed his friends for, Chap. 13. 7. Elihu will not offend him in that kind, nor his friends neither. For the phrase, see on Chap. 13. 8. For accepting, see on Chap. 21. 3. upon the word, *suffer*. For person, or face, see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word *countenance*. For man, see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{neither let me give flattering titles}* The word is used only here, and in the next verse, and Isa. 44. 5. & 45. 4. and there it signifies no more than to name. Neither can it be as Mercer, and others, to use hidden titles, intimating that he would not talk at rovers, as they did, telling Job that wicked men were confounded by God, but not calling him so; but would directly accuse him, for this is contrary to Elihu's scope, who desired to justify Job, and to the use of the word in Isaiah, where no such thing is intended. But seeing the word is plainly used in an ill sense here, as appears verse 22. it must needs be taken for giving flattering titles, as *Semper Augustus* to the German Emperors. He would not flatter Job nor them with high titles, though they were ancienter than he, but would carry the business by mere strength of argument, not by oratorical and theatrical insinuations.

*{unto man}* See on Chap. 14. 1.

**V. 22. ***{For I know not to give flattering titles, in so doing my maker would soon take me away}* Here he gives two reasons why he would not flatter. The one, because he had not been so bred, but in a plain downright way, not using to flatter great men. The other, because he feared God would inflict some great judgment upon him, if he should so do. I know not how to go about such an unusual work; and if I had a will to go about it, I should look upon God, as on one having some great judgment ready to destroy me.

*{For I know not}* I am not acquainted with such courses. Or, I allow not of such ways: As this word is used, Psal. 1. 6.

*{to give flattering titles}* See on verse 21. where the same word is used. Heb. *I know not how I should give flattering titles.*

*{in so doing}* This is not in the original, but well added, to show the true meaning of the text.

*{my Maker}* He that created me. Or, he that formed me in the womb. See on Chap. 10. 8. And on Chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *Bringeth forth*.

*{should take me away}* From among men by some violent death, Psal. 28. 3. Job 27. 21. For the word, see on Chap. 21. 3. upon the word, *Suffer*.

## CHAPTER 33

**Verse 1. ***{Wherefore Job I pray thee, hear my speeches, and hearken to all my words}* Now Elihu turns his speech from Job's friends unto Job himself and falls close to his work. In the 33, 34, and 35. Chapters, he repeats divers of Job's speeches, and answers them. In the 36. and 37. Chapters, he confutes those complaints which Job had rashly poured out against God. And sometimes he calls upon Job to answer for himself, that he might not say, he had no liberty to clear himself. In this chapter, there is, 1. His preface to Job, to persuade him to attend, and to be willing to be convinced, and that unto verse 8. 2. The confutation of some of Job's speeches, to v. 31. 3. The conclusion, to the end of the chapter. In the preface. 1. He calls upon Job for audience, verse 1, 2. 2. He uses arguments to persuade him, and that 1. From the matter he had to speak, verse 3, 4. 2. From the manner of pleading, verse 5, 6, 7. In his invitation. 1. He exhorts Job to hear him, verse 1. 2. He shows his willingness to inform him, verse 2. In this verse he intimates thus much, that though Job's friends were much to blame in condemning him, yet he had rather spend time in setting Job right, than in quarreling with them, and therefore principally desires audience of him whom it most concerned.

*{Wherefore}* Heb. *And truly.*

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{I pray thee}* He turns his speech to Job, and speaks by way of entreaty, wishing him not to think hardly of him because his friends dealt so hardly with him, nor to despise him because of his youth, but to give good ear to him.

*{hear}* See on Chap. 13. 17.

*{my speeches}* Which I shall utter for your good.

*{and hearken}* Give ear to them: so much the word imports in the original.

*{to all}* I will be very careful of my words, not to speak one word but what shall be profitable unto you; and therefore I would have you attend so diligently, that not one of my words be lost.

*{my words}* See on Chap. 19. 28. upon the word, *Matter*.

**V. 2. ***{Behold now I have opened my mouth, my tongue hath spoken in my mouth}* I have begun to speak (in the former chapter) and I am willing to proceed; be careful therefore to attend.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{now}* See on Chap. 32. 21. on, I pray you.

*{I have opened}* See on Chap. 32. 20. & 29. 19. upon the word, *spread out*.

*{my mouth}* See on Chap. 15. 5.

*{my tongue}* See on Chap. 15. 5.

*{hath spoken}* See on Chap. 13. 22.

*{in my mouth}* Heb. *in my palat*. A figure of the part for the whole. Or, *with my palat*; for both tongue and palate are instruments of speaking. So ב is translated, with Chap. 16. 10.

**V. 3. ***{My words shall be of the uprightness of my heart, and my lips shall utter knowledge clearly}* He persuades Job to hear him. 1. Because of his upright dealing with him, in this verse. And 2. Because of the same nature. He was a man like to Job, verse 4. In this verse he dissuades Job from being angry, if he should speak some things that might displease him; for if Job as a man might err, another man might see to set him right better than himself, we all favor ourselves too much. Job had found his friends too harsh, and Elihu might well fear that therefore he would not hear him patiently, but might fear that he would deal so with him also. He assures him therefore, that he would not be like his other friends, but would deal very candidly with him.

*{My words}* Which I shall now utter to show your error, O! Job.

*{shall be of the uprightness of my heart}* Rather, *shall be out of*; or *according to the uprightnesse of my heart*. I will speak as I think. A secret reproof to Job's friends, who, as Elihu conceives, spoke worse of him than they could think in their hearts. However Elihu did not intend to make the uprightness of his heart the subject of his speech, as the translation seems to carry it (see the like phrase, Psal. 45. 1.) but to intimate to Job that he would not dissemble with him. Heb. *My words shall be the uprightnesse of my heart.* That is, I will speak most uprightly. Or, *shall utter the uprightnesse of my heart*. So the beginning of the verse is supplied out of the end, Psal. 70. 1. For heart, see on Chap. 15. 12.

*{and my lips}* See on Chap. 15. 6.

*{shall utter knowledge}* Heb. *shall speak knowledge*. I will speak what I know to be true.

*{clearly}* I will as it were fan or scour my words from all chaff and dross, Zeph. 3. 9. Isa. 49. 2. He would not speak false or deceitful words. See on Chap. 22. 30. on the word, *Pureness*.

**V. 4. ***{The spirit of God hath made me, and and the breath of the Almighty hath given me life}* Some knit this verse to the former, and make this to be the sense of it, You may hearken to me, though I be younger than you are, for I am a man as well as you, and have a soul and body as you have, and therefore as well know what is right. Others knit it to the verses following, as if Elihu had said, I am not God, to terrify you, but a man altogether like yourself; and therefore you may boldly reason with me, and have a fair opportunity to clear yourself, if you can.

*{The spirit}* See on chap. 15. 2. upon vain knowledge.

*{of God}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{hath made me}* See on chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *Bringeth forth*.

*{and the breath}* An allusion to Gen. 2. 7. To man's creation at first, where the body came by formation, and the soul by inspiration. So do both also in the womb. The one is made, the other inspired by God's Spirit.

*{of the Almighty}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{hath given me life}* By breathing a living soul into me, chap. 10. 9, 12.

**V. 5. ***{If thou canst answer me, set thy words in order before me stand up}* Now he persuades Job to audience from the manner of his speaking, wherein. 1. He gives him leave to speak freely for himself, in this v. 2. He gives him a ground of free speech, in that he speaks not to God, but to a man like himself, v. 6. 3. He shows the effect of this ground, he neither can, nor will terrify or trouble him, as God might if he should reason with him, v. 7. The sum of this is, as if Elihu should have said, I intend not to take advantage of your afflictions to insult over you as your friends have done; but give you free leave, when I have spoken to use all your strength to answer for yourself.

*{If thou canst answer me}* I purpose to use undeniable arguments, yet if you think you can overthrow them, come into the lists. Others read it with a comma between, thus, *If thou canst, do thou answer me.* I give you free leave to do it, and to give answer to every one of my arguments, I will hear, as well as speak, v. 32. For Answer, see on chap. 20. 10. & 15. 13. on the words, *Restore, Turnest*.

*{set thy words in order before me}* Muster up your answers like so many soldiers to oppose me. A metaphor from military affairs, wherein soldiers are set in ranks to resist the enemy setting upon them. See on chap. 32. 14. & 13. 18.

*{stand up}* Broughton reads it, *Stand to it*; Deodat, *present thy selfe to the combat*. Heb. *Set thy selfe.* Or, *Settle thy self.* He continuing in his military metaphor. Either he means that Job should like a soldier take his ground to show his readiness to defend himself, or stand his ground and not give way to his arguments against him, if he be able to confute them. Thus valiant soldiers choose and maintain their ground.

**V. 6. ***{Behold I am according to thy wish in God's stead, I also am formed out of the clay}* I have heard your wish, O Job, that you might dispute with God, provided, that he would not daunt you with his majesty. Lo, you have your wish; I that am a man every way like yourself, will plead God's cause against you. Set then upon the work.

*{Behold}* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{I am according to thy wish}* Heb. *I am according to thy mouth.* You did wish for a free and equal handling of your cause, now you may have it. chap. 9. 34, 35. & 13. 18. & 31. 35. & 23. 3, 4. For mouth, see on chap. 15. 2. This agrees well with Job's desire in the places quoted. Others read it thus, *I am as thou*. Heb. *According to thy measure.* So this word is used only to set out a likeness of two things. Numb. 6. 21. & 7. 5. And this answers well to the words following of an equal formation.

*{in God's stead.}* Heb. *For God*, as chap. 13. 7. I will be his deputy, and plead his cause for him, and show, how you have wronged him. You shall not need so earnestly to seek him to dispute with him, I will undertake the business for him. Others read it, *I am Gods*, or, *I belong to God, as thou dost*, For I also am made by him; as follows. For God, see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{I also}* See on chap. 16. 4.

*{Am formed}* Heb. *Am cut out.* An allusion to the potters, who when they purpose to make an earthen vessel, cut out a lump of clay to make it of: So did God form Adam's body, Gen. 2. 7. To which Elihu now alludes, instancing rather in Job's and his joint original, than several framing in the womb. For conjunction works stronger on men's affections, than division. Neither was Elihu so formed in the womb, but in Adam his first parent, as Job also was.

*{out of the clay}* See on chap. 13. 12.

**V. 7. ***{Behold my terror shall not make thee afraid, neither shall my hand be heavy upon thee}* I cannot frighten you with my glory, nor lay such diseases upon you, as God can, and therefore you need not be afraid to reason freely with me. These were the two things that Job desired of God, chap. 9. 34. & 13. 21. And Elihu grants both, so that now Job was much to blame, if he could not clear himself upon his own terms. He has now a man, as he was, to reason the case with him, as he did desire, chap. 9. 32. And therefore must make his cause good, or be without excuse. Thus Elihu like a cunning disputant takes advantage of all Job's words.

*{behold}* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{my terror shall not make thee afraid.}* See on chap. 9. 34. & 13. 21.

*{neither shall my hand}* See on chap. 13. 21.

*{be heavy upon thee}* Lay load on you, or punish you sorely, as God has now done, and you fear he will do more, though you unjustly conceive he has no cause so to do. See the phrase, Psal. 32. 4.

**V. 8. ***{Surely thou hast spoken in mine hearing, and I have heard the voice of thy words, saying}* Here begins Elihu's charge against Job, wherein having finished his preface, he begins to confute those things, which he did dislike in Job's former speeches. The charge is laid down, in general in this v. In particular, v. 9, 10, 11. The answer holds to the end of the chapter: and consists of two heads. 1. That God is not tied to give an account of his actions, v. 12, 13. 2. That though his actions may be displeasing to flesh and blood; yet he makes men understand by the issue, that if they stoop to his proceedings, all is for their good. The sum of this v. is, as if he had said, I meddle not with those things, which you have spoken well, not with your life, I deny not, but your afflictions are heavy. I confess, you have spoken many things very truly, and wisely concerning God. But yet I must needs tax you for some things indiscreetly spoken, and such as you cannot deny; I will not charge you with odious consequences to take advantage against you, but with such things, as I have heard with my own ears.

*{Surely}* The thing is unquestionable. Deny you cannot. See if you can defend it.

*{thou hast spoken}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{in mine hearing}* Heb. *In mine eares.* See on Chap. 15. 21. on the word, *Ears*.

*{and I have heard}* See on Chap. 13. 17.

*{the voice}* See on Chap. 15. 21. on the word, *Sound*.

*{of thy words}* Of things indiscreetly spoken by you, whereof now I must charge you.

*{saying}* This is added to usher in the following accusations.

**V. 9. ***{I am clean without transgression; I am innocent, neither is there iniquity in me}* Three things are here laid to Job's charge by Elihu. 1. That he should deny himself to be sinful, in this v. 2. That he accused God of picking quarrels with him, v. 10. 3. Of afflicting him over-heavily, v. 11. True it is, that Job in his eager maintaining his innocency, and sense of his pains cast out such rash speeches against God, and was therefore worthy to be blamed, yet did he many times recollect himself, & speak meanly of himself, and reverently of God, acknowledging his own sin, and God's justice. The accusation in this v. seems to be gathered out of these places, Chap. 10. 7. & 16. 17. & 23. 10, 11. & 27. 5.

*{I am clean without transgression}* Elihu understands Job, as if he did not acknowledge himself guilty of any sin. For transgression, see on Chap. 7. 21.

*{I am innocent}* It comes from a word, that signifies to protect: thence also comes another word, that signifies an haven, as if Job had said. I am safe, my innocency will be a protection and haven to me. The Rabbins use the word for combing, as if he had said, I am as neat, as a man that has combed his head, and had not an hair amiss.

*{neither is there iniquity in me}* See on Chap. 7. 21.

**V. 10. ***{Behold, he findeth occasions against me, he counteth me for his enemy}* This accusation is picked out of Chap. 9. 17. & 13. 24. & 14. 17. & 16. 9. & 19. 11.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{he findeth}* See on Chap. 17. 10. God finds.

*{occasions}* Picks out somewhat to quarrel against my actions. Or, *Breaches.* That is, occasions to break off his favor from me, and to destroy me. So the word is used, Numb. 14. 34.

*{against me}* To accuse me, as giving cause of my own destruction.

*{he counteth me for his enemy}* These words are expounded on Chap. 13. 24. & 19. 11.

**V. 11. ***{He putteth my feet in the stocks; he marketh all my paths}* Here Elihu quotes Job's very words, Chap. 13. 27. where all these words were expounded. Let the reader see there. And the latter part also is charged upon God by Job, Chap. 14. 16.

**V. 12. ***{Behold, in this thou art not just: I will answer thee that God is greater then man}* Elihu's first answer is contained in this v. and the next. The ground is this, to wit, God's greatness. The conclusion from hence v. 13. An impossibility for any creature to bring God to an account for his actions. The sum of this v. is, as if he had said, I will not call in question your life or conversation. I believe you have carried yourself well formerly toward God and man, as you have professed, and many can bear witness. But I must needs blame you for your late irreverent expressions against God, as if you had forgotten the distance between God and you: Job's friends had touched upon this string before, of God's greatness; but it was to a different end. Their scope was to prove Job to be a wicked man, because the great God had so greatly afflicted him. But Elihu's intent is, to show Job his error in that he had in unsavory expressions through infirmity and out of the greatness of his pain carried himself irreverently toward God, which he had need to repent of, though he were a godly man. They sought to bring Job to despair; Elihu endeavors to bring him to repentance, which God effects afterwards.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{in this}* That you brag of your own innocency, complain of God's dealing too hardly with you, and desire to contend with him in judgment about it.

*{thou art not just}* You have offended, and cannot be justified. See on Chap. 9. 20.

*{I will answer thee}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{that God is greater then man}* Or, *that God is more then man.* More in majesty and power, in wisdom and justice. And therefore you have much forgotten yourself, and have not carried yourself so humbly and reverently toward him as you ought, but overboldly, in seeking to contend with him, as if you were his equal. For God, see on Chap. 4. 9. & 11. 5. For greater, on Chap. 32. 9. For man, on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

**V. 13. ***{Why dost thou strive against him? for he giveth not account of any of his matters}* What presumption is this, that you dare plead with God, who is supreme over all, and neither uses, nor is bound to give a reason to any man of his actions, as an inferior.

*{Why dost thou strive}* Contend with him in judgment, or plead with him. See on Chap. 13. 6. on the word, *Pleadings*.

*{against him}* Against God. Which is also understood in verse 10. See there.

*{for}* You have no reason so to do, but desire it in vain, for God will not be brought to a reckoning. Or, *that*. Why do you so often complain, that God will not give you a reason why he lays so many afflictions upon you. As Chap. 10. 2. & 13. 23, 24. & 19. 7. & 23. 4, 5.

*{he giveth not account}* Heb. *he answereth not*. If you demand a reason, he is not tied to give it. For answering, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{of any of his matters}* Heb. *of all his matters*. But it is well translated, any, as also, Psal. 147. 20. For though God please sometimes to condescend so low, as to give a reason of some of his actions, yet it sets out his majesty more to say, that he is not tied to give account of any one of them. For matters, see on chap. 19. 28.

**V. 14. ***{For God speaketh once, yea twice, yet man perceiveth it not}* Though God be not tied to give an account of his actions, nor will not admit man to contend with him in judgment, yet does he sometimes find out gracious ways, or means, to acquaint man with his will, who yet is so senseless, that he apprehends not what God aims at in his proceedings with him. So that if you O Job understand it not, the cause is not God's silence, but your dullness. Here begins Elihu's second answer to Job's former speeches, which is set down. 1. In general in this verse. 2. In particular, from verse 15, to verse 29. 3. He sums up altogether, ver. 29. 30.

*{For}* Rather, *When*. As it is translated, Chap. 7. 13. So it makes way for *Then*, in ver. 16. And here is no reason given, as this translation seems to intimate, nor no opposition to what went before, as they think, who translate it, *But*.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{speaketh}* Makes his mind known, as men do theirs by words. See on Chap. 13. 22.

*{once}* To some men, but once: to others, oftener.

*{yea twice}* A certain number for an uncertain, as appears by ver. 29. See the like phrase, Psal. 62. 11.

*{Yet man perceiveth it not}* He is so dull by nature, that he cannot perceive God's meaning. Others read it thus, *or twice, if a man perceive it not*. Or. *To him that perceives it not*. If a man regards not God's speech at first, he will speak again to him, till he do take notice of it, verse 16. 1 Sam. 3. 4, 6, 8, 10.

**V. 15. ***{In a dream, in a vision of the night, when deep sleep falleth upon men, in slumberings upon the bed}* Now he sets out the several ways God used to make his will known to his people by, before the Scriptures were written. 1. By dreams and visions, to verse 19. 2. By sicknesses and diseases, to ver. 23. 3. By prophets, to verse 29. In the first part, 1. The way of God's revealing himself is set down in this verse. 2. The benefit of it, from ver. 16 to v. 19. In this verse he informs them that which Eliphaz had done before, Chap. 4. 12, 13. That God did use to teach men by dreams, and night visions.

*{in a dream}* God has taught men, not only things that concern the public, but also such things as make for their own souls, in dreams, Gen. 41. 1, 28. & 20. 6.

*{it a vision of the night, when deep sleep falleth upon men}* See all these words expounded on Chap. 4. 13. Some read it, *in a dream of a vision of the night*. And that is best, for in dreams in the night men often see visions in their sleep, seldom when they lie awake. Neither does the speaker intend two ways of revelation here, but one.

*{in slumberings upon the bed}* Some make this all one with the former, and conceive that the word signifies any kind of sleep. Others, that it signifies only a light kind of sleep, which we call slumbering, and so it is commonly translated. And they make the sense to be this; that God does not only make his will known to men when they are sound on sleep, but also when they are between sleeping and waking.

**V. 16. ***{Then he openeth the ears of men, and sealeth their instruction}* Now follow three fruits of God's speaking to men in visions. 1. Instruction in this verse. 2. Humiliation, verse 17. 3. Salvation, verse 18. For the first. God's word being not then written, when he pleased to make men to know his will, he would not lose his labor, but made them ready to hearken to it, and ready to retain it. Others understand it of threatening them with afflictions, for neglecting him in dreams. But the former sense runs smoother with the words aforegoing and following.

*{Then}* When God has spoken to a man in a dream: verse 14, 15.

*{he openeth the ears of men}* Hereby is signified making men to hear; for when men's ears are stopped, they cannot hear till the obstacle be removed. God will make them to regard what he says, both with their ears, and heart. The same phrase is used, Chap. 36. 10, 15. Heb. *He revealeth, or uncovereth*. That men may hear the better. For ears, see on Chap. 15. 21. For men, see on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{and sealeth their instruction}* Leaves such an impression upon their spirits, by this extraordinary way of revealing himself to them, that they never forget it all their life after, Deut. 32. 34.

**V. 17. ***{That he may withdraw man from his purpose, and hide pride from man}* God, by making himself known to man by dreams and visions, brings him to repentance, and keeps him from pride.

*{That he may withdraw man from his purpose}* That he may keep him from doing mischief to others, as he did intend. So God withdrew Abimelech, Gen. 20. 6. And Laban, Gen. 31. 24, 29. Or that he may draw him away from his former sinful courses by repentance. For withdraw, see on Chap. 12. 20. on the word, *Removeth*. For man, see on Chap. 14. 1. From, is added out of the end of the verse, to make up the sense. *His purpose*. Heb. *His work*. See on Chap. 14. 15. By work, is meant evil work, as by a wife, a good wife, Prov. 18. 22. By wool, white wool, Isa. 1. 18.

*{and hide pride from man}* And drive all pride out of him; that which is not, is as it were hid, for no man can see it, Chap. 3. 10. Or, take away all occasions of pride from him, by revealing to him his own vileness. For pride, see on Chap. 20. 25. on the word, *Body*. For man, see on Chap. 14. 10. It may be Elihu might think that Job had bragged too much of his good deeds and honors, chap. 29. & 31. And therefore he puts him in mind of humbling himself.

**V. 18. ***{He keepeth back his soule from the pit, and his life from perishing by the sword}* God, by his former admonition of him in dreams, gives him wisdom to prevent destruction, that was ready to lay hold on him.

*{He keepeth back}* Or, *That he may keep back*. And then it runs parallel with the former verse. He preserves it, as men preserve choice things from loss, or hurt, Gen. 22. 12, 16.

*{his soul}* That is, the man himself: Or rather, *his body*. See on chap. 14. 22.

*{from the pit}* From the grave. It comes from a word that signifies corruption, for in the grave men's carcasses putrefy, and molder away. The same word is used, verse 24. It intimates death and destruction; for beside, that none but dead men are put in the grave; if a living man be cast into a pit where he cannot get out, he will die for want of food. Some go further, and think it sets out the manner of death. He will deliver him from the plots of enemies, who do as it were make pitfalls for him, as men do to catch birds and beasts: And so they oppose it to the end of the verse; intimating, that God will deliver him, both from plots of enemies, and from open hostility. See on chap. 7. 9.

*{and his life}* This shows what was meant by the soul before.

*{from perishing by the sword}* Heb. *from passing through, or, by the sword*: Or, *from passing over into the sword*. From destruction by war; God will not give him over into the power of the sword no more than of secret plots, or he shall not be destroyed by the sword no more than by diseases, not by God's hand nor man's, Ch. 36. 12. So we read of passing through the fire, not for escaping it, but for being burned by it, 2 Kin. 16. 3. Through the brick-kilns, 2 Sam. 12. 31. For men by sword or fire pass out of this world to another. For passing through, see on Chap. 15. 19. For the word, sword, some translate it, *dart*; for as a sword is taken out of the sheath, and thrust into the body, so a dart is cast forth to kill men. Their sense is, that he shall pass through a company of darters, or pikemen, as a deer through a company of archers, when every one shoots an arrow at it, and no man can hit it; so shall he escape all dangers.

**V. 19. ***{He is chastened also with pain upon his bed, and the multitude of his bones with strong pain}* He comes now to the second means, by which God made his will known to men before the Scriptures were written; intimating, that such as will not be taught by visions, shall be informed by sickness, which sickness is set out by the effects of it. 1. Pain, verse 19. 2. Loss of appetite, verse 20. 3. Consumption of flesh, verse 21. 4. Danger of death, verse 22. All these (save that we find not, that he kept his bed) Job had now experience of.

*{He is chastened}* The word signifies both verbally to reprove, and actually to correct; for when God corrects a man for sin, laying sore diseases upon him, he does, as it were from heaven, chide him for his fault. See on Chap. 15. 3. on the word, *Reason*.

*{also}* This is another way, whereby God makes men to know their sins, and his will.

*{with pain}* Arising from his disease which God inflicts on him. The word signifies both the disease of body, and the grief of mind arising thereupon.

*{upon his bed}* Not with an ordinary disease, or pain, but such an one as makes him keep his bed, Psal. 41. 3.

*{and the multitude of his bones with strong pain}* All his bones, which are many, are full of great pain by reason of the strength of his disease. So the same phrase is translated, Chap. 4. 14. Others read it, *though the multitude of his bones be strong*, yet his pain is so great, that he cannot bear it. Others thus, *and the contention of his bones is strong*: That is, the pain which God contending with him for his sin, inflicts upon him, Psal. 38. 3, 6. For multitude, see on Chap. 12. 5. on the word, *great*. For bones, see on Chap. 20. 11.

**V. 20. ***{So that his life abhorreth bread, and his soul dainty meat}* Loathing of meat follows sickness, and consuming of the substance of the body follows that, verse 21. and danger of life ensues, verse 22. See the like, Psal. 107. 18. All diseases weaken the appetite, but some are so sharp that they take it clean away, so that no meat pleases, though never so delicate; nay nothing is so much displeasing as meat, they cannot endure the sight of it.

*{So that his life}* His life is so full of pain that he cannot eat.

*{abhorreth}* As filth, which man's nature cannot endure. The word is nowhere else read, and this signification the Hebrew doctors put upon it.

*{bread}* See on Chap. 15. 23.

*{and his soul}* See on Chap. 14. 22.

*{dainty meats}* Heb. *meat of desire*. That is, such as men use to desire for the pleasant taste of it, and such as himself would have been glad of in his health. See the like phrase, Dan. 10. 3. 2 Chron. 32. 27. Psal. 107. 30. Amos 5. 11.

**V. 21. ***{His flesh is consumed away that it cannot be seen, and his bones that were not seen, stick out}* All his limbs being brought low, and made lean by reason of his disease, stand staring out in such a sort, that except his bones, there is no one thing to be seen in him that represents the shape of a man, nothing but leanness and deformity appears, he is become an anatomy.

*{His flesh}* It was full, fat, and beautiful before, but now nothing remains but skin and bone; the flesh is all gone, no footsteps remain to show what it was. For flesh, see on Chap. 14. 22.

*{is consumed away}* Is quite gone, so that nothing is left but skin and bones, Psal. 102. 5.

*{that it cannot be seen}* It is not there to be seen, no man can perceive it.

*{and his bones}* See on Chap. 20. 11.

*{that were not seen}* That could not be discerned before, because they were all covered over with flesh and fat, now the flesh being gone, they stick out. For the word, see on Chap. 16. 27. on the word, *Behold*.

*{stick out}* Are not kept in by the flesh as formerly. Some read it, *are diminished*. God's hand is so heavy upon him, that not only it consumes his flesh, but also grinds his bones to powder. Psal. 31. 10. & 102. 3. & 51. 8.

**V. 22. ***{Yea his soul draweth near unto the grave, and his life to the destroyer}* The failing of his appetite makes his flesh to consume, and his life to be in danger.

*{Yea his soul}* See on Chap. 14. 22.

*{draweth near}* Is in great danger of death. As appears by his wonderful recovery, ver. 26.

*{unto the grave}* See on verse 18. on the word *pit*.

*{and his life}* Which is the ground of all comfort. Life itself is departing from him.

*{to the destroyers}* Heb. *to those that kill men*. Which may be understood either of enemies, or diseases.

**V. 23. ***{If there be a messenger with him, an interpreter, one among a thousand to shew unto man his uprightness.}* This is the third way, whereby God used to make his will known to men in those days. Some he taught by dreams, others by sickness, others by men more skillful than themselves, others take all these means to have respect to one man. First God acquaints him with his will by a dream. If he will not take notice thereby, he inflicts sickness on him to make him look about what is amiss in him, for which God should correct him. If that work not on him, he sends another choice man to inform him. In this third way of revealing himself to men. First, he sets out man's taking notice of it, from ver. 23. to ver. 27. 2. God's taking notice of man's repentance, ver. 27, 28. In the first you have. 1. The success of the interpreter's message, in this verse. 2. The benefits which come thereby. From ver. 24. to ver. 27.

*{If there be a messenger}* A man sent of God to make known his will. Some understand it of an angel from heaven, but that is not likely, that God sent such ordinarily. Either he sent some prophet, or some teacher, which are called messengers, and angels. Malac. 3. 1. Rev. 1. 20.

*{with him}* With the sick man to tell him why God afflicts him. Or, with any other man. For so the antecedent is picked out of the words following. Psal. 87. 1, 2. & 116. 1.

*{an interpreter}* As a prophet, or a minister, is God's messenger, because sent by him; So he is an interpreter, because he is sent to declare to man what God would have him to do. Or, to the sick man why God afflicts him. For the word, see on Chap. 16. 20.

*{one among a thousand}* A rare man for gifts and parts picked out of a thousand men. See the like phrase, Eccl. 7. 28. He seems to intimate, that himself is such an one, and therefore Job may do well to hearken to him.

*{to shew}* To make plainly known unto him. See on Chap. 15. 18. upon the word, *Told*.

*{unto man}* See on Chap. 14. 1.

*{his uprightness}* God's upright dealing with him, that he does not afflict him without just cause, and therefore he ought to repent. Or, *man's uprightness*. That is, what is his duty to God, and how he ought to walk uprightly with him.

**V. 24. ***{Then he is gracious unto him, and saith, Deliver him from going down to the pit, I have found a ransom}* The benefits of the good success of the interpreter's message follow. Which are 1. Preservation of his life, in this verse, 2. Restoration of his health and strength, verse 25. 3. Restitution of the sense of God's favor, verse 26. How desperate soever the sick man's, or any other man's case be, yet God will find a way to save his life, when his messenger has brought him to repentance.

*{Then}* When he has hearkened to the voice of God's messenger, and given glory to God.

*{he is gracious unto him}* God will show him mercy. By healing him, and being reconciled to him, as vers. 25, 26.

*{and saith}* To that messenger. He gives him commission upon the man's penitency to promise him recovery. Or, God does make known some way to him, that he will deliver him. For the word, see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{Deliver him}* Acquaint him with my will. Tell him that I will deliver him. Or, he shall be as certainly delivered, as if God had sent one from heaven to tell him so. So works of God's grace are sometimes attributed to his ministers, and instruments, Obad. ver. 21. 1 Cor. 9. 22. 1 Tim. 4. 16. Jam. 5. 20.

*{from going down}* See on Chap. 21. 13.

*{to the grave}* See on verse 18. on the word, *Pit*.

*{I have found}* I have received, and accepted. For the word, see on Chap. 17. 10.

*{a ransom}* Or, *an atonement*. He has humbled himself and I am as well satisfied, as if he had paid a great ransom. Or, I have received satisfaction by Christ for him. That Job had some knowledge of this, appears in the notes on Chap. 16. 21. & 17. 3. & 19. 25. The word signifies literally, to plaster over a thing, to cover it. So does a ransom cover sin, Psal. 32. 1.

**V. 25. ***{His flesh shall be fresher then a childs: he shall return to the days of his youth}* Immediately after God has thus spoken, even the very rotten flesh of that miserable creature shall appear tender and smooth, like unto the flesh of a young child, so that a man would think him to be restored again unto the fresh flower of his youth.

*{His flesh}* His body. See on Chap. 14. 22.

*{shall be fresher then a childs}* It was consumed, verse 21. But now it is returned, and full, and soft, and tender, full of moisture like a child's. Heb. *then childhood*, 2 King. 5. 14. Or, *then in his childhood*. He shall not only be as strong and lusty, as in his childhood, but more lively than ever he was. For the word, see on Chap. 13. 26.

*{He shall return to the days of his youth}* He shall not only recover his health, and strength, but as it were grow young again, Psal. 103. 5. For return, see on Chap. 14. 13. on the word, *past*. For days, see on Chap. 14. 14. & 15. 23. For youth, see on Chap. 20. 11.

**V. 26. ***{He shall pray unto God, and he will be favourable unto him, and he shall see his face with joy; for he will render unto man his righteousness}* He had before set down one means of the sick or sinful man's recovery: the messenger's information; now he sets down another, which is his own supplication, without which another man's information can do no good. When he sees his error, even before he be recovered, or in another conflict he may pray boldly to God, and God being reconciled to him will hear him.

*{He}* The sick man, or the man whom God instructs by his messenger. Some understand it of the messenger praying for him, but that agrees not so well with what follows.

*{shall pray}* See on Chap. 22. 27.

*{unto God}* See on Chap. 5. 17. & 11. 5.

*{and he will be favourable unto him}* God will be pleased with him, and look cheerfully on him in his conscience, as a man does on his friend, when he is reconciled.

*{and he shall see his face with joy}* When a man is offended he looks angrily, but when he is pleased he looks cheerfully. So he shall see God to look cheerfully on him, when he has humbled himself before him. Numb. 6. 25. Psal. 67. 1. & 10. 1. Or, *God will make him to see his face with joy*. The words will bear either. For See, look on Chap. 19. 27. on the word *Behold*. The word Joy signifies joyful acclamation, such as men use after benefits received. Such should this man have cause to express, when God looks cheerfully on him again. For Face see on Chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Countenance*.

*{for he will render unto man his righteousness}* As he dealt with him before, as if he were an ungodly man, laying heavy plagues upon him, so he will deal with him now kindly, in taking away those plagues, and prosper him, as he used to do to righteous men, especially in those days. He will deal with him according to his present righteousness, and not according to his former wickedness. Or, he will give him the reward of his righteousness. So sin is taken often for the punishment of sin. For Render, see on Chap. 15. 13. on the word, *Turnest*. And on Chap. 20. 10. on the word, *Restore*. For Man, see on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

**V. 27. ***{He looketh upon men; and if any say I have sinned, and perverted that which was right, and it profited me not}* Now follows God's taking notice of man's repentance in this verse, and the next; wherein you have, 1. The notice itself in this verse. 2. The effect of it, verse 28. This may be a fourth way whereby God brings man to repentance, besides dreams, sickness, messengers, to wit, to move men's hearts to a voluntary confession of their sins by his Spirit, without the former helps.

*{He}* Some understand it of the sick man recovered, and confessing he was justly punished. And they read it, *He looketh upon other men, and saith*. Others understand it of the interpreter. But it is best understood of God taking notice of man's humiliation, and delivering him out of his troubles.

*{looketh upon}* Considers and observes men's ways.

*{upon men}* See on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{and if any say}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{I have sinned}* This is the confession of the penitent sinner. See the like, 2 Sam. 12. 13. For the word see on Chap. 7. 20.

*{and perverted}* For the sense of this word, see on Chap. 7. 21. on the word, Iniquity, which comes from this root.

*{that which was right}* I have left the right way, to go in crooked ways. For the word, see on Chap. 1. 1. upon the word, *Upright*.

*{and it profited me not}* So this word is used, Est. 3. 8. & 5. 13. It agrees with Rom. 6. 21. And there may be more intended. It was the cause of my former sickness, or trouble, and so was very hurtful and prejudicial to me.

**V. 28. ***{He will deliver his soul from going into the pit, and his life shall see the light}* Here is the benefit of true repentance, when man humbles himself unfeignedly before God, he brings him out of trouble, and prolongs his life.

The words run in the third person, as Elihu's words expressing the hope of the penitent sinner, while God's hand is yet upon him. But they may be read in the first person, as the words of the penitent sinner praising God for his deliverance, thus. *He hath delivered my soul from going into the pit, and my life sees the light*. See the like, Isa. 38. 17.

*{He will deliver}* The word signifies deliverance out of slavery, danger, trouble, or captivity.

*{his soule}* See on Chap. 14. 22. Himself is meant here, for the soul goes not into the grave.

*{from going into}* Heb. *From going through*. See on verse 18. and on Chap. 15. 19.

*{the pit}* See for this word also on verse 18.

*{and his life}* See for this also on verse 18.

*{shall see}* See on Chap. 19. 27. on the word, *Behold*.

*{the light}* He shall recover of his sickness, or escape his danger, and live longer to behold the light of the sun. A periphrasis of life, verse 30. Chap. 3. 20. Or of prosperity, which is often in Scripture compared to light, which is full of comfort, as trouble is of sorrow, which discomforts men. For the word, see on Chap. 17. 12.

**V. 29. ***{Lo all these things God worketh oftentimes with man}* In this verse and that which follows, is summed up what was spoken of God's ways of discovering himself to man before. Note, 1. The manner, in this verse. 2. The end, verse 30.

*{Lo}* See on Chap. 1. 11. on the word, *Behold*; for it is the same in the original.

*{all these things}* God uses all these ways of acquainting men with his will.

*{worketh}* He brings them strangely to pass.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3. 5.

*{oftentimes}* Heb. *twice, thrice*. Some take it to be meant of God's three several ways of dealing with men mentioned before. But it is rather a certain number for an uncertain (which is frequent in Scripture) intimating, that God does often speak to men by each of these ways, if they do not quickly regard him. If one dream, or affliction, or messenger move not a man, he sends more, so that no man could excuse himself by ignorance of God's proceedings, no nor Job himself. And therefore he had best now to hearken to Elihu God's messenger, lest God send greater afflictions on him.

*{with man}* With one and the same man, if he repent not upon the first admonition. For the word, see on Chap. 14. 10. & 22. 2.

**V. 30. ***{To bring back his soule from the pit, to be enlightened with the light of the living}* It is likely that Elihu spoke these words, as to bring Job to repent of his rash speeches against God; so to put him in hope thereupon of prosperity again.

*{To bring back his soule from the pit}* He was as it were buried alive before in his sickness or troubles, and of no account among men (Psal. 88. 5.) now God in restoring him again, does as it were bring him out of the grave again. Some understand it of resurrection to eternal life. But that is not so likely, for the whole dispute is about the troubles of the wicked, and prosperity of the godly. For bringing back, see on Chap. 15. 13. on the word, *Turnest, and on Chap*. 20. 10. on the word, *Restore*. For Soul, see on Chap. 14. 22. For the Pit, see on verse 18. of this Chap. It may be read, *To turn away his soule from the pit*. That is, to keep him from death. So this word is used, Mal. 2. 6.

*{to be enlightened}* That he may see it. Where to walk.

*{with the light of the living}* Psal. 56. 12. *in the light the living* is to enjoy this life; and children are said to see the light, when they come out of the womb, and begin visibly to live in the world. Others take it of prosperity. See on verse 28. For light, see on Chap. 17. 12.

**V. 31. ***{Mark well, O Job, hearken unto me, hold thy peace, and I will speak}* Here is the conclusion of Elihu's oration. Wherein, 1. He exhorts him to attention, in this verse. 2. He gives him fair leave to reply, verse 32. 3. He intimates his desire to proceed, if Job had nothing to reply, verse 33. Lest Job should say, he was not satisfied, Elihu intimates, that he has more to say of this argument, and permits him to reply, or else requires audience. So he makes way for his next speech.

*{Mark well}* Give good heed unto my words.

*{O Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{hearken unto me}* See on chap. 13. 17. on the word *hear*.

*{hold thy peace}* Do not interrupt me, but answer when I have done.

*{and I will speak}* Then I will declare my opinion further in this cause.

**V. 32. ***{If thou hast any thing to say, answer me, speak: for I desire to justify thee}* When I have done speaking, if there be anything you can except against, speak boldly, and without fear; for I desire nothing more than to hear such fair answers and weighty reasons from you, that I might justly clear you. Thus he shows, that although he could not approve of all that Job had said, yet he was more inclined to clear, than to condemn him, and therefore kinder than his other friends, and more worthy to be heard.

*{If thou hast any thing to say}* Heb. *If thou hast words*. If you have words to defend yourself, or that make for you, or for your cause, bring them forth. For words, see on Chap. 15. 3.

*{answer me}* See on Chap. 15. 13. on the word, *Turneth, and on Chap*. 20. 10. on the word, *Restore*.

*{speak}* I am not afraid to hear what you can object against what I say. Speak freely, and defend yourself.

*{for I desire to justify thee}* What I have spoken, is not out of hatred, but love. My desire is to see the business so carried, that I may pronounce you just.

**V. 33. ***{If not, hearken unto me; hold thy peace, and I shall teach thee wisdom}* If you have no more to say, then hearken to me; neither shall your silence and quiet attention repent you, when you shall perceive, what wisdom you have learned by hearkening to me.

*{if not}* If you have no words to answer to what I have objected against you.

*{hearken unto me}* See on Chap. 13. 17. on the word, *Hear*.

*{hold thy peace}* See on verse 31.

*{and I shall teach thee wisdom}* I shall acquaint you with God's wise proceedings, which you seem hitherto to be ignorant of.

## CHAPTER 34

**Verse 1. ***{Furthermore Elihu answered, and said}* It is likely that Elihu had now held his peace a while, to see, if Job would give any answer, as he had given him leave to do. But seeing Job answered nothing at all, as supposing himself in part convinced, and won, it may be by Elihu's milder dealing with him than his friends had done, he goes on in his speech, in hope to do more good on him. In this second oration of Elihu note, 1. The preface, from verse 1. to verse 5. 2. The charge against Job, from verse 5. to verse 10. 3. The prosecution of it, to verse 36. 4. The conclusion to the end. In the preface note, 1. A transition from Elihu's last discourse to this, verse 1. 2. A petition to Job's friends for audience, verse 2, 3. 3. A desire of a judicious proceeding, verse 4.

*{Furthermore}* Heb. *And*.

*{Elihu}* See on Chap. 32. 2.

*{answered}* Some conceive it not well translated, because Job had not replied on him, and therefore Elihu could not answer him, but it may well be rendered so, for it appears by what follows, that Elihu receiving no answer from Job, goes on to answer other things out of Job's former speeches. For the word see on Chap. 16. 1.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Hear my words, O ye wise men, and give ear unto me ye that have knowledge,}* Here is Elihu's petition to Job's friends for audience. He had granted it to them before, and now he desires the like of them. His desire is set down in this verse. The reason of it, the knowledge of the truth arising from thence; verse 3.

*{Hear}* See on chap. 13. 17.

*{my words}* Which I shall further speak in this cause.

*{O ye wise men}* Some think he speaks to all that were present, desiring that as they had heard Job with patience, so they would hear his answer, But if many hearers were present, it is not likely Elihu could call them all wise men. But it is most probable, that he means Job's three friends that disputed before, excluding Job, whom he accuses before them, verse 5. He bids them hearken to him, intimating, that though they could not convince Job, yet he would, and themselves should be judges. He was angry with them before, but now he gives them good words, to get audience. Or, the words may be taken ironically; You thought yourselves wise enough to convince Job; but could not do it, and should have been better able than I by your age and years; now you have showed your own folly and inability, hearken unto me. See on Chap. 17. 10.

*{And give ear}* See on Chap. 33. 1.

*{unto me}* Do not despise my words for my youth. 1 Tim. 4. 12.

*{ye that have knowledge}* You that are men of experience.

**V. 3. ***{For the ear trieth words, as the mouth tasteth meat}* Therefore I expect audience from you, because God has given you ears, whose office is to convey words to the soul, that it may judge of the truth or falsehood of them.
