You will be sure to use your palates, to try the taste of your meat, before you let it down, why not your ears as well for the use which God has appointed them? They are not given you only to hear the sound of words, but also to help you to the right understanding of them. Job spoke to the same purpose, and used the same words, Chap. 12. 11. See for the exposition of them there. Only you may observe, that ו which is translated *And*, might have been translated *As*, as well as here. So it is rendered, Chap. 5. 7. And often in the book of the Proverbs. And the word trying is a metaphor taken from the goldsmith, who tries his gold and silver by the fire: Something like this verse is to be found also, Job 6. 30. See there.

**V. 4. ***{Let us chuse to us judgment; let us know among ourselves, what is good}* Here is Elihu's second request to Job's friends, to wit, a desire of a judicious proceeding in the cause. As if he had said, It was Job's wish, that he might have some man, with whom he might try the matter in judgment. Go to then, let us take his offer, and having debated the matter to and fro according to equity and justice, let us prove and see what right itself will afford.

*{Let us chuse to us judgment}* Let us lay aside all animosities and prejudice, and try out the right in those things, which shall come in controversies before us. For judgment, see on Chap. 22. 4.

*{let us know among ourselves what is good}* Let us by mutual discourse help one another to know whether Job's opinion or mine be better. For Good, see on Chap. 21. 13. upon the word *wealth*.

**V. 5. ***{For Job hath said, I am righteous; and God hath taken away my judgment}* The charge against Job follows, which is double. The first, in verse 5, 6. The second in verse 7, 8, 9. In the first, are four accusations, two in this verse, and two in the next. In this verse, that he maintained his own righteousness, and accused God of unrighteousness.

*{For}* We had need to weigh the matter well, because Job has spoken dangerous speeches.

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{hath said}* See on Chap. 16. 1. Where he spoke these words shall be required in the particulars.

*{I am righteous}* Job had not said so in those words, but his manifold proclaiming of his own righteousness made Elihu to think that he meant so. To this purpose speaks Job, chap. 13. 18. & 13. 10. & 27. 6. & 31. 1, &c.

*{and}* Or, *But* as it is translated, chap. 3. 1.

*{God hath taken away my judgment}* These words Job had spoken indeed. Chap. 27. 2. See the interpretation of them there. The commentators for the most part conceive that Elihu misconstrues Job's words, as if he had denied himself to be a sinner; but by Elihu's answer he seems rather to understand aright, what Job had indiscreetly uttered in several passions, and passages, that God had handled him like an ungodly man, and would not let him clear himself.

**V. 6. ***{Should I lie against my right? my wound is incurable without transgression.}* In this verse is the third branch of this first accusation, a further maintenance of his own right; and the fourth a further charging of God with unrighteousness.

*{Should I lie against my right}* Should I being innocent, or a godly man, confess myself to be a wicked man, and so betray my right? I will never do it. Job had not said so in these words, but had spoken to this purpose, Chap. 27. 4, 5, 6. Which place it is like Elihu intends here. For the word Lie, see on chap. 24. 25. For Right, see on Chap. 22. 4. on the word *Judgment*.

*{my wound is incurable without transgression}* Neither are these words found together anywhere in Scripture, but seem to be gathered together out of several places. The former part of them out of Chap. 6. 4. & 16. 13. The latter, out of Chap. 9. 17. & 16. 17. For Wound, which in Hebrew is Arrow, see on Chap. 6. 4. The wound made in me by God's arrow is incurable. Psal. 38. 2, 3. Deut. 32. 23. Jer. 15. 18. For Transgression, see on Chap. 7. 21.

**V. 7. ***{What man is like Job, who drinketh up scorning like water?}* Now follows Elihu's second charge against Job. He makes way to it by vilifying Job's person, verse 7, 8. Propounds it verse 9. He disgraces him. 1. From his practice in this verse. 2. From his associates, verse 8. He speaks out of admiration, and indignation, to make Job's friends the more out of love with him. As if he had said, I wonder that Job is not ashamed to show himself more impudent than any man, in scoffing against God and men, and giving them just occasion by his false and blasphemous speeches to load him with scorns. The one he does, the other he bears, as willingly and plentifully as a thirsty man drinks up water.

*{What man}* No man is so impudent. An high expression of indignation. For Man, see on Chap. 14. 10. & 22. 2.

*{is like}* Dared speak so strangely as he has done? Has any of you heard the like?

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{who drinketh up scorning like water}* Who leaves no scorns against God, unuttered, nor refuses to bear any from men justly provoked against him for his evil speeches; as a thirsty man drinks up all the water before him, and never thinks he has enough. For the words see on Chap. 15. 16. Others read the whole verse thus, *What man like Job would drink up scorning like water?* That is, what man of Job's parts and wisdom would do it? It may be fools may. But the *Athnah* on the word Job in the original, shows the first reading to be best. *Drinking up scorning*, is like *drinking damage* Prov. 26. 6. *Drinking wine of violence* Prov. 4. 17. And drinking it like water argues plenty and abundance, as chap. 3. 24.

**V. 8. ***{Which goeth in company with the workers of iniquity, and walketh with wicked men}* Elihu's intent here was not to charge Job with keeping bad company in his prosperity, but with thinking hardly of God in adversity, as ungodly men use to do, when they are plagued for their sinful courses.

*{Which goeth in company}* Keeps the same way as it were with them, and delights in speaking evil of God, as if he were one joined in a league with them. That is, he thinks as they do, Chap. 21. 14. & 22. 17. Mal. 3. 14. That it is in vain to serve God, as appears by the charge in the next verse, *with the workers of iniquity*. See on Chap. 16. 3. & 15. 35. upon the word, *vanity*. Yet here it is better translated iniquity, for the charge is the heavier, and it is more suitable to the end of the verse.

*{and walketh}* See on Chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Passeth*.

*{with wicked men}* Heb. *with men of wickednesse*. That is, the worser sort of them that make a trade of sin. For men, see on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

**V. 9. ***{For he hath said, it profiteth a man nothing that he should delight himself with God}* Here it is a fresh charge upon Job, that he had affirmed that he was never the better for his former delight in the service of God, but as roughly handled as if he had been a most ungodly man.

*{For he}* Job.

*{hath said}* For the word, see on chap. 16. 1. Job had not said those words anywhere, but such as might easily bear such a sense, without a very favorable construction. And the rather, because they came from a discontented spirit. Elihu now accuses him of discouraging men from the service of God, and gathers it out of Job's speeches, Chap. 9. 22. & 21. 7, 8. & 30. 26. Wherein he had affirmed that God destroyed good men as well as bad, and prospered evil men, and afflicted him. From whence Elihu collects, that he thought it an unprofitable thing to serve God.

*{it profiteth a man nothing}* He gets nothing at all by it, and therefore as good not do it. For a man, see on Chap. 14. 10. & 22. 2.

*{that he should delight himself with God}* That he should take pleasure in worshiping God, and walking with him. For God, see on Chap. 5. 17. & 11. 5.

**V. 70. ***{Therefore hearken unto me ye men of understanding: far be it from God, that he should do wickedness, and from the Almighty, that he should commit iniquity}* Now begins Elihu's confutation of those evil opinions which he had fastened on Job before. Wherein 1. Elihu speaks to Job's friends, to verse 16. 2. To Job himself directly, to verse 34. 3. To his friends again, verse 35, 35. In the first part, he disproves Job's opinion that God had done him wrong, regarded not those that served him, more than others. 1. By an argument taken from God's justice, verse 10, 11, 12. 2. From his authority and power, verse 13, 14, 15. From God's justice. 1. From the inconsistency of injustice with God's nature, in this verse. 2. With his actions, verse 11. 3. With his will, verse 12. So that though in regard of his power he could, yet certainly he will not wrong Job, nor any man else, but will regard his servants more than others. The sum of this verse is, as if Elihu had said, you who are men of discretion and wisdom, hearken unto me while I confute Job's wicked opinion. God forbid that any of us should think so ill of God, as that he who is indeed Almighty, and in whom no good thing is wanting, should abuse his might and power, to do anything unjust, or unbeseeming him.

*{Therefore hearken unto me}* Seeing Job has uttered such strange things against God, give audience to me with quietness, that I speak for God. Thus Elihu, like a cunning orator in a weighty cause, often stirs up others to attention. For the word hearken, see on Chap. 13. 17. on the word *hear*.

*{ye men of understanding}* Heb. *ye men of heart*. The philosophers say, that men laugh with the spleen, are angry with the gall, love with the liver, are wise with the heart, brag with the lungs. See on Chap. 11. 12. And for men, see on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2. For heart, on Chap. 15. 12.

*{far be it}* See on Chap. 27. 5.

*{from God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{that he should do wickedness}* Chap. 8. 3. & 36. 23. Deut. 32. 4. Psal. 92. 15. Rom. 9. 14.

*{and from the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{that he should commit iniquity}* That he should deny justice to any, or punish any without a cause, or deal alike with good men and bad. For iniquity, see on Chap. 13. 7. on the word, *wickedly*.

**V. 11. ***{For the work of a man shall he render unto him, and cause every man to find according to his ways}* For God is so far from dealing unjustly, or doing any man wrong, that he rewards every man according to his work. As they sow, so they shall reap.

*{For the work}* Or, *For according to the work*, as in the end of the verse. Else the work must be put for the reward of the work, as Rev. 14. 13.

*{of a man}* See on Chap. 14. 1.

*{shall he render unto him}* He shall reward him according to the nature of his work, be it good or bad. And who then can say, that God deals unjustly with him? Psal. 62. 12. Prov. 24. 12. Jer. 32. 19. Ezek. 33. 20. Matth. 16. 27. Rom. 2. 6. 2 Cor. 5. 10. 1 Pet. 1. 17. Rev. 22. 12. The word intimates a full, or complete reward.

*{and cause every man to find}* The same thing is repeated in divers words for the certainty of it. He causes it to befall to every man. For man, see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{according to his ways}* According to the course of his life, and not according to some one particular action.

**V. 12. ***{Yea surely God will not do wickedly, neither will the Almighty pervert judgment}* But take this for an undoubted truth, that God is so exact in giving every man his due, that he will never turn aside from that way in all his dealings with men, neither shall any man have any just cause to complain of him, how hardly soever he deal with him.

*{Yea surely}* There is no question to be made of it. He that shall deny this truth, he deserves not to be disputed withal. And this truth confessed, will quickly end the business in question.

*{God}* See on chap. 8. 3.

*{will not do wickedly}* Will not do unjustly. Or, *will not condemn*. To wit, a good man, that is, not to be condemned. So Exod. 34. 7. *God will by no means clear*. To wit, the guilty, that ought not to be cleared.

*{neither will the Almighty pervert judgment}* All these words are expounded on Chap. 8. 3. See there.

**V. 13. ***{Who hath given him a charge over the earth? or who hath disposed the whole world?}* Here begins Elihu's second argument, to prove, that God would not do Job nor any other man wrong; taken from his sovereign authority over all men, which is set out, 1. By the supremacy and absoluteness of it in this world, in this verse. 2. By the irresistibleness of it, verse 14, 15. The sum of this verse is, that God cannot deal unjustly with any, because he is absolute, and may do what he will with the creatures; neither is there any above him, who gave him this charge over men, and may call him to account for not dealing well in his office. Therefore either God must be just, or all the world will be in a confusion without redress.

*{Who hath given him a charge over the earth?}* Who has committed the earth, and the inhabitants of it to God, to be ordered by him, or who has commanded him to take care thereof? Surely nobody. It is his right by creation, and there being none above him, none can expostulate with him about injustice. See the like phrase, Chap. 36. 23. 2 Chron. 36. 23. Ezr. 1. 2. Heb. *Who hath laid the charge of the earth upon him*. For Earth, see on Chapter 9. 24.

*{who hath disposed the whole world}* Heb. *all of it*. Or, *who hath placed him over the whole world?* Who has laid the charge of the whole world upon him? Repeating upon him out of the former part of the verse. For World, see on Chap. 18. 18.

**V. 14. ***{If he set his heart upon man, if he gather unto him his spirit, and his breath}* There is no power in any man to resist God. Every man had his life from him, and he can do no man wrong in taking it away again. And therefore Job has no just cause to complain of God for afflicting, who may kill him how and when he please, without rendering any reason to him.

*{If he set his heart upon man}* If he have a purpose to destroy him. See on Chap. 7. 17. For heart, see on Chap. 15. 12. Heb. *upon him*. That is, upon man, as appears by verse 11. & 15.

*{if he gather unto himself}* God gives life and breath unto a man, and when he takes it away again, he is said to gather it to himself. The meaning is, when he takes away his life; Psal. 104. 29. Eccl. 12. 7. See the like phrase, Psal. 26. 9. For the word, gather, see on Chap. 27. 19.

*{his spirit}* If he take away his soul out of his body. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 2. upon vain knowledge.

*{and his breath}* See on Chap. 33. 4. Others read the whole verse thus. *If he set his heart against man, he will gather unto himself his spirit and his breath*. That is, if God should set himself to inquire into the faults of any man's life, or resolve to proceed against him as an enemy, he would soon make an end of him, the stoutest man could not resist him. Others understand it of setting God's heart upon himself. If he should not be merciful to man, but look only at his own glory, he might soon destroy all mankind.

**V. 15. ***{All flesh shall perish together, and man shall turn again unto dust}* When God takes away the soul and breath, then all men without exception must needs die, and the soulless body fall to the earth out of which it was taken, Gen. 3. 19. Eccl. 12. 7.

*{All flesh}* Every man. As Gen. 6. 13. Isa. 40. 6.

*{shall perish}* Heb. *Shall breath out*, or, *give up the Ghost*. See on Chap. 13. 19.

*{together}* Without exception. None would escape. See on Chap. 24. 4.

*{and man}* Man taken out of the earth: so much the word imports. See on Chap. 14. 1.

*{shall turn again}* Shall return to his first original. See on Chap. 14. 13. on the word, *Past*.

*{unto dust}* See on Chap. 14. 8. and the word, Ground.

**V. 16. ***{If now thou hast understanding, hear this: hearken to the voice of my words}* Now Elihu turns his speech from Job's friends to Job himself, and comes closer to him to grapple with him, from hence to verse 34. And 1. He calls anew for audience in this verse. 2. He proceeds to new arguments, verse 17, &c. to verse 34. The sum of this verse is, as if he had said, Now O Job I come nearer to you. If therefore you have any wit or right understanding left in you, hearken and give ear unto those things which you shall hear me speak.

*{If now thou hast understanding}* See on Chap. 28. 28.

*{hear this}* Some understand it of what he had said before, as if he conceived, he had said enough, to convince Job. But it is more likely to be a preface to new matter. For the word, see on Chap. 13. 17.

*{hearken to the voice of my words}* See on Chap. 33. 8. And for, hearken, on Chap. 33. 1.

**V. 17. ***{Shall even he that hateth right govern, and wilt thou condemn him, that is most just?}* Elihu now seeks. 1. To convince Job of injury done to God, from this verse to verse 31. 2. He teaches him how to carry himself better from verse 31. to verse 34. In the first part, 1. He argues from God's just government, in this verse and verse 18. 2. From the effects of it, from verse 19. to verse 31. In these two verses. 1. He propounds God's government in this verse. 2. He illustrates it by princes' rule, verse 18. In this verse 1. He shows the unfitness of injustice in a ruler, 2. He asserts the contrary in God. As if he had said, Tell me I pray you, do you think him worthy of the name of a ruler or judge, who detests justice; by which all lawful authority is maintained? And what wickedness, yea what madness is it, that you should dare to charge him with any unjust, or wrongful dealing, who only is most just and most mighty?

*{Shall}* Certainly he shall not, or should not.

*{even he}* Such an one of all others were most unfit to rule.

*{that hateth}* That is so far from doing right, that he cannot abide it should be done.

*{right}* See on Chap. 22. 4. on the word, *judgment*.

*{govern}* Is such an one fit to rule others? If God were so unjust, as you accuse him to be, he were not fit to govern the world, Gen. 18. 25. Rom. 3. 5. The word signifies to bind. Chap. 5. 18. And by a metaphor to rule, or lay commands on others to bind them. So it is used, Isa. 3. 7. Of this binding power of rulers see on Chap. 12. 18.

*{and wilt thou condemn him}* See on verse 12. on the words, *Do wickedly*.

*{that is most just}* Or, *That is strong and just*. And therefore should by no means be condemned. For Most, see on Chap. 8. 2. on the word *strong*. And on Chap. 31. 25. on the word, *Much*.

**V. 18. ***{Is it fit to say to a King, Thou art wicked? and to Princes, ye are ungodly.}* Kings and princes expect such great respect from their subjects, that no man should dare to censure them, though they deal unjustly with them. How much more unfit is it then to tax God of injustice (as you O Job have done) who is greater than they, and always proceeds justly.

*{Is it fit?}* This is not in the original, but added by the interpreters to make up the sense. How unfit it is, appears by God's law to the contrary. Exod. 22. 28.

*{to say}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{to a King}* To one, that has the highest power over others.

*{thou art wicked}* Heb. *Belial*. A name given to the devil in the New Testament. 2 Cor. 6. 15. Some derive it from a word that signifies, without yoke; others, without profit. It signifies a very wicked man, that will be ruled by no law, nor is good for nothing. Some read it thus, *who saith to a King thou art wicked*. And make it depend thus on the former words. Will you condemn him of injustice, that is so just, that he spares not to tell kings and princes of their wickedness?

*{and to Princes}* Inferior rulers under kings, or of lower rank. See on Chap. 21. 28.

*{Ye are ungodly}* See on Chap. 3. 17.

**V. 19. ***{How much less to him, that accepteth not the person of Princes, nor regardeth the rich more then the poor? For they all are the work of his hands.}* He now shows God's justice by the effects of it from this verse, and to verse 31. And that 1. Upon princes in this verse. 2. Upon whole nations from verse 20. to verse 23. 3. Upon strong and and stout men to verse 26. 4. He sets out God's ends in such proceedings, to verse 31. The sum of this verse is, if it be not fit to say so to an earthly king, who may deserve such language by his injustice, how much less to a heavenly king, who as he is the maker of all, so need he not nor does he prefer great ones before meaner men in judgment?

*{How much less to him}* These words are added to fill up the sense.

*{that accepteth not the persons}* See on Chap. 13. 8. & 32. 21. This fault is common with men, and condemned often in Scripture, and God everywhere vindicated from this unjust course. As Deut. 10. 17. & 2 Chron. 19. 7. Acts 10. 34. Gal. 2. 6. Eph. 6. 9. Col. 3. 25. 1 Pet. 1. 17.

*{of Princes}* See on Chap. 29. 9.

*{nor regardeth}* Heb. *Acknowledgeth not*, to wit, neither in judgment, nor in way of favor preferring the rich before the poor.

*{the rich}* Or, *the great man*, or *the bountifull man*, as it is rendered. Isa. 32. 5. It comes from a word that signifies to cry aloud, as some learned men think, or to save, as others, because poor men are forced to cry to the rich for relief, and deliverance. Chap. 29. 12.

*{more then the poor}* See on Chap. 5. 16.

*{for they all are the works of his hands}* He has no reason to fear nor favor great or small, for he made them all. Chap. 31. 15. Prov. 22. 2. The great man must not look to fare the better with God for his greatness; nor need the mean man fear, that he shall fare the worse for his meanness. For the words, see on Chapter 14. 15.

**V. 20. ***{In a moment shall they die, and the people shall be troubled at midnight, and pass away: and the mighty shall be taken away without hand}* In this verse is set out God's judgment upon a nation, and the equity of it, in the two verses following. This verse sets out the sudden time, and strange manner of the destruction of the people. The time, sudden in a moment; unexpected at midnight. The manner various, by death, trouble, passing away, taking away strangely. As if Elihu had said we see wonderful, sudden, and general alterations, not only in one man or family, but in whole towns and cities, sometimes one people, sometimes another falling to decay even in a moment, and being driven out of their own country utterly to perish: yea and the mightiest princes, without any human violence offered unto them, to be cut off, and to come to nothing.

*{In a moment shall they die}* They shall die suddenly, and that both great and mean ones, not only particular persons, but whole nations also, when God will, who does what he will, and no man can hinder him. Gen. 19. 23, 24. For a moment see on Chap. 21. 13.

*{and the people of a town shall be troubled at midnight}* All the people of a town or city, notwithstanding their combination; when they are in a deep sleep in the midst of the night, and secure not fearing nor suspecting any harm shall come into trouble: and shake as leaves in a great wind. Chap. 27. 20. See there Exod. 12. 29. & 2 King. 19. 35.

*{and pass away}* Be carried captive out of their own country into another. Or pass away into the grave. Or *perish*, as Psal. 37. 36. Mat. 5. 18. Jam. 1. 10. For the word see on Chap. 14. 20.

*{and the mighty}* Great men that oppress others. Chap. 24. 22.

*{shall be taken away}* Heb. *They shall take away.* See the like, the personal used impersonally. Chap. 3. 3. Luke 12. 20. For taking away see on Chap. 12. 20. on the word, *Removeth*.

*{without hand}* By some great judgment of God without the help of man, in a miraculous manner. For hand, see on Chap. 15. 23. For the phrase, Dan. 2. 34. 45.

**V. 21. ***{For his eyes are upon the ways of man, and he seeth all his goings}* The equity of this judgment appears, in that God knowing all their ways most exactly, as in this verse and so as he cannot be deceived, verse 22. no doubt sees a just cause to inflict such a heavy judgment on them.

*{For his eyes}* The eyes of God, who was spoken of before. God's providence observes all things. Though he have no eyes, yet he knows more than we see. For the word Eyes, see on chap. 15. 15. on the word *Sight*.

*{are upon the ways of man}* He observes men's actions, as we do what we see. 2 Chron. 16. 9. Job 31. 4. Prov. 5. 21. & 15. 3. Jer. 16. 17. & 32. 19. For ways, see on chap. 17. 9. For Man on Chap. 1. 1.

*{and he seeth}* The same thing repeated in other words for certainty. For the word, see on chap. 19. 27. on the word, *Behold*.

*{all his goings}* All that he does. See on chap. 18. 7. on the word, *Steps*.

**V. 22. ***{There is no darkness, nor shadow of death, where the workers of iniquity may hide themselves.}* Wicked men after they have sinned, will like Adam leave no corner unsearched to hide themselves for shame and fear; but all in vain; for there is no darkness, not the most deadly, that can hinder God from beholding them, to bring them out to judgment, no not the grave itself. Much less then can they find any place so secret to sin in, where God cannot see them in the act of evil.

*{There is no darkness, nor shadow of death.}* See on chap. 3. 5. & 10. 21. & 28. 3. & 12. 22. & 26. 6.

*{where the workers of iniquity}* See on verse 8.

*{may hide themselves}* May be safe from God finding out, as malefactors hid are from men's. Psal. 139. 12. Amos 9. 2, 3. Heb. 4. 13.

**V. 23. ***{For he will not lay upon man more than right; that he should enter into judgment with God.}* Now he proceeds to the effects of God's justice upon some particular great men. And first he takes away the exception that the party afflicted might take against God's proceeding with him in this verse. 2. He lays down the judgment itself. verse 24. 3. The just ground of it, verse 25. The sum of this verse is, O! Job you think you have just cause to contend with God for afflicting you so heavily, but you are deceived; for God never gives any man just cause to commence a suit against him.

*{For he will not lay upon any man more than right}* God will not afflict any man above his desert (or above his strength to bear, as others interpret it) that so man might have just cause to complain of him, Ezr. 9. 13. Or, *God will not let man* (or, *man must not*) *set his heart any more*, to wit, to contend with God in judgment, as you, O Job, have often desired to do. as Chap. 13. 3. & 23. 3, 4. Therefore leave off these bold desires; for God will bear them no longer at your hands, chap. 4. 17, 18, 19. For Man, see on chap. 1. 1.

*{that he should enter}* Heb. *go on into judgement with God.* That he should deservedly draw God to answer for afflicting him more than he has deserved. For entering into judgment, see on chap. 22. 4. For Enter see on chap. 14. 20. 20. on the word, *Passeth*. For אל with see on Gen. 49. 29. For God see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

**V. 24. ***{He shall break in pieces mighty men without number, and set others in their stead}* God need not regard how strong ungodly men be, nor how many, for he can easily ruin them, and prefer others.

*{he shall break in pieces}* Like a potter's vessel. Psal. 2. 9. Jerem. 15. 12.

*{mighty men}* Or, *many men.* See on Chap. 8. 2. upon the word *Strong*.

*{without number}* He will give innumerable examples of his great power, in destroying the greatest ungodly persons. Heb. *without searching out.* So that no man is able to tell the number of them. Others understand it of the incomprehensibleness of the way of God's doing, so that man sees it done, but knows not how it is done, or why. Others that God needs not inquire into the matter, as judges do, for he knows cause enough to ruin them. Others take it for without end, so that their ruin is endless. The first is the best, because this phrase is often joined with number, as chap. 5. 9. & 9. 10. & 36. 26.

*{and set}* Heb. *Make to stand.* Raise them up to the same height, and make them abide in it.

*{others}* Men of low degree and rank.

*{in their stead}* In their room, or place, or dignity. Psal. 113. 7, 8. Dan. 2. 21.

**V. 25. ***{Therefore he knoweth their works and he overturneth them in the night, so that they are destroyed}* God takes notice of their ungodly actions, that he may have just occasion to destroy them.

*{therefore he knoweth}* Some understand it of God's making other men to take notice of their sins by his great judgments on them. But the word rather signifies taking notice of a thing, as chap. 21. 29. & 24. 13. These great men thought before, that God took no notice of their ways, but now they may see he does when he destroys them most justly for their wicked works.

*{their works}* Their sinful actions, for which he overthrows them.

*{and he overturneth them in the night}* That is, suddenly, and unexpectedly. See on verse 20. Others read it, *And he turneth into night.* That is, he turns their prosperity into adversity. Others, *and he turneth the night*, to wit, into day he takes away the dark covers of their sins, and makes them known to all men. See on verse 22. Others, *And when the night is turned*, then they are destroyed. That is, they perish as soon and as easily as the day gives place to the night.

*{so that they are destroyed}* Heb. *crushed.* As it is translated chapter 4. 19.

**V. 26. ***{He striketh them as wicked men in the open sight of others.}* Now he comes to God's ends in these proceedings against ungodly men. 1. For the terror of others in this verse. 2. To punish them for their impiety, verse 27. 3. For their cruelty, verse 28. 4. To give rest to others, verse 29. 5. To keep the people from further snares, verse 30. The sense of this verse is, that God handles wicked men as their sins deserve; and that not privily, but openly making them a spectacle to the whole world.

*{he striketh them}* The word imports such a grievous stroke, as that men shall admire it, and clap their hands at it in detestation. Chap. 27. 23.

*{as wicked men}* Or, *for wicked men.* So as he uses to smite ungodly men, such as they are. That is, he brings some eminent judgment upon them. For the word, see on chap. 3. 17.

*{In the open sight of others.}* Heb. *In the place of beholders.* Openly in the sight of their terror, and the comfort of others. Exod. 14. 30. So executions were wont to be done, and are still in public places, where all may see, that others may tremble, and acknowledge the acts of justice. Others understand it of that delight, which others should take in the destruction of these ungodly men; as the words may be taken, Obad. ver. 12. For Place, see on Chap. 18. 21. & 20. 9. For Beholding, see on Chap. 19. 27.

**V. 27. ***{Because they have turned back from him, and would not consider any of his ways}* Here is one cause of God's ruining them, to wit, their gross and affected ignorance and impiety. When men turn their backs to God's laws, and their faces to their own lusts, then they grow extremely wicked.

*{Because}* That for so. That is, for that they were so wicked. So For not, for Nor for Chap. 16. 17. See there.

*{they turned back}* Or, *departed.* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{from him}* Heb. *from after him.* That is, from following after him. They would not be guided by God's word, as they should have been.

*{and would not consider any of his ways}* They had no desire at all to know those good ways in which God commanded them to walk. See on chap. 21. 14. See the like Psal. 28. 5. Isa. 5. 12.

**V. 28. ***{So that they cause the cry of the poor to come unto him, and he heareth the cry of the afflicted}* They are so extreme wicked, that they not only neglect God's worship, but also do so grievously oppress the poor; that they cry unto God for vengeance, which he accordingly executes on these ungodly ones. Exod. 2. 23, 24. & 3. 7, 9.

*{so that they}* The ungodly men spoken of verse 27.

*{cause the cry of the poor to come}* By their violent dealings force them to cry out. For Cry, see on chap. 27. 9. For Poor, on chap. 5. 16.

*{unto him}* Unto God, who is spoken of in the former verses. Or, *upon him.* That God may execute upon every oppressor the vengeance due to him, for which the oppressed cry unto God.

*{and he heareth}* See on chap. 13. 17.

*{the cry}* See before in this verse.

*{of the afflicted}* See on chap. 22. 6. & 24. 9.

**V. 29. ***{When he giveth quietness, who then can make trouble? and when he hideth his face, who then can behold him? whether it be done against a nation, or against a man only?}* It appears that God rejects not the cry of the poor, because he gives them rest, and such rest, as no man can disturb. As on the contrary, if he send trouble, no man can find comfort, no not whole nations any more than one particular man.

*{When he giveth quietness}* Heb. *When he maketh quiet.* When he sets men that were oppressed before, in such a condition, that they may be free from fear or sadness.

*{who then can make trouble}* Who can disease those to whom God gives ease, or who can move them out of their quiet estate? Isa. 14. 3, 4. & 32. 15. So this word הרשיע is used, 1 Sam. 14. 47. and translated vexing. Sometimes it is translated condemning, as ver. 17. of this chapter. Sometimes doing wickedly, as ver. 12. All these significations depend one on another. Because men do wickedly, therefore the judges condemn them; and because they condemn them, therefore the executioners punish them. It must be troubling here, because it is opposed to quieting, not to clearing, or well-doing.

*{and when he hideth his face}* When in his anger, he does as it were turn his face away from a man, or nation, and leaves them in their miseries refusing to help them, or to deliver them. Psal. 10. 1. & 13. 1. See on Chap. 13. 24.

*{who then can behold him}* Who can look upon him, as on a friend smiling on him, or look for help from him? Psal. 34. 5. Others understand it of the man in trouble. No man will look kindly on him, on whom God frowns. And that by way of opposition answers well to the beginning of the verse.

*{whether it be done against a nation}* God is not like man, who cannot sometimes execute justice because of the multitude of offenders. He can as easily punish a whole nation, as a particular man. verse 20. Chap. 12. 23. or, *for a nation.* So this particle is used, Gen. 37. 8. Psal. 32. 6. and in this Chap. verse 36.

*{against a man}* See on Chap. 14. 1.

*{only}* So this word יחד may be translated. Ezra 4. 3. Psal. 33. 15. And so it must here in regard of the opposition of one man to a whole nation. Yet commonly it is translated together, as Chap. 24. 4. See more on Chap. 21. 26. on the word, *Alike*.

**V. 30. ***{That the hypocrite reign not lest the people be ensnared}* Lest any man should think, that God may pull down the people and common men of no great power, but hardly kings and great ones this is added to show, that God sometimes pulls them down also for the ease of the people, over whom they have tyrannized.

*{That the hypocrite}* Or, *the profane man.* See on Chap. 8. 13.

*{reign not}* Reign no longer: for he speaks not of keeping them from the crown, but of taking it away from such.

*{lest the people be ensnared}* Not by their bad life and example as some would have it, but lest the people by their tyrannous courses lose their liberties. Or *because of the snares of the people.* That is, God revenges the people's wrongs, and pulls kings down for oppressing subjects. The word Snares properly signifies such as hunters lay, or fowlers to catch beasts, or birds. So have tyrants crafty inventions to undo the people.

**V. 31. ***{Surely it is meet to be said unto God I have borne chastisement; I will not offend any more}* Elihu having as he conceived, convinced Job of injury done to God, now gives him counsel how to carry himself more humbly toward him for time to come. verse 31, 32. And 1. To acknowledge his error to God in this verse. 2. To seek for further information from God. verse 32. 3. To confess the rashness of his judgment in God's proceedings. verse 33. The sum of this verse is, as if Elihu had said to Job. You should not in this manner have strived with God, or accused him of unjust dealing with you (as v. 5.) but rather should have said, you have afflicted me, and I take it patiently, and not so only, but will amend by your corrections, and not corrupt my ways, as formerly I have done. Piscator reads it thus, *Hath Job said to God I have borne.* As if he had said, you all are witnesses, that Job has not humbled himself under God's correcting hand, but rather accused God of injustice. Deodate, *Surely thou shouldest addresse thy self unto God, who saith, I pardon, I will not destroy.* Saying, as verse 32.

*{Surely}* So this particle is translated. Chap. 8. 6. And fitly here, because it renders no reason of what went before.

*{it is meet}* These words are fitly added to make up the sense.

*{to be said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

*{unto God}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{I have borne chastisement}* Chastisement is added for perspicuity. For bearing see on chap. 21. 3. on the word, *Suffer*. I consider you are just, and will therefore patiently endure whatsoever you lay upon me, not murmur against you.

*{I will not offend any more}* Heb. *I will not corrupt*, to wit, my ways by doing evil.

**V. 23. ***{That which I see not, teach thou me; if I have done iniquity, I will do no more}* He teaches Job, what he should further say to God, to wit, that he should pray for a discovery of his secret sins, and promise amendment.

*{That which I see not}* Heb. *Beside that which I see.* I see many sins in me; but some there are which I cannot see. Lord discover them also to me, that I may be humbled for them. Seeing is here taken for knowing.

*{teach thou me}* See on chap. 6. 24. & 8. 10. You know them all, make me to know them also.

*{If I have done iniquity}* See on verse 8.

*{I will do no more}* Heb. *I will not adde.* I will give over my former wicked ways.

**V. 33. ***{Should it be according to thy mind? he will recompence it whether thou refuse or whether thou choose, and not I, therefore speak what thou knowest}* This verse may be read many other ways, as thus, *should he reward it according to thy mind? though thou hast despised the one, and chosen the other, yet will not I, therefore speak what thou knowest.* That is, why should God reward sin, as you would have him do it? though you have refused to submit to God's mercy, and call for justice, yet I dare not do so: if you know better teach me. Or thus, *Should it come from thee, how he should recompence it, when thou hast refused his correction? but thou shalt choose, and not I: therefore speak what thou knowest.* Should you now that have sinned, and refuse to be corrected, teach God how he should correct you? You may think so, but I do not. Show me better if you can. Or thus, *Should that come from thee, which he will punish? I speak it, because thou hast refused Gods correction, because thou hast chosen that which I would not: now speak what thou knowest.* Why should you speak against God's proceedings, to bring further trouble upon you? My desire of easing you is the cause why I speak thus. I should never have accused God, as you have done, if I had been so handled by him. But if you have anything to except against what I say, speak freely. The like leave he had given him, Chap. 33. 5. Or thus; *Did such a speech come from thee? he will recompence it, that thou hast rejected him; but thou choosest this way, and not I: now speak what thou knowest.* I never heard you speak so humbly as I have taught you (ver. 31, 32. Surely God will punish you; because you have spoken proudly against his proceedings with you. But this is nothing to me, who like not of such courses. If you can defend yourself, so do. The reason of so many various interpretations is the conciseness of the Hebrew tongue, which leaves many words to be added by the interpreters, who not knowing the manner of speech in those remoter times, must needs differ in their translations. The sense of the words, as they stand in our translations, shall now be showed in the particulars.

*{Should it be?}* Should God's correction be?

*{according to thy mind}* Heb. *from with thee.* As you would have it. See the like phrase, Hos. 8. 4. *He will recompence it, whether thou refuse, or whether thou choose, and not I.* He will punish you for your sins, whether you be willing, or unwilling. It is not my doing, but his, and therefore you must not be angry with me for saying so.

*{therefore}* So this particle is translated. Chap. 35. 16.

*{speak what thou knowest}* Say what you can for yourself, yet it will be to little purpose, God will have his will.

**V. 34. ***{Let men of understanding tell me, and let a wise man hearken unto me}* Here Elihu turns his speech to Job's friends again, desiring their consent to what he said, not doubting but they would give it, because he was confident that he said right, and they were wise enough to apprehend it. He speaks as one persuaded that all wise men will be of his mind, and therefore Job must be foolish, if he yield not to him. The thing wherein he supposes all wise men will agree with him, is expressed verse 35. That Job had spoken foolishly.

*{Let men of understanding}* Heb. *of heart.* See on verse 10. And on Chap. 9. 4. & 11. [...]. Or, *men of understanding will.* I am confident wise men men will say as I do.

*{tell me}* Or, *say with me.* That is, say as I do. So this particle ל is used for, with, Gen. 46. 26. Exod. 34. 12. Numb. 18. 11.

*{and let a wise man}* Let every wise man think so. Or, *and a wise man will.* All that have understanding will be of the same mind with me.

*{hearken unto me}* Broughton reads, it, *and the wise person that hears me.* For the word, *hearken*. See on Chap. 13. 17. on the word, *hear*.

**V. 35. ***{Job hath spoken without knowledge, and his words were without wisdom}* Here he shows wherein he expected the consent of all wise men, that had heard Job's speeches, and his answers, and that he doubts not but that they will confess that he has done that which Job's three friends could not, to wit, that he had convinced Job of foolish speeches uttered against God. See the like charge, Chap. 35. 16.

*{Job}* Or, *That Job.* For that particle is often understood, as verse 36. For Job, see on Chap. 1. 1.

*{hath spoken without knowledge}* Heb. *not in knowledge.* That is, foolishly.

*{and his words were without wisdom}* Heb. *not in wisdom.* That is, unwisely. He has uttered many things unwisely concerning God in his former speeches, as I have proved.

**V. 36. ***{My desire is, that Job may be tried unto the end, because of his answers for wicked men}* In this verse and the next is, the conclusion of the chapter, and of this speech of Elihu. His desire is set down in this verse. The reasons in the last verse. It is likely he thought that Job was not yet fully satisfied, he desires therefore that he may be further tried, especially about those answers which he gave concerning, or like unto wicked men in the point of God's providence. Others conceive that Elihu saw some discontented signs in Job's countenance, and therefore desires God to continue afflicting of him, till he submit, and speak better of God. But it is not likely that Elihu would wish the [continuance] of Job's sorrows, who still declared his desire, that Job might clear himself, and be eased.

*{My desire is}* So this word is used, Chap. 9. 26. *The swift ships*, Heb. *The ships of desire.* From אבה with the affix ה being lost according to the custom of the language, is made אבי. Others read it, *My Father, let Job be tried.* But that title was not then so ordinarily given to God by particular men. Neither does he desire trial from God by afflicting Job, but by men by disputing with him.

*{that Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{may be tried}* That his answers about ungodly men may be further scanned. So the word is used for searching out the true sense of words ver. 3. and Chap. 12. 11. See there.

*{unto the end}* Thoroughly, or to the utmost. Or, *unto victory.* Till he be overcome, and forced to confess his folly. Let him speak as much as he can, that we may answer him fully, and so stop the mouths of all wicked men, that speak as he does, to God's dishonor.

*{because of}* Or, *concerning.* For his intent is to call Job to further account for his former speeches. So this word is used, Chap. 36. 33.

*{his answers}* Those answers which he gave to his friends before. It comes from a word that signifies to return, for answers are returned to former objections or arguments.

*{for wicked men}* In defense of them, to encourage them in their sins, by blazing out their prosperity. Or, *in the businesse of wicked men.* Which he has given in that point. So this particle is used for of, or concerning, chap. 26. 14. Psal. 90. 13. Or, *among wicked men.* Such answers as he agrees in with wicked men. This was by Elihu charged on Job, verse 8. see there. So is this word used, 1 King. 9. 7. Heb. *men of wickednesse.* For men, see on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2. For wickedness, see on chap. 16. 3. & 15. 35. on the word, *vanity*.

**V. 37. ***{For he addeth rebellion unto his sin, he clappeth his hands amongst us, and multiplyeth his words against God}* Three causes are here given why Job should be thoroughly sifted. 1. His great impatience in himself. 2. His proud insulting over men. 3. His high words against God.

*{For he addeth rebellion unto his sin}* He sinned more lightly, and out of ignorance before, but now he grows most impatient and outrageous. Or, *For else he will adde.* If he be not convinced, he will grow worse and worse. Or, as the Dutch has it. *For should he adde rebellion to his sinne, he would clap, &c.* Should he grow worse, as it is likely he will, if he be not thoroughly dealt withal, it is likely he will triumph more over us, and insult more against God. No ground will hold him.

*{rebellion}* Sinning with a high hand. See on Chap. 7. 21.

*{unto his sin}* To his former sins of error, as that word primarily signifies, though used sometimes more largely. See on chap. 7. 20. & 14. 16.

*{he clappeth his hands amongst us}* He would insult over us if we should thus leave him, as if he had been too hard for us all in disputing. See on chap. 27. 23.

*{and multiplyeth his words against God}* He would speak more, or greater words against God than ever yet he did. For the word may signify either. See on Chap. 32. 9. & 33. 12. The particle ל is translated against Chap. 8. 4. For God, see on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

## CHAPTER 35

**Verse 1. ***{Elihu spake moreover and said}* This is the third and shortest oration of Elihu. 1. In this verse is the writer's preface. 2. A confutation 1. of one speech of Job's, from verse 2. to verse 9. 2. Of another, from verse 10. to verse 14. 3. Of a third, verse 14. to the end.

*{Moreover}* Heb. *and.* When Elihu had ceased a while, and expected what Job would answer to his former speech and perceived that he did not offer to speak, he begins to charge him afresh.

*{Elihu}* See on chap. 32. 2.

*{spake and said}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Thinkest thou this to be right, that thou saidst, my righteousness is more then God's?}* The charge is first produced, verse 2, 3. 2. Answered from verse 4. to verse 9. In the producing of it, 1. It is laid down, verse 2. 2. Proved, verse 3.

*{Thinkest thou this to be right?}* Can you in your own conscience think you have said well in this? Sure you cannot. For right, see on chap. 22. 4. on the word, *judgment*.

*{my righteousness is more then God's}* This Job had not directly said, but he gathers it by consequence out of other words of Job, which he cites, verse 3. By his righteousness he means the righteousness of his cause, not of his person, which he durst not compare with God's; and it was too much to compare with God in his cause too. Elihu gathers, that he did so, because he had so often commended his own righteousness, and complained of God's hard dealing with him, and therefore by consequence he preferred the justice of his cause before God's. Heb. *my righteousnesse is before Gods*. For God see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

**V. 3. ***{For thou saidst, what advantage will it be unto thee, and what profit shall I have, if I be cleansed from my sin?}* You have declared, that you are of that opinion, that there is no good to be gotten by leaving sinful courses.

*{for thou saidst}* Job had not said so in direct terms, but he gathered it out of divers speeches, of his, as Chap. 9. 22. & 10. 15. Wherein he puts no difference between good men and courses, and bad men and courses. Upon which it followed, that no good was to be gotten by leaving ill ways. And upon that it followed, that Job was more righteous than God, verse 2. Because he studied to please God, as became a good man to do, and God instead of rewarding him, as he did other good men, afflicted him, as he used to do to wicked men. For Saying, look on Chap. 16. 1.

*{what advantage will it be to thee}* He brings in Job asking himself, what good he could get by piety? As if he had said, No good was to be gotten by it. See on Chap. 34. 9.

*{and what profit shall I have}* See on chap. 21. 15.

*{If I be cleansed from my sin}* These words, if I be cleansed, are not in the original, but necessary to be added. Else Job should ask, what good he should get by his sins, which were a strange question for him to ask, and nothing at all to the present dispute. Others read, *what profit shall I have by it, more then by my sin?* That is, what good can I get by well-doing more than by ill-doing? For sin see on chap. 34. 37.

**V. 4. ***{I will answer thee, and thy companions with thee}* Here begins the answer to the wicked opinion object to Job, ver. 3. Wherein is, first, a promise of an answer in this verse. 2. The answer itself, from ver. 5. to ver. 9. In this verse he signifies to Job, that he had matter enough to answer him, & all his partners in such absurdities.

*{I will answer thee}* In those questions, which you have propounded. For to those only he answers, and not to the charge, verse 2. of Job's being more righteous than God in his own opinion, because that leaned upon no other ground, but these questions, verse 3. So that to overthrow these was to overthrow the whole foundation. Heb. *I will return thee words.* For answer, see on chap. 15. 13. on the word, *Turnest, and on chap*. 20. 10. on the word, *Restore*.

*{and thy companions with thee}* Some understand it of his three friends, who had disputed with him, & having said little to this particular, had as it were betrayed the cause, and by their silence might seem to consent to Job, and think as he did. But this seems not right; for Eliphaz had spoken to the same purpose, chap. 22. 2, 3. that Elihu does here in the verses following. Others understand it of some other present, who by gestures or otherwise, declared themselves to be of Job's opinion, and encouraged him in it. See on chap. 18. 2. Elihu would stop two gaps with one bush, and not only answer Job; but the rest also, if they had anything to object. But it may be understood of all others that were of Job's opinion though absent. If these were present, Elihu is confident, he could answer them all in this particular. Such are counted Job's companions, because they were of his opinion. See on chap. 34. 8. 36. For companions, see on chap. 16. 20. on the word, *Friends*.

**V. 5. ***{Look unto the heavens and see, and behold the clouds, which are higher than thou}* In this answer, Elihu first shows, that God could get no good by Job's righteousness, nor hurt by his sins, ver. 5, 6, 7. 2. That himself might, ver. 8. In the first, he 1. proves it by a comparison between Job and the heavens in this v. 2. By applying it, verse 6, 7. The sum of this verse is, that there is a great difference between the heavens and Job, so that his power cannot help, nor hurt them. Much less God, who is above the highest heavens, chap. 22. 12.

*{look unto the heavens and see}* He wishes him to consider the distance between God, and him, by the distance between heaven and him, which is great, yet not so great. For Heavens, see on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. For See, look on Chap. 19. 27. on the word, *Behold*. Two words of one signification are used to show, that he required an earnest observation. Others conceive, that by the first is meant observing of the truth of the thing, and by the other, looking on it with admiration, which agrees well with Isa. 42. 18. Look that you may see, understand the thing, that you may admire. However, these two words are often joined in Scripture, as 1 Sam. 17. 42. 2 King. 3. 14. Psal. 22. 17. Lam. 1. 12.

*{and behold the clouds}* Some take this to be the same in other words. Others by the heaven mean the cloudy heaven; for that it is visible, and translate this last part, *Behold the thin heavens*, that is, the starry heavens, which is also visible, and higher than the other. And this answers well to the original of the word, which comes from a word that signifies to beat or wear a thing till it be thin.

*{Which are higher than thou}* Or, *that they are*, or, *how they are, &c.* Consider that they are high above you, and too high for you to reach, to do good, or hurt to them, much more God, and therefore you cannot help him with your piety, nor hurt him with your impiety, as you think you may, and therefore expect immunity from troubles.

**V. 6. ***{If thou sinnest, what doest thou against him, or if thy transgressions be multiplied, what doest thou unto him?}* It may be you have some conceit, that you can do some hurt to God by your sins, but you are wonderfully deceived; for if you heap sin upon sin, he is never the worse, and therefore need not to fear to punish you for your sins.

*{If thou sinnest}* See on chap. 7. 20.

*{what doest thou against him?}* Or, *what workest thou against him?* You may toil yourself, but you can do no hurt to him, he is far above your reach.

*{or if thy transgressions}* See on chap. 7. 21.

*{be multiplied}* Be many, or, great. See on chap. 32. 9.

*{What dost thou unto him?}* Wherein can you do him any harm? For Doing, see on chap. 14. 9. upon the word, *Bring forth*.

**V. 7. ***{If thou be righteous, what givest thou him? or what receiveth he of thine hand?}* You think God is much beholding to you for your righteousness, but what good comes to him by it? As you cannot take anything from him, nor hurt him by your wickedness, so you cannot add anything to him nor help him by your goodness.

*{If thou be righteous,}* If you do righteous actions; for it is opposed to sinful actions, verse 6.

*{what givest thou him?}* What do you bestow on him, that may be beneficial to him? For it is opposed to doing against him, or hurting him, ver. 6. See on chap. 14. 4. upon the word, *Bring*.

*{or what receiveth he?}* What good or benefit receives he from you? Surely none at all. Chap. 22. 3. Psal. 16. 2. Rom. 11. 35. See on Chap. 13. 15. on the word, *Carry away*.

*{of thine hand}* Of you, or of that which is in your power to give, which he has not, and may be helpful to him. Sure, nothing at all can his wide hand that feeds all living creatures receive from your scanty hand, that cannot make food for yourself. Psal. 50. 9. &c. He names the hand, because it is the instrument of giving. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 23.

**V. 8. ***{Thy wickedness may hurt a man, as thou art, and thy righteousness may profit the son of man}* Seeing you cannot hurt God by your wickedness, nor help him by your goodness, it must needs follow, that the one may hurt, the other help yourself, and such as you are, or else there would be no difference indeed between well and ill doing in regard of success.

*{Thy wickedness}* See on Chap. 34. 20. He means wicked actions.

*{may hurt}* This is added out of the former verses to make up the sense: as also, May profit, is added for the same end.

*{a man}* See on Chap. 1.

*{as thou art}* That is yourself, or some other man like you, but cannot reach so high as God.

*{and thy righteousness}* Your righteous deeds.

*{May profit the son of man}* That is a man. See on Chap. 16. 21. upon the word, *Man*.

**V. 9. ***{By reason of the multitude of oppressions they make the oppressed to cry: they cry out by reason of the arm of the mighty}* Here begins the confutation of a second speech of Job, which is found, Chap. 24. 12. And 1. He acknowledges the truth of the thing in this verse. 2. He clears God from unrighteousness in not hearing the prayers of these oppressed ones, laying the fault upon themselves. 1. Because they were unthankful to God. 1. For former deliverances. verse 10. 2. For gifts given to them more than to other creatures. verse 11. 2. Because of the evil that is in them, as 1. Pride, wherewith they are charged, verse 12. 2. Vanity. verse 13. This verse runs, as if Elihu should have said. It is true O Job that you say, that God suffers many men to be unjustly oppressed by others, and rights them not, as soon as they complain. But the fault is in themselves, as I shall show by and by.

*{By reason of the multitude}* Or *of the magnitude.* For great oppressions make men cry out as well as many, and the word signifies both. See on chap. 32. 9. Others read, *Out of the multitude of the oppressed, whom they make to cry, &c. yet none saith*, verse 10. No marvel if God hear them not: he has delivered them before, and they regarded it not to praise him for it.

*{of oppressions}* Or *of the oppressed*, them they make to cry.

*{they make the oppressed to cry}* They lay such heavy load upon them, that they being not able to bear it, cry to God to right them. They, that is the oppressors, the nominative included in the verb, as Gen. 50. 26. *They embalmed Joseph*, that is, the embalmers did it. Or, it may be read impersonally, *The oppressed were made to cry.* See the like on Chap. 3. 3.

*{they cry out}* See on Chap. 19. 7. & 29. 12.

*{by reason of the arm}* Because of the strength of great men used to oppress them, as armed men beat others with a strong arm. For the word see on Chap. 22. 8.

*{of the mighty}* Of great men, that can do much wrong to others. See on Chap. 32. 9.

**V. 10. ***{But none saith, where is God my maker, who giveth songs in the night}* He now sets out the causes, why these oppressed ones were not delivered. They were ready more to complain of wrong done by men, than to praise God for good done to them, and therefore God would not hear them. It may be also a close blow upon Job, as if he had not been thankful to God in his prosperity, and therefore God would not hear him in his adversity.

*{But}* Heb. *And.* Yet it is often so translated to set out an opposition; as Chap. 30. 1.

*{none}* No one of these oppressed ones. Some take it of the oppressors, but that suits not well with the coherence. Heb. *And he saith not.*

*{where is God}* Some take it to be the voice of one praying for help. But it is rather a voice of praise. None looks up to God in a way of thankfulness for former benefits. For God, see on chap. 9. 17. & 11. 5.

*{my maker}* Heb. *My makers*. Alluding to the Trinity of persons, or speaking of God in the plural number by way of honor. See the like Isa. 54. 5. This title was given to God. Chap. 32. 22. For the word see on Chap. 10. 8. & 14. 9. upon the word, *Bringeth forth*. God may be called our maker, as our creator. Gen. 1. 26, 27. or as our raiser to honor, as he is said to make Moses and Aaron. 1 Sam. 12. 6. that is, to advance them, as it is there translated.

*{who giveth}* See on chap. 14. 4. upon the word, *Bring*.

*{songs}* Matter of songs. Cause to praise him.

*{in the night}* Gives cause in the night to praise him the day following for preserving us and ours in our sleep, when we could not so much as think of our safety, or, who gives us much comfort in the night putting into us many comfortable thoughts of his favor to us, that we may praise him, when we cannot sleep. Others interpret it of the stars shining in the night, and giving the beholders cause to praise God, Psal. 19. 1, 2. others of the melody of the birds, as the nightingale, which may stir us up to praise God. Others of comforting us in, or delivering us out of the night of our afflictions. But the two first are most natural. Psal. 42. 8. 149. 5.

**V. 11. ***{Who teacheth us more then the beasts of the earth, and maketh us wiser then the fowles of heaven}* God gives us reasonable souls, which are capable of wisdom, and which he has not given to beasts nor birds. Now those that consider not well of these favors to praise him for them, he suffers to be oppressed by ungodly men, and delivers them not, though they cry to him. Which yet he would do were they thankful for former mercies.

*{who teacheth us}* Who gives us a mind capable of all arts, and sciences, and preserves men before all other creatures.

*{more than the beasts of the earth}* Either wild or tame, though sometimes the word be used for wild beasts. They are called beasts of the earth in opposition to fowls of the air. Those go on earth, these fly in the air. For Beasts see on Chap. 18. 3. For Earth, on Chap. 14. 8.

*{and maketh us wiser}* Gives us more understanding.

*{than the fowles of heaven}* They may have some natural forecast, but want reason which God gives to man. See on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

**V. 12. ***{There they cry (but none giveth answer) because of the pride of evil men}* The parenthesis were better left out, that the latter end might depend upon God's not giving answer, not upon their crying. And the sense is, not that they cried out for the pride of the oppressors, for this is a part of Elihu's answer, not out of Job's objection. verse 1. 9. but the oppressed were so proud, that though they complained of their oppressors, yet would they not humbly pray to God for deliverance, as they ought in their present miseries, nor praise him heartily as they should, for former deliverances. verse 10. Therefore their complaint is said to be vain or empty, which would not be so, if they sought aright to God.

*{There}* In that condition, when they are afflicted verse 9.

*{they cry}* They pray to God for deliverance. See on chap. 19. 7.

*{but none giveth answer}* Heb. *But he answereth not.* To wit God, to whom they cry, does not deliver them. Chap. 27. 9. Prov. 1. 28. & 15. 29. Isa. 1. 15. Jer. 11. 11. Joh. 9. 31. For But, see on Chap. 30. 1. For answering see on chap. 16. 1.

*{because of the pride of evil men}* Because they be proud, though they be poor, and do not humble themselves before God, as they should. So the first word is translated because. Heb. *From the face*. Gen. 36. 7. Exod. 9. 11.

**V. 13. ***{Surely God will not hear vanity, neither will the Almighty regard it}* Here another reason is rendered, why God will not relieve these oppressed ones, not only for their pride verse 12. but also for the emptiness of their prayers. They pray not heartily.

*{Surely}* A note of asseveration. There is no question to be made of it. So this word is used Gen. 26. 9. & 29. 14.

*{God}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{will not hear}* Will not regard their prayers, or grant their requests. See on chap. 13. 17.

*{vanity}* Vain men, when they pray. So chap. 24. 20. wickedness is put for wicked men. And iniquity is so used Psal. 107. 42. For the word see on chap. 15. 31. And that God hears not such prayers, see chap. 27. 9. Prov. 1. 28, 29. Isa. 1. 13. 15. Jer. 11. 11.

*{neither will the almighty}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{regard it?}* Look upon vain men with a gracious eye, or Regard that which their oppressors do to them to deliver them upon their prayers.

**V. 14. ***{Although thou sayest, thou shalt not see him, yet judgment is before him, therefore trust thou in him}* Here begins a confutation of a third speech of Job's taken out of chap. 23. verse 9. where he complains, that he could not come to the sight of God to reason the case with him. And Elihu 1. answers the objection in this verse showing, that though God would not come to an account, when Job calls him, yet he might be confident, he would do him right however he judged of him. 2. He shows the reason, why God afflicted him so heavily, because he trusted not in him. verse 15. 3. That for the not taking notice hereof God would not hear him to ease his pain, verse 16. This verse is variously read; Deodate thus, *How much lesse will he hear thee who sayest that thou regardest him not? Judge thy self before him and then attend*. That is, If God will not hear vain men, much less will he hear you, who have professed that you will not humble yourself before him, but will maintain your innocence against him? See therefore your error, humble yourself before God, and wait on him for deliverance. Piscator thus, *In this also hast thou sinned, that thou sayedst, thou shalt not see him. Judge thy self before him, and look for him.* That is, I charge you now with another evil speech; that you have said in effect, that God will never appear to do you right. Condemn yourself for this evil speech, and then you may with comfort look for relief from him.

*{although thou sayest}* See on chap. 16. 1.

*{thou shalt not see him}* So as to try your cause with him.

*{yet judgment is before him}* He will certainly do right in his own time. See on chap. 19. 29.

*{therefore trust thou in him}* Wait for him, as a woman in travail waits for delivery.

**V. 15. ***{But now, because it is not so, he hath visited in his anger, yet he knoweth it not in great extremity}* This is the truth of Job's condition (says Elihu) he does not put his trust in God, but often complains he has no further hope of good from him, therefore God proceeds in anger against him justly, and he is so obstinate in defending his own righteousness, that he will not acknowledge God's justice, nor submit to him, though he be in great extremity. As if he would be broken before he would bow. Beza reads it thus, *But know now that his anger hath visited thee but a little* (Heb. *nothing*) *neither hath he made any great inquisition. But Job, &c.* That is, take notice, that God has dealt far better with Job than Job with him. God has not punished him, as he deserves, yet he complains of too much severity in God's proceedings, who has not inquired into the multitude of his sins, else would he have punished him much more. Job is too well used to complain thus of God. The same thing is objected to Job, Chap. 11. 6.

*{But now}* See on chap. 30. 1.

*{because it is not so}* This has relation to the end of the last verse, because Job has not humbled himself before God, nor trusted in him for deliverance.

*{he hath visited}* God has visited Job, and continues still to afflict him heavily. See on chap. 5. 24. & 7. 18.

*{in his anger}* See on chap. 4. 9. & 9. 5. 13. & 14. 13.

*{yet he knoweth it not}* Yet Job takes no notice of it.

*{in great extremity}* Heb. *In great abundance.* To wit, of afflictions.

**V. 16. ***{Therefore doth Job open his mouth in vain; he multiplyeth words without knowledge}* It appears by that which I have said, that Job has hitherto spoken to little purpose, and that his words are such, that argue no sound knowledge in him; he has unjustly lifted up his innocence above God's.

*{therefore}* So this word is translated, chap. 11. 6.

*{doth Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1. Or, *shall Job.* I have solidly confuted him, that it is in vain for him to reply. He shall but show his own ignorance.

*{open his mouth}* A periphrasis of speaking, because for that end most commonly we open our mouths. For Mouth, see on chap. 15. 5.

*{in vain}* See on chap. 27. 12.

*{he multiplyeth words}* For multiplying, see on chap. 8. 2. on the words, *Strong*.

*{without knowledge}* See on chap. 34. 35.

## CHAPTER 36

**Verse 1. ***{Elihu also proceeded, and said}* Elihu's last speech takes up this chapter and the next, wherein having confuted divers particular speeches of Job in the former chapters, now he seeks to allay his complaints, with setting out God's justice, mercy, and omnipotency, which Job seemed to obscure by his bitter complaining of God's harsh proceeding with him, that so he might bring Job to submit to God, that he might be set free from his pain and trouble. In this last oration of his, there is, first a preface to it, to ver. 5. of this chapter. 2. The body of it, to ver. 23. of the 37. chapter. 3. The conclusion of it, in the two last verses of Chap. 37.

*{Elihu}* See on chap. 32. 2.

*{also proceeded}* Heb. *Added*, to wit, to his former speeches he added this last. For the word see on ch. 27. 1. on the word *continued*.

*{and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Suffer me a little, and I will shew thee, that I have yet to speak on God's behalf}* The former verse contained the writer's preface, this, with the two next, Elihu's. And herein he labors to draw Job to attention: 1. Because he would speak for God, in this verse. 2. From God, verse 3. 3. He would speak nothing but truth, verse 4. Either Job offered to interrupt him, or showed some sign of non-satisfaction, which makes Elihu ask further leave to speak, and promise to give further satisfaction, if he might have quiet audience.

*{Suffering me a little}* Give me a little more time to speak my mind of this subject.

*{and I will shew thee}* See on chap. 15. 17.

*{that I have yet to speak on God's behalf}* Heb. *That there are yet words for God.* That I have yet many reasons more to clear God's upright dealing. For ל see on Chapter 13. 7. & 16. 21. For God, see on chap. 5. 17. & 11. 5.

**V. 3. ***{I will fetch my knowledge from afar, and will ascribe righteousness to my maker}* My argument shall be drawn from those things which are not near to us, but far distant, by which the praise of justice, due to God our maker, may be ascribed and given unto him. Such things as God has from the beginning showed to us, do most set out his glory.

*{I will fetch}* See on Chap. 21. 3. on the word, *Suffer*.

*{my knowledge}* That which I know, and shall utter.

*{from afar}* From God himself. Chap. 32. 8. 18. Or, *from God's first works, which lively set out God's justice, and his other attributes*. From those generals I will come to your particular case.

*{and will ascribe}* Heb. *Give.* See on chap. 14. 4. on the word *Bring*.

*{righteousness}* I will acknowledge, publish, and maintain, that God is most just. I will give him his due, the praise of his own justice.

*{to my Maker}* To him that framed me curiously in the womb.

**V. 4. ***{For truly my words shall not be false; he that is perfect in knowledge, is with thee.}* Assure yourself that I will utter nothing but what is undoubtedly true, and that you have one to discourse with you, who rightly understands the whole matter.

*{For truly my words shall not be false}* That is, they shall be most true. Build upon it.

*{he that is perfect in knowledge is with thee}* He speaks of himself in the third person, for modesty's sake. As if he had said, I would have you to know, that I thoroughly understand this business, and therefore will speak nothing but the truth in it. Others understand it of God. You have need to hearken diligently, for you have to do with God, who speaks to you by me.

**V. 5. ***{Behold, God is mighty, and despiseth not any, he is mighty in strength and wisdom}* Here begins the body or substance of the speech; wherein note a description of God's wise, powerful, and just proceedings. 1. In things political to verse 26. 2. In things natural, to verse 23. of the next chapter. Both parts begin with an *Ecce*, Behold, as treating of notable matters. In the first part, 1. he lays down God's excellency in this verse. 2. Proves it to ver. 16. 3. Applies it to Job in particular, to verse 26. It is wonderful to consider what power there is in God, yet it is accompanied with so much justice, that he will not contemn the meanest man, although he have wisdom answerable to his strength to bear him out against all the world.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{is mighty}* See on Chap. 8. 2. on the word, *strong*.

*{and despiseth not any}* Or, *yet despiseth not any.* So that particle is rendered. Psal. 119. 109, 110. Job had accused God before, that he regarded not men's wrongs, nor would right them when they complained, Chap. 19. 7. & 23. 13. & 30. 21. But God does not despise any man to deny to do him right for the lowness of his condition, which Job had charged him withal, Chap. 10. 3. He does not despise any man without a cause, as the verses following declare.

*{he is mighty}* See before in this verse.

*{in strength and wisdom}* Heb. *heart*, Chap. 9. 4. & 12. 13, 16. & 37. 23. Or, *in strength of heart*. That is, in wisdom, he is full of it. For heart, see on chap. 34. 10. and on chap. 9. 4. & 11. 12.

**V. 6. ***{He preserveth not the life of the wicked, but giveth right to the poor}* What he had said before of God's excellent strength and wisdom, that he now proves by many particular passages of God's providence, both toward good men and bad, one interweaved within another.

*{He preserveth not the life of the wicked}* Heb. *He maketh not (or, suffereth not) the wicked to live*. He does not withdraw them from the hand of justice, yea oftentimes he slays them himself; he favors not the enterprises of wicked men, but does justice to such as are afflicted: he hates the works of wicked men, and at length destroys their persons. Contrary to what Job had said, chap. 21. See Psal. 55. 23. & 104. 35. So this word is taken not for making alive, but for keeping alive, Gen. 6. 19. Exod. 1. 17. And for wicked, see on chap. 3. 17.

*{but giveth right to the poor}* Or, *afflicted*. As he destroys wicked men that do wrong; so he upholds such as are wronged. For but, see on chap. 30. 1. For giving, see on chap. 14. 4. on the word, *bring*. For right, see on chap. 22. 4. on the word, *judgment*. For poor, see on chap. 29. 12.

**V. 7. ***{He withdraweth not his eyes from the righteous, but with Kings are they on the Throne, yea he doth stablish them for ever, and they are exalted}* Though the righteous be never so poor, yet are they still the objects of God's special providence: he not only delivers them from oppressors, but sometimes also prefers them to royal dignity, and establishes them in it to their dying day against all oppositions, when he has once settled them in it.

*{He withdraweth not his eyes from the righteous}* His eyes are always upon them, how low soever their condition be, he never fails them, Psal. 33. 18. & 34. 15. For eyes, see on chap. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*.

*{but with Kings are they on the Throne}* God raises up good men that are poor to sit with kings, Psal. 113. 7, 8. 1 Sam. 2. 8.

*{yea he doth establish them for ever}* He does not only set them up, but also settle them in their thrones. For ever, is put for a long time in Scripture language, Psal. 132. 14. See for the word on chap. 34. 36. upon the word, *End*.

*{and they are exalted}* Or, *when they are on high*. When they are once raised, he suffers them to come down no more, but upholds them in their high state as long as they live. So ו is translated when, Psal. 139. 16.

**V. 8. ***{And if they be bound in fetters, and be holden in cords of affliction}* But if it so come to pass, that some of these righteous persons, or such as seem so, for some of these perish, verse 12. thus preferred fall into sin, and are hampered with their transgressions, as with chains and fetters, then God uses the means of recovering them, as verse 9. &c.

*{And} Or*, or. So it is rendered, Exod. 21. 15, 17. Or, *But*; as chap. 30. 1.

*{if they}* If those righteous persons advanced, abuse their honor, God will make them to know it. For it is opposed to hypocrites, verse 13. Some understand it of evil men. Others of all sorts of men: but the first agrees best with the words foregoing and following.

*{be bound in fetters}* Kept in and straitened by their sins. Or, it may be understood literally, if they be carried from a throne to a prison for their sins, and change golden chains into iron fetters, verse 13. chap. 13. 27. Psal. 107. 10.

*{and be holden}* Heb. *caught*. Be taken, as a bird, or beast in a net, or snare, so as they cannot get themselves out.

*{in cords of affliction}* Or, *poverty*. In trouble, or want, afflicting them, and straitening them, as cords do the body. For cords, see on Chap. 18. 10. on the word, *snare*.

**V. 9. ***{Then he sheweth them their work, and their transgressions, that they have exceeded}* Though God suffer them to be bound in chains, yet he does not utterly reject them, but rather opens the eyes of their mind, that they may thoroughly consider what they have done, and to see that they have done amiss, and may repent, and mend for time to come. So that the end of these instructions is not their destruction, but correction.

*{Then}* Heb. *And*. Yet well translated Then here, as also chap. 13. 22.

*{he sheweth them}* Sets their work before them, and makes them to see it. So much the words import. They were blind before, now correction, or the work of God's Spirit upon it in their souls, clears their spiritual eyesight. See on chap. 15. 18. upon the word, *told*.

*{their work}* The quality of their works, that they are evil, and displeasing to God. For they knew what they had done before, but did not consider that they had done ill, till God's rods were upon them. Now he shows them what sins of theirs were the cause of this affliction, as the words following in this verse do declare.

*{and their transgressions}* See on chap. 7. 21.

*{that they have exceeded}* That they have been many and great, so as justly to provoke God thus to scourge them.

**V. 10. ***{He openeth also their ears to discipline: and commandeth that they return from iniquity}* As God by his Spirit inwardly, and by afflictions outwardly, sometimes opens men's eyes to see their own sins; so sometimes he opens their ears, that they may hearken to the words of such ministers as he sends to them in their sorrows, whose words before in their prosperity they regarded not, Chap. 33. 23.

*{He openeth also their ear to discipline}* See on Chap. 33. 16. where all these words are. The ear stopped cannot hear till it be unstopped, nor the ear of the mind, till cleared by God.

*{and commandeth}* See on Chap. 16. 1. on the word, *said*.

*{that they return}* See on Chap. 14. 13. on the word, *past*.

*{from iniquity}* See on Chap. 15. 35. & 16. 3. on the word, *vanity*.

**V. 11. ***{If they obey and serve him, they shall spend their days in prosperity, and their years in pleasures}* If men thus taught of God by and in their afflictions hearken to him, and serve him better upon recovery, they shall pass the rest of their life in abundance of all good things, and shall end their days with joy. Here is a hint for Job to repent of his unkind speeches against God, and then to expect a lasting prosperity, and herein Elihu was no false prophet. For (though not upon his) yet upon God's speech Job did humble himself, Chap. 40. 4, 5. & 42. 3, 6. and had a lasting prosperity, Chap. 42. 10. &c.

*{If they obey}* See on Chap. 13. 17. on the word *hear*.

*{and serve him}* See on Chap. 21. 15.

*{they shall spend}* Heb. *they shall finish*. The word כלה signifies. 1. To complete or finish a work according to a man's desire, Gen. 2. 2. 2. To make an end of a thing to the destruction of it, Psal. 90. 9. 3. To desire or expect a thing, as all expect complement and perfection. To long for a thing, 2 Sam. 23. 15. Here it is taken in the first sense for spending and ending men's days happily.

*{their days}* See on Chap. 14. 14.

*{in prosperity}* See on Chap. 21. 13. on the word, *wealth*.
