*{and their years}* They shall enjoy prosperity a long time, not only for days, which may be many, and yet end in a year: but for many years.

*{in pleasures}* They shall have abundance of pleasure all their life long. From this word Naomi had her name, Ruth 1. 20.

**V. 12. ***{But if they obey not, they shall perish by the sword, and they shall die without knowledge}* But if they will not hearken unto God, in their affliction, when he declares the cause of it to them that they may amend, then they shall die a violent death, because they would not be taught how to prevent it.

*{But}* See on Chap. 33. 2.

*{if they}* Those righteous men spoken of before; verse 7. For such may lose their lives for disobedience, if they amend not, when God visits them for sin. 1 Cor. 11. 30. or such as seem so, who may be taken away by the sword and sent to hell.

*{obey not}* See on Chap. 13. 17. on the word, *Hear*.

*{they shall perish by the sword}* See on Chap. 33. 18. Heb. *They shall passe away by the sword*.

*{and they shall die}* See on Chap. 13. 19.

*{without knowledge}* Heb. *Because they were without knowledge*. Because they would not be wise, and take notice wherefore God afflicted them, though he gave them secret hints of it, therefore God gave them over to the sword.

**V. 13. ***{But the hypocrites in heart heap up wrath, they cry not, when he bindeth them}* But men that sin not out of human frailty, but out of a mischievous mind and most impure affections of heart, they take no warning, neither do they seek to God for help, but by continuance in sin make God more angry with them still, till he do consume them.

*{But}* See on Chap. 30. 1. Or, *yet*, as is translated, Psal. 119. 109, 110. Though they see some good men repent, when God corrects them, and others cut off for not returning to God, yet they amend not by God's stroke.

*{the hypocrites}* Or, *profane men*. See on chap. 8. 13.

*{in heart}* See on chap. 15. 12.

*{heap up wrath}* Heb. *Put wrath*. Add more to that which was before. Provoke God daily more and more, Rom. 2. 5. 2 Chron. 28. 13, 22. For wrath, see on chap. 4. 9. & 9. 5, 13. & 14. 13.

*{they cry not}* They pray not to God for help, and deliverance. This may be a hint to Job to try, whether he be a hypocrite or no, who did complain of God in his trials, but did not humbly pray to him to deliver him, or, They are suddenly destroyed, and have no time to pray for deliverance. For the word, see on chap. 19. 7.

*{when he bindeth them}* When he corrects them. See on verse 8.

**V. 14. ***{They die in youth, and their life is among the unclean}* God deals with them, as with those cursed young men, who having spent their youth in all filthiness, and uncleanness came to an untimely end by loathsome diseases procured by their lusts.

*{They die in youth}* They are taken away by God's hand before they be old, chap. 15. 32. & 22. 16. & 33. 22. Psal. 55. 23. Heb. *Their soul dieth*. That is, themselves, or their life is taken away. For soul, see on chap. 14. 22. For youth, see on chap. 20. 11.

*{and their life is among the unclean}* They lie among filthy persons in the grave. See life attributed to dead men, chap. 14. 22. or Their life shall be taken away among the Sodomites; that is, They shall be destroyed by some extraordinary judgment, as the Sodomites were. The word does not so much look at Sodom as at the conversation of the inhabitants, they were ready to execute all filthiness even the confusion of sexes. So were these young men, and so much the word imports. So this word is used, Deut. 23. 17.

**V. 15. ***{He delivereth the poor in his affliction, and openeth their ears in oppression}* But God deals otherwise with godly men, for he frees them, out of their troubles, when he has by private monitions of his Spirit brought them to humble their souls before him.

*{He delivereth}* God does, who is spoken of verse 11. The word חלץ signifies to pull off a thing, as the shoe, Deut. 25. 9, 2. To deliver out of trouble, Psal. 6. 4. 3. To draw out of a multitude, or to choose men to be armed for soldiers. Numb. 31. 3. 4. To fatten, for as are beasts fatted, that go in choice pastures, and are chosen before others, Isa. 58. 11. Here it is taken in the second sense, for delivery out of trouble.

*{the poor}* Or, *the afflicted*. See on chap. 29. 12.

*{in his affliction}* Or, *in his poverty*.

*{and openeth their ears}* Or, *when he hath opened their eares*. When he has taught them to make a good use of their afflictions. For ו When see Psal. 139. 16. For the other words, see on chap. 33. 16.

*{in oppression}* When they are oppressed by others, and cry to him for help.

**V. 16. ***{Even so would he have removed thee out of the strait into a broad place, where there is no straitness, and that which should be set on the table, should be full of fatness}* Now he comes to apply to Job in particular, what he had said in general, and that from hence to verse 26. wherein he tells Job. 1. What might have been, if he had carried himself well in his affliction, in this v. 2. What is on him, because he did not, v. 17. 3. What will be, if he repent not, to v. 26. In this v. he tells Job, that if he had made a good use of his afflictions, God would both have delivered and prospered him: and this he sets out by two similitudes, one taken from a man that dwells in a large ground, where he has liberty enough; and the other from a man that keeps a plentiful table full of all manner of delicacies. Liberty and wealth should have attended on Job, if he had carried himself wisely in his troubles.

*{Even so}* As he uses to deal with such as are truly humbled under his rod, so would he have dealt with you.

*{would he have removed thee}* He would have delivered you, v. 20, 11. The word signifies to persuade. Now because those that are persuaded by others, are removed from their former purposes, therefore it also signifies to remove. As 2 Chron. 18. 31.

*{out of the strait}* Out of your troubles, which have enclosed you like a prisoner in a narrow room, where you can scarce stir or breathe. Heb. *out of the mouth of the strait*. A similitude from a wild beast, that has a lamb in its mouth, or a pit that gapes as it were, to devour such as fall into it. Hereby is intimated deliverance from some great trouble or danger. For Mouth, see on chap. 15. 5.

*{into a broad place}* Into much liberty, Psal. 18. 19.

*{where there is no straitness}* Into an exceeding large place. He would have given you abundance of liberty.

*{and that which should be set on thy table}* The dishes of meat appointed for your food. Or *the rest of thy table*. You should quietly have enjoyed a plentiful table, Psal. 23. 5. The word שלחן signifies. 1. The table itself, Exod. 25. 23. 2. A table furnished with meat, Psal. 78. 19. 3. An altar, Ezek. 41. 22. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{should be full of fatness}* You should have the fattest meats, and daintiest delicacies on your table. Others understand this v. of God's former dealing with Job, and read it, *Even so hath he removed thee*. And, *That which was on thy table, was full of fatnesse*. You have had experience O Job of what I have said, in your former prosperity. But it does not appear, that Job was in poverty or strait before, and therefore the former interpretation is better.

**V. 17. ***{But thou hast fulfilled the judgment of the wicked: judgment and justice take hold on thee}* But you have taken a quite contrary course; you have so behaved yourself in reasoning with God that it is too manifest, how guilty and worthy you are of that judgment which God is wont to lay upon heinous offenders. And so it came to pass, as you yourself see that that judgment, which he most justly has given upon you does still stand, and remain unmovable.

*{But}* So this particle is translated, chap. 30. 1.

*{thou hast fulfilled the judgment of the wicked}* You have carried yourself like a wicked man in charging God to deal unjustly with you in afflicting you so heavily, and so have confirmed them in their proud carriages against God. See on chap. 34. 8. others understand it of Job's ill carriage in his prosperity, either in the course of his life, or in favoring wicked men in judicature. But Elihu never charges Job with anything but miscarriages under God's hand in his affliction. For fulfilling, see on chap. 15. 2. on the word *Fill*. For wicked, see on chap. 3. 17.

*{judgment and justice take hold on thee}* Therefore God has just cause to pass a sentence of proceeding still in anger against you: and his sentence so takes hold of you, that you cannot get out of your misery. These are joined together, Psal. 9. 4. where it is interpreted cause and right. Some by justice understand the fair prosecution of the cause in the trial of it, and by judgment the pronouncing a faithful sentence. Or, *Judgement and justice should uphold thee*. But that agrees not well with the coherence. For justice, see on Chap. 22. 4. on the word *judgment*. For taking hold, תמך is translated holding, Am. 1. 5, 8. Sometimes it signifies upholding, as Psal. 63. 8. But the first signification suits best with this place.

**V. 18. ***{Because there is wrath, beware lest he take thee away with his stroke, then a great ransom cannot deliver thee}* Here Elihu counsels Job, what to do for time to come. 1. Not to provoke God further, to v. 21. 2. To take heed of iniquity. to v. 24. 3. To give honor to God, to v. 26. In the first part, first he exhorts him, v. 18. 2. Removes impediments of hearkening, as hope of ransom to free him, v. 18, 19. or force, v. 19. or hiding, v. 20. I perceive that God is angry with you by the continuance of your plagues. Beware you offend him no further by your obstinacy, lest he make an end of you. Assure yourself, if you had ever so much to give, it would not be accepted to save your life.

*{because there is wrath}* Because it appears by God's heavy hand on you, that he is very angry with you. For wrath, see on Chap. 19. 29.

*{beware}* This word is not in the original, but well added by the translators to make up the sense. As also Isa. 36. 18.

*{lest he take thee away}* Lest God destroy you, and so take you clean out of this world. For the word, see on v. 16. on the word, *Removed*.

*{with his stroke}* See on Chap. 34. 26.

*{then}* So this particle is translated, Chap. 25. 1. When you have provoked God so far, that he resolves to destroy you, it will be in vain to think of any ransom. Therefore prevent it beforehand by repentance.

*{a great ransom}* All that you can have to give, if you had all the world, Psal. 49. 7, 8, 9. For great, see on Chap. 22. 5. For ransom, see on Chap. 33. 24.

*{cannot deliver thee}* Heb. *Turne thee aside*. To wit, from death and destruction.

**V. 19. ***{Will he esteem thy riches? no not gold, nor all the forces of strength}* Do you think that if you could recover as much wealth, as you had before, or were full of gold, or had an army of strong men to assist you, that God would be hired or forced to release you from destruction? No certainly, you should then but feed yourself with vain thoughts.

*{will he esteem thy riches?}* He will make no account at all of wealth, that cannot free you, Prov. 10. 2. & 11. 4. For Esteem, see on Chap. 28. 17. on the word, *Equal*. For Riches, see on Chap. 34. 19. on the word, *Rich*. Others read it. *Wilt he regard thy crying?* That is, your prayer in your adversity. So the word is used, Chap. 30. 24.

*{no not gold}* This agrees well with the words before. Or, *no not any defence*. Which agrees well with the words following. For the word, see on Chap. 22. 24, 25. Others read it, *No not in affliction*.

*{nor all the forces of strength}* Not any force or strength, though the greatest in the world, can deliver you if God purpose to destroy you.

**V. 20. ***{Desire not the night, when people are cut off in their place}* As neither riches nor strength can shelter you from God's stroke, so wit cannot prevent it. Think not to find any hiding place to secure you. God's stroke lights on evildoers in the darkest night, Psal. 139. 12.

*{Desire not the night}* Some understand it of desiring the night for rest, which Job had done. Job 7. 13. Others of the night of death, which he had desired, Chap. 7. 15. But it is rather to be understood of the darkness of the night, wherein he might think to hide himself from God's stroke; but all in vain. For desire, see on Chap. 7. 2.

*{when people are cut off in their place}* You cannot be safe in the darkest night, for God has destroyed nations in the dark, as the Egyptians in the Red Sea, and Sennacherib's army. Chap. 27. 20. and 34. 20. Are cut off in their place. Heb. *Ascend under them*. See the like phrase, Chap. 5. 26. Psal. 102. 24. As the light of a candle, when it is ready to go out, flies up and then vanishes away. Or, as the corn is first taken up by the hand of the reaper, and then cut off, and laid flat on the ground. One verb included in another, as Psal. 68. 18. Received gifts for men, to wit, to give to them, as it is expounded, Eph. 4. 8. So here, They go up, that they may be cast down lower, Psal. 102. 10.

**V. 21. ***{Take heed, regard not iniquity; for this hast thou chosen, rather than affliction}* Here is Elihu's second exhortation to Job to take heed of speaking unjustly of God in regard of his troubles, in this verse, which he backs with reasons from God's power, and wisdom. ver. 22. and from his authority, and justice, ver. 23. The sum of this verse is, as if he had said, Now I warn you to take heed of speaking unequally of God's ways any more. I see I had need to give you such an admonition; for you are readier sin to do, than patiently to bear what God has laid on you.

*{Take heed}* Be very wary for time to come,

*{regard not}* Turn not your face to it in way of approbation, as men do to things they like. For the word, see on Chap. 5. 1 & 21. 5.

*{iniquity}* See on Chap. 15. 35. & 16. 3. on the word, *Vanity*. By iniquity here he means Job’s unjust speeches against God’s proceedings with him.

*{for this hast thou chosen}* To wit, iniquity, and rash censuring of God’s ways, as appears by your former speeches. See on Chap. 34. 33.

*{rather then affliction}* You had rather accuse God of afflicting you unjustly, than bear your affliction patiently. Others read it thus, *For, for this cause hast thou chosen any thing rather than affliction*. Because your heart dislikes God’s dealing with you, you had rather say any evil of him than be afflicted justly by him. Others thus, *for this thou hast chosen because of thine affliction*. You are so transported with the sense of your pains, that you care not what you say of God.

**V. 22. ***{Behold God exalteth by his power; who teacheth like him?}* Look well at God, and you shall see, that though many in the world give many monuments of their power and wisdom, yet none comes near to God in working or informing.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{exalteth}* Himself, by his seat in heaven, or his great works on earth. Or, he exalts the afflicted by delivering them, or the poor by preferring them, Psal. 9. 13. & 18. 48.

*{by his power}* By his great and infinite strength, whereby he can do what none else can.

*{who teacheth like him?}* No man, can teach so wisely as God, and therefore none can direct him how to act better than he does. Therefore you ought not to find fault with his dealings with you. Isa. 40. 13. Rom. 11. 34. 1 Cor. 2. 18. Or, no man teaches in so strange a way as God does. He can teach you wisdom, by these sore afflictions. For Teaching, see on Chap. 6. 24. & 8. 10.

**V. 23. ***{Who hath enjoined him his way? Or, who can say, thou hast wrought iniquity?}* Who has showed God how he ought to govern the world? No man had any such power over him to prescribe him his way. And though he be most free, yet never any man could charge him with one unjust act. Therefore you, O Job, do most rashly and arrogantly complain of his proceedings against you, as if you could teach him to govern better, or had taken him tripping in his dealings, and got some great advantage against him.

*{Who hath enjoined him his way}* Who has prescribed to him what he should do? For enjoining, see on Chap. 34. 13. on the words, *Giving him a charge*. For Way, see on Chap. 17. 9.

*{or who can say}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{thou hast wrought iniquity}* Who can charge God with any unrighteous actions? Chap. 8. 3. & 34. 10. Deut. 32. 4. 2 Chron. 19. 7. Rom. 9. 14. For Iniquity, see on Chap. 16. 3. & 15. 35. on the words, *vain, and vanity*.

**V. 24. ***{Remember that thou magnify his work, which men behold}* This is Elihu’s third exhortation to Job, to give glory to God, rather than to stand upon his own innocence. Which is set down in this verse, and a reason given of it in the next. The sum of this verse is, that Job give God the glory of afflicting him, which other men stand and gaze at, as wonderful. Others understand it of the works of creation, which agrees with the following part of the Chapter.

*{Remember}* Think seriously of it, which now you seem by your ill carriage to have forgotten.

*{that}* So this particle is translated, ver. 10.

*{thou magnify}* Set it out at large, and speak highly of it.

*{his work}* His strange correcting you; or his work of creation.

*{which men behold}* Other men dare not judge or censure God for afflicting you so heavily, as you do, but look at it with admiration. Others understand it of God’s works in the sky, as the meteors, which are admired by men. Some read it, *whereof men sing*. For which they praise God. For Man, see on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

**V. 25. ***{Every man may see it, man may behold it a far off}* Though you will not, yet all other men take notice of God’s special hand upon you, although they be not able to see the reason of it exactly, as being above their reach. Some understand it of the meteors, which all men behold though they do not thoroughly conceive of God’s secret manner of forming them in the clouds.

*{Every man}* Every man of understanding. For foolish men take no notice of God’s proceedings, Psal. 92. 6, 7. For the word Man, see on Chap. 14. 1.

*{may see it}* Take notice of it easily.

*{man}* See on Chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{may behold it}* See on Chap. 35. 5. on the word, *Look*.

*{a far off}* Imperfectly discern it, not fully see it, as things very high, or far remote, are seen but in part, as it were through a glass, and not so plainly as things that are nearer to us.

**V. 26. ***{Behold God is great, and we know him not, neither can the number of his years be searched out}* Now Elihu begins to set out God in his government of the world in regard of natural things, to ver. 23. of the next Chapter. And first he sets out the greatness and eternity of God in this verse. 2. He proves it by the meteors, and seasons afterwards. Seeing that we know God’s works but in part, ver. 25. certainly we cannot know God himself fully, neither is there any brain of man that can reach his greatness, or any arithmetician that can number his years.

*{Behold}* See on ver. 5.

*{God}* See on Chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{is great}* In power, glory, and majesty.

*{and we know him not}* So great, that we are not able fully to comprehend him, either in his essence or works.

*{neither can the number of his years be searched out}* Spoken after the manner of men, to set out God’s eternity; for he was before there were any years. He were a very old man that could live so many years, that the skillfullest arithmetician in the world could not reckon them. God’s continuance is beyond all count, Psal. 102. 24, 27.

**V. 27. ***{For he maketh small the drops of water, they pour down rain according to the vapour thereof}* The rest of Elihu’s speech is spent in describing the meteors and changes of the air, as rain, thunder, snow, &c. so interweaved one with another, that they cannot well be divided. God shows his great power daily, by dividing the drops of water in the cloud, so that the rain being thus dissolved, pours down upon the earth according to the proportion, and greatness of the vapor, whereof it is made.

*{For he maketh small the drops of water}* He lets them not fall down like a flood, lest they should drown the earth, but does as it were divide them in the cloud, and make them little. Others read it, *He draweth up*. To wit, vapors from below by the heat of the sun, which being dissolved in the cloud, fall down again in rain. Others thus, *He withdraweth the drops of raine*. He causes it not to rain for a time, and when he pleases it rains again; as in the end of the verse. For water, see on Chap. 14. 9.

*{they pour down rain according to the vapour thereof}* As the vapors drawn up from below are greater or lesser, so there is more or less rain. He purifies the vapors, and so distills them in rain. See on Chap. 28. 1. upon the word, *Fine*. For vapor, see on Chap. 18. 12. on the word, *Destruction*.

**V. 28. ***{Which the clouds do drop and distil upon man abundantly}* Though there be much water in the clouds, yet God orders it so, that it falls down by drops, yet in such plenty, that it waters many countries, where abundance of men dwell.

*{Which}* Which rain. Or, *From whence*. Out of which vapors.

*{the clouds}* The receptacles of the rain. See on Chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. on the word *Heaven*.

*{do drop}* Send down on the earth by small drops one after another.

*{and distil}* The same thing in other words.

*{upon man}* See on Chap. 14. 1.

*{abundantly}* In great plenty. Or, *upon many men*. For the rain falls at times all the world over, and waters many men’s grounds.

*{29. Also can any understand the spreading of the clouds, or the noise of his Tabernacle?}* Moreover, is there any one who knows how far the lightnings will spread themselves in the clouds, as verse 30. or that knows the nature of the rattling thunderclaps, which come forth of the clouds, wherein God sits in secret, and unseen, as in a tabernacle, creating many strange meteors, to send down upon the earth?

*{Also can any understand the spreading of the clouds?}* Can the wisest man in the world tell how far the clouds will spread, or on what places of the world they will pour down rain? As verse 27, 28.

*{or the noise of his Tabernacle}* Some understand this of the noise the winds make in the air. Others of the thunderclaps, Psal. 104. 7. God’s tabernacle, is said to be in the clouds, Psal. 18. 11. & 104. 3.

**V. 30. ***{Behold he spreadeth his light upon it, and covereth the bottom of the Sea}* It is a wonderful thing to consider, how God spreads the lightning over the dark clouds, and makes them look bright like fire, yea which is more strange, he makes the lightnings dart so abundantly through the waters of the sea, that they do as it were cover all the bottom of it.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1. 12.

*{he spreadeth his light}* He scatters it all abroad, as a cloth is spread. For Light, see on Chap. 17. 12.

*{upon it}* Upon the cloud mentioned, ver. 29. or upon him, that is, upon man, as ver. 28.

*{and covereth the bottom of the sea}* Heb. *The roots of the sea*. He not only makes the lightning cover the earth, but also pierce deep into the seas. For Root, see on Chap. 5. 3. & 14. 8. For the Sea, see on Chap. 14. 11.

**V. 31. ***{For by them judgeth he the people, he giveth meat in abundance}* Here he sets out the infinite wisdom of God, who by the same means can work good to his, and hurt to his enemies; he can make rain, lightning, thunder, beneficial, or hurtful, as he pleases, by drowning the fruits of the earth by a flood, or cherishing them by moderate rain, and blasting trees by thunder, or clearing the air without hurting any thing, Chap. 37. 13.

*{For by them}* By rain, lightning, and thunder.

*{judgeth he the people}* He punishes them by drowning and blasting some people, or their corn and cattle.

*{he giveth meat in abundance}* By watering the earth in other places, and at other times, so that it brings forth abundance of corn. For giving, see on chap. 14. 4. on the word, *bring*. For abundance, see on chap. 8. 2, on the word, *strong*.

**V. 32. ***{With clouds he covereth light, and commandeth it not to shine by the cloud that cometh betwixt}* In this verse is a preparation for rain, when God raises up clouds to darken the light of the sun.

*{with clouds}* The word כף signifies a crooked or hollow thing, and is used. 1. For the hand, as chap. 16, 17. 2. For a hollow vessel, spoon, or platter, er. 52. 18, 19. 3. The hollow place, or middle of a sling, 1 Sam. 25. 29. 4. The hollow place of the thigh, Gen. 32. 25. 5. A cloud, which is an hollow thing, chap. 26. 9. So here 1 Kings 18. 44. For clouds first appear small like a man’s hand shut, and then spread out like the palm of a man’s hand.

*{he covereth the light}* Hides the sun from our eyes, and so sends dark and rainy days. It may be read thus, *he covereth the light over the clouds*. That is, he spreads it over them, as verse 30. And when he will he takes away the light again, as in the end of this verse. And this suits well with the former verse, and shows fair and foul weather to be at God’s will, to send when he pleases. For light, see on chap. 17. 12.

*{and commandeth it not to shine}* Not to shine is added to make up the sense. Heb. *and commandeth it*. Or, *layes a command upon it*. But it is observed, that this word with the preposition על (as here) in Scripture language signifies most commonly to forbid, or command not to do a thing, Gen. 2. 16. & 28. 6. 1 King. 2. 43.

*{by the cloud that cometh betwixt}* To wit, to darken it. The cloud is added to make the sense plain. Others read, *and commandeth it, (or gives it a command) concerning the showre that is coming*, as verse 33.

**V. 33. ***{The noise thereof sheweth concerning it, the cattle also concerning the vapour}* The thunder gives warning that rain is coming, and cattle that flee to shelter, when the vapor ascends. For of a tempest coming he speaks, verse 27. 28, &c.

*{The noise thereof sheweth concerning it}* The thunder, which is called the noise of the cloud, because it arises from the renting of the cloud. Or, *his noise*. That is, God’s, who makes it to thunder. This bears witness that a tempest is coming, and gives warning to living creatures to keep out of the way. For showing, see on chap. 15. 18. on the word, *told*.

*{the cattle also}* Hogs, and sheep, and oxen, that go to hide themselves when they perceive a tempest coming. This was an ordinary thing in that country, where were store of cattle, Chap. 1. 3.

*{concerning the vapour}* Heb. *concerning that which goeth up*. Those cattle are much more quick-sensed than men, and can perceive the vapors going up to cause rain, before men can see or feel them. For ascending, see on chap. 20. 6. Others read it thus; *He declareth his will to it, whether it should fall upon the cattel, or on some plant that ascendeth.* He directs the storm to the destruction of cattle, or of corn and trees, which he will. As Exod. 9. 25.

## CHAPTER 37

**Verse 1. ***{At this also my heart trembleth, and is moved out of his place}* It seems that while Elihu was speaking of the thunder, God to confirm what he had said, caused it to thunder, that Job might be the more affected with what he said, and prepared with reverence to receive what God should say in the Chapters following. That there was a storm, appears chap. 38. 1. And the law was delivered with lightning and thunder, Exod. 19. 16. This unexpected coming of the thunder might well affright Elihu, as it did, in this verse. That it did then thunder, appears in verse 2.

*{At this also}* Or, *Even at this*. For it does not appear that he trembled at any thing before, till this unexpected thunder came. Others understand; At this which I shall say, for I intend yet to speak more gloriously of God’s great works. Or, *Therefore*, as it is translated, Gen. 2. 24.

*{my heart trembleth}* I am heartily afraid. For heart see on chap. 15. 12. The thunder has made great ones tremble, who denied that there was a God.

*{and is moved out of his place}* Leaps up and down as it used to do in a great fright. Or, is ready to leap out of my body, as the ship was said to be broken, Jon. 1. 4. And the net, Luke 5. 6. when they were but like to break. For place, see on chap. 18. 21. & 20. 9.

**V. 2. ***{Hear attentively the noise of his voice, and the sound that goeth out of his mouth}* Now he sets down what it was that made him afraid, the terrible crack of thunder then present, and exhorts others to regard it, that they may reverence God in his power as well as he.

*{Hear attentively}* Heb. *Hear in hearing*. There needed no great attention, for thunder speaks loud, and will be heard, but he would have them hear with the ears of the mind, and carefully to observe God’s great power, that spoke so terribly as no man could. For the word, see on chap. 13. 17.

*{the noise}* The word originally signifies an angry noise which makes others to tremble, as the roaring of the lion does the beasts, Am. 3. 8. Psal. 29. 3. It is God’s frighting, which is heard in the thunder, it terrifies the stoutest hearts. It makes so horrible a noise, that it is heard far and near.

*{of his voice}* Of God’s thunder, wherein he so speaks, that his people may take notice of his power, Psal. 29. 4. & 81. 7. And thereby he awakens men’s dullness, that they consider of his works. For the word, see on chap. 15. 21. on the word, *sound*.

*{and the sound}* The rumbling as it were, or inward noise of the thunder within the clouds, as doves mourn inwardly. See on chap. 27. 4. on the word, *utter*.

*{that goeth out}* See on chap. 20. 25. on the word, *cometh out*.

*{of his mouth}* Out of God’s mouth. For he makes himself known by the thunder, as men do by their words which come out of their mouths.

**V. 3. ***{He directeth it under the whole Heaven, and his Lightning unto the ends of the Earth}* He makes the noise of the thunder to ring in the air all abroad, as he directs it to any place under the whole heaven, and the lightning extends itself even to the most remote parts of the earth.

*{He directeth it}* Or, *maketh it to go right forward*. He guides it, and makes it to be heard where he pleases. It is not meant of the lightning nor thunderbolt (though God guide them also) but of the noise, or thunder itself, spoken of verse 2.

*{under the whole Heaven}* Wheresoever it is heard on earth, God directs it thither. Or, he makes it to be heard far and wide. See on chap. 28. 24.

*{and his Lightning}* Heb. his light. Hereby it appears that the former part of the verse was meant of the noise of the thunder, and not of the lightning here distinguished from it. For light, see on chap. 17. 12.

*{unto the ends}* Heb. *the wings*. A metaphor either from birds that spread their wings wide when they fly, or from the skirts of garments, which are at the ends of them, for the word signifies both. כנף is used. 1. For a wing, as Gen. 1. 21. 2. The utmost part of any thing. As 1. Of a garment, Num. 15. 38. 2. For the sails of a ship, which are spread like wings, and make ships as it were fly, Isa. 18. 1. 3. For the wings of an army, Isa. 8. 8. 4. For the farthest parts of the earth, Isa. 11. 12. 5. For the springing of the morning light, Mal. 4. 2. 6. For the swiftness of the wind, which seems to fly, it is so quick, and carries ships away apace, Psal. 18. 10. Here it is taken in the fourth borrowed sense for the corners of the earth, as also Job 38. 13.

*{of the Earth}* Of the world. See on chap. 15. 19.

**V. 4. ***{After it a voice roareth, he thundereth with the voice of his excellency, and he will not stay them when his voice is heard}* The thundercrack is before the lightning in nature, but we see the lightning first, which God sends as a forerunner, or warning piece, when he means to speak in thunder. Now the lightning is first seen, because the light passes through the air swifter than the noise, and enlightens it in an instant, whereas the noise goes by degrees. Thus God sends his light as an harbinger to his excellent and high voice. He delays not to send out such flames when he intends to thunder, or to send the thundercracks when it lightens.

*{After it}* After the lightning. v. 3.

*{A voice}* See on chap. 15. 21. on the word *Sound*; and in this chap. v. 2.

*{roareth}* The thunder makes an hideous noise far above the roaring of a lion, which the word originally signifies.

*{he thundereth}* See on chap. 26. 14.

*{with the voice}* See before on v. 2.

*{of his excellency}* Of his height. The word גאון sometimes signifies pride, as chap. 35. 12. because proud men think themselves higher than others. But God is indeed higher than all, and speaks accordingly in the thunder.

*{and he will not stay them, when his voice is heard}* After the thunder he presently sends rain and hail, spoken of in the end of the former Chapter. Others understand it of the lightnings, and read thus, He will not have it to be behind, when his voice is to be heard. The word stay, comes from a word which signifies the heel. The way to stay one, is to take him by the heel, Gen. 25. 26. For When, see on chap. 7. 13. His voice. God’s. See before in this v. Is heard. See on chap. 13. 17.

**V. 5. ***{God thundereth marvellously with his voice, great things doth he, which we cannot comprehend}* A transition from the thunder to other wonderful works of God, as if he had said, It cannot be denied that this voice of God is very wonderful; but other things no less admirable are done by God above the reach of our shallow capacity, as the bringing snow. v. 6, &c.

*{God}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{thundereth}* See on chap. 26. 14.

*{marvellously}* So as no creature can make such a noise. Others read it, *God thundereth marvellous things with his voice*. And refer it to those strange things, which are wrought by the thunders, as melting the money without hurting the purse, breaking the sword within the scabbard, exhausting the wine within the barrel, breaking the bones within the flesh, which are things above our reach. For Marvelous, see on chap. 5. 9.

*{with his voice}* See on chap. 15. 21. on the word, *Sound*.

*{great things doth he}* See on chap. 5. 9.

*{which we cannot comprehend}* Above our reach and capacity, chap. 36. 26, on the word, *Know*. Or, which we take no notice of. But pass them by carelessly, as if they were not worthy of our observation.

**V. 6. ***{For he saith to the snow, Be thou on earth; likewise to the small rain and to the great rain of his strength}* For he it is, that no sooner speaks the word, but the snow covers the earth like wool: and at his command both gentle showers, and tempestuous storms come upon the earth.

*{For}* This word giving a reason of what went before, shows plainly, that the marvelous things spoken of, v. 5. were the effects of the thunder, but new meteors such as follow.

*{he saith}* He commands the snow to come, and it comes, Gen. 1. 3. For the word, see on chap. 16. 1.

*{to the snow}* Snow and rain, and all the meteors are at God’s command, Psal. 147. 15, 16.

*{be thou}* Not only come, but abide till I call you back.

*{on the earth}* See on chap. 15. 19.

*{likewise to the small rain, and to the great rain of his strength}* Heb. *And to the showre of raine, and to the shower of rains of his strength*. That is to the gentle rain dropping out of the clouds, and to the violent storms that fall as evidences of God’s great strength. Others read it thus, *Or to showers of raine*: then showers of much raine come.

**V. 7. ***{He sealeth up the hand of every man; that all men may know his work}* Now he shows what befalls men in this v, and beasts in the next, when great snow, or rain falls upon the earth. Their work is hindered, that they sitting at home may have time seriously to consider of God’s great work abroad.

*{He sealeth up}* He hinders them from working, while the snow or rain lasts, and makes them keep within doors, as if they were shut up in their houses under God’s seal, as Noah was in the ark, Gen. 7. 16. For the word there used for shutting up come סגריר rain, Prov. 27. 15. because it shuts men up in their houses, and hinders them from working in the fields. For sealing, see on chap. 9. 7.

*{the hand}* The labor. He hinders them from working, Exod. 9. 19. For the word, see on chap. 15. 23.

*{of every man}* Of every man that has work to do abroad in the fields, for within doors men may work. For Man, see on chap. 14. 1.

*{that all men}* For man, see on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{may know}* May take notice, that God has sequestered them from their field labors.

*{his work}* That the snow and rain that shut them in, came from God. Others read it, *That every man may know his workmen*. That masters of families may have time to call their servants to account. A frigid interpretation, and nothing to the setting out of God’s glory by so wonderful a work, which is the scope of this place.

**V. 8. ***{Then the beasts go into dens and remain in their places}* So great is the force of a tempest, that not only it drives men home, but even wild beasts to their dens, which use to prey in the night notwithstanding the darkness, if the weather be calm, Psal. 104. 20, 21.

*{Then}* When the rain and snow falls verse, 6.

*{the beasts}* It is true of all beasts, but especially of wild beasts, which must yield notwithstanding their great strength. They have their name from life or liveliness in the Original.

*{go}* See on chap. 15. 21. on the word, *Come*.

*{into dens}* Into the dark places to shelter them from the storm, where they use to lie in ambush to catch their prey. For so much the word signifies in the Original, chap. 38. 40.

*{and remain}* Abide here till the storm be over. The word signifies dwelling also, for men use to abide where they dwell.

*{in their places}* Heb. *In their dwellings*. That is, in their holes.

**V. 9. ***{Out of the south cometh the whirlwind and cold out of the North}* Now he passes on from snow and rain to whirlwinds, frost, and ice. At God’s command sometimes the south wind gathers clouds together, whereof comes a tempest, and sometimes the north wind comes and scatters them, and so brings cold weather, but clear sky, verse 22.

*{out of the south}* Heb. *Out of the chamber*. See on chap. 9. 9. Others understand it out of a secret place, as Job 23. 9. Psal. 135. 7. But the south is best, because it brings tempest, and it is here opposed to the north, which brings fair cold weather.

*{cometh}* See on chap. 15. 21.

*{the whirlwind}* See on chap. 21. 18. on the word, *storm*.

*{and cold out of the North}* Heb. *out of the scattering windes*, which are the northern winds which dispel the clouds, and purify the air, and therefore are called by some learned men the brooms of heaven. Then follows fair cold weather, when the sky is cleared.

**V. 10. ***{By the breath of God, frost is given, and the breadth of the waters is straitened}* When God please, a cold blast comes forth which brings frost, whereby the waters, that ran at large before, are as it were shut up into a narrow prison by the ice, which like the prison walls encloses them.

*{By the breath of God}* By a cold wind coming from God, as if he did breathe it out of his mouth, Psal. 18. 15. For the frost comes from the excessive cold winds, and therefore it is most rife in northern countries, in so much that the sea is frozen in those parts. For God, see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{frost is given}* Heb. *He giveth frost*. But it may be translated passively. See the like on chap. 3. 3. That which here is translated frost, is divers times in this book translated ice. It comes from a word, that signifies to make bald, for it covers the grass which is as it were the hair of the ground, and makes it smooth like a bald head. For giving, see on chap. 14. 4. on the word, *Bring*.

*{and the breadth of the waters is straitened}* The waters are kept in from overflowing by the ice. Or, They are congealed into ice. And therefore ice is defined by some to be much water in a little room, chap. 38. 30. For Breadth and straitened, see on chap. 36. 6. For water, see on chap. 14. 9.

**V. 11. ***{Also by watering he wearieth the thick clouds; he scattereth his bright cloud.}* As God sometimes sends frost and ice, so at other times he sends so much rain to water the earth, that the clouds are as it were weary with pouring it down. He also drives away those clouds of light, which were in the firmament before, as if he meant to send nothing but rain.

*{Also}* God varies his actions. Sometimes he sends fair weather, ver. 9. and sometimes foul, as in this verse.

*{by watering}* By watering the earth. By pouring down continual showers to that end. Or, by driving them to and fro to water divers parts of the earth, and then dissolving them quite into rain.

*{he wearieth the thick cloud}* Though the cloud be thick, and full of rain, yet he makes it spend all its store, and so makes it weary, as a man is at night, that has spent all his strength in laboring all the day.

*{he scattereth his bright cloud}* The cloud which was covered over with lightning before, chap. 36. 30. is now quite spent and dispersed, and appears not at all. His bright cloud. Heb. *[The] cloud of his light*. For Light, see on chap. 17. 12. Others read it. *And his light scattereth the cloud*. That is, the sun disperses them.

**V. 12. ***{And it is turned round about by his counsels, that they may do whatsoever he commandeth them upon the face of the world in all the earth}* Thus you see that God turns the clouds up and down as he pleases by his wisdom, that they may accomplish his ends, and that not in some places alone, but all the world over.

*{and it}* The cloud spoken of before in the former verse.

*{is turned round about}* Sometimes clouds are raised, sometimes scattered, sometimes driven as in a circle, or forward and backward from one place to another, sometimes bright, and sometimes dark.

*{by his counsels}* The word in the Original is derived from the ropes of the ship, in managing of which for the best advantage of the ship, consists the seaman’s skill. So God orders all these meteors severally for his own ends by his infinite wisdom.

*{that they may do}* That the clouds and other meteors before mentioned may effect what God would have them to do.

*{whatsoever he commands them}* A metaphor intimating, that the clouds and meteors are as ready to do what God requires, as a good servant is to do what his master commands. See the like, Levit. 25. 21.

*{upon the face of the world}* Publicly in all the habitable world, wheresoever any men dwell. For the word, Face, see on chap. 14. 20. on the word, *Countenance*. For World, see on chap. 18. 18.

*{in the earth}* See on chap. 15. 19.

**V. 13. ***{He causeth it to come whether for correction, or for his land, or for mercy}* Here he sets out the end of God’s various disposing of the clouds, and producing so many several meteors out of them, to wit, either to correct men for their sins by floods or lightnings, or to make the earth fruitful by sending plenty of rain in due season, or to cool men by gentle showers in extremity of heat.

*{he causeth it to come}* Heb. *He causeth it to finde*. He makes it to find a way to send out showers or other meteors, for the ends appointed by God.

*{whether for correction}* To chastise men for their sins. Heb. *for a rod*, chap. 9. 34. For parents use to correct their children with rods; so does God the world with floods and lightnings, chap. 36. 31. Exod. 9. 18, 23. 1 Sam. 12. 18, 19. Ezr. 10. 9. Others read it, *for a tribe*. For some particular family. As parents used a rod for correction, so the chief of every family carried a rod or scepter in his hand, as a sign of authority. From thence the name came to be put for the whole tribe. Num. 17. And so God causes it to rain on one place and not on another, Am. 4. 7.

*{or for his land}* To make his land fruitful. That is, all parts of the world, where men dwell; for all is God’s, Psal. 24. 1. Others restrain it to the land where God’s people dwell. Hos. 9. 3. Others take it for the wilderness; which God only looks after, and men do not husband. God makes the rain to fall there, that the wild beasts may have food. So the sea is [said to be] God’s, Psal. 95. 5. chap. 38. 26, 27. Psal. 65. 12. & 104. 16. For land, see on chap. 15. 19. on the word, *Earth*.

*{or for mercy}* Or for other good uses besides fructifying the earth, as to purge the air with lightning, to cool the earth with showers in summer, &c. Others understand the former clause of watering the earth in an ordinary way, to produce an ordinary crop, and this of an extraordinary fruitfulness. Others understand it of that mercy spoken of before, verse, 7. That men may by the sight of these extraordinary works of God be brought to repentance, and escape destruction. The word sometimes signifies reproach, but most commonly mercy, and that signification best fits here.

**V. 14. ***{Hearken unto this O Job; stand still, and consider the wondrous works of God}* Either Elihu conceives Job attended not, or that he was ready to interrupt him before he had done; and therefore he calls upon him by name for attention, that laying aside all disquieting thoughts, he would hearken to him, discoursing of the mighty works of God, that so he might not contest with God any longer, because of his misery, but might humble himself before the mighty God, as he ought.

*{Hearken}* See on chap. 33. 1.

*{to this}* To this discourse of God’s various proceedings without blaming him. To that which I shall say further of it, if you have been negligent before.

*{O Job}* See on chap. 1. 1.

*{stand still}* Hear quietly, and do not reply till I have done. So this word is used, chap. 32. 16.

*{and consider the wondrous works of God}* Lay aside your passion occasioned by the greatness of your pains, and weigh well the strangeness of God’s actions, which I have spoken of, and shall speak of further, that you may stoop unto him for the good of your soul. For wondrous works see on chap. 5. 9. on the word, *Marvelous*. For God, see on chap. 8. 3, 5.

**V. 15. ***{Dost thou know when God disposed them, and caused the light of his cloud to shine?}* Can you tell, when God is working in the clouds, or how he prepares them within to send forth lightning and thunder? See on chap. 36. 29.

*{Dost thou know}* God may be preparing for fair weather or foul weather in the clouds, and you be quite ignorant of it.

*{when God}* See on chap. 5. 17. & 11. 6.

*{disposeth them}* When he secretly orders the clouds for the ends above said, v. 11, 12. and that unseen of men. Or *his wonderful works*, v. 14, 16.

*{and caused the light of his cloud to shine}* Or, *That he may cause, &c*. That he may bring lightning and thunder, verse 3. 11. chap. 36. 30. Others understand it of the rainbow, a sign of fair weather after rain. Others of the sunshine scattering the dark clouds. These things neither Job nor any creature can foretell, much less God’s strange proceedings with the sons of men, and therefore Job must not censure God, though he be in misery. For light, see on chap. 17. 12.

**V. 16. ***{Dost thou know the balancings of the clouds, the wondrous works of him who is perfect in knowledge?}* When God lifts up the vapors to the clouds, or keeps them there in an equal distance from the earth, as if he weighed them, or means to send them down again in rain, does he make you acquainted with his purpose? Some understand it of God’s equal balancing the clouds at first, so that they continue still in their place, and fall not down to drown the earth. But this agrees not well with the rest about the daily producing of new meteors.

*{Dost thou know}* See on v. 15.

*{the balancings of the clouds}* How God makes them bigger or lesser, higher or lower, according as he means to use them for the good or hurt of the creatures, and that so exactly, as if he weighed them with a balance in every change.

*{the wondrous works}* See on chap. 5. 9. on the word, *marvelous*.

*{of him which is perfect in knowledge}* Of God, v. 14. See also on chap. 36. 4. 1 Sam. 2. 3.

**V. 17. ***{How thy garments are warm, when he quieteth the earth by the South wind}* Now he brings Job nearer home, to convince him the more of ignorance in the reasons of God’s actions. Can you tell beforehand when, or the reason why after storms brought in by the south wind verse 9. the sun should grow so hot that you can scarce able to endure your clothes upon your back? For in those hot countries clothes though light, were very burdensome.

*{How}* Do you know thoroughly how it comes to pass, or when this change of weather will be? If not, how can you tell greater things?

*{thy garments}* See on chap. 6. 15. on the word, *deceitfully*.

*{are warm}* Or, *are hot*. For so the word originally signifies.

*{when he quieteth the earth}* Then God takes away the storm from it, and sends heat. Or, impersonally, *when the earth is quieted*. For earth, see on chap. 15. 19.

*{by the South wind}* Which brings heat, Luke 12. 55. As the north does cold, verse 9. Or, *from the South*. That is, from the sun shining in the south, for there is no mention of wind in the Original, and that the sun is hottest when it comes into the south, and that suddenly after a great storm, which made men’s garments wet and cold before, is the more strange. The word has its Original from an high habitation, for the sun is highest when it is in the south.

**V. 18. ***{Hast thou with him spread out the sky, which is strong, and as a molten looking-glass}* When the air is darkened with clouds, and rent to pieces as it were with thunder, chap. 26. 9, 11. Can you do as God often does, make it strong again, and clear like a glass that has passed through the fire? The sum of Elihu’s argument in this matter of the meteors is this. If God call not man in other things, which he daily does, as a counselor or judge of his actions, nor cannot justly be required by any man to do so, nor may any man presume against his ordering the meteors and seasons, which yet concern the great good or hurt of many thousands, it is a very equal thing, that a particular man should not expect of God a reason of his particular dealing with him, but patiently submit unto him, not questioning his justice at all though he never so grievously afflict him.

*{Hast thou with him}* Or, *Dost thou with him*. For he speaks of a thing often done, after every storm, not of spreading the sky at first. See the reason on verse 16. You cannot do that which God often does, nor are not helpful to him in that work, and therefore know not the reason of that settlement.

*{spread out the sky}* Make it all clear and fair after a storm, as if it were covered over with a clean cloth. For sky, see on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. on the word, *Heaven*.

*{which is strong}* Though it be but a slight substance, yet none can alter the face of it but God.

*{and as a molten looking-glass}* That is, very clear: For he speaks of a fair sky after a storm, as nor is not in the Original, and therefore it may be read, *which is strong as a molten looking-glass*. If those were made of metals melted, as some conceive, then no doubt they were very strong.

**V. 19. ***{Teach us what we shall say unto him, for we cannot order our speech by reason of darkness}* If you would have us to plead your cause, bring out some of those many arguments you did brag of (Chap. 23. 4.) For we are so ignorant, that we can find nothing for you to say against God. A sharp irony.

*{Teach us}* Heb. *Make us to know*.

*{what we shall say}* In your defense, or if we were in the like case; and had such a desire to plead with God, as you have expressed, chap. 13. 3. & 23. 3. & 31. 35. For the word, see on chap. 16. 1.

*{unto him}* Unto God mentioned, verse 18.

*{for we cannot}* So the future is translated, chap. 11. 7.

*{order our speech}* Our speech, is added by the translators to make up the sense. See it fully expressed, chap. 32. 14. For order, see on chap. 13. 18.

*{by reason of darkness}* Because of the darkness of our understandings, which make us unable to dive into the reasons of God’s actions. חשך Darkness is sometimes put for trouble, as chap. 15. 22. Sometimes for death, as chap. 15. 23. Sometimes for ignorance, as Isa. 9. 2. So here he glances at Job’s presumption, that would control God’s works.

**V. 20. ***{Shall it be told him that I speak? if a man speak, surely he shall be swallowed up}* It was the custom of those times, very likely and is so still, that suits were entered in a book, and then days appointed for the hearing of them, as it is now in matters of law. The words may better be rendered thus; Shall it be recorded unto him that I will speake? would any man speake when he shall be swallowed up? As if he had said: Think you that I will enter into the lists with God to maintain your cause against him? Sure no man but a mad man, would speak in a cause wherein he knows beforehand he must needs be undone.

*{Shall it be told him}* Shall God be made acquainted with my purpose, or bad speeches of his proceedings like yours, or shall it be registered for God to take notice of it. Such books of accusation are mentioned, chap. 19. 23. & 31. 35.

*{that I speak}* That I will take your cause in hand, and plead it against him. Others, that I will speak so rashly of his dealings as you have done.

*{if a man speak}* Or, *would any man speake*. To wit, in your behalf, or as you have done, so אם is used by way of question, chap. 17. 2. For man, see on chap. 1. 1. For speak, see on chap. 16. 1. on the word, *said*.

*{surely he shall be swallowed up}* Or, *when he shall be swallowed up*. When he knows beforehand that God would overthrow him by his power and majesty. For אם surely, see on chap. 34. 35. For כי when, on chap. 7. 13. For swallowing, see on chap. 20. 15.

**V. 21. ***{And now men see not the bright light which is in the clouds, but the wind passeth, and cleanseth them}* Some understand the words thus. You see O Job the sun shining brightly, so that you are not able to look upon it now the wind has cleansed the clouds; how think you then that you can behold God’s majesty? But it rather seems to be a preparation for the whirlwind coming, in which God intended to speak, chap. 38. 2. God had made way by lightning and thunder before, verse 2. Now he darkens the air again, so that they could not see the sun. An argument of God’s coming to speak in anger. And the words may be read thus at the end of the verse. *But when the wind passeth, and cleanseth them, then faire weather will come,* &c. verse 22.

*{And now}* At this instant see a sudden change of the face of the sky.

*{men see not the bright light which is in the clouds}* The clouds have shut in the light of the sun, so that men cannot behold it. For *seeing,* see on chap. 19. 27. on the word, *behold*. For *light,* see on chap. 31. 26. For *clouds,* see on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8. on the word, *heaven*.

*{but}* Or, *but when.* It is translated, *and when,* 1 Sam. 13. 1. For *but,* see on chap. 30. 1.

*{the wind}* See on chap. 15. 2. upon *vaine knowledge.*

*{passeth}* See on chap. 15. 19.

*{and cleanseth them}* When the wind has scattered the dark clouds, then it will be fair again.

**V. 22. ***{Fair weather cometh out of the North: with God is terrible majesty}* When God pleases to darken the air, and after that to make it clear again by sending northern winds to purge it, then he shows that he is both terrible and glorious, worthy both to be feared, and to be praised.

*{Faire weather.}* Heb. *Gold.* Shining and bright things in Scripture are compared to gold. As oil, Zech. 4. 12. So here the bright beams of the sun.

*{cometh}* Or, *will come.* For it may be then it was cloudy. See on verse 21.

*{out of the North}* By means of the northern winds clearing the air, verse 9. Prov. 25. 23. It has his name from hiding, because in the remote parts of the north the sun is not seen some part of the year.

*{with God}* Or, *in God.* So it is translated, Psal. 42. 5, 6. Exod. 29. 3. For *God,* see on chap. 5. 17. & 11. 5.

*{is terrible majesty}* He is to be feared and honored. The word majesty may come from ידה to confess. It argues some special greatness, that makes men acknowledge and confess it. Others read it in way of a doxology, *unto God be reverend Majesty.* Let his glory be acknowledged in these meteors, and strange changes in the air.

**V. 23. ***{Touching the Almighty we cannot find him out; he is excellent in power, and in judgment, and in plenty of justice: he will not afflict}* This verse and the last contain the conclusion of Elihu's speech. The doctrinal part concerning God's incomprehensibleness, greatness, and justice, is in this verse. The practical part of reverence due to him therefore is in the next verse. The sum of this verse is, that though God be above man's reach in his essence, and infinite in power, yet he never abuses his power to the wronging of any man. A fit conclusion for so serious a discourse.

*{touching the Almighty}* See on chap. 8. 3, 5.

*{we cannot find him out}* See on chap. 11. 7. We cannot comprehend the infiniteness of his essence, nor approach to the brightness of his glory, nor pierce into the secrets of his providence.

*{he is excellent}* Very great, chap. 36. 26. It comes from a word that signifies to increase. Men that increase in wealth grow very rich. God is greater than they in power, though he grow not, for he always was so.

*{in power}* In strength and ability to do what he please, chap. 9. 4. & 12. 13, 16. & 36. 5.

*{and in judgment}* See on chap. 22. 4.

*{and in plenty}* See on chap. 22. 5. on the word, *Great*.

*{of justice}* Of upright dealing toward all.

*{he will not afflict}* Not willingly, but when he is as it were forced by men's ill carriage, Lam. 3. 33. or without just cause. Others read the end of the verse thus, *He will not afflict him in judgement and plenty of justice.* That is, he will not use such extremity, as he might without violation of justice in afflicting men. Others thus, *He will not oppresse judgement and plenty of justice.* That is, he will not pervert it, for that is a kind of oppression of it. Others thus, *He will not (or, need not) answer.* He being so just, that he will by no means abuse his power, there is no reason why any should call him to account for his actions, neither will he give any answer, if they do. For the word, see on chap. 16. 1. on the word, *Answered*.

**V. 24. ***{Men do therefore fear him: he respecteth not any that are wise of heart}* In regard of God's great power, and justice, it is fit, that men should have a reverent account of his actions, and the rather because he regards not the censure of those that think themselves wise enough to control him in his actions.

*{men}* See on chap. 5. 17. & 9. 2.

*{do}* Or, *should.* So the future is translated, chap. 34. 6. Wise men do fear God: All men should do it.

*{therefore}* Because of his infinite power and justice.

*{fear him}* Reverence God, and have an high account of all his actions, and not rashly dispute against them, though they know not the causes of them. See on chap. 1. 1. & 15. 4.

*{he respecteth not}* He regards not. See on chap. 19. 27. on the word, *Behold*.

*{any that are wise of heart}* That think themselves able to convince him of unwise and unjust dealing. Others read it, *But he feareth not all wise in heart.* Men should be so wise, as not to dispute against any of his proceedings. But he knows all are not so. A secret nip for Job, who had desired often to reason with God about the cause of his afflictions. Others read it thus, *No men, though never so wise in heart can see him.* He is invisible, and incomprehensible. For the phrase, see on chap. 9. 4. For *Heart,* on chap. 15. 12.

## CHAPTER 38

**Verse 1. ***{Then the LORD answered Job out of the whirl-wind, and said}* That though Job had rightly maintained, that he was not punished as an hypocrite, nor was worse than his accusers, yet some impatient speeches escaped from him, which needed correction. As Elihu had in part showed before, so God does it now more fully; and that in two orations, to each of which is adjoined Job's submission. The first from hence to verse 6. of the 40. chap. The second, to verse 7. of the 42. chap. In the first note, 1. God's long oration, chap. 38. & 39. 2. His call for an answer, chap. 40. 1, 2. 3. Job's short and submissive answer, chap. 40. 3, 4, 5. In God's long speech observe, First, the preface, in this chap. verse 1, 2, 3. 2. The speech itself to the end of chap. 39. In the preface note, first a transition of the penman, verse 1. 2. God's objurgation of Job for his boldness, verse 2. 3. His permission of him nevertheless to speak for himself, verse 3. Job had often desired God's judgment in the cause. So had Job's friends, chap. 11. 5. They were all weary with disputing, and God now comes beyond all their expectation, and determines the business. He had now long enough suffered his servant Job to be hurried by Satan and his instruments. He now raises a storm, and out of it speaks to Job as follows, verse 2. &c. It is worth observing, how much God's speech goes beyond Elihu's for matter and manner.

*{then}* When the rest had done disputing. So this particle is translated, chap. 20. 1.

*{the LORD} Jehovah* in Heb. *He that is, was, and is to come*; for it has a touch of all three tenses in it. And it comes from being; for God has his being in himself, and gives being to all creatures.

*{answered}* To Job's former complaints, and stopped his mouth, which his three friends could not do. For the word, see on chap. 16. 1.

*{Job}* Not his friends, nor Elihu; for Job was the person tried, and chiefly concerned in the business. For *Job,* see on chap. 1. 1.

*{out of the whirlwind}* Which was raised before, together with lightning and thunder, chap. 37. 2, 21. To make way for God's speaking in state, yet hiding his glory, and to make Job and his friends the more attentive. Thus God did use in former times to speak to his servants, Deut. 4. 12. 1 King. 19. 11. &c. Ezek. 1. 4. &c. Nah. 1. 3. Heb. 12. 18.

*{and said}* See on chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{Who is this, that darkeneth counsel by words without knowledge}* Here is a check to Job, and likewise an implication of what God intended to prove against him, to wit, that he had spoken ignorantly concerning God's afflicting of him, and by his complaints of injustice had obscured or cast a cloud upon God's righteous dealing, to make the world believe that God had done him wrong.

*{who is this}* Which of you is it? As if God had expected that Job being guilty should take it to himself. Or, as if God had pointed to Job. Or, what manner of man is this, that dares thus to obscure God's counsels? chap. 42. 3.

*{that darkeneth counsel}* That casts a black color on the sovereign brightness of the justice, and wisdom of my providence, accusing my judgments of folly and injustice. Who by searching out the counsel of his own corrupt reason makes it the more obscure, and declares his own folly, Rom. 1. 22.

*{by words without knowledge}* See on chap. 35. 16.

**V. 3. ***{Gird up now thy loins like a man, for I will demand of thee, and answer thou me}* The same challenge is made to Job, chap. 40. 7. Job had put God to his choice, chap. 13. 22. And now God chooses the replying part, and puts Job to answer for himself. As if he had said, if you will needs dispute with me, prepare yourself like a man to battle. As he girds his loins, so do you bring forth your best reasons for yourself. See what you can answer to what I shall demand, if you cannot, see your own folly in accusing, and challenging me to this weighty dispute.

*{Gird up now thy loins}* Their garments being loose in those hot countries, as appears by the phrase of covering the feet, Judg. 3. 24. They did use to gird their clothes to them, that they might not be troublesome, when they did eat, Exod. 12. 11. or went to labor or travel, 1 King. 18. 46. or to fight, 1 King. 20. 11. So here by way of metaphor, prepare yourself for this tongue combat, so well as you can. See the like phrase also, Prov. 31. 17. Jer. 1. 17.

*{like a man}* Like a valiant man that means to grapple with his adversary. You have bragged much, now see what you can do. For *a man* see on chap. 14. 10. & 22. 2.

*{for}* I mean to set to the work: you have a strong adversary therefore, bestir yourself. For *I* see on chap. 17. 10.

*{I will demand of thee}* You think yourself wise enough to censure my proceedings. I will convince you of ignorance by certain questions. Answer them if you can.

*{and answer thou me}* Heb. *And make thou me to know.* You think yourself a very understanding man, see if you can either tell me, or teach me the right in what I shall ask.

**V. 4. ***{Where wast thou, when I laid the foundations of the earth? declare, if thou hast understanding}* To convince Job of ignorance he begins, with the creation of the world, interlining many things which cannot well admit of a division, but must be handled in order, as they stand. He loads Job with many deep questions, the least whereof he could not answer. The sum of this verse is, You are but of yesterday: I am from eternity. You were taken out of the earth, and therefore could have no being when I made the earth. Much less could you be my counselor or companion in the work. And shall I need your counsel in governing of the world, that needed it not in making it? See your error and repent.

*{Where wast thou}* You that take so much upon you now, had no being, when I did that great work, chap. 15. 7.

*{when I laid the foundations of the earth}* A metaphor from builders, who when they go about some great fabric first lay a deep, and strong foundation that the building may continue. So God though he founded the earth on the center, a thing of nothing, yet laid it so fast, it cannot be moved; or by the foundations of the earth, may be meant the body of the earth, which lies lowest, and like a strong foundation upholds as it were the other elements, and the heavens. For the phrase see Psal. 102. 25. & 104. 5. Prov. 8. 29. For *the earth,* see on chap. 15. 19.

*{declare}* See on chap. 15. 13. on the word, *Told*.

*{if thou hast understanding}* Heb. *If thou knowest understanding.* Dan. 2. 21. 1 Chron. 12. 32. & 2 Chron. 2. 12. Isa. 29. 24.

**V. 5. ***{Who hath laid the measures thereof, if thou knowest? or who hath stretched the line upon it}* Having spoken of the creation of the earth in general in the former verse: He now goes on in the manner of building, to set out the greatness of this work, too great for Job or for any creature's brain or hands. First the builder chooses the ground to build on fit for what he intends to do. Then he measures it by a line, that the building may be proportionable. After that the foundation is laid, and then the walls are raised, and the building is firm. So did God firmly make and settle the earth, verse 5, 6.

*{who hath laid}* Wisely disposed, as a builder does a plot of ground, in which he means to build. Who chose a fit place for the earth, and left room for the other elements fit for each of them? None but I did it: none but I could do it.

*{the measures thereof}* Who has proportioned the length, breadth, height, depth, thickness of it?

*{if thou knowest}* Tell if you can, who did it. For כי *if,* see chap. 21. 15. Others read it, (*For thou knowest.*) You take upon you to censure my actions, as if you knew all things better than I. A tart irony, as verse 21. So כי is translated *For.* Psal. 135. 14.

*{or who hath stretched the line upon it}* Who proportioned it into levels, hills, dales, as by a line? Zech. 1. 16. & 2. 1. For *stretched,* see on chap. 26. 7. There is mentioned in the prophets a line for building, as before: and a line for destroying, as Isa. 34. 11. The first is here meant.

**V. 6. ***{Whereupon are the foundations thereof fastened? or who laid the corner stone thereof}* Upon what props did I set such an infinite weight, and by whose aid did I as it were lay an high cornerstone to uphold, and keep together so great a building.

*{whereupon are the foundations thereof fastened}* Heb. *The sockets thereof made to sinke.* For foundations are laid in the ground, and the beams on them joined with sockets. All this is to show the firmness and unmovableness of the work. See Psal. 104. 5.

*{or who laid the corner stone thereof}* Who made the parts to keep so close together, and to keep up one another? It is in vain to enquire here, what is the foundation of the earth and the cornerstone; for all is metaphorical to show the firmness of the work, as buildings last, that have good foundations, and cornerstones. For *laid,* see on chap. 6. 24. & 8. 10. For *stone,* see on chap. 14. 19.

**V. 7. ***{When the morning stars sang together, and all the sons of God shouted for joy}* Some join these words immediately to the former, and understand it all of the angels, whom they conceive to be made the first day, and being as it were the stars of heaven for brightness, to praise God at the laying of the foundation of the earth that day, like the shoutings at laying the headstone of the temple, Zech. 4. 7. Ezr. 3. 10, 11. Others join it to verse 4. *where wast thou when the morning stars,* &c. And these suppose the stars and angels both made the fourth day, and the stars to praise God materially, as the heavens do, Psal. 19. 1. And the angels vocally, as musicians sing or play upon instruments. And that at the first upon that strange sight of the creation of the stars, and not of themselves.

*{when the morning stars}* The stars new created, Psal. 148. 3. or rather the angels created in the first morning of the world, that they might behold the creation of all other creatures, though they had no hand in it, and first praise God for it, before man was made. Thus it must be understood, for else stars and angels praise God still. So angels are compared to stars for brightness, Rev. 1. 20. And are called angels of light, 2 Cor. 11. 14. And they are called morning stars because they were the first living creatures made in the morning of the world. Christ is also called the morning star, Rev. 22. 16. For *Morning,* see on chap. 7. 18.

*{sang together}* At the same time in a harmony. Or, together with the laying the foundation of the earth, or with the creation of the stars or angels, or *Sang alone.* When there were yet no other creatures to praise God, man being not yet made. So this word is translated *only;* chap. 34. 29. And should be, *alone not together;* as it is Ezr. 4. 3.

*{and all the sons of God}* All the angels, for there were no other sons of God then, See on chap. 1. 6.

*{shouted for joy}* See chap. 33. 26. Ezr. 3. 11.

**V. 8. ***{Or who shut up the Sea with doors, when it brake forth, as if it had issued out of the womb}* God goes on to set out his mighty power in bounding the sea. It did at first overflow the earth being the higher element, till God was pleased to prepare as it were a great house to enclose it, and doors to shut it in, that there might be a dry land for men and beasts to inhabit. This none could do but God.

*{Or}* So it is translated, chap. 15. 7.

*{who}* This is added out of verse 5. to make up the sense.

*{shut up}* Kept the sea within certain bounds, that it might not drown men and beasts, chap. 7. 12. & 26. 10. Psal. 104. 9. & 33. 7. Gen. 1. 9. Prov. 8. 29.

*{the Sea}* That huge heap of waters, into which all the rivers run. See on chap. 14. 11. Eccl. 1. 7.

*{with doors}* With banks and downs, and sometime with the weak sands. The hollow places of the earth, wherein it is lodged, are as it were the house of the sea. The borders are like doors.

*{when it brake forth}* Out of the earth at first, and then overran it all. Moses makes no mention of the creation of the sea, yet it seems it was brought forth out of the earth, because the fountains of it are mentioned, verse 16. Gen. 7. 11. Prov. 3. 20. The word signifies a breaking forth with a kind of force or violence.

*{as if it had issued out of the womb}* Some understand it of coming out of the womb of God's providence; but it is most likely it came out of the womb of the earth, as before a metaphor taken from childbearing. And after that God shut it within certain bounds, as verse 10, 11. For *Issuing out,* see on chap. 20. 25. on the word, *Comes out*. For *womb,* see on chap. 24. 20.

**V. 9. ***{When I made the cloud the garment thereof, and thick darkness a swaddling-band for it}* The Lord goes on in the metaphor. When the child is born, garments and swaddling bands are provided for it to compass it about and cover it; so has God enclosed and covered the sea with clouds and darkness. As though the sea were but a little babe in the hands of God to order it, as he lists, which yet the greatest potentates cannot curb.

*{when I made the clouds the garments thereof}* Understand, where were you then? out of verse 4. To wit, when I having separated the sea from the earth did first make the clouds to cover it as garments do the child, that comes naked into the world. See for *Garment,* on chap. 24. 7, 10. on clothing, and covering.

*{and thick darkness a swaddling-band for it}* Some understand it of the clouds, as before, but it may well be understood of fogs and mists, which arise often upon the sea, and are between the clouds and the sea, as the swaddling bands between the upper garment and the child.

**V. 10. ***{And brake up for it my decreed place, and set bars and doors}* Because the clouds and fogs could not keep the sea from running over the earth, God has therefore imprisoned it in those hollow places, which he determined to hold the waters of the sea, and has shut it in there, as it were with strong doors, and added bars to them to make them sure. See on verse 8.

*{And brake up}* Where were you when I broke it up? The word שבר signifies. 1. To cleave or break any bodily substance, Jer. 19. 10. 2. To break the heart with grief making it as unprofitable, as a broken stick, Psal. 69. 20. 3. To pull down the pride or violence of wicked men by taking away the upholders or instruments of it, Psal. 3. 7. 4. To satisfy hunger or thirst whereby the rage of them is broken. Psal. 104. 11. So we call it breaking our fast, when we first eat in a morning. 5. To buy corn, Gen. 41. 57. 6. To sell corn, Gen. 41. 56. 7. To make war to cease, Hos. 2. 18. Here it is taken in the first sense, for God's making hollow places in the earth to hold the waters of the sea.

*{for it}* For the sea.

*{my decreed place}* See on chap. 26. 10. on the word, *Bounds*. Others read it, *And established my decree upon it.* But שבר signifies breaking rather than establishing.

*{and set bars and doors}* Made strong banks about it, and other bounds to keep it within compass.

**V. 11. ***{And said, Hitherto shalt thou come, but no farther, and here shall thy proud waves be stayed}* When I had shut the sea within its bounds, then I laid a command on it to keep within those limits, and not to suffer the greatest waves to go beyond them.

*{And said}* And commanded. See on chap. 16. 1.

*{Hitherto shalt thou come}* You must come as far as the appointed shore. For *Come,* see on chap. 15. 21.

*{but}* So it is translated, chap. 30. 1.

*{no further}* Heb. *Thou shalt not adde.* To wit, to go beyond your bounds, or to cover the earth any more, Psal. 124. 9.

*{and here shall thy proud waves be stayed}* Heb. *and here it shall set it self against the pride of thy waves.* That is, the shore which I have set for a bound shall oppose and keep in your strongest wave, Psal. 89. 9. For *wave,* see on Chapter 15. 28. on the word, *heaps*.

**V. 12. ***{Hast thou commanded the morning since thy days? and caused the day-spring to know his place?}* God now sets out his own power, and Job's weakness, by the production of the morning light. Does the daylight obey you, and the sun arise at such times and places as you do appoint? Were not these things set in order long before you were born?

*{Hast thou commanded}* Have you so commanded, that it came at your command? As when God said, Let there be light there was light, Gen. 1. 3. So this word is used, Lev. 25. 21. Psal. 133. 3. & 68. 28. Isa. 45. 12. For what God commands must stand, Psal. 33. 9. & 148. 5.

*{the morning}* The morning light, which shows men what lay in darkness before. For the word, see on chap. 7. 18.

*{since thy days}* Since you were born. Were there not mornings before you were born? or did you ever once in all your days command the light to come out of darkness? For *days,* see on Chap. 14. 14.

*{and caused the day-spring to know his place?}* Made it to know when and where it should break forth. The word dayspring comes from blackness, for it is not clear light at first.

**V. 13. ***{That it might take hold of the ends of the Earth, that the wicked might be shaken out of it}* He sets forth here a double benefit of the morning light. First that it suddenly spreads itself over the earth, and turns darkness into light. Secondly, that it puts wicked men to flight, as thieves, adulterers, murderers, who sin in the dark, and fly as soon as morning light appears.

*{That it might take hold of the ends of the earth}* Not only give light in some places, but everywhere, taking possession as it were of the whole world, wheresoever the light of the sun comes. *Ends.* Heb. *wings,* Chap. 37. 3. For *Earth* see on Chap. 15. 19.

*{that the wicked might be shaken out of it}* Some understand it of their hiding themselves so close in the daylight for fear of being discovered, that none of them dare act those wickednesses in the light, which they do boldly in the dark. Herein they are like wild beasts, ravening in the night, and lurking in the day, Psal. 104. 20, 22. See Chap. 24. 13, 17. John 3. 20. Others understand it of apprehending malefactors in the morning for sins done in the night, and bringing them suddenly to judgment, by sentence whereof they are either driven out of the land by banishment, or out of the earth by death. For judgment in the morning, see Ruth 3. 14. with 4. 1. Psal. 101. 8. Jer. 21. 12. Exod. 18. 13.

**V. 14. ***{It is turned as clay to the seal, and they stand as a garment}* In this verse is an exposition of the former part of the former verse. In the next verse, of the latter. The light suddenly running over the world, sets a new stamp upon the earth, the sunbeams shining upon it as lace upon a garment, and the flowers and grass that cover the earth, and were not covered themselves in the night, now in the morning discovering themselves, as pictures do on the clay when the stamp of the seal is set upon it. So there is a double metaphor to set out the renewed face of the earth every morning. The one from the clay altered by the seal: The other from men putting on new attire in the morning, and appearing glorious, that lay obscure in the night.

*{It}* The earth appears beautiful, that was hidden in the dark before.

*{is turned}* It has as it were got a new form. Or, *is renewed,* to the same shape it had the day before.

*{as the clay to the seal}* As if it had a new stamp put upon it by the sunbeams, as the clay has by the impression of the seal. For *clay,* see on Chap. 13. 12.

*{they stand as a garment}* The beautiful things that grow on the earth, are like a stately garment, or ornament on a man, Matth. 9. 28, 29.
