**V. 15. ***{And from the wicked their light is withholden, and the high arm shall be broken}* Here is an exposition of the second part of verse 13. A second effect of light is, that wicked men are judged, condemned, and executed; and so their pride, oppression, and other deeds of darkness cease.

*{And from the wicked their light is withholden}* The coming of the daylight, as it brings malefactors to judgment, so it deprives them of the light of worldly happiness, as honors, riches, pleasures, yea and life itself, which are in Scripture phrase set out by the name of light, Chap. 18. 5, 6. For *wicked,* see on Chap. 3. 17.

*{and the high arm shall be broken}* As they shall lose all their comfort, so also shall they be deprived of all their strength, wherein they prided themselves, and wherewith they oppressed others. It is called an high arm, because the arm uses to be lifted up to strike, Psal. 10. 15. & 37. 17. Ezek. 30. 22.

For *arm,* see on chap. 22. 8. on the word, *mighty*.

**V. 16. ***{Hast thou entered into the springs of the Sea? or hast thou walked in the search of the depth}* He shows that it is not possible for Job to attain to the depth of God's counsels, that cannot comprehend the depth of the sea, verse 16, 17. nor the breadth of the earth, verse 18. nor the height of heaven, verse 19, 20, 21. God had spoken of the creation of the sea before, verse 8. Now he speaks of the depth of it, which was far above Job's reach, as the making of it at first.

*{Hast thou entered into the springs of the Sea?}* Have you ever been at the bottom of the sea to see how the waters there abundantly come out of the earth? see on verse 8. *Entred,* Heb. *came.* For *spring,* see on chap. 28. 11. on the word, *overflowing*. It signifies weeping. Springs pour out water, as eyes do tears. One word in the original signifies an eye, and a spring. For *sea* see on chapter 14. 11.

*{or hast thou walked in the search of the depth?}* Have you gone up and down in the bottom of the sea, and observed those secret parts there, which no man can by any search find out. Or, *or hast thou walked in the depth by search?* Have you found any unknown way to the bottom of the sea by curious enquiry? The depth is expounded by the sea, chap. 26. 14.

**V. 17. ***{Have the gates of death been opened unto thee, or hast thou seen the doors of the shadow of death?}* Are those deadly and dark places in the bottom of the sea, or in the bowels of the earth, made known to you, where dead men lie rotting.

*{Have the gates of death been opened unto thee}* Do you see how dead men descend to their resting place, or what they endure there? For *gates of death,* see Psal. 9. 13. & 107. 18.

*{been opened unto thee}* Heb. *revealed.* No man living can see those secrets.

*{or hast thou seen the doors of the shadow of death}* The same thing in other words. Have you seen those dark and dismal places where dead men lie, which were enough to strike the dead also with terror and fear. For *seen,* look on Chap. 19. 27. on the word, *behold*. For *shadow of death,* see on Chap. 3. 5. & 10. 21.

**V. 18. ***{Hast thou perceived the breadth of the earth? declare, if thou knowest it all}* Though the earth be better known to man than the depth of the sea, yet no man ever saw it all. There are so many wildernesses and bogs in it, that as no man can pass through them all, so Methuselah's life would not serve to do it. In vain therefore should Job think to apprehend the invisible reasons of God's proceedings, that could not give an account of all visible things in the world.

*{Hast thou perceived the breadth of the earth?}* Have you traveled all the world over, and seen what is in every corner of the earth? The word breadth contains also the length, and circumference of the earth, see on Chap. 36. 16. The breadth of a thing is the largeness of it in Scripture language opposed to straitness and narrowness, as Deut. 19. 8. Ezek. 41. 7. And therefore it is the breadths in Heb. in the plural number including the length and circumference. For *Earth,* see on chap. 15. 19.

*{declare, if thou knowest it all}* If you can tell what is in all parts of the world: Or as others, if you can resolve all that I have asked you, speak out, that it may appear you can do it. For *declare,* see on Chap. 15. 18. on the word, *told*.

**V. 19. ***{Where is the way where light dwelleth, and as for darkness where is the place thereof}* Tell if you can where the sun lodges, and the light of the day when the sun goes down, or what becomes of the darkness when the sun arises.

*{Where is the way}* What way leads to the lodging of the light? A poetical expression, as if the sun setting retired himself to his chamber, as we do at night. Psal. 19. 5.

*{where light dwelleth}* Where it abides when it is not seen abroad. For men are supposed to be at home, when they are not employed without doors. For *light,* see on Chapter 17. 12. & 25. 3.

*{and as for darkness}* Which comes by the absence of the sun.

*{where is the place thereof}* Whither goes it when it goes from home? A metaphor from men, who when they go from one place, go to another. Not that darkness does so remove. But the plain meaning of it is, You cannot tell what is become of it.

**V. 20. ***{That thou shouldest take it to the bound thereof, and that thou shouldest know the paths to the house thereof}* Are you able to direct the light or darkness to the place where it should abide, till such time as it is to come forth again.

*{That thou shouldest take it to the bound thereof}* Or, *at the bound thereof.* That you like a guide should take it by the hand as it were, and lead it to the place of abode. One verb included in another. See on chap. 15. 8. & 16. 11. & 18. 8. Others understand it of bringing it to the longest day of the year, and then to the shortest, which are as bounds of light and darkness. For *take,* see on Chap. 15. 12. on the word, *carry away*.

*{and that thou shouldest know the paths to the house thereof}* That you should so perfectly understand all the paths thereof, that you can guide it to its resting place. By house here, is meant place, as verse 19. For the word, see on chap. 15. 28.

**V. 21. ***{Knowest thou it because thou wast then born, or because the number of thy days is great}* You were not so much as born when I set a distinction between day and night, between light and darkness. Your days are not of so long continuance. How then can you know certainly what was done before you were born.

*{Knowest thou it}* Do you perfectly understand what I have demanded of you, so that you can give me a full answer.

*{because thou wast then born}* Had you any being then, if not, how could you know it.

*{or because the number of thy days is great}* You must be a very old man if you have lived ever since the beginning of the world. For day, see on chap. 14.1. For great, see on chap. 22.5. Others read the whole verse ironically thus: *Thou knowest it, for thou wast then born, and for that the number of thy years is great.* Others thus; *Didst thou know that thou shouldst be born at such a time, and that the number of thy years should be great.* But the first reading is best, and most agreeable to the coherence.

**V. 22. ***{Hast thou entered into the treasures of the snow, or hast thou seen the treasures of the haile?}* God now proceeds to the meteors, and begins with snow and hail. As if he had said, you O Job have often seen it snow and hail, and it may be have wondered at the store of it, and whence it should come; but you were never above in the clouds, where I lay it up, as men do treasures in great abundance to bring forth when they mean to use them.

*{Hast thou entered}* See on verse 16.

*{into the treasures}* Or, *into the store-houses.* For God uses snow and hail as weapons rather than treasure, verse 23. God has also treasuries for the winds, out of which he brings them when he pleases, Psal. 135.7.

*{of the snow}* See on Chap. 37.6.

*{or hast thou seen}* See on Chap. 19.27. on the word, *behold*.

*{the treasures}* See before in this verse.

*{of the haile}* Which also falls abundantly, as out of a treasury.

**V. 23. ***{Which I have reserved against the time of trouble, against the day of battle and war}* Here God sets out his armory to us, wherein are such weapons as no man can produce, to destroy his enemies, namely, snow and hail, as Exod. 9.24. Josh. 10.11. Isa. 30.30. With these and such like meteors God judges the people, Chap. 36.31. And seals up the hand of every man, Chapter 37.7.

*{Which}* Some restrain it to the hail, because no mention is made of hurt done by snow in Scripture. Yet experience shows that men and beasts have been buried in a great snow, and drowned in floods at the thaw.

*{I have reserved}* Laid up in the clouds my magazine and arsenal.

*{against the time of trouble}* Against the time when I intend to bring trouble on the sons of men for their sin. Or, *against the time of the enemy.* For the word signifies both, and battle and war follow in this verse.

*{against the day}* See on chap. 14.1.

*{of battle}* The word properly signifies a war near at hand, wherein the enemies approach one to another, but may be used for any war.

*{and war}* When God lays judgments on his enemies, he is said to have war with them. Exod. 17.16. Isa. 27.4.

**V. 24. ***{By what way is the light parted which scattereth the East-wind upon the earth?}* It may be understood either of parting the light from the clouds, in sending forth lightnings, which often come from the East, Matth. 24.27. and raise an East wind by troubling the air, as light is used, Chap. 36.30. & 37.3. and this agrees well with the next verse. Or, of parting the light from the darkness, when the Sun rising brings light, 2 Sam. 23.4. and it may be shines in one place, and not in another, and raises the East wind, which often follows the rising Sun. Job could not tell, how either of these wonderful works could be done.

*{By what way}* See on verse 19.

*{is the light}* See on Chap. 17.12. & 37.15.

*{parted}* From the clouds or darkness, as before.

*{which scattereth the East wind upon the earth}* As the thunder coming from the East may raise an East wind, so also the rising Sun. For the Sun is called *Ventorum pater*, the Father of the Winds, and the East wind is *Subsolanus*, as being under the command of the Sun. Others read it, *And which way the East wind scattereth it self upon the earth*. That is, which way it will be scattered, or in what country it will blow. Or, *scattereth the clouds which are over the earth.* As chap. 37.11. For earth see on chap. 15.19.

**V. 25. ***{Who hath divided a water course for the overflowing of waters? or a way for the lightning or thunder?}* Who sends the rain down upon the earth in abundance, and that in several places as he pleases himself, as if he had made leaden pipes in the clouds? Or who has rent the clouds that the lightning and thunder may come forth?

*{Who}* Not you surely, nor any else beside myself.

*{hath divided}* Or, *derived.* For the Hebrew word for a River comes from this root. And thence also comes Pelagus the sea, out of which the rivers are divided.

*{a water course}* A place to convey the rain from heaven to earth where God will. Chap. 28.26. So here verse 26, It comes from a word that signifies to ascend, or to be on high, for such places use to begin on high, and carry water to lower grounds. Such are the windows of heaven, Gen. 7.11.

*{for the overflowing of waters}* Of the waters above, that abundance of rain may fall. For he speaks not of floods on earth to destroy, but of plentiful and seasonable rain to nourish the fruits of the earth, as appears, verse 27.

*{or a way for the lightning of thunder}* See on Chap. 28,26. where all these words are found.

**V. 26. ***{To cause it to rain on the earth, where no man is; on the wilderness, wherein there is no man?}* God's providence extends itself to places not inhabited. He sends rain to such places where no men are to water the earth, or eat of the fruits of it, to show it to be his work alone, and that he feeds all living creatures, as verse 17. Psal. 104.27. & 145.15.

*{To cause it to rain on the earth}* To make way for showers to water the earth. For Earth, see on Chap. 15.19.

*{where no man is}* Where there are no towns, nor Cities of habitation. That is, on deserts, as follows. For Man, see on Chapter 1.1.

*{on the wilderness}* Psal. 107.35. Though he mean on all desert places in the world, yet God may allude to the wilderness of Arabia, not far from Job. See on Chap. 1.19.

*{wherein there is no man}* It is repeated again, to show the certainty of it, and that God gives rain even to the most desert places. For Man, see on Chap. 14.1.

**V. 27. ***{To satisfy the desolate and waste ground, and to cause the bud of the tender herb to spring forth}* That the store of rain falling upon the wilderness, which otherwise would be dried up, may make it bring forth herbs and plants to feed the wild beasts, for which no man takes care, to show God's abundant store of provision for all living creatures.

*{to satisfy}* To water it abundantly, so as if it had sense, it may say, it had enough, and may bring forth enough to satisfy the wild beasts there.

*{the desolate and waste ground}* Broughton elegantly, *The waste and vast ground.* For the words see on Chap. 30.3.

*{And to cause the bud of the tender herb to spring forth}* That is, to bring out of the earth green herbs for food of the beasts.

**V. 28. ***{Hath the rain a father, or who hath begotten the drops of the dew?}* The rain comes not into the world as children do, who are formed out of the substance of their parents, or as herbs and plants out of seeds, but is formed by me only, and at my pleasure. It falls when and where I will.

*{Hath the rain a father?}* Is there any in the world, that can bring forth rain when the earth needs it? Surely no. For Father, see on Chap. 15.10.

*{or who hath begotten the drops of the dew?}* It is not in the power of any creature to produce them. Some read it, *who hath conceived,* as a mother does children; but the word being masculine will not bear it. And it is used for begetting. 1 Chron. 4.2. The word drops has its name from roundness in the original. For dew see on Chap. 29.19.

**V. 29. ***{Out of whose womb came the Ice? and the hoary frost of heaven who hath gendered it?}* An elegant similitude or metaphor from those that beget others, or bear them in their bodies for a time. The Ice is not like an infant conceived in the womb, or begotten, but being congealed falls on the earth by God's only appointment.

*{Out of whose womb came the Ice}* Or, *out of whose belly.* It may be taken either from the Father or from the Mother. For though it be ordinarily spoken of the mother, yet it is spoken of David, Psal. 132.11. *Of the fruit of thy body.* Heb. *Of thy belly.* The meaning is, The ice, and also the frost, as follows, comes not by ordinary generation, but by God's great power working without the means of men. For Womb, see on Chap. 15.2. on the word *Belly*. For Came, see on Chap. 20.25. on the word, *Comes out*. For Frost, see on chap. 37.10.

*{and the hoary frost of heaven who hath gendered it?}* The hoary frost has its name from covering or daubing over, because it is a fast covering of the earth, Psal. 147.16. By Heaven is meant the air or clouds out of which comes the matter that causes the frost. For Heaven, see on chap. 9.8. & 11.8. The word Engendering may be Begetting, as Psal. 2.7. See on the former verse.

**V. 30. ***{The waters are hid as with a stone, and the face of the deep is frozen}* Such is the force of the frost, that it brings ice, which covers the rivers all over, yea and the deep sea in many parts, and that with a cover as hard as stone, and of a great thickness, so that laden carts may go on it, and men make tents on it, as if it were changed into firm land, nay into hard rocks. Strabo in his second book writes of the entrance of the Lake Meotis, that a Captain of Mithridates overcame the enemy in a pitched battle upon the ice in the Winter, and in a sea battle in the summer with ships.

*{The waters are hid}* They appear not, they look as if they had lost their nature. They hide themselves, as if they were ashamed to be seen. For water, see on chap. 14.9.

*{as with a stone}* Heb. *as a stone.* They are as hard as a stone, and as it were paved over with it. For the words, see on Chapter 14.19.

*{and the face}* The uppermost part of the water which is in sight, as the face of man is. For Face see on Chap. 14.20. on the word *Countenance*.

*{of the deep}* Of the Sea. See on verse 16. So in Greenland, and sometimes nearer home the Sea is frozen.

*{is frozen}* Heb. *Taken.* The water is as it were taken captive and put into a prison. See on chap. 36.8.

**V. 31. ***{Canst thou bind the sweet influences of the Pleiades, or loose the bands of Orion?}* He passes on from the Meteors to the stars. And sets out the four quarters of the year by four kind of Stars. The Summer and Autumn in the next. The sum of this verse is, are you able to keep off the pleasantness of the Spring, when the Pleiades rule, or the bitter storms of Winter, which Orion then brings forth. No question but the stars have a great force in the variety of weather and seasons. See for these Stars on chapter 9.9.

*{Canst thou bind}* Can you hinder them from their kindly operation in their season? A man bound cannot do that which he can being at liberty. The word קשר signifies, 1. to bind one thing to another, as Prov. 3.3. Jer. 51.63. 2. To bind men together in a league or conspiracy, as Gen. 44.30. 2 King. 21.23. Here it is taken in the first sense, for binding them so as they cannot stir.

*{the sweet influences}* Heb. *The delights.* That is, the green herbs, flowers and other delicacies that the Spring brings forth. From this root the garden of Eden had her name.

*{of the Pleiades}* See on chap. 9.9. Or, *the seven Starres.* Amos 5.8.

*{or loose}* Or set at liberty in winter those bitter storms, that Orion brings, whereby men's work is hindered, and the earth bound up with frost and ice, chap. 37.6,7. For the word see on chapter 12.18.

*{the bands}* It has its name from drawing. Can you untie those bands, wherewith Orion draws foul weather after him?

*{of Orion}* See on Chap. 9.9.

**V. 32. ***{Canst thou bring forth Mazzaroth in his season? or canst thou guide Arcturus with his sons?}* Or can you bring forth those stars that bring in the Summer, or lead those along that usher in the harvest?

*{Canst thou bring forth}* Cause them to appear, as men do that are brought out of their lodgings, or fruits out of the earth?

*{Mazzaroth}* Or, *the twelve signes.* So some take it. But others take it for the Southern Stars, which are called the chambers of the South. Chap. 9.9. Others for Northern stars, as the word in the Masculine gender is used, Chap. 37.9. But this must be left to Astronomers to determine.

*{or canst thou guide}* Heb. Guide them, direct them which way they should go.

*{Arcturus}* See on Chap. 9.9.

*{with his sons}* With the lesser stars of that constellation standing about him as sons at a father's table. Psal. 128.3. For Sons see on chap. 14.21.

**V. 33. ***{Knowest thou the ordinances of heaven? canst thou set the dominion thereof in the earth?}* God having before spoken of some particular stars, now comes to speak of the whole course of heaven, and the influence thereof in things here below. Are the heavenly bodies guided and their motion by your wisdom? or have you given them power to work upon inferior bodies? These things exceed your wit and strength.

*{Knowest thou the ordinances of heaven?}* Do you know the various motions of the heavenly bodies, which they keep constantly, as if they walked by a rule? Jer. 31.35. For Ordinances see on chap. 26.10. on the word, *Bounds*. For heaven, see on chapter 9.8. & 11.8.

*{canst thou set the dominion thereof in the earth?}* Can you give power to the stars to rule day and night, and cause diversity of weather, and of seasons, to which things below must submit? Gen. 1.16.8. For earth, see on chap. 15.19.

**V. 34. ***{Canst thou lift up thy voice to the clouds, that abundance of waters may cover thee?}* Have you that power over the clouds, that they will hear as soon as you command, and pour down plenty of waters, or rain to cover your fields, and make them fruitful.

*{Canst thou lift up}* Can you speak so loud that the clouds which are so high above you may hear you?

*{thy voice} Thy commanding voyce.* So it is translated, Numb. 9.23. For Voice see on chap. 15.21. upon the word, *Sound*.

*{to the clouds}* Heb. to the cloud. As if he would command some particular cloud to give rain when and where he should please.

*{that abundance of waters may cover thee}* See on chap. 22.11. Where all these words are used, though by waters there be meant troubles.

**V. 35. ***{Canst thou send lightnings that they may go, and say unto thee; Here we are?}* Are you the great Emperor of heaven and earth, at whose command the thunder and lightnings as so many soldiers are ready to go where, and do what you bid them?

*{Canst thou send}* Send them about your business, as your servants out of the clouds. See on Chap. 14.20. on the word, *Sendest him away*.

*{lightnings}* That is too hard a work for any creature to do.

*{that they may go}* Whither you send them, or command them to go. For Go, see on chap. 14.20. on the word *Passes*.

*{and say unto thee}* For Say see on chap. 16.1.

*{Here we are}* Heb. *Behold us.* We are at your command; ready to do whatsoever you shall bid us. Gen. 22.1. Or to go wheresoever you shall send us. Isa. 6.8. Others understand it of their return to give an account of their message, as if they should say, we have done your will. But lightnings go forth, and return not; and therefore the former interpretation is more natural.

**V. 36. ***{Who hath put wisdom in the inward parts? or who hath given understanding to the heart?}* These things are marvelous which I have spoken of, and although men have not wisdom enough to invent, or work such things, yet he has knowledge to understand, so much as is revealed of them. Neither has he that knowledge of himself, but from me.

*{Who hath given wisdom in the inward parts}* Who has bestowed that reasonable faculty upon man, and placed it in his soul, whereby he is able to conceive the truth about these strange works of God, and to find out many secrets of nature. None could do it, but I alone. The inward parts (as the word is translated here, and Psal. 51.6.) is translated reins, which by some is conceived to be the seat of wisdom, It comes from a word that signifies to plaster over or hide a thing, as the reins or kidneys are covered with fat. But the soul, the true seat of wisdom must be meant here, yet as exercising its wisdom in the reins and heart, and inward parts, by regulating the affections, and meditations of outward things presented by the senses.

*{or who hath given understanding to the heart?}* None but God, Chap. 32.8. Eccl. 2.26. The same thing expressed in other words, For Given, see on chap. 14.4. on the word, *Bring*. The word Heart is read only in this place, and is therefore the harder to be interpreted. It seems to be derived from a word that signifies a picture; for in man's mind are the representations of things. Others translate it, *to the Cock;* who gave skill to that creature to distinguish the hours of the night? The first is best, for God has given most knowledge to man.

**V. 37. ***{Who can number the clouds in wisdom? or who can stay the bottles of heaven}* Besides the difference of the clouds, there is a great number of them, which will pass any man's capacity to reckon up, He may as well number the waves of the Sea, or stars of heaven, Gen. 15.5. Neither can any man hinder the rain from overflowing the earth, when God pleases to send it.

*{Who can number the clouds in wisdom}* Or, *by his wisdom;* Who has so much understanding, as to be able to tell how many clouds there be in heaven. So the word is used, Psal. 22.17. or *who can declare the clouds, &c.* Who can tell the nature of them thoroughly, or the several uses of them. So this word is used Psal. 2.7. or *who can make the clouds like a Saphire.* Who can make the sky bright, and clear after rain? For the Sapphire has his name from hence in the original. For Number see on Chap. 14.16. For clouds see on Chap. 35.5.

*{or who can stay the bottles of heaven}* Heb. *Who can cause to lie down?* Who can hinder the clouds from pouring down rain. The clouds are compared to bottles, which hold the rain, till God please to pour it down on the earth; when men pour out the water they hold up the bottles: when they intend not to pour it out, they set them down. For lying down see on chap. 14.11. Some read, *The showers of heaven.* For it comes from a word, that signifies to fall, others, *the consort,* or *harmony of heaven;* because the word sometimes signifies a musical instrument. The Chaldee Paraphrase reads, *The clouds which are like bottles.* They hold the rainwater, as bottles hold wine. For heaven see on Chapter 9.8. & 11.8.

**V. 38. ***{When the dust groweth into hardness, and the clods cleave fast together}* Who is the cause of drought, when there is want of rain, when the earth grows hard like a molten pillar or bell, and the clods cleave one to another, and run together, so as that they leave clefts in the earth? No creature can bring the earth to this dry and hard condition.

*{when the dust}* See on Chap. 14.8. on the word, *Ground*.

*{Groweth into hardness}* Heb. *Is poured into hardnesse.* When it is as hard as metal poured out into a bell or pillar. So this word is used for a hard piece. Chap. 37.18.

*{and the clods}* See on chap. 21.33.

*{cleave fast together}* Run one to another for lack of moisture, as it were for succor.

**V. 39. ***{Wilt thou hunt the prey for the Lion? or fill the appetite of the young lion}* God having showed his great power in the Elements and Meteors, now sets it out in the living creatures. Therefore some begin the next Chapter here, because it is the beginning of a new matter. The creatures he speaks of are the beasts of the earth, the birds of the air, and fish of the Sea. He chooses out thirteen in all, in which God's great power is wonderfully seen. 1. The lion, verse 39,40. 2. The raven, 41. 3. The wild goat. Chap. 39.1. 4. The hind, verse 1. also. 5. The wild ass. Chap. 39.5. 6. The unicorn, verse 9. 7. The peacock, ver. 10. 8. The ostrich. ver. 11, 9. The horse. ver. 19. 10. The hawk. ver. 26. 11. The eagle. ver. 27. 12. The Elephant. Chap. 40.15. 13. The Whale. Chap. 41.1. God shows his power in these, partly in his provision for them, which men take no care for, nor can effect: and partly in the extraordinary strength, or other qualities, which he has bestowed upon them; in some whereof they go beyond men.

*{wilt thou hunt the prey for the lion}* Will you undertake to provide food for those devouring creatures in the wilderness? This is too great a work for you: it must be my work. Psal. 104.21. He mentions hunting the prey, because lions live on other beasts taken by hunting. By the lion here is meant the old lion, that either cannot go out of his den to hunt for food, but expects it to be brought to him, or lies in some thicket, till by God's providence some beast comes within his reach. As ver. 40. This appears to be the meaning because it is opposed to young lions in this verse And they are said to be such lions as want strength to hunt for themselves in the next verse. And the young ravens are mentioned v. 41. which cannot provide for themselves. And thus the word is translated old lion, Nah. 2.11. It is more for God's honor to provide for lions that are either too old, or too young to provide for themselves, than for such as are strong enough to seek their own food.

*{or fill the appetite of the young lion}* God stirs up the middle sort of lions which are strong to provide food not for themselves only, but also for such as by reason of age or youngness, cannot provide for themselves. This is too great a work for man. He uses the word of filling because though a little may serve the old lions, whose stomach decays with age, yet young lions have a strong appetite, and God gives them as much as they desire. *The appetite.* Heb. *the life.* The word חיה is used so chap. 33.18. & 24.22. where it is joined to the soul, which is sometimes translated will or desire, as Psal. 27.12. Psal. 107.9. And appetite (as here) Prov. 23.2. And greedy. Isa. 56.11. Some translate it, *The troop or company* For all the lions' whelps, which are many. Can you feed them all? So the word is translated Psal. 68.30. The last word is used for young lions, Nah. 2.12. opposed to old. It signifies one so young that he dare not go far from his den, but lies in it, as in an ark, or covered place. So the original word imports.

**V. 40. ***{When they crouch in their dens, and abide in the covert to lie in wait}* When they either lie resting in their dens for want of strength, or hid in some thickets, till a prey come in their way.

*{When they couch}* Lie down for ease, or weakness.

*{in their dens}* Which are as it were their houses, chap. 37.8.

*{and}* It may may be read, *Or.* For neither is in the original; and then the former part of the verse may be of the young lions, that have not yet gone out of their dens to hunt for prey: And the latter part of the old lions which have not strength enough to follow the prey, but lie hid in some thicket, till it fall in their way.

*{abide}* Run not abroad, but lie still there.

*{in the covert}* In some other thicket, which is, not their den, for beasts use not to come by lions' dens to be taken. The word signifies a Tabernacle to abide in for a time, not a house to dwell in. It is opposed to their dens or houses mentioned in the beginning of the verse.

*{to lie in wait}* To lie in ambush as it were to prey upon some beast, that goes by that way. See on chap. 37.8. on the word, *Dens*.

**V. 41. ***{Who provideth for the raven his food? when his young ones cry unto God, they wander for lack of meat}* Who takes care to feed ravens? Yea who takes care to feed the young ones, whose cry none hear but God, in their nests? And when they begin to fly, and are forsaken of the old ones: none gives them food but God.

*{who}* None can do it but I. Mat. 6.26.

*{provideth}* Not only creates food for birds, but makes it ready at hand; and as it were lays it in their way.

*{for the raven}* For the old raven, that has young ones How should it feed the young that could not feed itself without God's provision.

*{When}* Who then provides food for the young ravens, when the old one being gone abroad to seek it, they cry for hunger in the nest? God gives food to the old ones for themselves, and their young.

*{his young ones}* Heb. *His children.* As is translated chap. 21.11.

*{cry}* Make a great noise for hunger, as if they cried for relief. See on chap. 19.7.

*{to God}* Psal. 147.9. They are said to cry unto God, because men oftentimes hear them not: and if they hear them, yet regard them not; but God takes notice of it, as if they directed their voice to him, and supplies their want. For God see on Chap. 8.3,5.

*{they wander}* The old ones leave the nest, and flit up and down to get food for themselves and their young ones: but cannot get it, unless God give it. Or, *And when they wander.* So *And* is added in the former verse. and *when* understood in the latter part of it, out of the former. And then the sense is, that when the young ones begin to fly, and leave the nest, or are driven out of it by the old ones, they could not live, if God did not provide food for them.

*{for lack of meat}* Heb. *because there is no meat.* When they find none in the nest nor the old ones will no longer feed them, they fly abroad to seek for food, and God gives it them.

## CHAPTER 39

**Verse 1. ***{Knowest thou the time, when the wild goats of the rock bring forth? or canst thou mark, when the hinds do calve?}* In the beginning of this Chapter, is set out God's great care of such cattle, as are remote from men, and bring forth their young with great difficulty, yet safely through God's providence without man's help. He demands of Job, if he know 1. The time. 2. How long they go with young, before they be delivered ver. 2. 3. The manner of their bringing forth ver. 3. 4. How soon their young ones grow strong and leave them. ver. 4. The sum of this verse is. Are you acquainted with the time, when the wild goats or hinds bring forth, young, that you might help them in their difficult, and dangerous bringing them forth.

*{Knowest thou the time}* These wild creatures bring forth their young in rocks inaccessible; and therefore men that cannot come there, cannot help them being ignorant of the time; God only assists them.

*{when the wild goats}* They have their name from ascending in the original, because they use to be in high rocks for safety 1 Sam. 24.2. Psal. 104.18. Others derive it from a word that signifies profit, but by the contrary, because there is much danger in hunting them.

*{bring forth}* Which they use to do with great difficulty, as also the hinds do.

*{or canst thou mark}* Can you observe that time to know it certainly? Do you keep their reckoning? As we use to speak of women with child. For the word, see on Chap. 7.20. on the word, *Preserver*.

*{when the hinds}* That these bring forth their young very hardly, is collected out of Psal. 29.9. Where among other hard things effected by thunder, as breaking strongest trees, and shaking the greatest hills, this is recorded for one, *Making the hinds to calve.*

*{Do calve}* The word itself carries a great deal of pain with it, for it signifies to bring forth with grief.

**V. 2. ***{Canst thou number the months that they fulfill, or knowest thou the time when they bring forth}* Do you know exactly how many months those wild creatures go with young before they be delivered? if not, because you converse not with them, nor know not the time when they come together, how should you know either how long they go with their burden, or when they will lay it down?

*{Canst thou number}* The number was not so great but might easily be reckoned: but the meaning is, that men certainly know not how many months they bear their young within them, though some writers conceive it to be about eight months. For the word, see on Chap. 14.16.

*{the months}* See on chap. 29.2.

*{that they fulfill}* That must be complete from the time they are with young, till they must lay down their burden.

*{or knowest thou the time when they bring forth}* Or, *that thou shouldest know the time, &c.* If you neither know when they came together, nor how long they go with young, how should you know the time of their delivery? For the words, see on verse 1. where all these words are found.

**V. 3. ***{They bow themselves; they bring forth their young ones, they cast out their sorrows}* A description of the travail of these wild beasts. They bow themselves of their own accord, and bring forth without the help of any Midwife, and presently all their sorrow leaves them.

*{They bow themselves}* Or, *How,* or, *when they bow themselves.* Do you know the manner how, or the time when they bow themselves, that they may with the more ease bring forth their young?

*{they bring forth}* They are as it were cleft asunder with the greatness of their pain, because of the natural straitness of the passage. So this word is translated Cleaving. Chap. 16.13.

*{their young ones}* See on Chap. 38.41.

*{they cast out their sorrows}* They make grievous exclamations in the time of travail. Or, they violently cast out their young ones, the cause of their sorrows, and then their pangs cease. For Cast out, see on chap. 14.20. on the words *Sendest him away*. The word for Sorrow signifies such great pangs, as like cords gird them sorely in time of travail.

**V. 4. ***{Their young ones are in good liking, they grow up with corn: They go forth, and return not unto them}* Although the young ones be brought forth with so much pain and straitness, that one would think they should be very weak, yet they are strong, neither do they long trouble their dams, but soon leave them to feed, and fat themselves in the cornfields.

*{Their young ones}* Heb. *Their sons.* See on Chap. 14.21.

*{are in good liking}* They are slick and fat. Or, *are recovered.* So this word is translated Isa. 38.16. They soon recover from the weakness which came upon them by their painful entrance into the world.

*{They grow up}* The word signifies to grow much, or great. They are quickly grown great and strong.

*{With corn}* They soon leave sucking their dams, and are well fed with corn. Some read, *in the field,* or, *by the field.* That is, by that which grows in the field. Hence some collect, that they use to be born about Harvest time, when corn is in the fields.

*{They go forth}* To seek for food for themselves. For the word, see on Chap. 20.25.

*{and return not unto them}* They come not back to their dams to suck, because they find food enough in the fields. This shows they live by God's providence. For return, see on Chap. 14.13. on the word, *past*.

**V. 5. ***{Who hath sent out the wild ass free? or who hath loosed the bands of the wild ass?}* Who has made the wild ass so stout, that he will not be caught nor tamed, nor be brought in subjection to do any work for man, as not only other creatures, but other asses do.

*{Who hath sent out}* All creatures are as it were bound with bands to do those services God requires. But he has as it were manumitted or set free some from all service of men, as the wild ass among the rest. For the word, see on chap. 14. 20.

*{the wild ass}* That will not come near men, nor be employed by them. From this word in the Hebrew comes *Ferae*, wild beasts, for they are as wild as any.

*{free}* From men, so that he is not in their power. For the word, see on chap. 3. 19.

*{or who hath loosed the bands of the wild ass?}* Not that the wild ass was ever bound, but is kept from those bands which other cattle are subject to, to be employed by man for the saddle, the cart, or the plow, from all which the wild ass is as free as if he had those bands taken off, which lay upon all creatures at first to serve man. For *looseth*, see on Chap. 12. 18.

**V. 6. ***{Whose house I have made the wilderness, and the barren Land his dwellings}* To whom I have given a large dwelling in the wilderness, far from the sight of men, where he is well content to be at liberty with very mean food, rather than to live among men, and fare better.

*{Whose house}* The place where he abides; as men do in their houses. For the word, see on Chap. 15. 28.

*{I have made}* I have appointed, or set out for him.

*{the wilderness}* A solitary place where men live not, yet though it be not so fruitful as other places, it affords food for these wild creatures, Job 24. 5. Jer. 2. 24. It is likely he alludes to the wilderness of Arabia not far off, Chap. 1. 19. wherein were wild asses swifter than horses, as Xenophon reports in the third book of Cyrus his expedition. The wilderness and Arabia have both of them their names from the same root in Hebrew. Others read it, *the plain*, as if it were opposed to wild goats, that live on hills and rocks, verse 1. But the former reading is better, wild places agree best with wild beasts.

*{and the barren land}* Heb. *the salt place*. As barren as if it had been sown with salt, Judg. 9. 45. Psal. 107. 34. Jer. 17. 6.

**V. 7. ***{He scorneth the multitude of the City, neither regardeth he the crying of the driver}* He lives in places remote from the noise and tumults in cities, and where he is not called upon to go to work, as tame beasts are by the drivers.

*{He scorneth}* Heb. *He laugheth at*. He is as free from it as one that stands by, and laughs to see others in a rage, or cattle scared with the noise and cry of drivers (so laughing is used for slighting, chap. 5. 22. Psal. 2. 4.) And if the drivers should offer to urge him to work, as they do tame beasts, he would scorn it, and show them a light pair of heels, and be gone. See the great difference between the wild ass and tame. One very quick, the other very dull, and observe God’s power in putting so great difference between creatures of the same kind. Some commentators conceive this to be a reason why God instances in this creature. For the word see on ch. 29. 24.

*{the multitude}* He fears not a great many. He can by his swiftness escape them all. Or, *The noyse*. If a whole city should seek to scare him, to bring him to work like other beasts, he would not regard it. So it is translated Noise, Am. 5. 23.

*{of the city}* Of the citizens. The place for the persons, as it is used, Prov. 11. 10. He is not afraid of a whole city.

*{neither regardeth he}* Heb. *Heareth*. He will not be brought to labor by any cry or noise of any. For the word see on chap. 13. 17. on the word, *Hear*.

*{the crying}* Or *Noise*, as Chap. 36. 29. which they make to drive the beasts to labor.

*{of the driver}* Heb. *of the exactour*. Such were the taskmasters of Egypt, who urged the Israelites to their labor. Exod. 5. 6. So do the drivers call on the beasts to hasten them in their work. But such the wild ass had none to trouble him. See on Chap. 3. 18. where it is translated oppressor. Such men use importunate cries to urge poor men to hard labors.

**V. 8. ***{The range of the mountains is his pasture, and he searcheth after every green thing}* That which he finds by diligent search, and ranging up and down the mountains is his food: he looks not that men should bring him hay in winter, as they do tame beasts, but finds green grass all the year long, and variety on the hills. And it is a marvelous thing, that this wild ass being no ramping beast, that lives on flesh, as lions and wolves do, can find grass to live on in the deserts, and escape the rage of those ravenous creatures, and not be devoured by them.

*{the range}* Some understand it of the choicest things, that grow in the mountain. Others of things that grow in unknown places that none can find out but himself. It comes from a word that signifies to search, and intimates that he ranges up and down the hills, and feeds on what he likes.

*{of the mountains}* There are many mountains in deserts, as well as low grounds. It may be the wild ass keeps there for fear of lions, and other beasts of prey, lest while he seeks food below, he be devoured himself.

*{is his pasture}* Affords him food, as the pastures do the tame beasts.

*{and he searcheth after every green thing}* He seeks for green herbs, and grass to eat, and lives altogether upon it.

**V. 9. ***{Will the unicorn be willing to serve thee, or abide by thy crib}* As before the freedom of the wild ass was set out in opposition to the service of the tame ass, so here the liberty of the unicorn in opposition to the servile nature of the ox. God here brings in man as it were hiring the unicorn to serve him about the ground, as the ox does, which he can by no means bring him to, because he will not be tamed. Yet God provides for him, and man may see his sin in the refusal of service by these great creatures, that have strength enough to do it, and were subject to him before the fall. The service he propounds is, 1. Set down in general in this verse. 2. in particular verse 10, 11, 12. And that 1. In sowing the ground ver. 10, 11. 2. In bringing home the crop ver. 12.

*{Will the unicorn}* This must be a wild beast that will not be tamed, nor brought to serve man. Many conceive there is no such creature as an unicorn, that has but one horn, and that growing in the midst of his forehead, because it appears not certainly, that ever any man saw such a creature. Also this creature has more horns than one, Deut. 33. 17. where the horns of an unicorn are mentioned in the original, though it be unicorns in the translation. Some would have it to be the rhinoceros here meant, which is an enemy to the elephant. But that has a trunk or snout rather than an horn, or, if an horn on the snout, but a small one. And the naturalists write, that it may be tamed; and therefore it is not likely to be the beast here meant. It is more like to be the wild bull, for oxen are joined to this beast elsewhere. Deut. 33. 17. Psal. 29. 6. Isa. 34. 6. Such creatures also are strong, and have great horns. And very likely there were some such in those parts, as untamable, as wild asses. And this is the more probable, because the wild ass was before opposed to the tame: and so here the wild bull may be to the tame ox. It has his name from a word, that signifies height, for it is an high creature in respect of many other wild beasts, and the horns are long.

*{Be willing to serve thee}* Can he be tamed, that he may readily go to cart, and plow as tame oxen will.

*{or abide by the crib}* When he has done your drudgery all day, will he come to your yard at night to eat what you have provided for him, and lie there all night to be at your service next day again. So the word signifies to lodge all night. See on chap. 29. 19.

**V. 10. ***{Canst thou bind the unicorn with his band in the furrow? or will he harrow the valleys after thee.}*

*Hast thou power to bind him as thou dost thy oxen*, with his bands to draw the plow before you to make way for the seed, or to follow you with the harrow to lay the ground even, and cover it?

*{canst thou bind the unicorn?}* The word sometimes signifies binding men together in a conspiracy, as 1 Sam. 22. 8. but ordinarily signifies the binding of one thing to another, as of cattle with cords to keep them from being unruly, and tie them to their labor. So here. For the word see on chap. 38. 31. For the unicorn see on verse 9.

*{with his band}* The word signifies a twisted thick rope able to hold the strongest beast, and it is called his band, because it is used to bind him.

*{in the furrow}* Or, *For the furrow*. Or, *To the furrow*. That is, to plow, and make furrows. And the cattle are bound; that they may go right, as they are guided by the driver, to make straight furrows. So ב is used chap. 24. 5. They go forth to their work. For the word see on chap. 31. 38.

*{or will he harrow}* Will he do any kind of husbandry work for you? Harrowing is to break the clods, that the corn may be cherished in the earth, and come forth the more easily. Isa. 28. 24. Hos. 10. 11.

*{the valleys}* The furrows, as before, though some conceive the ridges between the furrows to be meant here, which lie as valleys between them, yet valleys may here be meant properly, for they are most commonly used for corn, and hills for pasture, because more difficult to plow. It has its name in the original from deepness, because it lies lower than the mountains.

*{after thee}* Though you go before to guide him, yet he will not follow. The plowman puts the horse before, the harrower leads him after.

**V. 11. ***{Wilt thou trust him because his strength is great? Or wilt thou leave thy labour to him}* Will you be so confident, that he will do your work, as the tame ox, because he is as strong, or stronger rather? or will you leave that which you have so long labored for to be brought home by him? Sure you dare not confide in him.

*{wilt thou trust him}* To plow and harrow your ground, as verse 10. or to bring home the crop, as verse 12.

*{because his strength is great}* Much strength is required for plow and cart. Therefore the strongest men and beasts are employed in that labor. He has strength enough to do it, but will not. For Great see on chap. 22. 5.

*{or wilt thou leave thy labour to him}* Men use to leave things to be done with such as they trust, but he will fail you if you think to employ him in bringing home your harvest. So labor is put for that which is got by labor chap. 20. 18. Psal. 128. 2.

**V. 12. ***{Wilt thou believe him that he will bring home thy seed, and gather it into thy barn}* God brought in (verse 9.) Job as hiring the unicorn to do his country work. Now he tells him, that if that beast could, and should say, he would undertake it, yet Job had no reason to believe him, that he would bring home the crop that comes of his seed into the barn, no more than he would plow or harrow. Some understand the former part of carrying the seed into the field, but that is but a small labor, and it seems to be expounded in the end of the verse of carrying it home. Others understand it of plowing and harrowing the field so, as that the seed may yield such plenty that the barn be filled, which is done by the labor of the ox, but cannot by the unicorn, who has neither will, nor skill to do it.

*{wilt thou believe him}* See on Chap. 15.22.

*{that he will bring forth}* See on Chap. 15.13. on the word *Turnest, and on Chap*. 20.10. on the word *Restore*.

*{thy seed}* זרע is taken 1. For the seed of trees and herbs, and corn, Gen. 1.29. 2. Seed of living creatures. Gen. 38.9. 3. Posterity, which comes, of man's seed. Psal. 22.23. 4. For ripe corn, that comes of the seed corn. Hag. 2.19. So here, for it is ready to be brought home, and carried into the barn. And so was labor put for that which comes of labor, verse 11.

*{and gather it}* That is, when it is gathered out of the field, carry it into their barn. And one verb included in another. See on Chap. 18.8. For the word see on chap. 27.19.

*{into thy barn}* Or gather the corn in, which use to lie in your barn: for so the same word translated *Floore*. Deut. 15.14. is used for the corn.

**V. 13. ***{Gavest thou the goodly wings unto the peacocks? or wings and feathers unto the Ostrich}* God now sets out his excellency in the adorning and providing for some stately birds, although very foolish. Two are named in this verse, and the latter largely spoken of in the following verses.

*{gavest thou}* These words are not in the original, but added to make up the sense: which are expressed in the like case, verse 19.

*{the goodly}* The word signifies rejoicing or delighting in a thing, as Chap. 20.18. Prov. 7.18. The peacock takes great pleasure in his feathers.

*{wings}* Or *Feathers*. For the beauty of a peacock is in his tail, which has feathers in it, as well as the wing. For the word see on Chap. 37.3. on the word *ends*.

*{unto the peacocks}* It has the name in Hebrew from the great noise it makes, which may be heard afar off.

*{or wings}* It comes from a word, that signifies strong: and such are the wings of ostriches.

*{and feathers}* It has his name from flying, for they are instruments thereof. Some make a distinct bird of it, and take it for the ostrich, but it is commonly used for the feathers of a bird Ezek. 17.3,7.

*{unto the ostrich}* This word is elsewhere translated the stork, which shows piety to the aged parents, according to the derivation of the word in the Hebrew, but here it is taken for the ostrich by the contrary, for she shows no pity to her young, verse 16. It cannot be the stork here, for it has no such beautiful feathers, as the peacock, nor does not so carry it self to the young ones, as is described in the verses following: neither does it lay eggs on the ground: but in nests in high trees, Psal. 104.17.

**V. 14. ***{Which leaveth her eggs in the earth, and warmeth them in the dust}* The blockishness and unnaturalness of the ostrich is now set out in this, and some verses following, that whereas other birds carefully lay their eggs in nests out of danger, and sit on them to hatch them, she clean contrary covers them in the sand, and flies away, leaving them to the hot sun to hatch them. This sets out God's providence in bringing them forth in so strange a manner, which none else could do.

*{Which leaveth}* Takes no further care of them after she has laid them.

*{her eggs}* Out of which she might expect young ones.

*{in the earth}* See on chap. 15.19.

*{and warmeth them}* Leaves them for the sun, and hot sand to warm, that they may bring forth young ones, which is no unusual thing in very hot countries.

*{in the dust}* See on Chap. 14.8. on the word, *Ground*.

**V. 15. ***{And forgetteth that the foot may crush them, or that the wild beast may break them}* The naturalists give other reasons why she leaves her eggs thus, to wit, lest she should break them with her weight, and because she cannot hatch them well for want of feathers on her belly, but God tells the true cause, her unnaturalness and blockishness. Men make her wise and provident, but God sets her out as foolish and careless, that considers not how easily they may be utterly spoiled by the foot of any beast passing that way.

*{and forgetteth}* That is, when she leaves them there, she does not consider the danger of it. She carries her self, as one that had forgot it, for she cannot in property of speech be said to forget what she never knew.

*{that the foot}* That is the foot of some beast, as follows. For men seldom came that way, unless on horseback to hunt wild beasts.

*{may crush them}* May break them, before they be ready to bring forth young. Heb. *May crush it*. That is, each egg. See the like, chap. 38.20,33.

*{or that the wild beast}* Heb. *The beast of the field*. That lives not about houses, as tame beasts do, but in the desert. For the word Beast see on Chap. 37.8.

*{May break them}* Heb. *May thresh them*. That is, break them all to pieces.

**V. 16. ***{She is hardened against her young ones, as though they were not hers: her labour is in vain without fear}* Contrary to the nature of other fowls she has a hard heart, that can so leave her eggs, as if she had no relation to the young ones that should come out of them: and had labored to no purpose in laying them, that fears not any hurt that may befall them, nor takes any care for the hatching of them.

*{she is hardened}* Others read it, *God hath hardened her*. Because the word is the masculine gender, and signifies actively, as Isa. 63.17. And God is expressed in the like case in the verse following.

*{against her young ones}* Against her eggs, out of which she might look for young ones. So seed is put for the corn that comes out of it, verse 12. Heb. *Her children*. See on chap. 14.21.

*{as though they were not hers}* Heb. *For that they were not hers*. She counts them none of hers, and therefore is hardhearted toward them.

*{her labour}* Her pains in laying the eggs.

*{is in vain}* Empty, and to no purpose. No good comes of it. As good no eggs, as no hope of young, for ought she knows.

*{without fear}* Because she has no care what becomes of her eggs, she fears no evil that might come to them. For the word see on Chap. 15.21. on the word, *Dreadfull*.

**V. 17. ***{Because God hath deprived her of wisdom, neither hath he imparted to her understanding}* God who has given some small knowledge to other birds and beasts, at least so much as might teach them how to take care of their eggs, and young ones, has not given her so much knowledge. And no wonder then if she be so unnatural.

*{because}* Here is the reason of her carelessness set down; because God has not communicated that wise care to her. The creature can have no more good than God pleases to bestow on it.

*{God}* See on Chap. 5.17. & 11.5.

*{hath deprived her of wisdom}* Heb. *hath made her to forget wisdom*. That is, has not given it to her. Now that a man has not, and receives not, does no more good by or with it, than if he had forgotten it. So forgetfulness is used, Chap. 8.13.

*{neither hath he imparted to her understanding}* He has not given her any the least part of that forecast that is in other fowls. For the word imparted, see on chap. 27.17. on the word *Divide*.

**V. 18. ***{What time she lifteth up herself on high, she scorneth the horse and his rider}* Here is an answer to a secret objection. If the ostrich who is so heavy of body, be so dull of wit, then it may easily be taken. No, says God, I have provided for that also. As soon as she sees the hunter coming, she lifts up her self, and though she cannot fly up very high, yet partly with fluttering her wings, which are like sails driven with the wind, and partly with the help of her feet she runs so fast, that the swiftest horse cannot overtake her. The naturalists write of her, that when she thus stretches out her self in her flight, she is higher than horse and rider both, and runs so fast, that the swiftest horses give over pursuing her; for she is as swift as a bird that flies. And that the Arabians use to try their horses' swiftness by overtaking them. See Ælian and Oppian, and Xenophon in Cyrus his expedition, 1. Book, in Arabia.

*{what time}* Some knit these words to what went before. As soon as she has hid her eggs in the sand, she hastes away. But the time is rather intimated in the words following, to wit, when she is pursued by the hunter on horseback.

*{she lifteth up herself on high}* She lifts up her neck on high, that she may run the swifter.

*{she scorneth the horse and his rider}* She slights the hunter, and deludes him, and will not be taken by him, notwithstanding the swiftness of his horse. For Scorning see on verse 7.

**V. 19. ***{Hast thou given the horse strength? hast thou clothed his neck with thunder?}* God having before showed how the horse was slighted by a foolish bird, takes occasion from thence to show wherein this creature excels the ostrich, to wit, in his strength and courage, which is so useful to man in time of war. As if he had said to Job; You see the horse is a strong beast, but how came he by his great strength, and that terrible power of neighing, that does as it were clothe his neck, and terrify the enemies in war? Sure you did not give it him. He must needs have it from me.

*{hast thou given}* For this word see on ch. 14.4. on the word *Bring*.

*{the horse strength}* The horse is a very strong creature, and very useful in war. See on chap. 26.14. on the word, *Power*.

*{hast thou clothed his neck with thunder?}* Have you given him such a strength in his neck, that he can neigh till his eyes sparkle, as if he did both thunder and lighten. So men are said to be clothed with honor and shame, which yet do not cover their bodies. Others understand it of his mane which is an ornament of his neck, and a stately carriage, as it were of arrogance. Or, it may be taken of indignation, when shaking his mane he goes furiously into the battle. For it comes from a word, that signifies to be moved with indignation, as well as to thunder. See on Chap. 26.14.

**V. 20. ***{Canst thou make him afraid, as a grasshopper? The glory of his nostrils is terrible}* Can you with noise make him afraid, and cause him to fly from you like a grasshopper. Nay he will make such a noise with his nostrils, that he will rather terrify you.

*{Canst thou make him afraid}* Or, *quake*, as Broughton reads it, others read, *Canst thou make him to move*. The horse is a heavy body, yet runs into the battle as nimbly, as a grasshopper leaps. But that is not by any strength from you. The former is better, because the original word carries fear with it.

*{as a grasshopper}* Make him so afraid, that he shall run away as nimbly as a grasshopper. It has his name from a word of multitude, for grasshoppers go in troops to devour the fruits of the earth. They are part of God's great army, Joel 2.25. where the same word is translated, *Locust*.

*{the glory of his nostrils is terrible}* His snorting and sneezing makes men afraid. For glory see on ch. 37.22. on the word *Majesty*. Heb. *Terrors*. That is, very terrible. For the word see on chap. 9.34. & 13.21. on the words, *Fear and Dread*.

**V. 21. ***{He paweth in the valley, and rejoiceth in his strength: he goeth on to meet the armed men}* When the army comes into the valley to fight, see you not, how the wanton horse cannot stand still, but is making holes in the ground with his feet. Judg. 5.22. And is so proud of his strength, that the rider can hardly keep him in till the enemy come, but he will run forth to meet him, and is no whit afraid of his terrible weapons?

*{He paweth}* Heb. *They digge*. That is, his feet dig. It may be read, *When they digge in the valley he rejoyceth, &c.* That is, when the army go to entrench themselves, as if they durst not fight, his courage fails not, The horse would fain be in the battle. The word חפר signifies, 1. To dig, as Chap. 11.18. 2. To seek by digging, for to that end men dig, that they may seek for mines, or some good thing. Chap. 3.21. 3. To be ashamed, because when men find not what they dig and and seek for, they are ashamed. Isa. 1.29. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{in the valley}* For there battles use to be fought, especially of horsemen, that cannot so well fight on hills. For the word see on ver. 10.

*{and rejoiceth in his strength}* Is so confident of his strength, that he cheerfully goes on to the battle. Psal. 19.5.

*{he goeth on to meet the armed men}* Heb. *The armour*. He is so stout, that without fear he runs upon the pikes. For Goeth on, see on chap. 14.2. on the word, *Cometh forth*. Or it may be meant of coming out of the trenches, when men dare not. As before in this verse. The last word נשק signifies, first, Battle, as Psal. 140.7. 2. Armor. 1 King. 10.25. But here it must be arms rather than armor. Offensive weapons, as Pikes and Spears, which gall the horses rather than defensive, which are to preserve men from wounds, as shields and bucklers.

**V. 22. ***{He mocketh at fear, and is not affrighted, neither turneth he back from the sword}* He counts that a sport and play whereof others are afraid. He is not daunted at the noise of arms, or trumpets: neither is he afraid of the glittering sword.

*{He mocketh}* He is as little afraid, as men are of those things at which they laugh. See on verse 7. on the word, *Scorneth*.

*{at fear}* At those terrible things that men are afraid of. See on Ch. 15.21. on the word, *Dreadfull*. Here is meant such things as fright men in battle.

*{and is not affrighted}* Neither noise, nor enemies abate ought of his courage.

*{neither turneth he back}* As fearful cowards use to do, that they may fly from danger. For the word see on Chap. 14.13. on the word, *Past*.

*{from the sword}* Heb. *From the face of the sword*. Or, *Because of the sword*. That is for fear of the sword. For the word see on ch. 27.14.

**V. 23. ***{The quiver rattleth against him; the glittering spear and the shield}* Though arrows, spears and darts rattle about his ears, yet he is not afraid.

*{The quiver rattleth against him}* The arrows out of the quiver being sent with a great force, come singing as it were against him: for the string drawn home causes a great noise, and we use to say, when the arrow comes with great force, it sings. Others read, *The quiver ratleth over him*. That is, the arrows in the rider's quiver that rides on him might scare him with their noise. Horsemen then used arrows as well as footmen, for they had no guns.

*{the glittering spear}* The brightness whereof, as well as the sharpness, might make him afraid. Heb. *The flaming speare*. For it shines like a flame. Gen. 3.24.

*{and the shield}* Rather the javelin, or some such offensive weapon, that might push the horses, or be flung at them: for horses need not fear shields. It is translated a spear. Chap. 41.29. Josh. 8.18. and opposed to offensive weapons here.

**V. 24. ***{He swalloweth the ground with fierceness, and rage: neither believeth he that it is the sound of the trumpet}* He runs so swiftly and fiercely, as if he would not only overcome men, but destroy even the whole earth: and whereas the enemies think to scare him with the sound of their trumpets, he regards it as little, as men do things they do not believe to be true.

*{He swalloweth the ground}* Some understand it of digging holes in the ground, as verse 21. But it is rather to be understood of his swift course, whereby he runs over the ground as fast, as if he did swallow it up at a draught. For ground see on Chap. 15.19.

*{with fierceness and rage}* Or, with commotion and unquietness. As 2 Sam. 22.8. Psal. 77.18. Isa. 13.13. & 14.16. Joel 2.10.

*{neither believeth he, that it is the sound of the trumpet}* Some understand it, He is so glad to hear it out of his desire to the battle that he cannot believe it for joy. Luke 24.41. So far is he from any fear of the battle. Others read, *Neither stand he still, when the voyce of the trumpet is heard*. That is, he can hardly be kept back by the rider, when the sign is given to begin the battle. For the word, Believeth see on Chap. 15.22. The trumpet was of old, as now it is, sounded in the beginning of the battle as a sign when to give the onset. Numb. 10.9. & Chron. 13.12.

**V. 25. ***{He saith among the trumpets, Ha, Ha; and he smelleth the battle afar off, the thunder of the captains, and the shouting}* He rejoices when by the loud sound of the trumpets, and the noise of the captains to encourage the soldiers, and their shouting, he receives the approaching battle, as if he cried out for joy, and triumph.

*{He saith among the trumpets, Ha, Ha;}* He is as joyful as men that show their joy by such expressions. And is ready to show it in his kind by neighing. Some read it, *in the sufficiency of the trumpet*. That is, when it blows so loud, and clear, that all may take notice of a call to the battle, ha, ha. A note of rejoicing, as Psal. 34.21.25. & 42.15.

*{and he smelleth the battle afar off}* He knows, when he hears the trumpets that the battle is at hand. By smelling is meant perceiving. So smelling is translated touching, that is, perceiving the force of fire. Judg. 16.19. See more on Job 14.9. On the word, *Sent*. Afar off is long before sometimes: as Isa. 22.11. & 2 King. 19.25.

*{the thunder of the captains, and the shouting}* The great noise the captains make to encourage their soldiers to fight, and the acclamation of the soldiers in token of forwardness. These also are tokens to the horse of the approaching battle. For thunder see on chap. 25.14. For shouting see on ch. 33.26. on the word, *Joy*.

**V. 26. ***{Doth the hauke fly by thy wisdom, and stretch her wings toward the South}* God now instances in certain birds of prey, shows his power and providence in the swiftness of their flight, and have a natural forecast given them for their good, which no man could bestow upon them. The sum of this verse is, could your understanding reach so far, as to make wings for the hawk to fly withal, or to teach her how to use them, or direct her course. Southward toward the warm sun to cherish her new feathers, when she has cast her old, or against winter to leave colder countries to fly to warmer?

*{Doth the hauke fly by thy wisdom}* Do you teach her how to fly? Others read it. *Doth the hauke get her feathers by thy wisdom?* To wit, at first, or when she has cast them. See on verse 13. on the word *wings*.

*{and stretch her wings toward the South}* That is, Do you direct her to fly that way? For wings see on chap. 37.3. on the word *Ends*. The south in the original is taken from a word that signifies the right hand, because when a man turns his face to the rising sun, the south is on his right hand. See on chap. 9.9.

**V. 27. ***{Doth the Eagle mount up at thy command, and make her nest on high}* The Lord here begins to set out his powerful providence in guiding the eagle, which is accounted the prince of fowls, as the lion of wild beasts. And that 1. in the height of her flight in this verse. 2. of her nest in this verse, and the next. 3. in her quick sight to discern her prey afar off. verse 29. 4. in smelling out dead carcasses afar off, that she and her young ones may feed on them. She flies higher than other birds, that she may the better be undiscerned, and with greater force rush down upon her prey. She builds her nest on high for the safety of her young ones. Job never taught her these things.

*{doth the Eagle}* Some take it for the vulture, because they conceive, some things here spoken fit not the eagle.

*{mount up}* Fly right up, till she be quite out of sight.

*{at thy command}* Heb. *At thy mouth*. See on chap. 33.6. & 15.5.

*{and make her nest on high}* On the top of high rocks in places inaccessible. v. 28. Jer. 49.16. Obad. v. 4. It may be read, *And that she make her nest on high*. Is it at your command that she does so?

**V. 28. ***{She dwelleth, and abideth on the rock, upon the crag of the rock, and the strong place}* As the eagle can fly up to the highest rocks, so there she picks out a dwelling and lodging, and makes her nest in the clefts on the very craggy tops of the rocks, which are to her; and hers, as an invincible castle made by nature, not by art.

*{she dwelleth and abideth on the rock}* There she remains day and night, unless sometime, she fly abroad for her pleasure, or to get food for her self, or her young. The word abiding is tarrying all night. See Chap. 29.19.

*{upon the crag of the rock}* Heb. *upon the tooth of the rock*. For crags of rocks are like great teeth in fashion, and sharpness. For the word see on chap. 16.9. on the word *Teeth*.

*{and the strong place}* It is derived from a word, that signifies to hunt, because men that are hunted like wild beasts, fly to such strong places for refuge. And such places seeks the eagle for the safeguard of her self, and her young.

**V. 29. ***{From thence she seeketh the prey, and her eyes behold afar off}* In this verse he sets out the quick sight, and nimble industry of the eagle to provide food for her self and hers. She spies it afar off, and quickly seizes on it.

*{From thence}* From the very top of the rocks, where her nest is.

*{she seeketh}* Heb. *She diggeth*. See on verse 21. Men seek by digging, and the eagle by flying.

*{the prey}* Heb. *Meat*. For her self, and hers.

*{and}* Or, *which*. For she sees it first, and then seeks it, and neither word being in the original, the one may be added, as well as the other.

*{her eyes}* See on Chap. 15.15. on the word *Sight*.

*{behold afar off}* Heb. *That which is afar off*. This notes the curious sight of the eagle, that can at a great distance discern her prey.

**V. 30. ***{Her young ones also suck up blood: and where the slain are, there is she}* Whereas other birds drink water, the young eagles do not, but eat the flesh of men, and beasts newly killed, and suck their blood. The carcasses are brought to them by the old ones, which can smell dead corpses afar off, yea as some naturalists report, before the battle be fought; Neither can any carcass be any where, but they soon resort to it, to get food for themselves, and theirs.

*{Her young ones also}* Nor she alone but her young ones also live on flesh and blood, it comes from a word that signifies to put forth, and such young ones are meant, as are putting forth feathers, and cannot yet fly to shift for themselves.

*{suck up blood}* They suck it out of the dead carcasses till they leave none. For blood see on Chap. 16.18.

*{and where}* Heb. *And in the place in which*.

*{the slain are}* Such as are killed by men, especially in battle; where store of dead corpses lie neglected, and unburied. See on ch. 24.12. on the word wounded: for the same word signifies both.

*{there is she}* Thither presently flies the old eagle to provide for her, and hers. See how our Savior applies this. Matth. 24.28. Luke 17.37.

## CHAPTER 40

**Verse 1. ***{Moreover the LORD answered Job and said}* When the Lord had stayed a while, that Job might have space to answer, if he would, God seeing that he held his peace speaks again, and calls upon him for an answer.

*{Moreover}* Heb. *And*.

*{the LORD}* See on Chap. 38.1.

*{answered}* See on Chap. 16.1.

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1.1.

*{and said}* See Chap. 16.1.

**V. 2. ***{Shall he that contendeth with the Almighty instruct him? he that reproveth God let him answer it}* Do you think your self able to teach God, how to guide the world, that you stand out against him thus to censure his actions? Is it not more fit, that you should be called to account your self for blaming God?

*{shall he that contendeth}* To wit, in judgment. Not that will fight with God, but that will plead against God's proceedings. See on Chap. 13.6. on the word, *Pleadings*.

*{with the Almighty}* See on Chap. 8.3,5.

*{instruct him}* Teach him to guide the world better; or do greater things than I have told you. Others read, *Is it wisdom to contend with the Almighty?* Others thus, *should he be instructed that contends with the Almighty?* He deserves not to be reasoned with, but censured.

*{he that reproveth}* See on Chap. 13.3. on the word, *Reason*.

*{God}* See on Chap. 5.17. & 15.1.

*{let him answer it}* Let him answer for so doing. Or, let him answer any one of those things which I have said before in the rest. He intimates to Job, that he should either answer; or yield the cause. For the word see on Chap. 16.1.

**V. 3. ***{Then Job answered the LORD and said}* Job being now convinced by so many arguments, and so plainly called upon by God for an answer, humbly confesses his fault.

*{Then}* See on Chap. 38.1.

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1.1.

*{answered}* See on Chap. 16.1.

*{the LORD}* See on Chap. 38.1,2.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 16.1.

**V. 4. ***{Behold I am vile, what shall I answer thee? I will lay my hand upon my mouth}* Job now shows his repentance, and desires pardon for his oversights, acknowledges his own meanness, and that he is overcome by arguments, that he has nothing more to say against God, or for himself, and therefore resolves to be forever silent in that argument. Yet seeing he does not make so full an acknowledgment of his sin, and repentance as was fit, God sets on him again, verse 6. and brings him to it, Chap. 42.2.

*{Behold}* See on Chap. 1.12.

*{I am vile}* I am light, and have no substance or weight in me; and therefore am worthy to be condemned. So much the word in the original imports.

*{what shall I answer thee?}* You have showed your greatness, and goodness by so many demonstrations, that I have nothing to oppose. For the word see on Chap. 20.10. & 15.13. on the words, *Restore, Turnest*.

*{I will lay mine hand upon my mouth}* See on Chap. 21.5. where all these words are handled.

**V. 5. ***{Once have I spoken, but I will not answer: yea twice, but I will proceed no further}* My sin already is too great, that I have divers times spoken presumptuously against God's proceedings, but I will so order my self for time to come, that I will offend in that kind no more.

*{Once have I spoken, yea twice}* See on Chap. 33.14.

*{but I will not answer}* I will not defend what I have unadvisedly said. For the word see on Chap. 16.1.

*{but I will proceed no further}* Heb. *I will not adde*. I will not only not defend what I said before, but also not say so any more.

**V. 6. ***{Then answered the LORD unto Job out of the whirl-wind, and said}* Here begins a new speech of God's to Job. The reason of it see on verse 4. of this Chapter. In which note, 1. A preface set before God's speech in this verse, 2. A challenge, verse 7. 3. A reproof, from verse 8. to verse 15. 4. Proofs of God's greatness, from the greatest living creature in the sea, from verse 15. to the end of this Chapter, and in the land, Chap. 41. All these words were expounded on Chap. 38.1. See there.

**V. 7. ***{Gird up thy loins now like a man. I will demand of thee, and declare thou unto me}* This verse was all expounded on chap. 38.3. The words are all there. So that this is a second challenge to Job, and shows, that God has not yet received full satisfaction from him.

**V. 8. ***{Wilt thou disannul my judgment? wilt thou condemn me, that thou mayest be righteous?}* It is not enough, that you defend your own innocency, but that you must do it by accusing me of injustice. Either I have wronged you by afflicting you without a just cause, or you have wronged me by accusing me, that I have done so. One of these must needs be granted, for there is no mean between them. Now therefore lay aside your passions, and judge in your own conscience whether it be more likely that I have wronged you, or you me.

*{Wilt thou also}* Will you not only clear your self, but also wrong me.

*{disannul}* Make it void. For an unjust sentence is in law and equity no sentence, neither ought it to be executed, Psal. 51.4. Rom. 3.4.

*{my judgment}* See on Chap. 22.4.

*{wilt thou condemn me, that thou mayest be righteous?}* Will you accuse me of injustice in afflicting you, that your cause may be esteemed just? For the word, see on Chap. 9.20.

**V. 9. ***{Hast thou an arm like God? or canst thou thunder with a voice like him?}* Or if you should confess, that you cannot deal with me by right, do you think you can do it by the might of your arm, or terror of your voice. In both these you must fall far short of me.

*{Hast thou}* Heb. *Or hast thou?* If you think you cannot prevail one way, have you any hope to prevail another? So ו is translated, Or, Exod. 21.15,17. Some read, *And if thou have an arme, &c. Then deck thy self*: verse 10. Show what power you have. But the first reading is more emphatical and moving.

*{an arm}* See on Chap. 22.8. on the word, *Mighty*.

*{like God}* See on Chap. 8.3,5.

*{or canst thou thunder with a voice like him?}* That the thunder is God's voice see on Chap. 37.2,5. And for the words, see there also.

**V. 10. ***{Deck thyself now with majesty and excellency, and array thyself with glory and beauty}* If you have such a power, and thundering voice as God, then clothe your self with state, such as becomes God, and mount up, and speak out of a whirlwind, as I do, that we may be on equal terms, and try out the matter in controversy between us.

*{Deck thyself now}* As kings on high days put on their crowns and robes to show their state, so do you, appear as glorious as you can. From this word in the original comes another that signifies ornaments. Thus God decks himself, Psal. 93.1. & 96.6. & 104.1,2. Job 29.14.

*{with majesty}* Such state, as proud men use to set out themselves withal. For the word signifies pride also.

*{and excellency}* The word signifies height and pride, which often go together. Set out your self in the best manner, as men highly advanced use to do.

*{and array thyself}* Be covered all over with it as with a garment, let no rag of infamy be seen upon you. See on Chap. 39.19. on the word, *Clothed*.

*{with glory}* See on chap. 37.22. on the word *Majesty*.

*{and beauty}* So many words are used to the same purpose to set out the greatness of that state that he had need of, that would contend with God, that Job might the more be humbled, who was already brought so low.

**V. 11. ***{Cast abroad the rage of thy wrath, and behold every one that is proud, and abase him}* Let the enemies that are proud and lift up themselves against you, see what you can do in the height of your anger, that with a look only you can in a moment abate their pride, and abase their pomp and greatness. This must needs humble Job much, that was so much contemned by others, and could not help himself.

*{Cast abroad}* Scatter the effects of your anger in thunder, lightning, and whirlwinds, as I do.

*{the rage}* Heb. *The rages*. Show what you can do in your utmost anger. For the word see on Chap. 21.30. on the word, *Wrath*.

*{of thy wrath}* See on Chap. 4.9. & 5.13. & 14.13.

*{and behold}* Look angrily upon him. See on Chap. 19.27.

*{every one}* Or, *any one*; be he as great as may be. See on ch. 8.12.
