*{that is proud}* See on verse 10. on the word, *Majesty*.

*{and abase him}* Lay him as low as a valley, that looks as high as a mountain. That is the force of the Heb. word.

**V. 12. ***{Look on every one that is proud, and bring him low, and tread down the wicked in their place}* The same thing in effect with the former verse, to affect Job the more. Only it is enlarged by the overthrow not of proud men only, but of all ungodly men also. A work worthy of God, and too great for Job to do.

*{Look on every one that is proud}* See on the former ver.

*{and bring him low}* Cast him down from his greatness.

**V. 12. ***{and tread down}* Not only lay him low, but also tread upon him, which is a great disgrace, Josh. 10.24.

*{the wicked}* See on chap. 3.17. Bring down all troublesome persons and oppressors.

*{in their place}* Heb. *under them*. Bring them very low.

**V. 13. ***{Hide them in the dust together, and bind their faces in secret}* All that rebel against you, and all the ungodly of the earth, kill them, and bury them in the ground, that they may appear no more in this world to trouble you or others.

*{Hide them}* Lay them in the grave where they may not be seen.

*{in the dust}* In the bowels of the earth bury them, Psal. 22.15.

*{together}* Not at one time, or in one place, but alike, or in the same condition, that one may appear no more than another, chap. 3.18.

*{and bind their faces in secret}* Some take it to be a metaphor taken from men condemned to die, whose faces were covered, to show that they were not worthy to see the light. See on chap. 9.24. Esth. 7.8. But it is rather taken from dead men, whose faces use to be bound up and covered. Make them to lie, as prisoners and obscure persons in the grave, Prov. 10.6. Joh. 11.44. & 20.7. For face, see on chap. 14.20. on the word, *Countenance*.

**V. 14. ***{Then will I also confess unto thee, that thine own right hand can save thee}* If you can do all these things, then I will acknowledge, that you are strong enough to uphold your self, and have some color to contend with me. But if you cannot do them (as you know you cannot) then submit your self to me, and be content, that I should deal with you, as I think fit.

*{then}* When you have done all things mentioned, v. 9. &c.

*{also}* As you do the one so I will acknowledge the other. See on chap. 16.4. *Confesse unto thee*. Or, *Give praise unto thee*.

*{that thine own right hand}* See on chap. 23.9.

*{can save thee}* Can keep you out of all danger.

**V. 15. ***{Behold now Behemoth, which I made with thee, he eateth grasse as an ox}* That, you may learn hereafter to measure your self by the length of your own foot, and to keep your self within your bounds, behold I set before you two great beasts, the one on earth, the other in the sea, both my creatures that you may know by your disability to deal with them, that you are utterly unable to contend with me. The former is spoken of in this chap. the latter in the next.

*{Behold now}* God stirs up Job to consider well of this huge beast, as if he had said, if you do not yet understand how weak a man you are, and how unfit to grapple with me, see how you can deal with this great beast. For Behold, see on chap. 1.12.

*{Behemoth}* In Heb. *Beasts*. It is plural, as some conceive, to show that it is so great, that it is, as it were, compounded of many beasts, or in strength is equal to many. Others use the singular before it, and read, *The beast of beasts*. That is, the principal beast; as wisdoms, Prov. 9.1. That is, the wisdom of wisdoms, or chief wisdom. Like the title of the Canticles, The song of songs, that is, the most excellent song. To find out this beast what it is, this may be taken for a ground, that God having showed his power in divers ordinary creatures before, now would sum up all in two of the greatest, which are the elephant, and the whale. The elephant is an high beast, and being but one, may stand for many to set out God's power, &c. It is the greatest beast on earth. He is called, the chief of God's ways, v. 19. Some would have it understood of beasts in general, but that cannot be. 1. Because he spoke of particular beasts and birds before. 2. Because the Leviathan is a particular fish spoken of in the next chapter. 3. Because here is a particular description of this beast, many parts whereof will not agree with many other beasts. It being then a particular beast, it is most likely to be the elephant. 1. Because, that is the greatest beast. 2. Because all the parts of the following description agree well with it. And because the elephant is not named any where in Scripture, therefore he gives unto him the common name of beasts by way of excellency, as Christ is called the Son of man. This great beast is described. 1. By his making and preserving cause in this v. 2. By his parts, as his loins, and navel, v. 16. 3. By his tail and stones, v. 17. 4. By his bones, v. 18. 5. By the power God has over him, v. 19. 6. By the place of his feeding, v. 20. 7. Of his abiding, v. 21,22. 8. By his huge swallow, v. 23. 9. By his fearlessness of being taken, v. 24. For the word, see on chap. 18.3.

*{which I made with thee}* Though he be so great, yet he is my creature, as well as you. Or, I made him the same day with man. Or, to live with man on earth, not with fishes in the sea, as the Leviathan does. Or, to excel other creatures, as you do. For the elephant is said to come nearest to man in his senses. So God is said to advance (Heb. to make) Moses and Aaron, that is to make them more excellent than others, 1 Sam. 12.6. Others, *whom I have made to serve you*. For elephants are used in war: But it is a great question, whether so anciently men knew how to tame them more, than how to take whales. And God to convince Job instances for the most part in creatures not tamed. For the word, see on chap. 14.9. upon the word, *Bring-forth*.

*{he eateth grasse like an ox}* Though he be so huge a beast, yet is he not ravenous. He feeds not upon other cattle, as lions and wolves do, but on grass, as do the oxen, that labor for man. If he should feed on flesh, so great a beast must needs destroy a multitude of lesser. For eateth, see on chap. 21.25.

**V. 16. ***{Lo now his strength is in his loins, and his force is in the navel of his belly}* He now begins to set out the great strength of the elephant in this v. in his loins and in his navel, which as they are greater, so stronger than in other creatures.

*{Lo now}* As in the former ver. he had called on Job to view this great beast in general, so he repeats the note of admiration here to draw him down to the consideration of the particulars, wherein the great strength of this great beast consists: For Lo, see on chap. 1.12. on the word, *Behold*.

*{his strength is in his loins}* These are counted the seat of strength in living creatures, and therefore are girded up, when men go to war or labor, Psal. 69.23. Nah. 2.1. Eph. 6.14. Prov. 31.17.

*{and his force is in the navel of his belly}* The navel is the center of the body, where the principal channels of the veins and arteries meet, and from whence they pass into several parts of the body, and therefore it is esteemed another seat of strength. Heb. *Navels*, in the plural number; it may be because of the several folds of it, or parts that meet there. For the word Belly, see on chap. 15.2.

**V. 17. ***{He moveth his tail like a cedar: the sinews of his stones are wrapped together}* Some understand this of the elephant's moving of his tail which way he will. Others of his yard, when he goes to generation, because mention is made of his stones afterwards. But neither of these are like a cedar, but are observed by the naturalists to be lesser, than answers to the proportion of so great a beast. Beza reads it thus, *He moveth his prominent part, which is as the cedar, the sinews of the terrible thereof are wrapped together*. That is, though his snout be very great, yet he turns it about hither and thither, as he please: which is wholly compact of sinews, wrapped, and as it were twisted together, able to terrify any man, seem he never so stout and courageous.

*{He moveth}* He turns it, which way he please: for the original word has something of will and pleasure in it. Or, *He setteth up*. He makes it to stand straight or upright.

*{his tail}* It being small, it is more likely to be meant of his snout, which is like a tail, and the tail sometimes is used for the end of a thing, as Isa. 7.4. Neither is it likely so great a member, and useful as the trunk would be omitted, and so small an one, and unprofitable, as the tail mentioned.

*{like a cedar}* As the cedar is moved and shaken with the wind, Or, *which is as a cedar*, great and high. The word ארז signifies, 1. A cedar tree, 1 King. 4.33. 2. Board of cedar, 1 King. 6.18. 3. Great men, which are higher than others, as cedars are taller than other trees, Zeph. 11.2. Here it is taken in the first sense.

*{the sinews of his stones are wrapped together}* They hang not down as in other creatures, but are hidden in, or carried close to the belly by nerves and sinews. As the tail, or yard, or snout, is compared to the cedar, so the sinews are compared to branches platted one within another. For sinews, see on chap. 10.11. The word for stones is not so used elsewhere, but for Fears, and may be translated, *his terrible sinews*.

**V. 18. ***{His bones are as strong pieces of brass, his bones are like bars of iron}* He further sets out the strength of this beast by the solidness of his bones, which are harder than the bones of other creatures; and therefore compared to the hardest brass and iron. Some think it to be an intimation, that he cannot bow like other creatures.

*{his bones}* See on chap. 20.11.

*{are as strong pieces}* Or, *as conduit pipes of brasse*. See on chap. 6.12.

*{his bones}* A different word in the original, yet the thing is the same. His bones I say, are harder than brass, they are like iron. Some to make a difference understand by one word the hollow ones like brass pipes, and by the other, the solid ones like iron bars. Others make it a distinction between greater bones, and lesser. Others between higher and lower, those in the body, and those in the legs. Others between the teeth and other bones in the body.

*{are like bars of iron}* Very strong, and very hard. The word Bar in the original may come from a word, that signifies to cast, or throw. Those iron bars, which men sling to show their strength, are hard and heavy. So are the elephant's bones. For Iron, see on chap. 28.2.

**V. 19. ***{He is the chief of the ways of God: he that made him, can make his sword to approach unto him}* This creature is the greatest and strongest of all the beasts on earth, that God made, and accordingly God has armed him with an excellent sword, to wit, his trunk, wherewith he defends himself, and destroys his enemies.

*{He is the chief}* Some understand it of a beginning in time, as the word is used, Gen. 10.10. They conceive that God made the elephant before any other beast. But that being uncertain, it may better be taken for the first in dignity, that is, the chief, for the elephant excels other beasts in strength, bigness, and docibleness. So the word is used, 1 Sam. 15.15,21.

*{of the ways of God}* Of the works of the Lord, or of the creatures which he has made. For God, see on chap. 8.3,5.

*{he that made him, can make his sword to approach unto him}* Some understand it thus, None can kill him but God. But there are ways now found to kill elephants. Others thus, None dare fight with him: but such as do kill them do it by sleight and cunning. Others thus, God has appointed the rhinoceros to be his sword, which uses to smite the elephant in the belly, and so to kill him. But it may be read thus, *He that made him, made his sword to be near him*. That is, God has given him a trunk ready at hand to fight for himself, which is as a sword to him. For made see on chap. 14.9. on the word, *Bring forth*. For Sword, see on chap. 27.14.

**V. 20. ***{Surely the mountains bring him forth food, where all the beasts of the field play}* Two things are set out in this verse. The one, that though the elephant be a great beast, and need much food, yet God makes the mountains to afford him plenty without man's help. The other is, that he is so mild of nature, that he lets the lesser beasts sport by him, and yet devours them not, as lions and wolves do. He has a sword, yet God curbs him, that he uses it not to destroy other beasts.

*{Surely}* So this particle is translated, chap. 28.1. The interpreters translate it so, because it gives no reason of what went before. Yet it may be read with reference to what follows, *Because the mountains bring him forth food, therefore all the beasts of the field play there*. So it yields a reason of the security of the beasts, because God has provided the elephant other food. So כי is translated Therefore, chap. 35.16. Or, *Surely the mountains bring forth food, though all the beasts of the field play there*. And then it is a proof of God's abundant provision there in those desert places, to satisfy the huge elephant, and a multitude of other beasts.

*{the mountains bring him forth food}* See the like provision for the wild ass, chap. 39.8. and for other beasts, Psal. 104.14. & 147.8. For Bring forth, see on chap. 21.3. on the word, *Suffer*. *Him*. For him to eat. *Food*; That which grows naturally out of the earth, as grass and such like.

*{where}* Heb. *and there*.

*{all}* Or, *Any*. Any beast may sport by him, he hurts none. So this word is translated, chap. 8.12.

*{the beasts}* Heb. *Every beast*. Yet the verb following is plural, to show, that any beast, though never so small, may be secure there. For the word, see on chap. 37.8.

*{of the field}* The wild beasts. So it is rendered, chap. 39.15.

*{play}* Not only feed quietly without danger, but abide there with delight, notwithstanding the great strength of the elephant. For the word, see on chap. 29.24. on the word, *Laughed*.

**V. 21. ***{He lieth under the shady trees, in the covert of the reed and fens}* Though the elephant feed on the mountains, yet being an hot creature, and living in hot southerly countries, he often comes down into the valleys near rivers, where are great trees, reeds, and fens to shelter him from the scorching beams of the sun, and water plenty to wash, and cool him in, and drink his fill.

*{he lieth}* Or rather, *Sleepeth*. For the elephant uses not to lie down, because he knows not how to get up again, but leans against a great tree when he sleeps, as the naturalists write. For the word, see on chap. 30.17. on the words, *Take no rest*.

*{under the shady trees}* Where the sun may not annoy him.

*{in the covert of the reed and fens}* In close places, and moist. For Covert, see on chap. 22.14. on the word, *Coverings*; and on chap. 24.15. on the word, *Disguiseth*. For Reed, see on chap. 31.22. on the word, *Bone*.

**V. 22. ***{The shady trees cover him with their shadow, the willows of the brook compass him about}* It seems there are great trees in those parts, that may serve to be a shadow to so great a creature as an elephant.

*{the shady trees}* See on ver. 21.

*{cover him with their shadow}* Heb. *With his shadow*. That is, Each tree can shade an elephant, they are so great. Or, *which are his shadow*.

*{the willows of the brooke}* That grow by the river's side, Psal. 1.3. The word signifies valleys also. See on chap. 30.6.

*{compass him about}* As the high trees hide him overhead from the heat of the sun, so the willows compass him about underneath to shelter him from the wind and cold.

**V. 23. ***{Behold he drinketh up a river, and hasteth not; he trusteth that he can draw up Jordan into his mouth}* An hyperbolical speech, to express the greatness of the elephant's draught proportionable to his bigness. He drinks as greedily when he comes to the water, as if he would swallow up the whole river. Yet he drinks when he will, and as much as he will, and fears not that any should disturb him, as the dogs do that drink in Nilus, and haste away lest a crocodile should hurt him. And though Jordan be a very great river, yet he is so dry and so great, that he conceives that he could, and enters in as boldly as if he would, drink it up at a draught.

*{Behold}* See on chap. 1.12.

*{he drinketh up a river}* He drinks very much at a time. Heb. *He oppresseth, or, robbeth a river*. He takes away the water of the river violently by drinking, as thieves take men's goods away by stealing.

*{and hasteth not}* Drinks much but takes his own leisure. Fears not any disturbance. The word signifies to haste away for fear. Others read; *He hindereth the river that it hasteth not*. His body is so big, that it stops the course of the stream.

*{he trusteth}* He thinks he can do it, because he is so great and strong.

*{that he can draw up Jordan into his mouth}* That he can swallow the whole river at one draught. He can take it by force or violence. For Draw up, see on chap. 38.8. on the word *Brake forth*. Jordan was a great river, not far off, and well known in those parts, so called, as some conceive, because it came out of the tribe of Dan. And others conceive that there is an allusion to the Dead Sea, into which Jordan falls, yet it swells not. For mouth, see on chap. 15.5.

**V. 24. ***{He taketh it with his eyes; his nose pierceth through snares}* He looks upon the river as if he would drink it all up, and puts in his trunk so violently, that he breaks through snares set for him in the water. Others read thus, *Will any take him in his sight? or bore his nose with gins?* Intimating that none dare to come to take him openly by force, or to lead him away by the nose. This agrees well with what is spoken of the Leviathan, chap. 41.1,2.

*{he taketh it}* See on chap. 15.12. on the word, *Carry-away*.

*{with his eyes}* See on chap. 15.15. on the word, *Sight*.

*{his nose}* See on chap. 4.9. & 9.5,13. & 14.13. on the words, *Nostrils, Anger, Wrath*.

*{pierceth through snares}* Escapes them. For the word, Snares, see on chap. 34.30. on the word, *Ensnared*.

## CHAPTER 41

**Ver. 1. ***{Canst thou draw out Leviathan with an hook? or his tongue with a cord, which thou lettest down?}* The Lord passes now from the land to the sea, and sets out his power in the great Leviathan, which is far greater than the elephant. This is ordinarily conceived to be the whale; but reverend Beza opposes it, and would have it to be the crocodile. 1. Because some things spoken in the description of this fish agree not to the whale. The contrary will appear in the explication of particulars. 2. That whales were unknown in Idumæa, as being far off in the wide sea, and never coming into the Arabian Gulf. To which it is easy to answer, that good authors report that great whales have been seen in the Arabian gulf, and that the parts near did daily use divers parts of the whale. Neither was the Mediterranean sea far off, where whales were. And many things are heard of, that are never seen in some parts of the world. It may be elephants were not ordinary in Idumæa neither, yet God instances in them. Now that it should be the whale, and not the crocodile, appears. 1. Because as God did instance in the greatest beast, to show his power, so it is suitable to instance in the greatest fish. 2. Because this fish is in the sea, and not in the river Nilus, v. 31,32,33. But the crocodile is in the river, and in the daytime often on the land: 3. The whale resists men, and cannot be tamed. ver. 25. But the crocodile flies, and yields to be trod on. 4. This fish can lie on sharp stones, v. 30. But the crocodile cannot. 5. This fish has a tongue, v. 1. The crocodile has none. See Cocceius on this ver. God speaks of the Leviathan here, as of a little fish, that might be taken with a hook, or played withal by his power, though he be terrible to men. The sum of this verse is, That you O Job may see your presumption in pleading with me; look upon the whale, and see if you be able to deal with it by force. If not, how can you contend with me, that made that great creature, and can destroy it when I will?

*{Canst thou draw out Leviathan with an hook?}* Smaller fishes may be taken with a hook, but the whale will break all hooks. It is translated by way of interrogation, though there be no interrogative sign in the Heb. For we find the next ver. interrogative; And it is not true otherwise; for the whale cannot be taken with a hook. For the future English, *Can*. See chap. 8.11. *Draw out*. Of the sea, as lesser fishes are by an angle drawn out of the river. *Leviathan*. The whale, as before. It has his name from addition. Either because of the greatness of the members, or because greater than any other living creature. See for this word, on chap. 3.8. on, Their mourning.

*{or his tongue}* Rather, *Or by his tongue*. Can you make your hook lay hold upon his tongue, and so draw him to land? For it is not meant of drawing his tongue out of his mouth. For the word, see on chap. 15.5. By is added, Isa. 51.19.

*{with a cord}* That is, with a hook hanging on a cord, which sometimes lays hold on the nose, sometimes on the tongue, and sometimes on other parts of the fish. The whale's tongue being so great, a hook might quickly lay hold on it, yet not draw out the whale. For the word, see on chap. 18.10. on the word, *Snare*.

*{which thou lettest down}* Into the waters to catch fish. Heb. *which thou drownest*.

**V. 2. ***{Canst thou put an hook into his nose? or bore his jaw through with a thorne?}* Some take this verse to be an exposition of the former, and to intend the taking of the fish. But it may rather be an allusion to the custom of fishermen, who when they have taken fishes with an angle, use to hang them on a twig or bulrush, and so carry them away. So could not Job do with a whale, if he had taken him.

*{Canst thou put an hook into his nose}* Either to take him, or to carry him home when you have taken him. The Hebrew word for an hook אגמון 1. signifies a pond, or standing water, Psal. 114. 8. 2. A bulrush, because they grow by ponds, Isa. 58. 5. So it may be here, for it is not unlikely they had strong bulrushes which might serve to carry fish, home on. Others take it metaphorically for an hook to take fish, which is crooked like a bulrush bowing down. For nose, see on chap. 4. 9. & 5. 5, 13. & 14. 13. on the words, *nostrils, anger, wrath*.

*{or bore his jaw through with a thorne}* Make an hole in it to put a twig of thorn through to carry it home being taken. Others understand it of fishing with a thorn, or an hook sharp like a thorn.

**V. 3. ***{Will he make many supplications unto thee? Will he speak soft words unto thee?}* Will he like a prisoner taken in war, entreat you not to hurt or kill him, or pray you to set him at liberty, or speak gentle words to you, as Balaam's ass did to him, Numb. 22. 30. No, his stomach is too great he will resist to death.

*{Will he make many supplications unto thee}* Will he often and earnestly entreat you to spare him, as the Gibeonites did Joshua, Josh. 9. 9. &c. Spoken of beasts after the manner of men. See the like, chap. 39. 18.

*{Will he speak soft words unto thee}* Will he flatter, or humor you that he may get loose again. For speak, see on chap. 13. 22.

**V. 4. ***{Will he make a covenant with thee? wilt thou take him for a servant for ever?}* If by gentle words he cannot get loose from you, will he ever be brought to submit to you as a slave, to serve you all his life long? His stomach is too great to stoop so low.

*{Will he make a covenant with thee}* Heb. *Will he cut a covenant with thee?* For the sense of these words, see on chap. 31. 1.

*{wilt thou take him for a servant for ever}* Have you power to keep him in perpetual subjection, or make him do your drudgery so long as he lives? Or, *That thou shouldst take him for a servant for ever.* His life is his ever, he has no being after death. For take, see on chap. 15. 12. on the word, *carry away*. The word, forever, comes from a word that signifies to be hidden. For whether it be taken for eternity, or for a long time: no man knows when it will end.

**V. 5. ***{Wilt thou play with him as with a bird? or wilt thou bind him for thy maidens?}* If you cannot bring down his stubborn heart to do your work, can you make sport with him as a little bird? Or, can you tie him with a string for your little girls to play withal, as you do young birds? So great a fish is not so tractable.

*{Wilt thou play with him as with a bird}* He means playing with him not as with a companion, but as making him an object of their sport, as the Philistines did Samson, Judg. 16. 25. For play, see on chap. 29. 24. on the word, *laughed*. The word for bird, signifies any small bird, but more especially a sparrow, which is most conversant with men, and fittest to make sport withal. So it is translated, Psal. 84. 3.

*{or wilt thou bind him for thy maidens}* Will you tie him in a string that your maidservants may make sport with him for themselves, or for your little daughters, who take great delight in little birds so tied, that they may not escape till they be satisfied. *And* for *or*, see Exod. 21. 15, 17. For bind, see on chap. 38. 31.

**V. 6. ***{Shall thy companions make a banquet of him, shall they part him among the merchants?}* Fishermen use to go out in companies, especially for taking greater fishes. But it seems the taking of the whale, which now is very perilous, was not then known. For neither could the fishermen make a feast with the whale among themselves, nor sell him to the merchants, as now, to divide among them for their profit, as they did with other fishes, because they knew not how to catch or kill them.

*{Shall thy companions}* Those that join together to take the whale.

*{make a banquet of him}* Or, *for him*, Can they catch him, that they may feed on him, or make merry because they have taken him? One verb included in another, see on chap. 15. 8. & 16. 11. & 18. 8. The word for making a banquet signifies also to dig, as chap. 6. 27. But that signification fits not here.

*{shall they part him}* Shall they sell him to divers merchants that they may divide him among themselves?

*{among the Merchants}* The same word signifies Canaanites also. It seems they were great merchants, and well might they be so, living near the Mediterranean Sea. Neither could their little land maintain so many hundred thousands without the help of the sea.

**V. 7. ***{Canst thou fill his skin with barbed irons, or his head with fish spears?}* Can you find any way to take the whale? If not by an angle, can you pierce him with sharp irons, or take him with an eel spear? It seems they knew not any way then to take the whale.

*{Canst thou fill}* It must be many iron darts cast upon him that must kill him, he must be pierced in many places.

*{his skin}* It seems that is so hard, that iron will scarce pierce it.

*{with barbed irons}* Heb. *with thornes.* That is, with iron darts sharp like thorns.

*{or his head}* It is not one fish spear can do it, it must be many, more than you can come to strike into his head. For the word, see on chap. 19. 9.

*{with fish-hooks}* An instrument wherewith they smite fish in the water, to catch, or kill them, like our eel spears.

**V. 8. ***{Lay thine hand upon him, remember the battle, do no more.}* If when you cannot take him by sleight, or any of the former ways whereby fish are taken, you will adventure to do it by force, you will soon be weary of that combat, and glad to shift for yourself by flight, and not dare to adventure on him any more. Others understand it of laying hold on him when he is sleeping near some rock, yet he cannot so be taken, v. 9. Neither dare any awake him, verse 10.

*{Lay thine hand upon him}* Use violence to take him. For hand, see on chap. 29. 9.

*{remember the battle}* You will have cause to think all your life long what fierce encounter you have had, and how hardly you have escaped.

*{do no more}* You will have no maw to meddle with him any more.

**V. 9. ***{Behold the hope of him is in vain, shall not one be cast down even at the sight of him?}* If none of these means can take him, then there is no hope of it, for if a man do but look at such a terrible creature, it is enough to make him faint, and fall flat on his face for fear.

*{Behold}* See on chap. 1. 12.

*{the hope of him}* The expectation of him that would take the whale. In ordinary speech it should have been your hope, for he spoke to Job before. But the person is changed, to show, that not only Job, but no man else, though much stronger than he, could take the whale.

*{is in vain}* Heb. *is lying.* It will deceive him if he make account to take this great fish. For the word, see on chap. 24. 25.

*{shall not one be cast down even at the sight of him}* How little hope a man may have to take him his terrible countenance shows, which will make him give over for very fear. Others read it thus, *will he be cast down even at the sight of him?* It may be you think that though you cannot take him none of the former ways, yet he will lie down for fear as soon as he sees you, and then you may do with him what you will, but you are much deceived. Others thus, *if his hope be in vaine, will he be cast down even at the sight of him?* That is, if the whale be so wearied that he have no hope to escape, yet he will never yield, but stand it out to the death. For even, see on chap. 18. 5. on the word, *yea*. For not omitted in a question, see on chap. 20. 4.

**V. 10. ***{None is so fierce that dare stir him up, who then is able to stand before me?}* In this verse God fitly applies to the present disputation, what he had said before of this great fish. You would wonder to see the stoutest of men dare to awake the whale when he is asleep, or provoke him to fight with him when he is awake. How then can you O Job, or any man else contend with me, who gave him that great strength, and have much more strength myself.

*{None is so fierce}* Or, *bold.* Heb. *cruell.* For cruelty makes men fierce.

*{that dare stir him up}* Heb. *That he should dare to stir him up.* He will soon forget his fierceness, and be daunted at the sight of so great an adversary, if he provoke the whale to fight with him. This is the battle mentioned, verse 8. The particle כי ordinarily signifies for, or because, but sometimes that, as here, and chap. 36. 10, 24. For stir up, see on chap. 31. 29.

*{who then is able to stand before me?}* If I will ruin any man, how can he help it, that cannot stand before a whale? Heb. *and who is he that can stand before me?* So is translated then, and infers a conclusion upon a former reason, 2 King. 18. 24. For stand, see on chap. 33. 5. on the word, *set in order*.

**V. 11. ***{Who hath prevented me that I should repay him? Whatsoever is under the whole Heaven is mine}* Some include the latter part of the former verse, and all this verse, in a parenthesis, because at the 12. verse God goes on again to speak of the Leviathan. The sense is, who did me a good turn first, that I should be forced to requite it? Yea I am so far from receiving courtesies, that I have given being, and all things they have to all creatures in the world, and therefore may dispose of them as I will, and who can then have any color to plead against me that I do him wrong?

*{Who hath prevented me}* Who did me a good office before I did him any? Or who has directed me how to govern the world, Rom. 11. 34, 35. I can do no man wrong, because I am obliged to none, but all are engaged to me for all they have. For prevented, see on chap. 30. 27. For repay, see on chap. 34. 11. on the word, *render*.

*{Whatsoever is under the whole Heaven is mine}* The air, sea and earth, and all that is in them belongs to God, so does the heaven also: But he mentions things below, because he had to do with one on earth. See Exod. 19. 5. Deut. 10. 14. Psal. 24. 1. & 50. 12. 1 Cor. 10. 26, 28. For heaven, see on chap. 9. 8. & 11. 8.

**V. 12. ***{I will not conceal his parts, nor his power, nor his comely proportion}* A transition from the general description of him to the particulars of his members in the following verses, which contain as it were a picture of this terrible beast. That you O Job may know what little hope there is to overcome this great fish, and may see my great power in making so huge a creature, I will give you a special character of him.

*{I will not conceal}* I will speak largely of it. A meiosis. See the like, chap. 14. 11. & 15. 18.

*{his parts}* His several and large members, wherein his strength consists. The word signifies boughs of trees, and so members, which are to the body, as boughs to the tree. It signifies also door bars, which are made of boughs of trees. See more on chap. 18. 12. on the word, *strength*.

*{nor his power}* Heb. *The matter*, or, *The word of his power.* I will speak of his great strength, as well as of his great members, for great members without strength are but burdens to the body. The word strength is plural in the original, either to set out the greatness of his strength, or to intimate that he is strong in every member.

*{nor his comely proportion}* Heb. *nor the grace of his disposition.* That is, the lovely proportion of his parts, that are not some great, some little, but all suitable to one another. Others understand it of his mustering of his members in battle array, when men come to take him; for the word signifies sometimes setting an army of soldiers in battle array, that they may be ready for to fight. But the former interpretation agrees better with the particular description following.

**V. 13. ***{Who can discover the face of his garment, or who can come to him with his double bridle?}* No man dares approach to this terrible fish as to an horse in the stable, to take off his cloth, and bring a bridle to put into his mouth, that he might ride on him. No man can draw him out of the sea, which is as a garment to cover him. Others understand it of flaying off the skin of the whale when he is taken, but that agrees not well with the following, verse.

*{Who can discover the face of his garment?}* Who can take that off from him that is seen as a garment about him and hides a great part of him? That is the sea. Who can take that shelter from him? For face, see on chap. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*.

*{or who can come to him with his double bridle}* Or, *within.* Who dare come nigh him to bring him forth to any service? For come, see on chap. 14. 14.

**V. 14. ***{Who can open the doors of his face? His teeth are terrible round about}* He goes on with the similitude of an horse. If any man durst come near him, yet his jaws being like two great doors, and armed with huge teeth within, he would be afraid to open them to put in a bridle. All this shows how dangerous it is for a man to meddle with a whale. It is objected that whales have no teeth, therefore the whale cannot be meant here. It is answered. 1. That they have long things in their mouths like rods, with a kind of hair in the end, wherewith they feed on the froth of the sea. 2. That there are divers kinds of whales, some whereof have huge and terrible teeth, as good authors affirm. See Pineda on the place.

*{Who can open the doors of his face?}* Who can force him to gape that he may bridle him? For open, see on chap. 12. 18. on the word, *looseth*. For face, see on chap. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*.

*{His teeth are terrible round about}* They are all so large round about his mouth, that they make men afraid to look on them. For teeth, see on chap. 16. 9. For terrible, Heb. *terror*, see on chap. 9. 34. & 13. 21. on the words, *fear, and dread*.

**V. 15. ***{His scales are his pride, shut up together as with a close seal}* In this verse is set out the hardness of his scales, they are like so many brass bucklers, and the closeness of them, that nothing may come between to hurt him; which is set out by the similitude of a thing close sealed up. It is objected that whales have no scales. It is answered. 1. That some kinds have great and thick ones like shields. See Cocceius on the text. 2. That it may be read otherwise. *His height is the strength of shields shut up with a close seale.* That is, his back is covered with a thick skin, no more to be pierced than a shield. And if there be any divisions in it like scales, they are so close as if they were sealed together.

*{His scales}* The word is never so translated elsewhere. It is translated strong pieces, chap. 40. 18. And such lumps or flakes of flesh the whale may have with a thick covering. See v. 23. Heb. *His strong pieces of shields.* For shields, see on chap. 15. 26. on the word, *bucklers*.

*{are his pride}* He is so proud of them, that he thinks none can pierce through them to hurt him. Or, *His height.* That is, his honor. The word signifies both.

*{shut up together}* His scales are shut up together. Or the whale himself is safe, like one shut up.

*{as with a close seal}* As things fast sealed up, that cannot be parted. Heb. *A straight seal.*

**V. 16. ***{One is so near to another, that no air can come between them}* These scales or flakes are so close joined, and in so good order, that the searching wind that finds out all crannies, cannot enter between them, much less any weapon to hurt the whale.

*{One is so near to another}* Heb. *One comes so near to one.* That is each to other.

*{that no air can come between them}* For air see on, chap. 15. 2. on vain knowledge. For come, see on chap. 14. 14.

**V. 17. ***{They are joined one to another, they stick together that they cannot be sundered}* As no wind can come between these scales or flakes, so no force can separate them. Either they knew not then the late way of taking whales by wounding them in some tender parts, or else their whales had harder scales or skins than ours.

*{they are joined one to another}* Heb. *A man to his brother.* A common Scripture phrase in things that have no life nor sex, as well as in those that have. The same phrase is, Exod. 25. 20. For a man, see on chap. 1. 1. For brother, see on chap. 19. 13.

*{they stick together}* Like captives taken in war, and fettered one to another. So much the word imports.

*{that they cannot be sundered}* No force or art can make a separation between them.

**V. 18. ***{By his neesings a light doth shine, and his eyes are like the eyelids of the morning}* His neesing does cause much water to fly up into the air, which shining clear, looks as if the sun were rising out of the sea, and his eyes are very bright and shining, and that afar off in regard of their greatness, as the morning light quickly spreads itself over the earth.

*{by his neesings a light doth shine}* Like the light of the sun. See on chap. 17. 12. & 31. 26.

*{and his eyes}* See on chap. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*.

*{are like the eyelids of the morning}* See on chap. 3. 9. on dawning of the day. The words are the same in the original. The morning light coming suddenly affects men more than the noon light, which is far greater, but comes by degrees. This sets out the great brightness of the eyes of the whale.

**V. 19. ***{Out of his mouth go burning lamps, and sparks of fire leap out}* Here God goes on to set out the great heat that is within this great fish, as in a great kitchen, to digest his meat, breaking forth as it were in firebrands, and fiery flames, and sparkles cast forth in abundance by him as if he spit fire, v. 19, 20, 21. These things and many others agree not with the crocodile which Beza would have to be meant here.

*{out of his mouth}* See on chap. 15. 5.

*{go}* See on chap. 14. 20. on the word, *passeth*.

*{burning lamps}* See on chap. 12. 5.

*{and sparks of fire leap out}* For fire, see on chap. 15. 34. & 20. 26. They leap out, or send themselves out, as it were out of a prison. See on chap. 19. 20. on, escaped.

**V. 20. ***{Out of his nostrils goeth smoke, as out of a seething pot or caldron}* He sets out the great heat coming from the whale by another similitude taken from the smoke, that ascends out of some huge pot; or caldron, when it boils.

*{out of his nostrils goeth smoke}* Smoke is an usual companion of fire. How hot then is this fish within, when one chimney will not serve to vent the heat of it? It sends fire out of the mouth, and smoke out of the nose. For goeth, see on chap. 14. 2. on, cometh forth. The word עשן smoke is used. 1. Literally for a dark vapor arising from heat, Gen. 15. 17. 2. To set out a great, and devouring fire, Isa. 34. 10. For fire brings smoke. 3. A dark cloud like smoke, Isa. 4. 5. 4. A vain, empty, weak, helpless thing like smoke, Psal. 68. 2. 5. To set out trouble, for smoke is very troublesome to the eyes, Isa. 14. 31.

*{as out of a seething pot}* Heb. *As out of a pot blown.* For blowing makes the pot boil quickly, and fiercely, Jer. 1. 13. See on chap. 20. 26. where it is translated blown.

*{or a caldron}* It is supposed to be a great brazen kettle in which dyeing stuff to color cloth was boiled. This word אגמון everywhere in Scripture, save here, signifies a rush, or a bulrush which grows by ponds. And אגם which is near it in letters, signifies a pond. Some take this for a pond, that sometimes sends out vapors like smoke. Others for a great caldron like a pond, which when it boils does the like. So a great vessel used in the temple is called a sea, 2 Chron. 4. 2. See on v. 2. on the word *hook*.

**V. 21. ***{His breath kindleth coals, and a flame goeth out of his mouth}* Here the whale is compared to a great wind, or a pair of smiths' bellows, that blowing the coals raises a mighty flame. No such thing in the crocodile.

*{His breath}* Heb. *His soul.* See on chap. 14. 22. The soul is the cause of breath, and when it leaves the body, the breath fails.

*{kindleth coals}* Or, *Would kindle coales.* It is so hot, that it might do it, for there are no coals in the sea to kindle. The word signifies sometimes a live, or burning coal, and sometimes a dead one not kindled, as here. See Prov. 6. 28. Isa. 44. 19. 2 Sam. 22. 9. Psal. 18. 8.

*{and a flame goeth out of his mouth}* A bright beam like a flame of fire. For goeth out, see on chap. 14. 2. on the word *cometh forth*. For mouth, see on chap. 15. 5.

**V. 22. ***{In his neck remaineth strength, and sorrow is turned into joy before him}* His head and other parts are strongly joined together by his neck, and those swelling waves, or storms, that affright passengers are a pleasure to him.

*{In his neck}* In the conjunction of the head to the members as some would have it, that conceive, no fishes have necks; others think that whales, and dolphins, and great fishes, that breathe have necks, and strong ones too, which cannot easily be broken, like the necks of other living creatures, yet this proves not the crocodile to be here meant, for it has no more neck, than a whale. The strength of his neck is set out in this verse, of his flesh, verse 23. of his heart, verse 24.

*{remaineth strength}* Heb. *Lodgeth*, or, *abideth all night.* His strength makes him cheerful, and fearless, as appears by the opposition in the end of the verse.

*{and sorrow is turned into joy before him. Heb. And sorrow rejoiceth before him}* Some understand it of the unweariableness of the whale. Though he be in the sea in all storms and tempests, yet he is never out of heart, nor fearful, nor grieved with anything, that can befall him. Others understand it of his frightening all that come in his way, and putting them to grief: and read thus, *And before him danceth fear.* He makes such a combustion in the sea by stirring the waters before him, that none dare come near him.

**V. 23. ***{The flakes of his flesh are joined together, they are firm in themselves, they cannot be moved}* There is so firm a conjunction of all the parts of his body, and the flesh so hard, that he is like a molten pillar, no part whereof can be taken from another.

*{The flakes}* Heb. *The fallings*, All is fast and hard about him, even those parts that hang loose in other fishes. Or, *the refuse, or vilest parts*, as the word is translated, Amos 8. 6. Some take it for flakes of flesh distinguished by veins like branches of oak or ash.

*{of his flesh}* There is a kind of flesh in fish, distinct from the bones. Lev. 11. 11. & 1 Cor. 15. 39. For flesh see on chap. 14. 22.

*{are joined together}* Cleave fast one to another. See ver. 17.

*{they are firm in themselves}* Every one is firm, and so is their knitting together. Or, *He is firm in himself.* That is the whole whale is so. The word in the original is, molten. Bell metal, while it is casting is soft and loose, but when it is cast, is is hard and firm,

*{they cannot be moved}* One part cannot be taken from another. Or, *He cannot be moved.* The whale is so strong, that no man can make him give place, unless he will himself.

**V. 24. ***{His heart is as firm as a stone, yea as hard as a piece of the neither millstone}* Neither is his heart anything inferior to the might of his body, but, as strong, as any rock, and as, hard and unmovable to abide all brunts, as the nether millstone.

*{His heart}* See on chap. 15. 12.

*{is as firm}* As hard. See on ver. 23.

*{as a stone}* See on chap. 14. 19.

*{yea}* If any stone be harder than others, the whale's heart is as hard or firm as it. So this particle is translated chap. 14. 10.

*{as hard}* See on ver. 23.

*{as a piece of the neither millstone}* The nether millstone is the hardest, because the whole weight lies upon it.

**V. 25. ***{When he raiseth up himself, the mighty are afraid: by reason of breakings they purify themselves}* Having set out the terror of this great fish before, by the strength of his members, now he sets it out by his carriage. When he lifts up himself like some moving mountain in the waters, he strikes such terror into the stoutest men, whether mariners, or passengers, that by reason of the huge waves which he raises before him, they thinking death at hand, and that they shall either be devoured by him, or drowned in the sea, prepare themselves for death by confessing their sins, and craving pardon for them.

*{When he raiseth up himself}* When the whale begins to move on the top of the waters, Broughton reads it, *At his statelinesse.* For the word see on chap. 21. 3. on the word, *suffer*.

*{the mighty are afraid}* Not timorous people, or mean men only, but the strongest and greatest are struck into a great fright. For afraid see on ch. 19. 29. It signifies such a fear, as strangers have in a foreign country where they are exposed to many dangers, and have no succor. For the word signifies to be a pilgrim, and to fear.

*{by reason of breaking}* By reason of the waves, which are as cleavings of of the sea raised by the whale's motion. Broughton reads *of shivering.*

*{they purify themselves}* They cry to God as dying men do for mercy, and pardon of sins. So Jonah's mariners did in a tempest, John 1. 5. Others understand it of a bodily looseness brought on them for fear. Others of wandering up and down like men distracted that know not what they do. Psal. 107, 27, 28.

**V. 26. ***{The sword of him that layeth at him, cannot hold; the spear, the dart, nor the habergeon}* Here it seems also, that the way of taking the whale was not then known. Which is showed by the unprofitableness of some several weapons, that might be used to this end, in this verse &c. to verse 30. If any man dare be so bold as to come near him to smite him with a sword, it it is to no purpose; for it will break before it can wound the whale. And if he be afraid to come near him, and cast spears, darts, or javelins at him afar off, neither will they pierce or wound him,

*{The sword}* See on chap. 27. 14.

*{of him that layeth at him}* That strikes at him to wound or kill him.

*{cannot hold}* Heb. *will not stand*, or, *abide*, or, *rise.* It will be broken, and not be taken off whole as it was before. See on chap. 14. 12. on the word, *riseth*.

*{the spear}* It comes from a word that signifies to pitch a tent, because they were weapons used in war, and as some write, pitched round about their tents instead of a trench, for defense. However it is an offensive weapon. See on chap. 39. 33.

*{the dart}* A weapon used afar off to cast at the enemy. Some would have it to be a sling, but that is mentioned verse 28. Others join it to the former, and read, *The spear that goes forth*.

*{nor the habergeon}* Or, *Breastplate.* As his offensive weapons will not hurt the whale, so his defensive ones will not safeguard him. Yet it may well be an offensive weapon, as a javelin, for such are mentioned before and after it.

**V. 27. ***{He esteemeth Iron as straw, and Brass as rotten wood}* He makes as little account of, and is as little hurt with weapons of iron or brass, as if one cast a straw, or a piece of rotten wood at him.

*{he esteemeth}* He slights it, and fears no hurt from it, as if he had reason, and knew it could not wound him.

*{iron}* See on chap. 28. 2.

*{as straw}* Heb. *For straw.* An hurtless thing.

*{and brass}* See on Chap. 6. 12.

*{as rotten wood}* Heb. *For rotten wood.* For wood see on chap. 14. 7. on the word, *tree*. For rotten on Chap. 13. 28.

**V. 28. ***{The arrow cannot make him flee: sling stones are turned with him into stubble}* Before guns were invented, arrows, and slings were much in use, and went together in the battle. But the whale feared neither of both, no more than if men had flung stubble at him.

*{the arrow}* Heb. *The son of the bow*. Arrows are shot out of bows, as children come out of their parents. They are called sons of the quiver. Lam. 3. 13.

*{cannot make him flee}* He is not so afraid as to run away from it.

*{sling stones}* Such as are flung out of a sling. For stones see on Chap. 14. 19.

*{are turned with him into stubble}* He blows them away, as one would blow stubble away,

*{Darts are counted as stubble; he laugheth at the shaking of a spear}* He adds here other warlike weapons, peradventure unknown to us, to show, that then they could not take the whale with any weapons.

**V. 26. ***{Darts}* A dart was mentioned before, as also a spear, ver. 26. But as they were other words in the original, so very likely they were other weapons unknown to us, and therefore want names in our language,

*{are counted}* See on ver. 27. on the word, *esteemeth*.

*{as stubble}* See on ver. 28.

*{he laugheth at the shaking of a spear}* If one should shake a spear at him to scare him, he would regard it no more, than we do things, we laugh at. See on chap. 29. 22. on the word, *mocketh*.

*{the shaking}* See on chap. 39. 24. on the word, fierceness,

*{of a spear}* See on chap. 39. 23. on the word, *shield*.

**V. 30. ***{Sharp stones are under him: he spreadeth sharp pointed things upon the mire}* His skin is so hard, that he can rest himself on the sharp tops of rocks in the sea, and not hurt himself no more than if he lay on the soft mire.

*{sharp stones are under him}* Heb. *Sharp pieces of the potsherd.* That is, things as sharp, and pricking as the pieces of a broken potsherd, yet he feels them not.

*{He spreadeth sharp pointed things upon the mire}* The whale is here compared to some hardy man scorning to lie soft on the mire, but laying as it were sharp stones upon it, to show what he can endure without hurt. The word חרוץ in the original for sharp pointed things signifies, 1. Decision. Joel 3. 14. 2. A thing broken. Lev. 22. 22. 3. Gold. Prov. 3. 14. 4. An harrow. 2 Sam. 12. 31. 5. A wall, or breach, or ditch. Dan. 9. 25. 6. A sharp piercing thing like an harrow, as here others understand it of stones and darts cast at him to kill him, which are so far from hurting him, that he lies down upon them, as on soft mire, without any hurt, and so shows all their weapons and labor to be in vain.

**V. 31. ***{He maketh the deep to boil like a pot: he maketh the sea like a pot of ointment}* And what is all this to the crocodile who comes not in the sea, or what fish can do all this, but an huge whale? The Lord now sets out the great strength of this fish by the stirring of the sea from top to bottom by his wallowing in it, as if it were too narrow for him, and by casting up the waters in abundance, and making such a foam, as an apothecary does when he boils his ointments.

*{He maketh the the deep to boyle like a pot}* As the water in the pot set over the fire rises up so does the sea by the blustering of the whale. By the deep is meant the sea, which is the deepest of all waters. Chap. 28. 14. Exod. 15. 5. For boil, see on chap. 30. 27.

*{he maketh the sea like a pot of ointment}* As the apothecary putting many spices in the pot, raises a great scum on the top of the water, so does the whale in the sea. For sea, see on chap. 14. 11.

**V. 32. ***{He maketh a path to shine after him; one would think the deep to be hoary}* As he troubles the sea before him, so he leaves a bright path behind him, that men may see where he went. He makes it so white, that it looks like an old man's head, whose hair is turned all white. How powerful is this creature, that raises such stirs before and behind him in the great sea?

*{He maketh a path to shine after him}* By swimming in the sea, and violent moving of the waters, he leaves a white foam behind him, like a path wherein men walk. So do ships also, and much more whales, which are far bigger. For path see on chap. 18. 10. on the word, *way*.

*{one would think the deep to be hoary}* It seems this path is very wide according to the greatness of the whale, and therefore God speaks as if the whole sea were made white by him. For thinking see on verse 27. on the word, *esteemeth*. So a man would think that sees the whale past. Some read it, *he thinks.* The whale turning about, and looking behind him, thinks he has made all the sea white. So verse 27. He esteemeth,

*{the deep}* The sea. See on verse 31.

*{to be hoary}* To be all white, and looking old, that looked green and youthful before, as if the sea were so scared, that the color were suddenly altered.

**V. 33. ***{Upon earth there is not his like, who is made without fear}* Here is the conclusion of all concerning the whale in this verse and the next. In this verse God sets out the whale, and prefers him before other creatures for greatness and strength. And not only before all the fishes in the sea, where he has command without controversy; but also before all beasts on earth the stoutest of which are more fearful, and easier to be subdued, than he.

*{upon earth}* Heb. *upon the dust.* See on chap. 14. 8. on the word, *ground*.

*{There is not his like}* Heb. *There is no comparison of him.*

*{who is made without fear}* Or, *Who behave themselves without fear.* There is not any one creature on earth so fearless. He fears none, and all are afraid of him. For made see on chap. 14. 9. on the word *bring forth*. Broughton reads it thus, *His like are not upon the land which do deal without fear.* No creature carries itself so stately, or stoutly, as the whale. Cocceius thus, *his dominion is not over the earth, who is made without fear*, That is, God has shut the whale into the sea, Chap. 7. 12. For had he set him on the land, and given him members like the wild beasts, he would have destroyed all living creatures.

**V. 34. ***{He beholdeth all high things: he is a King over all the children of pride}* He does with a kind of contempt look down upon the tallest and greatest creatures, as far below him. And no marvel, for as he is greater, so is he prouder than the proudest of them.

*{he beholdeth}* He sees it as it were far below him, he despises it, and contemns it, and looks upon it without fear. For looking on one with contempt, see Canticles, 1. 6. For the word see on ch. 19. 12.

*{all high things}* Or, *Any high thing be it never so high*. The word is translated excellency. chap. 40. 10. The meaning is, The highest and tallest of the living creatures.

*{He is a king over all the children of pride}* He exceeds the proudest creatures in greatness and strength, and carries himself as stately, as if they were all at his command. For children of pride see on ch. 28. 8. though there it be meant of the young lions, which are proud creatures, here it is meant over all the wild creatures, which are proud, and insult over the tame ones, which are weaker than they. Cocceius reads it thus, *He that beholdeth all high things, is king over all the children of pride.* Though the whale be the greatest creature yet some are so proud, they will not stoop to him, but he must stoop to God, and so must you O Job. And this Job does in the beginning of the next chap.

## CHAPTER 42

**Verse 1. ***{Then Job answered the LORD and}* This chapter contains the conclusion of the whole business wherein is set down, 1. Job's repentance to the seventh ver. 2. God's sentence upon his three friends to the tenth v. 3. Job's restoration to the end of the chapter. For the 1. This is a more full profession of repentance than that in the beginning of chap. 40. See there on ver. 4. Note 1. The writer's preface. v. 1. 2. Job's confession of God's great power and wisdom. v. 2. 3. A confession of his own rash speeches concerning God's proceedings. v. 3. 4. His desire to be further instructed by God v. 4. 5. A thankful acknowledgment of God's further revealing himself to him. v. 5. 6. A manifestation of his repentance, and submission by outward signs, v. 6.

*{Then}* When God had so powerfully manifested his majesty to Job in the manner, and matter of his speech, then Job yields. For the particle ו see it so translated, Chap. 16. 1.

*{Job}* See on Chap. 1. 1.

*{answered}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

*{the LORD}* See on Chap. 38. 1.

*{and said}* See on Chap. 16. 1.

**V. 2. ***{I know that thou canst do every thing, and that no thought can be withholden from thee}* I do now acknowledge, that there is nothing, which you are not able to do, and that as your counsels are wise and just, so you want not power to bring to pass, whatsoever you have decreed. A thing that no man can do, for the greatest, and wisest, are often crossed in their designs. A short confession, but accepted of God, who looks not for many words, but for much faith.

*{I know}* Job knew this before, but his troubles had so distracted him, that for want of consideration he spoke sometimes as if he thought otherwise, but now being better instructed by God, he acknowledged his error ingenuously and freely.

*{that thou canst do every thing}* That you are almighty and can do whatever you will, or have determined.

*{and that no thought can be withholden from thee}* Or, *And that no thought of thine can be hindred.* That is, that you cannot be hindered from doing anything, that you intend to do. Gen. 18. 14. Jer. 32. 27. Luke 1. 37. Others understand it of Job's thoughts, as if he had confessed, that God knew all the hard thoughts he had of God's proceeding with him. But what need that confession, when Job had spoken so plainly in that point, that all his friends knew his meaning?

**V. 3. ***{Who is he, that hideth counsel without knowledge? therefore have I uttered that I understood not, things too wonderful for me, which I knew not}* Here it appears that Job marked God's words well, Chap. 38. 2. and was affected with them. For he confesses, that God had met with him in those words; for he had by unskillful speeches cast a cloud upon God's proceedings with him, and had spoken more than was fitting in things far above his capacity.

*{Who is he}* Or, *what manner of man is this?* As if he struck at Job in particular,

*{that hideth counsel without knowledge}* See on chap. 38. 2. where all these words are. Only for *Darkneth* there, it is, *Hideth*, here, which is all one in sense, for things in the dark can no more be seen, than things hidden.

*{therefore have I uttered, that I understood not}* I confess I am the man that have done so by passing my verdict on things in which I was not versed. Such unwise speeches came from him chap. 19. 6, 7. and elsewhere Broughton reads, *Therefore I tell, that I had not understanding.* That is, I confess now, that I spoke unwisely then. For uttered see on Chap. 15. 18. on the word, *told*.

*{things too wonderful for me, which I knew not}* I took upon me to speak of things far above my reach, and therefore no wonder if I spoke unadvisedly, and foolishly. Psal. 40. 5. & 141. 1. 139. 6. The whole verse may more plainly be read thus, *Thou hast said, who is he that hideth counsell without knowledge? therefore I confesse, that I have uttered, &c.* That is, you have acquainted me with my error, which I saw not before. See the like addition, Psal. 27. 8.

**V. 4. ***{Hear, I beseech thee, and I will speak, I will demand of thee, and declare thou unto me}* He suspects, there is more yet amiss in him, and desires to be further informed by God in those things, that are too high for him. I have formerly desired to contend with you Chap. 13. 22. and you have justly cast it in my teeth, chap. 38. 3. Ch. 40. 2. as if I thought I could teach you: but now I submit and desire to be instructed by you. For hear see on Ch. 13. 17.

*{I beseech thee}* Now Job speaks submissively unto God, as becomes him,

*{and I will speak}* I will seek resolution of you in what I am ignorant of, as follows in the verse. For the word, see on chap. 13. 22.

*{I will demand of thee}* I will not dispute with you, but desire to be instructed by you.

*{and declare thou unto me}* Heb. *make me to know.*

**V. 5. ***{I have heard of thee by the hearing of the ear, but now mine eye seeth thee}* I have been religiously bred in the knowledge of God, and heard many things of your infinite wisdom and power by tradition from my ancestors, but never had that honor to see a visible demonstration of your presence in a cloudy tempest, and to hear you speaking to me immediately till now. Thus God sometimes did appear, and speak to some eminent men in some weighty business in the Old Testament, that they might be confirmed, and teach others. Gen. 31. 10. Num. 12. 8. & 14. 14. 1 King. 22. 19. Job 38. 1. & 41. 6.

*{I have heard of thee by the hearing of the ear}* I have heard others speak highly of you. For heard, see on chap. 13. 17. For ear, see on chap. 13. 17. also.

*{but now mine eye seeth thee}* Job could not see God's essence, which is invisible, but saw some cloud, out of which God spoke to him, which was an extraordinary favor. For eye see on ch. 15. 15. on the word, *sight*. For seeth, see ch. 19. 27. on the word, *behold*.

**V. 6. ***{Wherefore I abhor myself, and repent in dust and ashes}* This familiar and powerful revelation of yourself, and of your will toward me, makes me not only to dislike my former impatient carriage, but even to loathe myself, and to repent heartily that ever I should so sin against you, and for the demonstration hereof, I will throw myself down in dust and ashes remembering that I am no better by nature, and therefore have very much forgotten myself in speaking so indiscreetly of my God.

*{Wherefore}* Because you have so graciously manifested yourself to me and showed me my error. Here ends the Hebrew meter, which begun at the 1. v. of ch. 3. The rest is in prose. So the historical part is in prose, the conferences in verse, and very likely so composed by the writer, rather than so spoken suddenly by the parties.

*{I abhor myself}* See on chap. 7. 16. on the word *loath*.

*{and repent}* The word נחם signifies sometimes to grieve for a man's sins here, and sometimes to take comfort, as Ezek. 32. 31. For comfort always follows true repentance.

*{in dust and ashes}* Throwing myself down upon the ground, Jer. 6. 26. & 25. 34. 2 Sam. 12. 16. Or sitting on the ground in dust or ashes. chap. 2. 8. Isa. 58. 4. Jon. 3. 6. Or casting dust and ashes on his head. chap. 2. 12. These things were in those days signs of great sorrow and hearty repentance. For dust see on chap. 14. 8. on the word, *ground*. For ashes, see on chap. 2. 8. & 13. 12.

**V. 7. ***{And it was so, that after the LORD had spoken these words unto Job, the LORD said to Eliphaz the Temanite, my wrath is kindled against thee, and against thy two friends, for ye have not spoken of me the thing that is right, as my servant Job hath}* Here begins the second part of the chapter. Wherein is first God's accusation of Job's three friends in this ver. 2. The way of reconciliation prescribed by God, v. 8. 3. Practiced by them, v. 9. Elihu is not here blamed, for he did not condemn Job for an hypocrite or wicked man, as they did; neither meddles with his former life, but only checked him for standing too much upon his own innocency, & by reason of his pain casting some aspersions upon God's dealing with him in his passion, & so made way for God's speech. Yet God commends not him neither, for he did not always make a right construction of Job's words. Now God passes sentence on Job's side, that (although he had spoken foolishly sometimes in his passion, for which God had formerly reproved him) yet he was in the right for the substance of the disputation, and his friends in the wrong; and therefore God was well pleased with him, but angry with them.

*{And it was so}* It came so to pass.

*{that after the LORD had spoken these words unto Job}* It is likely that Job's friends stayed to hear both Elihu's speech, and God's, and that presently after God had done, and Job had submitted, he passes this sentence on them for Job's greater comfort. For the Lord, see on chap. 38.1. For spoken, see on ch. 13.22. For words, see on chap. 19.28, on the word, *matter*. For Job see on chap. 1.1.

*{The LORD said to Eliphaz the Temanite}* God directs his speech to him, as to the chief, who took upon him first to speak to Job, chap. 2.11. & 4.1. For the Lord, see on chap. 33.1. For said, see on chap. 16.1. For Eliphaz the Temanite, see on chap. 2.11.

*{my wrath is kindled against thee}* I am very angry with you. See on chap. 32.2.

*{and against thy two friends}* Bildad and Zophar, chap. 2.11. who were indeed Eliphaz his friends, rather than Job's, for they joined with him in condemning Job. For friend, see on chap. 16.29.

*{for ye have not spoken of me the thing that is right, as my servant Job hath}* You have not spoken so truly of my proceedings as he has done; for you have maintained, that my severe judgments lighted only upon ungodly men, which had been enough to make Job despair, and did drive him to many passionate speeches. But he recovered himself, and maintained stoutly against you, that I did sometimes afflict mine own servants heavily to try their graces, as now I have done to him at Satan's instigation. And therefore he shall now find comfort, for he has done me right, and overcome Satan, and you, whom he has used as instruments to discourage him. For spoken, see on ch. 13.22. Of me, that is, concerning me, and my proceedings. So אל is used for, of, Gen. 20.2. Others read, *To me*. You would have me to judge between you and Job, and so have directed your speeches to me, chap. 11.5. I pronounce that you have not maintained the truth in this controversy. The word נכון for right signifies sometimes established, as ch. 21.8. Sometimes ready at hand, as ch. 15.23. Sometimes right, as here. Job is called God's servant, a great honor, and it may be an hint to them, that they in their wrath had served Satan against God and Job, Eph. 4.27. For Job, see on chap. 1.1. Some understand it thus, Job has spoken unadvisedly of me, you have spoken worse; but he has confessed his fault, you have not done so. But it is not likely that God would object this unto him, for he had showed Job his errors, but not them.

**V. 8. ***{Therefore take unto you now seven bullocks, and seven rams, and go to my servant Job, and offer up for yourselves a burnt offering, and my servant Job shall pray for you; for him will I accept; lest I deal with you after your folly, in that ye have not spoken of me the thing which is right, like my servant Job}* Now God in mercy shows unto them the way of reconciliation. Two things God requires of them, First, that as they had greatly sinned, so they should provide a great sacrifice. Secondly, that they should bring it to Job, whom God makes the priest to offer, and pray for them. The Lord withal intimates that he will then be reconciled to them, and threatens that if they do not so, he will punish them for their foolish carriage in disparaging him in their former conference.

*{Therefore}* Because you have provoked me to anger by your sins.

*{take unto you now seven bullocks, and seven rams}* A great sacrifice to put them in mind of the greatness of their sin. Some conceive that each of them was to offer so many, and that had been a very great sacrifice. But that appears not in the text. For take, see on ch. 15.12. on the word, *carry away*. Seven bullocks. Seven was a number much used, both in sacrifices and other things. ch. 2.13. Numb. 23.1. 1 Chron. 15.26. Neither was there any greater sacrifice ordinarily offered, nor so great enjoined in the law for the greatest sin, or person, whether you look at the number, or greatness of the cattle. It is likely also that they were rich, for God did accept of meaner sacrifices from poorer men. And seven rams. Rams according to the translation. Strength in the original.

*{and go to my servant Job}* God was pleased to send them to Job. 1. Because they had wronged him, and therefore must reconcile themselves to him, before they offer a sacrifice to God, Mat. 5.24. So careful is God of the credit of his servants, that he will not be reconciled where they are wronged, till they be righted. God would have them by this means to acknowledge Job for a good man, whom they had condemned for an hypocrite, and so entreat him to speak for them, whom they had lately used so scornfully. So we read in the book of Martyrs, in Cranmer's life that King Hen. 8. would not be reconciled to Sir John Gostwick for accusing the Archbishop falsely, till he came to speak for him. Thus God tries their modesty and submission. 2. Because God would have them to know that Job was reconciled to them as well as he. For go, see on ch. 14.20. on the word, *passeth*. For servant, see on verse 7. For Job, see on chap. 1.1.

*{and offer up for yourselves a burnt offering}* Let Job do it for you, do you bring it to him, and he shall offer it to me for your good. For the Israelites did not offer their own sacrifices, but the priest. Thus God honors Job, makes him a priest to offer sacrifice for them, and three times in this verse calls him his servant. For the words, see on chap. 1.5. There was afterwards some difference between sin-offerings and burnt-offerings, but very likely that was not known in Job's time.

*{and my servant Job shall pray for you}* He shall not only offer up your sacrifice, but he shall also by prayer obtain pardon of your sins committed against me and him. See the like of Abraham, Gen. 20.7. For servant see on v. 7. For Job, on Chap. 1.1.

*{for him will I accept}* I am so offended at you, that I will neither receive offering nor prayer from you, but I will take both from him in your behalf, so much do I accept of his person and faithful service. The word translated *for*, commonly signifies *but*, yet here the sense requires the former, because it gives a reason of what went before. So this word is rendered, Prov. 23.18. For him will I accept Heb. *his face will I accept*. See on chap. 32.21. Though the phrase be there used in a bad sense, and here in a good. As also Gen. 19.21.

*{lest I deal with you after your folly}* Or, *lest I do that which may be a disgrace unto you*. For deal, see on chap. 14.9. on the word, *bring forth*. Folly is put for the punishment of folly, as sin oftentimes for the punishment of sin, 1 King. 13.34.

*{in that ye have not spoken of me the thing which is right, like my servant Job}* See on v. 7. where all these words are which are repeated here, to show that God was very angry with them.

**V. 9. ***{So Eliphaz the Temanite, and Bildad the Shuhite, and Zophar the Naamathite went, and did according as the LORD commanded them: the LORD also accepted Job}* Here they are all three named, lest any should mistake, and think Elihu to be one of them with whom God was angry. And as these three showed their ready obedience in doing what was required, and Job his readiness to be reconciled, so God showed his infinite mercy in forgiving so speedily.

*{So Eliphaz the Temanite, and Bildad the Shuhite, and Zophar the Naamathite}* See on chap. 2.11.

*{went}* See on chap. 14.20. on the word, *passeth*.

*{and did}* See on chap. 14.9. on the word, *bring forth*.

*{according as the LORD commanded}* v. 8. They brought their sacrifices to Job, and entreated him to pray for them. For the Lord, see on chap. 38.1. For commanded, see on chap. 13.22. on the word, *speak*.

*{The LORD also accepted Job}* See on v. 8. Heb. *the face of Job*.

**V. 10. ***{And the LORD turned the captivity of Job when he prayed for his friends: also the LORD gave Job twice as much as he had before}* Here begins the last part of this chapter, and book, the restoration of Job after all his misery, which is set out 1. In general, in this verse. 2. In particular, to the end of the chapter. And that 1. In the access, and gifts of his friends, v. 11. 2. In wealth, v. 12. 3. In children, v. 13,14,15. 4. In long life, and numerous posterity, v. 16. 5. In a comfortable death. v. 17. The entreaty that Job had made for his friends that dealt so hardly with him, God took in so good part, that he turned all his sorrows wherewith Satan had as it were held him captive into joy, and gave him twice so much as he had taken from him.

*{And the LORD turned the captivity of Job}* Some understand it of restoring those riches to him which were taken from him before. But it is rather to be understood of taking away Satan's commission, who by God's permission held Job hitherto as a prisoner, and bound him with bands of poverty, and sores, which now God frees him from; but whether immediately, or by degrees, is not recorded. Thus Job speaks of himself as of a prisoner. chap. 13.27. And David, Psal. 69.33. See the like phrase, chap. 36.8. For the Lord, see on chap. 38.1. For turned, see on chap. 14.13. on the word *past*. Captivity must be interpreted as before, for Job was never led away into captivity. For Job, see on chap. 1.1.

*{when he prayed for his friends}* Some understand this of the act of his prayer. While he was praying for them, God either by fire from heaven to devour the sacrifice, or by sudden healing his sores, or sending all his friends to him in that instant, showed that he liked so well of his charity, that he was fully reconciled both to him and his friends. Others read, *when he had prayed*. Intimating, that after the end of his prayer God did either presently, or by degrees give him health and riches again. For prayed, see on v. 8. The Hebrew word for friends is singular, and may intimate, that Job prayed for each of them severally. For the word, see on chap. 16.20.

*{also the LORD gave Job twice as much as he had before}* God not only restored, but doubled Job's riches, as appears by comparing chap. 1.3. with ver. 12. of this chap. Only his children were not doubled, as is manifest by comparing ch. 1.2. with v. 13. of this ch. And it appears that God intended the doubling of his wealth by the particulars mentioned v. 11,12. And he gave him as many children as before; and twice so much wealth to enjoy while he lived, and leave his children when he died. Heb. *Added all that had been to Job unto the double*. For the Lord, see on chap. 38.1. For Job, on chap. 1.1.

**V. 11. ***{Then came there unto him all his brethren, and all his sisters, and all they that had been of his acquaintance before, and did eat bread with him in his house, and they bemoaned him, and comforted him over all the evil that the LORD had brought upon him; and every man also gave him a piece of money, and every one an ear-ring of gold}* This verse contains the beginning of the restoration of Job's comfort, honor, and wealth, by the access and gifts of his friends.

*{Then}* After God had accepted Job, and he had prayed for his friends. So this particle is used, Ch. 16.1. Others read it, *for*, as giving a reason of his future prosperity from the gifts of his friends. So it is rendered Chap. 11.4. Others read, *therefore*. As making it an inference from what went before. These friends came not at him in his misery; but when God made him prosper again, then they would own him. So it is rendered, Chap. 35.16.

*{came there unto him}* To visit him. It is likely that God changed their minds to come to Job for his comfort.

*{all his brethren, and all his sisters}* All his kindred male and female. For brethren, see on chap. 19.13.

*{and all they that had been of his acquaintance before}* All his neighbors and friends that conversed familiarly with him before his afflictions, but then left him, as he complains bitterly, chap. 19.13. &c. Heb. *all that knew him*.

*{and did eat bread with him}* Feasted with him. So this phrase is used Gen. 31.54. For eat, see on ch. 21.25. For bread see on ch. 15.23.

*{in his house}* For Job's house was not blown down; nor his goods within doors hurt. For house see on chap. 15.28.

*{and they bemoaned him, and comforted him over all the evil}* For all these words, see on chap. 2.11.

*{that the LORD had brought upon him}* So Job acknowledges, chap. 1.21. Neither could Satan do any thing to him without commission from God, chap. 1.12. & 2.6. For the Lord, see on chap. 38.1.

*{every man also gave him a piece of money}* This they did as a pledge of their renewed friendship, and toward the recovery of his losses. Heb. *A man*. That is every man. For gave, see on ch. 14.4. on the word, *bring*. Some conceive they gave him each of them a lamb to stock him again. But it appears to be a piece of money (peradventure with a lamb stamped on it) by the use of the same word, Gen. 33.19. Josh. 24.32. So Angels are called with us from the stamp of the coin. And that was a greater gift than a lamb, and more suitable to the golden earring. So Steven interprets Moses, Act. 7.16.

*{and every one an ear-ring of gold}* In way of congratulation of his recovery out of his malady. This gift is conceived to be a gold ring with some jewels hanging on it worn in the ear, or nose, or hanging down from the forehead.

**V. 12. ***{So the LORD blessed the latter end of Job more than his beginning, for he had fourteen thousand sheep, and six thousand camels, and a thousand yoke of oxen, and a thousand she asses}* God not only gave plenty of cattle to Job, but twice so many as he had before. See on ver. 10. Thus Bildad proves a true prophet, chap. 8.7.

*{So the LORD}* See on chap. 38.1.

*{blessed}* By making his cattle wonderfully to increase.

*{the latter end of Job more than his beginning}* See on chap. 8.7. And for Job, see on chap. 1.1.

*{for}* The words following prove the former to be true by a particular enumeration of his cattle. Thus this particle is translated, chap. 11.4.

*{he had fourteen thousand sheep, and six thousand camels, and a thousand yoke of oxen, and a thousand she asses}* For these words, see on verse 10. and on chap. 1.3.

**V. 13. ***{He had also seven sons, and three daughters}* Had Job had no children to have left this great estate unto, his comfort had been the less, Gen. 15.2. And children are a greater blessing than cattle.
