Therefore God gave Job many children after his restoration, to show that he was fully reconciled to him, and to comfort him for the loss of his former children. It is not mentioned by what wife he had them, but it is likely by the same, because neither her death, nor any other wife of his is mentioned. For the words, see on ver. 10. and on chap. 1.2. There they are put before his estate, here after it, for no doubt God gave his riches before he could have so many children.

**V. 14. ***{And he called the name of the first, Jemima, and the name of the second, Kezia, and the name of the third, Keren-happuch}* The names of Job's daughters are set down in this verse, their beauty, and inheritance in the next. Their names are set down 1. To show the truth of the history. 2. To take occasion to commend their beauty. 3. Because their names are lost in marriage, whereas the names of sons remain. Hence the male זכר has his name from remembrance in the original. The females נשים from forgetfulness. These names imposed by Job, may have relation to the beauty of his daughters; which being one of the excellencies of nature, and some part of God's image, and a thing much looked upon in women, and accounted the portion of maids, he thought fit to set out their names. The first had her name from the day to show the brightness of her beauty. The second from a most precious and sweet smelling spice, to show, what great account would be made of her comeliness. The third from the horn, wherein proud persons used to put their coloring stuff to make them beautiful, intimating that she was more beautiful by nature, than they by all their art. These names also may have relation to Job's former misery, and present delivery to nourish thankfulness in him. The name of the first may be opposed to his former darkness, or trouble (now taken away) of which he complains chap. 19.8. The second opposed to his former corruption, and stench of his wounds (now healed) of which he complains ch. 7.5. The third opposed to his former weeping and slurried countenance (now made clean and fair) of which he complains. ch. 16.16. Thus godly men used to put themselves in mind of their miseries, and God's mercies by the names of their children. So did Lamech. Gen. 5.29. And Joseph, Gen. 41.51,52.

*{And he}* Job gave the names. This was done sometimes by the father, as Gen. 4.5.29. Sometimes by the mother, as Gen. 29.32,33. & 1 Sam. 1.20.

*{called the name}* Imposed that name upon her. For called see on chap. 14.15.

*{of the first}* Heb. *Of the one*. For the first is the one, and only one till there come a second.

*{Jemima}* See before in this verse for the signification of it.

*{and the name of the second Keziah}* See also before.

*{and the name of the third Keren happuch}* The horn of beauty. See before. The word horn sets out abundance or excellency. See on ch. 16.15. The other word is translated sometimes painting, and sometimes glistering, and fair colors. See 2 King. 9.30. Jer. 4.30. & 1 Chron. 29.2. Isa. 54.11. All these places point at rare beauty.

**V. 15. ***{And in all the land there were no women found so fair as the daughters of Job: and their father gave them inheritance among their brethren}* Here is an exposition of what went before. Those names were not given to Job's daughters without a cause, because there were none in all the country to be compared with them in beauty, and comeliness. Here is also an addition of an unusual favor, and a great token of their father's love, that he equaled them to his sons in portions.

*{and in all the land}* Not in all the earth, but in all that country, where they dwelt. chap. 1.1. For the word see on chap. 15.19. on the word *Earth*.

*{were no women found}* The verb is singular joined with a noun plural, to show, that not one woman was so beautiful, as any one of Job's daughters in all that country. For women see on verse 14. Broughton reads. *No woman-kind*. Found for Being, see Mal. 2.6. 2 Chron. 19.3. For Finding see on chap. 17.10.

*{so fair}* So beautiful and comely.

*{as the daughters}* See on chap. 1.2.

*{of Job}* See on chap. 1.1.

*{and their father}* See on chap. 15.10. Job is here intended, though not named.

*{Gave them inheritance among their brethren}* This was a great favor, and unusual, arguing much love in Job, and done to that end that he might the better prefer them in marriage, or, as others think, to keep them from marrying idolaters, having a sufficient means to live plentifully of themselves. The verb *found* is masculine: so are the affixes in the original, for Job counted them as his sons in the division of his estate. The like is observed in God's building houses for the Egyptian midwives. Exod. 1.21. For gave see on chap. 14.4. on the word *bring*.

*{among their brethren}* Heb. *In the midst of their brethren*. This not only sets out the place but also an equal portion. For Brethren see on chap. 19.13.

**V. 16. ***{After this lived Job an hundred and forty years, and saw his sons, and his sons' sons, even four generations}* The length of Job's life which also was a blessing that he might the longer enjoy the comfort of his goods and children is set out in this v. both in the number of his years of prosperity after his misery, and in seeing a great posterity. The Jews conceived that he lived twice so long after his troubles as before, and so should be aged 70 years, when his troubles began, because God doubled what he had before. A weak argument: for why should his years be doubled more than his children? It is likely he was in the strength of his years, for he had had 10 children. And the trial was the greater; that those years, which brought comfort to others, should bring so much trouble upon him. That God might heap all manner of blessings upon his victorious champion, he lived after this conflict, and trial an hundred, and forty years, and that in continual health, and prosperity, in so much, that he saw his children's children unto a fourth generation.

*{and after this}* So it is in the original. Some conceive that Job's whole life is set down here: but that is against the letter of the text, which mentions only the time of his life after his restoration, to show God's great mercy, who gave him so long time of comfort afterwards. But how long Job lived in all is uncertain, because the Scripture mentions not his age, when his troubles began.

*{lived Job}* He continued here in this world.

*{an hundred and forty years}* A large time of prosperity after his misery. For Job see on chap. 1.1.

*{and saw}* This was a great delight to him in his old age. For the word see on chap. 19.27. on, Behold.

*{his sons, and his sons' sons}* He had a succession of young ones coming out of himself to take pleasure in all that long time of his life. For Sonnes see on chap. 14.21.

*{even four generations}* Something more, than Joseph saw, for he lived to see but the third generation. Gen. 5.23.

**V. 17. ***{So Job died being old, and full of days}* An happy conclusion of a godly life. So thus it is translated ver. 12.

*{Job}* See on chap. 1.1.

*{died}* See on chap. 3.11.

*{being old}* See on chap. 12.20. on the word, *Aged*.

*{and full of days}* Heb. *Satisfied with days*. He lived to a very old age, till he was weary of the world, and willing to go to heaven. See the like of Abraham Gen. 25.8. whom Job very likely did equal in age, if not exceed. And it is likely, that he lived about Isaac's length of years, of whom as much is said, Gen. 26.35.22. Herein Eliphaz was a true prophet, ch. 5.26. For Ful see on ch. 14.1. See there also for days.
